## Biology (1439 Questions)

### Acellular Life (57 Questions)

**Q1.** Which of the following is a key characteristic that considers viruses non-living?
 **A.** They have DNA or RNA.
 **B.** They can mutate.
 **C.** They lack cellular structure. ✓
 **D.** They are obligate intracellular parasites.
**Answer:** They lack cellular structure.

**Q2.** Which component of a virus protects the viral genome from host nucleases?
**Answer:** Tail fibers

**Q3.** What type of nucleic acid is predominantly found in plant viruses?
 **A.** Double-stranded DNA
 **B.** Single-stranded DNA
 **C.** Double-stranded RNA
 **D.** Single-stranded RNA ✓
**Answer:** Single-stranded RNA

**Q4.** An additional lipoprotein layer derived from the host cell membrane that surrounds some viruses is called the:
 **A.** Envelope ✓
 **B.** Matrix protein
**Answer:** Envelope

**Q5.** The protein subunits that make up the capsid of a virus are called:
**Answer:** Glycoproteins

**Q6.** Viruses that cause diseases like smallpox and polio are categorized as:
 **A.** Bacteriophages
 **B.** Plant viruses
 **C.** Animal viruses ✓
 **D.** Fungal viruses
**Answer:** Animal viruses

**Q7.** Which of the following viral symmetries is characteristic of Tobacco Mosaic Virus (TMV)?
 **A.** Icosahedral
 **B.** Polyhedral
 **C.** Helical ✓
 **D.** Complex
**Answer:** Helical

**Q8.** David Baltimore's classification system for viruses is primarily based on:
 **A.** Host specificity
 **B.** Mode of replication and genome type ✓
 **C.** Presence or absence of an envelope
 **D.** Size and shape of the virion
**Answer:** Mode of replication and genome type

**Q9.** What is the primary function of glycoprotein spikes on the viral envelope?
 **A.** Energy production for the virus.
 **B.** Replication of the viral genome.
 **C.** Recognition and attachment to host cell receptors. ✓
 **D.** Breakdown of host cell DNA.
**Answer:** Recognition and attachment to host cell receptors.

**Q10.** Which of the following is NOT typically found in the core of a virion?
 **A.** Viral genome
 **B.** Enzymes for replication
 **C.** Ribosomes ✓
 **D.** Reverse transcriptase
**Answer:** Ribosomes

**Q11.** A virus with a polyhedral or spherical shape typically exhibits which type of capsid symmetry?
 **A.** Helical
 **B.** Filamentous
 **C.** Icosahedral ✓
 **D.** Complex
**Answer:** Icosahedral

**Q12.** The term "virus" originates from a Latin word meaning:
 **A.** Small
 **B.** Toxin
 **C.** Poison ✓
 **D.** Invisible
**Answer:** Poison

**Q13.** Which of the following is considered a "living characteristic" of viruses?
 **A.** Can be crystallized and stored.
 **B.** Lack cellular structure.
 **C.** Behave as inert infectious particles outside host.
 **D.** Reproduce using host cell machinery. ✓
**Answer:** Reproduce using host cell machinery.

**Q14.** Which type of virus is characterized by having a double-stranded DNA genome and a complex structure, often described as "tadpole-shaped"?
 **A.** Plant virus
 **B.** Bacteriophage ✓
 **C.** Animal virus with envelope
 **D.** Retrovirus
**Answer:** Bacteriophage

**Q15.** The property of viruses that allows them to be crystallized and stored without losing infectivity is considered a:
 **A.** Living characteristic
 **B.** Non-living characteristic ✓
 **C.** Metabolic characteristic
 **D.** Genetic characteristic
**Answer:** Non-living characteristic

**Q16.** Which of the following is an example of an animal virus that is a double-stranded DNA (dsDNA) virus?
 **A.** TMV
 **B.** HIV
 **C.** Adenovirus ✓
 **D.** Poliovirus
**Answer:** Adenovirus

**Q17.** What is the specific enzyme found in retroviruses that converts their single-stranded RNA genome into double-stranded DNA?
 **A.** DNA polymerase
 **B.** RNA polymerase
 **C.** Reverse transcriptase ✓
 **D.** Ligase
**Answer:** Reverse transcriptase

**Q18.** Viruses are obligate intracellular parasites because they lack:
 **A.** A protein coat
 **B.** A nucleic acid genome
 **C.** Metabolic enzymes ✓
 **D.** Specific host receptors
**Answer:** Metabolic enzymes

**Q19.** Which of the following is a structural component found in bacteriophages but generally not in animal viruses like HIV?
 **A.** Capsid
 **B.** Nucleic acid
 **C.** Tail fibers ✓
 **D.** Envelope
**Answer:** Tail fibers

**Q20.** The number of capsomeres in a virus's capsid is:
 **A.** Variable and changes with host.
 **B.** Always 252 for all viruses.
 **C.** Specific to the particular virus. ✓
 **D.** Determined by the host cell.
**Answer:** Specific to the particular virus.

**Q21.** Which classification type would include viruses that cause Rous sarcoma and warts?
 **A.** Bacteriophages
 **B.** Plant viruses
 **C.** Animal viruses ✓
 **D.** Fungal viruses
**Answer:** Animal viruses

**Q22.** Which of these viral components is responsible for protecting the viral genome from denaturation by pH and temperature outside the host cell?
 **A.** Tail fibers
 **B.** Glycoproteins
 **C.** Capsid ✓
 **D.** Reverse transcriptase
**Answer:** Capsid

**Q23.** Why are non-enveloped viruses generally more stable and able to survive longer outside the host than enveloped viruses?
 **A.** They have a stronger capsid.
 **B.** Their genetic material is more robust.
 **C.** Their outer protein coat is less susceptible to degradation than a lipid envelope. ✓
 **D.** They do not require a host to replicate.
**Answer:** Their outer protein coat is less susceptible to degradation than a lipid envelope.

**Q24.** Tobacco Mosaic Virus (TMV) is an example of a virus primarily classified by its:
 **A.** Complex structure
 **B.** Enveloped nature
 **C.** Helical symmetry ✓
 **D.** Icosahedral symmetry
**Answer:** Helical symmetry

**Q25.** Which of the following enzymes is found within the viral core of retroviruses like HIV?
 **A.** DNA ligase
 **B.** RNA primase
 **C.** Integrase ✓
 **D.** Helicase
**Answer:** Integrase

**Q26.** The early evidence for viruses, demonstrated by Iwanowsky (1892) with tobacco mosaic disease, showed that the causative agent was:
 **A.** A bacterium that could be cultured.
 **B.** A toxin produced by plants.
 **C.** A filterable agent smaller than bacteria. ✓
 **D.** A fungus visible under a light microscope.
**Answer:** A filterable agent smaller than bacteria.

**Q27.** Viruses are distinct from prions and viroids primarily because viruses possess:
 **A.** A protein coat.
 **B.** A nucleic acid genome. ✓
 **C.** The ability to cause disease.
 **D.** Resistance to heat.
**Answer:** A nucleic acid genome.

**Q28.** The causative agent of AIDS was identified in which year?
 **A.** 1980
 **B.** 1982
 **C.** 1984 ✓
 **D.** 1986
**Answer:** 1984

**Q29.** Which of the following describes the shape of the Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV)?
 **A.** Tadpole-shaped
 **B.** Rod-shaped
 **C.** Spherical ✓
 **D.** Filamentous
**Answer:** Spherical

**Q30.** What are the two main glycoproteins embedded in the outer lipoprotein envelope of HIV?
 **A.** gp41 and gp160
 **B.** gp120 and gp41 ✓
 **C.** gp24 and gp17
 **D.** gp120 and gp60
**Answer:** gp120 and gp41

**Q31.** The enzyme responsible for converting the single-stranded HIV RNA genome into complementary DNA (cDNA) within the host cell is:
 **A.** Integrase
 **B.** Transcriptase
 **C.** Reverse transcriptase ✓
 **D.** RNA polymerase
**Answer:** Reverse transcriptase

**Q32.** Which specific type of host cell does HIV primarily attack due to the presence of CD4 receptors?
 **A.** B cells
 **B.** Red blood cells
 **C.** Helper T cells ✓
 **D.** Natural killer cells
**Answer:** Helper T cells

**Q33.** In the HIV life cycle, after the double-stranded cDNA is formed, what is the next step?
 **A.** Reverse transcription
 **B.** Transcription of viral mRNA
 **C.** Integration into host cell DNA ✓
 **D.** Assembly of new virions
**Answer:** Integration into host cell DNA

**Q34.** What is the term for the integrated viral DNA within the host cell chromosome during the HIV life cycle?
 **A.** Provirus ✓
 **B.** Prophage
 **C.** Plasmid
 **D.** Virion
**Answer:** Provirus

**Q35.** Which of the following is an early symptom that may appear during the "Asymptomatic carrier" stage of AIDS?
 **A.** Severe weight loss
 **B.** Persistent night sweats
 **C.** Swollen lymph glands ✓
 **D.** Opportunistic infections
**Answer:** Swollen lymph glands

**Q36.** The primary reason HIV leads to the development of AIDS is that it:
 **A.** Directly causes skin cancer.
 **B.** Produces toxins that poison the body.
 **C.** Destroys T-cells. ✓
 **D.** Targets and destroys red blood cells.
**Answer:** Destroys T-cells.

**Q37.** Diseases that attack an AIDS victim due to a weakened immune system are known as:
 **A.** Autoimmune diseases
 **B.** Genetic diseases
 **C.** Opportunistic diseases ✓
 **D.** Communicable diseases
**Answer:** Opportunistic diseases

**Q38.** Antiretroviral therapy (ART) for HIV is best described as a treatment that:
 **A.** Completely cures HIV infection.
 **B.** Boosts the immune system directly.
 **C.** Controls the virus and prolongs life but is not a cure. ✓
 **D.** Prevents attachment of HIV to cells.
**Answer:** Controls the virus and prolongs life but is not a cure.

**Q39.** Which of the following is a common mode of HIV transmission?
 **A.** Sharing food utensils.
 **B.** Mosquito bites.
 **C.** Casual contact like hugging.
 **D.** Sharing used syringes/needles. ✓
**Answer:** Sharing used syringes/needles.

**Q40.** The HIV matrix proteins form a shell located:
 **A.** Inside the capsid.
 **B.** Between the envelope and the capsid. ✓
 **C.** Outside the envelope.
 **D.** Embedded within the viral RNA.
**Answer:** Between the envelope and the capsid.

**Q41.** Which stage of AIDS is characterized by persistent swollen lymph glands, night sweats, and persistent diarrhea?
**Answer:** Asymptomatic carrier

**Q42.** The uncoating of the HIV virion after entry into the host cell results in the release of:
 **A.** Only viral proteins.
 **B.** Viral RNA and enzymes. ✓
 **C.** Only viral DNA.
 **D.** Entire intact virion.
**Answer:** Viral RNA and enzymes.

**Q43.** Which of the following is NOT a common control measure against the transmission of HIV?
 **A.** Screening blood for transfusion.
 **B.** Sharing toothbrushes. ✓
 **C.** Not using used syringes/needles.
 **D.** All of the options.
**Answer:** Sharing toothbrushes.

**Q44.** The lipid bilayer of the HIV envelope is primarily derived from what source?
 **A.** Viral biosynthesis.
 **B.** Host cell membrane during budding. ✓
 **C.** Host cell nucleus.
 **D.** Environmental lipids.
**Answer:** Host cell membrane during budding.

**Q45.** The enzyme that facilitates the incorporation of HIV's double-stranded DNA into the host cell's DNA is:
 **A.** Reverse transcriptase
 **B.** Protease
 **C.** Integrase ✓
 **D.** RNA polymerase
**Answer:** Integrase

**Q46.** Which of the following conditions is considered an 'opportunistic disease' that may attack an AIDS victim?
 **A.** Common cold
 **B.** Influenza
 **C.** Skin cancer ✓
 **D.** Measles
**Answer:** Skin cancer

**Q47.** HIV is primarily named so because it directly leads to:
 **A.** The formation of antibodies against itself.
 **B.** A decrease in Helper T cells. ✓
 **C.** An increase in red blood cell count.
 **D.** The direct destruction of all white blood cells.
**Answer:** A decrease in Helper T cells.

**Q48.** What happens to the host cell after mature HIV virions bud off from it?
 **A.** It becomes immortal and continues replication.
 **B.** It immediately undergoes lysis.
 **C.** It loses functionality and eventually dies. ✓
 **D.** It converts into a different cell type.
**Answer:** It loses functionality and eventually dies.

**Q49.** Which of the following is NOT a mode of HIV transmission?
 **A.** Sexual contact.
 **B.** Mosquito bites. ✓
 **C.** Mother to newborn during birth.
 **D.** Blood transfusion with unscreened blood.
**Answer:** Mosquito bites.

**Q50.** The severe weight loss and weakness are characteristic symptoms of which stage of AIDS?
**Answer:** Asymptomatic carrier

**Q51.** The enzyme that breaks down large structural proteins into smaller, functional units during HIV's biosynthesis is:
 **A.** Reverse transcriptase
 **B.** Integrase
 **C.** Protease ✓
 **D.** Ribonuclease
**Answer:** Protease

**Q52.** After HIV's single-stranded cDNA is synthesized, what enzyme disintegrates the viral genomic RNA?
 **A.** Reverse transcriptase
 **B.** Ribonuclease ✓
 **C.** Transcriptase
 **D.** Protease
**Answer:** Ribonuclease

**Q53.** Which statement accurately describes the effect of Antiretroviral Therapy (ART) on HIV-positive individuals?
 **A.** It eliminates the virus completely from the body.
 **B.** It significantly delays the progression of the disease and can extend lifespan. ✓
 **C.** It is a one-time treatment that guarantees a cure.
 **D.** It has no side effects and is inexpensive.
**Answer:** It significantly delays the progression of the disease and can extend lifespan.

**Q54.** What is the role of gp120 glycoprotein in the HIV life cycle?
 **A.** Fusion of viral and host cell membranes.
 **B.** Integration of viral DNA into host genome.
 **C.** Attachment to CD4 proteins on host cells. ✓
 **D.** Cleavage of viral proteins.
**Answer:** Attachment to CD4 proteins on host cells.

**Q55.** The process by which HIV enters the host cell after fusion of the viral envelope with the cell membrane is:
 **A.** Exocytosis
 **B.** Direct Fusion ✓
 **C.** Endocytosis
 **D.** Active transport
**Answer:** Direct Fusion

### Bioenergetics (81 Questions)

**Q58.** Which of the following best defines Bioenergetics?
**Answer:** Bioenergetics is specifically defined as the quantitative study of energy relationships and conversions in biological systems, adhering to the laws of thermodynamics.

**Q59.** What is the primary source of energy for almost all life on Earth?
**Answer:** All life on this planet Earth is powered, directly or indirectly, by solar energy.

**Q60.** How do plants primarily convert light energy into chemical energy?
**Answer:** The chloroplasts of plants capture light energy and convert it into chemical energy stored in sugars and other organic molecules.

**Q61.** The accumulation of which molecule in the atmosphere made the evolution of respiration possible?
**Answer:** With the emergence of photosynthesis on earth, molecular oxygen began to accumulate slowly in the atmosphere. The presence of free oxygen made possible the evolution of respiration.

**Q62.** What is the role of ATP in biological systems as described in the context of bioenergetics?
**Answer:** ATP is described as a kind of chemical link between catabolism and anabolism because it couples energy-releasing (catabolic) reactions with energy-requiring (anabolic) reactions.

**Q63.** Which of the following processes releases a great deal of energy and couples some of this energy to ATP formation?
**Answer:** Respiration releases great deal of energy, and couples some of this energy to the formation of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) molecules.

**Q64.** What are the energy-poor inorganic compounds that are reduced to energy-rich carbohydrates during photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Photosynthesis is the process where energy-poor inorganic compounds (carbon dioxide and water) are reduced to energy-rich carbohydrates (glucose).

**Q65.** From which reactant does the oxygen released during photosynthesis originate?
**Answer:** Oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water, a concept hypothesized by Van Niel and later confirmed using isotopic tracers.

**Q66.** What are the primary sites of photosynthesis in plants?
**Answer:** Chloroplasts: These are the primary sites of photosynthesis in plants, found mainly in mesophyll cells.

**Q67.** Which part of the chloroplast contains the thylakoids, where light-dependent reactions occur?
**Answer:** Chloroplasts contain stroma, and thylakoids, where light-dependent reactions occur on grana. Grana are stacks of thylakoids.

**Q68.** Which of the following pigments are primarily responsible for absorbing violet-blue and orange-red wavelengths of light in plants?
**Answer:** Chlorophylls mainly absorb violet-blue and orange-red wavelengths, giving plants their green color.

**Q69.** What is the main function of carotenoids in photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Carotenoids broaden the spectrum of light used for photosynthesis and protect chlorophyll from intense light.

**Q70.** Which set of products is directly generated from light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** The light-dependent reactions directly use light energy to produce ATP and NADPH, which are then used in light-independent reactions.

**Q71.** Which of the following statements is TRUE regarding photosynthesis and respiration in plants?
**Answer:** Photosynthesis primarily occurs during the daytime, whereas respiration continues during the day and night.

**Q72.** The concept that oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water was initially hypothesized by:
**Answer:** Oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water, a concept hypothesized by Van Niel.

**Q73.** In plants, chloroplasts are primarily found in which type of cells?
**Answer:** Chloroplasts are found mainly in mesophyll cells.

**Q74.** Which part of the chloroplast is the site for light-independent (dark) reactions?
**Answer:** Dark reactions occur in the stroma of the chloroplast.

**Q75.** Photosystem I (PSI) is characterized by a special pair of chlorophyll 'a' molecules known as P700. What does 'P700' refer to?
**Answer:** P700 refers to the reaction-center chlorophyll 'a' that absorbs light energy of 700 nm.

**Q76.** What is the primary product of cyclic photophosphorylation?
**Answer:** Cyclic photophosphorylation involves only Photosystem I and generates ATP but not NADPH or oxygen.

**Q77.** The process of photolysis in Photosystem II involves the splitting of what molecule to replace lost electrons?
**Answer:** In Photosystem II, electrons lost are replaced by electrons from water (photolysis), releasing oxygen.

**Q78.** Which of the following is NOT produced during non-cyclic photophosphorylation (Z-scheme)?
**Answer:** Non-cyclic photophosphorylation produces ATP, NADPH, and oxygen. Glucose is produced in the Calvin Cycle.

**Q79.** The mechanism by which ATP is synthesized, involving proton pumping across membranes during photosynthesis, is known as:
**Answer:** Chemiosmosis is the mechanism for ATP synthesis in photophosphorylation, using redox reactions to pump protons across thylakoid membranes.

**Q80.** Light-independent reactions (Calvin Cycle) primarily occur in which part of the chloroplast?
**Answer:** The Light-Independent Reactions (Calvin Cycle) occur in the stroma.

**Q81.** What is the main purpose of the light-independent reactions?
**Answer:** light-independent reactions ( Calvin cycle ) use ATP and NADPH from light reactions to fix carbon dioxide and synthesize carbohydrates.

**Q82.** The carbon fixation phase of the Calvin Cycle involves the incorporation of CO₂ into an organic molecule. What is the CO₂ acceptor molecule?
**Answer:** The CO₂ acceptor in the carbon fixation phase is RuBP (Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate).

**Q83.** Which of the following environmental factors is considered most crucial for the process of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Light is the energy source for photosynthesis, making light intensity a primary factor.

**Q84.** At very low light intensities, what is the relationship between light intensity and the rate of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** At low light intensities, light is a limiting factor; the rate of photosynthesis increases as light intensity increases.

**Q85.** What happens to the rate of photosynthesis if the light intensity continues to increase beyond the saturation point for a plant?
**Answer:** Beyond the saturation point, increasing light intensity will not increase the rate because other factors become limiting.

**Q86.** Why is water considered an essential factor for photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Water is split during photolysis in the light-dependent reactions, providing electrons and releasing oxygen.

**Q87.** What is the consequence of severe water shortage on the rate of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Water shortage leads to stomatal closure, reducing CO₂ intake and slowing photosynthesis.

**Q88.** Which gas is a necessary raw material for the light-independent reactions of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** The Calvin Cycle uses ATP and NADPH from light reactions to fix carbon dioxide and synthesize sugars.

**Q89.** If the carbon dioxide concentration in the atmosphere is very low, how would it affect the rate of photosynthesis, assuming other factors are optimal?
**Answer:** Low CO₂ concentration limits the Calvin Cycle, even if other factors are optimal.

**Q90.** What is the typical atmospheric concentration of carbon dioxide (CO₂)?
**Answer:** Atmospheric CO₂ is typically around 0.03–0.04%.

**Q91.** An increase in carbon dioxide concentration generally leads to what effect on the rate of photosynthesis, up to a certain point?
**Answer:** Increased CO₂ enhances photosynthesis until another factor becomes limiting.

**Q92.** Why might, very high temperatures negatively affect the rate of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** High temperatures can denature enzymes involved in photosynthesis, slowing the reactions.

**Q93.** Which of the following factors, when in short supply, acts as a 'limiting factor' on the rate of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** According to Liebig's Law, the scarcest essential factor limits the process.

**Q94.** In a closed environment, if a plant is actively photosynthesizing, what happens to the carbon dioxide concentration over time if no new CO₂ is introduced?
**Answer:** Plants consume CO₂ during photosynthesis, so its concentration decreases.

**Q95.** Which statement best describes the interaction between light and water in photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Light energy drives photolysis of water during the light-dependent reactions.

**Q96.** If a plant is exposed to green light, how would its photosynthetic rate likely compare to exposure to red or blue light?
**Answer:** Chlorophyll reflects green light, so photosynthesis is less efficient under green light.

**Q97.** Under what conditions would increasing the concentration of carbon dioxide NOT lead to an increased rate of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** According to the concept of limiting factors, only the most limiting factor controls the rate.

**Q98.** Where do the light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis primarily occur within the chloroplast?
**Answer:** Light-dependent reactions occur on grana, which are stacks of thylakoids within the chloroplast.

**Q99.** Which of the following molecules are the direct products of the light-dependent reactions, essential for the light-independent reactions?
**Answer:** Light-dependent reactions produce ATP and NADPH, which are used in the light-independent reactions.

**Q100.** Photosystem II (PSII) contains a special pair of chlorophyll 'a' molecules referred to as:
**Answer:** Photosystem II is associated with P680, named for its absorption of 680 nm light.

**Q101.** In non-cyclic photophosphorylation, what is the fate of the electrons excited from Photosystem II?
**Answer:** Electrons from PSII are passed to PSI through an electron transport chain after being captured by an electron acceptor.

**Q102.** The process by which water molecules are split during the light-dependent reactions, releasing electrons, protons, and oxygen, is called:
**Answer:** Photolysis refers to the splitting of water using light in Photosystem II.

**Q103.** Which of the following is a direct product of water splitting (photolysis) in Photosystem II?
**Answer:** Photolysis of water in PSII releases oxygen as a direct byproduct.

**Q104.** What is the role of the electron transport chain located between Photosystem II and Photosystem I in non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
**Answer:** Electrons passed through the ETC generate ATP during non-cyclic photophosphorylation.

**Q105.** Which Photosystem is involved in cyclic photophosphorylation?
**Answer:** Cyclic photophosphorylation involves only Photosystem I.

**Q106.** What is the key difference in products between cyclic and non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
**Answer:** Cyclic photophosphorylation generates only ATP, whereas non-cyclic also produces NADPH and oxygen.

**Q107.** The chemiosmotic mechanism in photosynthesis is responsible for the synthesis of:
**Answer:** Chemiosmosis generates ATP by using the proton gradient created across the thylakoid membrane.

**Q108.** Which component is responsible for collecting light energy over a broad area and funneling it to the reaction center in a photosystem?
**Answer:** Pigments in the antenna complex absorb light and transfer energy to the reaction center.

**Q109.** In non-cyclic photophosphorylation, what is the ultimate electron acceptor at the end of the electron transport chain following Photosystem I?
**Answer:** Electrons reduce NADP+ to NADPH at the end of the non-cyclic pathway.

**Q110.** The Z-scheme describes which of the following processes?
**Answer:** Z-scheme represents non-cyclic photophosphorylation involving PSII and PSI.

**Q111.** When light energy excites electrons in the reaction center of Photosystem I (P700), what is the immediate consequence?
**Answer:** Excited electrons are passed to an acceptor to begin electron transport.

**Q112.** The energy released during the transfer of electrons along the electron transport chain in thylakoid membranes is used to:
**Answer:** This creates a proton gradient for ATP synthesis via chemiosmosis.

**Q113.** Which of the following describes a key feature of the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** Calvin Cycle occurs in the stroma using ATP and NADPH from light reactions.

**Q114.** What is the first stable organic product formed after carbon fixation in the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** CO2 is fixed to RuBP forming an unstable 6C compound which splits into two PGA.

**Q115.** The reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle primarily involves the conversion of PGA to G3P using energy from:
**Answer:** Both ATP and NADPH are required to convert PGA to G3P.

**Q116.** What is regenerated during the final phase of the Calvin Cycle to allow the cycle to continue?
**Answer:** RuBP is regenerated to keep the Calvin Cycle running.

**Q117.** How many carbon atoms does the RuBP have before combining with CO2 in the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** RuBP is a 5-carbon sugar that combines with CO2.

**Q118.** Which enzyme is responsible for catalyzing the carbon fixation step in the Calvin Cycle, combining CO2 with RuBP?
**Answer:** RuBisCO catalyzes the fixation of CO2 to RuBP.

**Q119.** The products of the light-dependent reactions, ATP and NADPH, are consumed in which phase of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** ATP and NADPH are used in the Calvin Cycle to synthesize carbohydrates.

**Q120.** How many phases does the Calvin Cycle primarily consist of?
**Answer:** Calvin Cycle has three phases: carbon fixation, reduction, and regeneration.

**Q121.** In the reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle, what is directly formed from 3-PGA using the energy from ATP and NADPH?
**Answer:** 3-PGA is reduced to G3P using ATP and NADPH.

**Q122.** What is the primary fate of G3P molecules produced during the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** Some G3P is used to form glucose; the rest regenerates RuBP.

**Q123.** Which of the following describes the role of ATP in the regeneration phase of the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** ATP is required to regenerate RuBP from G3P.

**Q124.** If the supply of ATP and NADPH from the light reactions suddenly stopped, which phase of photosynthesis would be immediately affected?
**Answer:** Light-independent reactions depend on ATP and NADPH from the light reactions.

**Q125.** Why are the light-independent reactions also known as 'dark reactions'?
**Answer:** Dark reactions are so named because they do not directly use light.

**Q126.** Which molecule represents the initial input of carbon into the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** The Calvin Cycle begins with the fixation of carbon dioxide.

**Q127.** Where does the energy required for the light-independent reactions ultimately come from?
**Answer:** ATP and NADPH from light reactions supply the energy.

**Q128.** Which of the following events occurs FIRST in the light-dependent reactions?
**Answer:** Light energy first excites electrons in Photosystem II (P680), starting the Z-scheme.

**Q129.** What is the primary role of Photosystem I (PSI) in non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
**Answer:** PSI accepts electrons from PSII and uses them to reduce NADP+ to NADPH.

**Q130.** The flow of electrons in the Z-scheme starts from water and ends at:
**Answer:** Electrons from water end up reducing NADP+ to NADPH in the Z-scheme.

**Q131.** If a plant is unable to synthesize NADP+, which process would be directly inhibited?
**Answer:** Without NADP+, NADPH can't be formed, halting the reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle.

**Q132.** The enzyme ATP synthase plays a vital role in both photosynthesis and respiration. In photosynthesis, where is it located?
**Answer:** ATP synthase is located in the thylakoid membrane where chemiosmosis occurs.

**Q133.** Which of the following is a key characteristic of a C3 plant's carbon fixation in the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** C3 plants use RuBisCO to fix CO2 directly to a 3-carbon compound.

**Q134.** If a plant is deprived of carbon dioxide, which of the following molecules would accumulate in the chloroplast?
**Answer:** Without CO2, RuBP can't be used in fixation and builds up in the chloroplast.

**Q135.** The energy stored in the proton gradient across the thylakoid membrane is directly used by ATP synthase to:
**Answer:** The proton motive force powers ATP synthase to produce ATP.

**Q136.** Which molecule serves as the energy currency that powers most cellular work, including many steps in the Calvin Cycle?
**Answer:** ATP provides energy for biosynthesis, including in the Calvin Cycle.

**Q137.** Considering the overall process of photosynthesis, which phase is primarily responsible for converting light energy into chemical energy stored in energy carriers (ATP, NADPH), and which phase utilizes these carriers to synthesize organic molecules?
**Answer:** Light-dependent reactions make ATP/NADPH; light-independent reactions use them to make sugars.

### Biological Molecules (94 Questions)

**Q139.** What is the primary focus of biochemistry?
**Answer:** Biochemistry is defined as the branch of biology that deals with the chemical compounds of living beings and the various chemical reactions (metabolism) that occur within them.

**Q140.** Which of the following best describes bioelements?
**Answer:** Bioelements are approximately 25 elements out of 92 naturally occurring elements on Earth that are found in living beings.

**Q141.** What percentage of the human body is constituted by the six major bioelements?
**Answer:** The six commonest bioelements (Oxygen, Carbon, Hydrogen, Nitrogen, Calcium, Phosphorus) constitute '99%' of the protoplasm in the human body.

**Q142.** Which of the following is considered a 'major bioelement' in the human body?
**Answer:** The six major bioelements that constitute '99%' of the human body are Oxygen (65%), Carbon (18%), Hydrogen (10%), Nitrogen (3%), Calcium (2%), and Phosphorus (1%). Potassium, Sulphur, and Iron are listed as minor or trace elements.

**Q143.** What distinguishes minor bioelements from major bioelements?
**Answer:** Minor bioelements are defined as those found as less than '1%' of the protoplasm, while trace elements are less than 0.01%.

**Q144.** Which of the following is NOT one of the four fundamental kinds of biological molecules?
**Answer:** The four fundamental kinds of biological molecules are carbohydrates, proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids. Water is an inorganic biomolecule, not one of the four fundamental organic macromolecules.

**Q145.** What percentage of a mammalian cell is composed of water?
**Answer:** According to Table 2.1, water constitutes '70%' of both bacterial and mammalian cells.

**Q146.** Which of the following organic biomolecules has the highest percentage in a mammalian cell?
**Answer:** In mammalian cells, proteins make up 18%, carbohydrates 4%, lipids 3%, and DNA 0.25%.

**Q147.** What is the approximate number of naturally occurring elements found in living beings?
**Answer:** Approximately 25 elements out of 92 naturally occurring elements on Earth are found in living beings and are called bioelements.

**Q148.** What is the branch of biology that deals with the composition and structure of chemical compounds in living organisms and the chemical processes within them?
**Answer:** Biochemistry is defined as the study of the composition and structure of different chemical compounds found in living organisms and the chemical processes taking place within or related to living organisms.

**Q149.** Which biological molecule is primarily responsible for providing a quick and readily available source of energy for cellular activities?
**Answer:** Carbohydrates, particularly glucose, are readily broken down by cells to release energy for immediate use, serving as a primary fuel source.

**Q150.** The diverse functions of biological membranes, such as transport and cell recognition, are largely attributed to which two types of biological molecules?
**Answer:** Biological membranes are primarily composed of a lipid bilayer, which provides the structural framework, and various proteins embedded within or associated with it, which carry out diverse functions like transport, enzymatic activity, and cell recognition.

**Q151.** Which biological molecule is crucial for carrying genetic information from the nucleus to the ribosomes for protein synthesis?
**Answer:** RNA (Ribonucleic Acid), specifically messenger RNA (mRNA), is responsible for carrying the genetic code from DNA in the nucleus to the ribosomes in the cytoplasm, where it is translated into proteins.

**Q152.** What is a key role of lipids in living organisms, besides energy storage?
**Answer:** Lipids, particularly fats, serve as long-term energy reserves, but also play crucial roles in insulating the body and cushioning vital organs against physical shock. They also form cell membranes.

**Q153.** Which class of biological molecules is responsible for a vast array of functions including enzymatic catalysis, structural support, transport, and defense?
**Answer:** Proteins are highly versatile macromolecules that perform a wide range of functions, acting as enzymes to speed up reactions, providing structural support (e.g., collagen, keratin), transporting substances (e.g., hemoglobin), and defending the body against pathogens (e.g., antibodies).

**Q154.** What is the primary reason for water's high polarity?
**Answer:** Water is highly polar due to the bent shape of the molecule and the unequal sharing of electrons, where oxygen is more electronegative, resulting in a partial negative charge on oxygen and partial positive charges on hydrogen atoms.

**Q155.** The ability of water to form hydrogen bonds is due to:
**Answer:** Hydrogen bonds form between the partially positive hydrogen atom of one water molecule and the partially negative oxygen atom of an adjacent water molecule due to the polar nature of water.

**Q156.** Water is often called the 'universal solvent' due to its ability to dissolve a wide range of substances. This property is primarily linked to its:
**Answer:** Water's high polarity allows it to form hydration shells around charged and polar molecules (hydrophilic substances), effectively dissolving them.

**Q157.** What term describes the tendency of water molecules to stick to other water molecules?
**Answer:** Cohesion is the property of water molecules to stick to each other due to extensive hydrogen bonding, which is responsible for surface tension.

**Q158.** The property of water that allows plants to transport water upwards through xylem vessels is primarily:
**Answer:** Cohesion among water molecules and adhesion of water molecules to the xylem walls create a continuous column of water that can be pulled upwards during transpiration.

**Q159.** Water has a high specific heat capacity. This property is vital for:
**Answer:** Water's high specific heat capacity helps living organisms resist drastic temperature changes, thus maintaining a stable internal environment (homeostasis).

**Q160.** Why does ice float on liquid water?
**Answer:** As water freezes, hydrogen bonds stabilize, forming an open, crystalline lattice that spaces the molecules further apart, making ice less dense than liquid water, allowing it to float.

**Q161.** The high heat of vaporization of water is important because it allows organisms to:
**Answer:** A large amount of energy is required to break the hydrogen bonds and convert liquid water to water vapor. This property allows for effective cooling as heat is removed from the body during evaporation (e.g., sweating).

**Q162.** Hydrolysis reactions, which involve the breaking of a bond by the addition of a water molecule, demonstrate water's role as a:
**Answer:** In hydrolysis, water actively participates in the chemical reaction by splitting into H+ and OH- and adding across a bond, thus acting as a reactant or reagent.

**Q163.** What is the process called when water molecules spontaneously dissociate into hydrogen ions (H+) and hydroxide ions (OH-)?
**Answer:** Water molecules have the ability to spontaneously dissociate or ionize into a hydrogen ion (H+) and a hydroxide ion (OH-).

**Q164.** The surface tension of water, allowing insects to walk on its surface, is a direct result of:
**Answer:** Cohesion, the strong attraction between water molecules due to hydrogen bonding, creates surface tension at the air-water interface.

**Q165.** When a substance like oil separates from water, forming distinct layers, this phenomenon is known as:
**Answer:** Hydrophobic exclusion describes the tendency of nonpolar substances (like oil) to aggregate and separate from water, as water molecules prefer to hydrogen bond with each other rather than with the nonpolar molecules.

**Q166.** Which property of water is crucial for aquatic life in cold climates, preventing entire bodies of water from freezing solid?
**Answer:** The lower density of ice means it floats on the surface, insulating the water below and preventing it from freezing completely, allowing aquatic organisms to survive.

**Q167.** What type of bond is responsible for the unique properties of water, such as its high boiling point compared to similar-sized molecules?
**Answer:** The extensive network of hydrogen bonds between water molecules requires a significant amount of energy to break, leading to its unusually high boiling point.

**Q168.** Water has a pH of 7. This neutrality is a result of water's:
**Answer:** The ionization of water into H+ and OH- ions is fundamental to the pH scale and the concept of neutrality (where [H+] = [OH-]).

**Q169.** Water molecules adhere to the walls of narrow tubes, a phenomenon called capillarity. This is an example of:
**Answer:** Adhesion is the attraction between water molecules and molecules of other substances, such as the cellulose in plant cell walls or glass in a narrow tube.

**Q170.** When a plant wilts due to lack of water, which property of water is directly related to its ability to maintain turgor pressure in plant cells?
**Answer:** Water, acting as a solvent, fills the plant cells, and its cohesive properties help maintain turgor pressure within the cells, which prevents wilting.

**Q171.** Why is water considered an excellent thermal buffer in biological systems?
**Answer:** Water's high specific heat capacity allows it to absorb excess heat when metabolic activity is high and release it when the environment is cold, thus buffering temperature fluctuations.

**Q172.** What is the approximate pH of pure water at 25°C?
**Answer:** Pure water at 25°C is neutral, meaning the concentration of H+ and OH- ions are equal, resulting in a pH of 7.

**Q173.** In what state are water molecules most spread out due to hydrogen bonding?
**Answer:** In ice, hydrogen bonds form a stable, crystalline lattice where molecules are more spread out compared to the constantly breaking and reforming bonds in liquid water, leading to its lower density.

**Q174.** What is the general molecular formula for monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Monosaccharides are true carbohydrates represented by the general molecular formula (CH2O)n, where 'n' is the number of carbon atoms, ranging from 3 to 7.

**Q175.** Which of the following is a characteristic of monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Monosaccharides are the simplest sugars, defined as polyhydroxy aldehydes (aldoses) or polyhydroxy ketones (ketoses).

**Q176.** Glucose is classified as a:
**Answer:** Glucose is a simple sugar, specifically a hexose monosaccharide, as it cannot be hydrolyzed into simpler sugar units.

**Q177.** What type of monosaccharide is glucose, based on its functional group?
**Answer:** Glucose is an aldose because it contains an aldehyde functional group (-CHO).

**Q178.** How many carbon atoms are typically found in monosaccharides?
**Answer:** The number of carbon atoms in monosaccharides ranges from 3 to 7.

**Q179.** Which of the following is an example of an oligosaccharide?
**Answer:** Oligosaccharides are carbohydrates that yield 2 to 10 monosaccharide units upon hydrolysis. Sucrose (cane sugar), maltose, and lactose are common disaccharides, a type of oligosaccharide.

**Q180.** What type of bond links two monosaccharides together to form a disaccharide?
**Answer:** Monosaccharide units are joined together by a covalent bond known as a glycosidic bond, formed by a condensation reaction.

**Q181.** Sucrose is a disaccharide formed by the condensation of which two monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Sucrose is commonly known as cane sugar or table sugar and is formed by the condensation of one molecule of glucose and one molecule of fructose.

**Q182.** Lactose, the sugar found in milk, is composed of which two monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Lactose is formed by the condensation of one molecule of glucose and one molecule of galactose.

**Q183.** Which polysaccharide is the main storage carbohydrate in plants?
**Answer:** Starch is the principal carbohydrate stored in plant cells as an energy reserve.

**Q184.** Which polysaccharide provides structural support in plant cell walls?
**Answer:** Cellulose is a fibrous polysaccharide that forms the major structural component of plant cell walls.

**Q185.** Glycogen is the main storage polysaccharide in:
**Answer:** Glycogen is the primary storage form of glucose in animals, especially abundant in the liver and muscle cells.

**Q186.** What is the key difference between alpha-glucose and beta-glucose?
**Answer:** Alpha-glucose and beta-glucose are anomers, differing in the orientation of the hydroxyl group (-OH) on the anomeric carbon (carbon 1) when in ring form.

**Q187.** Which polysaccharide is a component of the exoskeleton of insects and crustaceans?
**Answer:** Chitin is a structural polysaccharide found in the cell walls of fungi and the exoskeletons of arthropods (insects, crustaceans).

**Q188.** The conversion of a linear monosaccharide into its ring form involves a reaction between the carbonyl group and a hydroxyl group. This ring form is more stable in:
**Answer:** In aqueous solutions, monosaccharides with five or more carbon atoms predominantly exist in cyclic (ring) forms, which are more stable than their open-chain counterparts.

**Q189.** Maltose is a disaccharide formed from two units of:
**Answer:** Maltose is a disaccharide commonly known as malt sugar, composed of two glucose units joined by an alpha-1,4 glycosidic bond.

**Q190.** What type of reaction is involved in the formation of disaccharides from monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Disaccharides are formed from two monosaccharides by a condensation reaction, where a molecule of water is removed.

**Q191.** Which term refers to isomers that are mirror images of each other, like D-glucose and L-glucose?
**Answer:** D-glucose and L-glucose are stereoisomers, specifically enantiomers, meaning they are non-superimposable mirror images of each other.

**Q192.** Why are laboratory-manufactured 'left-handed' sugars not metabolized by human enzymes?
**Answer:** Biological systems, including human enzymes, are highly stereospecific. Most naturally occurring sugars are D-isomers, and the enzymes that process them are structured to recognize and act upon these 'right-handed' forms, making L-isomers non-metabolizable.

**Q193.** What is the primary role of starch in a potato plant?
**Answer:** Starch is the primary storage polysaccharide in plants, allowing them to store excess glucose as a readily available energy reserve.

**Q194.** Which carbohydrate is sometimes referred to as 'animal starch'?
**Answer:** Glycogen is the animal equivalent of starch and is often referred to as 'animal starch' due to its role as the primary glucose storage form in animals.

**Q195.** Which of the following is a characteristic of polysaccharides?
**Answer:** Polysaccharides are complex carbohydrates (macromolecules) formed by the polymerization of hundreds to thousands of monosaccharide units linked by glycosidic bonds. They are generally not sweet and are less soluble or insoluble in water.

**Q196.** The hydrolysis of a disaccharide yields:
**Answer:** Hydrolysis is the reverse of condensation; it involves the addition of a water molecule to break the glycosidic bond, yielding two monosaccharide units from a disaccharide.

**Q197.** Which of these properties is true for monosaccharides?
**Answer:** Monosaccharides are simple sugars, generally sweet in taste, readily soluble in water, and can be oxidized.

**Q198.** In the open-chain structure of a monosaccharide, all carbon atoms except one have a:
**Answer:** In the open-chain form of a monosaccharide, all carbon atoms except one (which is part of an aldehyde or ketone group) have a hydroxyl (-OH) group attached.

**Q199.** What are the basic building blocks of proteins?
**Answer:** Proteins are polymers made up of repeating monomer units called amino acids, linked together by peptide bonds.

**Q200.** Which of the following functional groups are characteristic components of an amino acid?
**Answer:** Every amino acid contains at least one amino group (-NH2) and one carboxyl group (-COOH), both attached to the central carbon atom (alpha-carbon).

**Q201.** The unique characteristic of each amino acid is determined by its:
**Answer:** The R-group, or side chain, differs among the 20 amino acids and gives each its unique properties.

**Q202.** What type of bond links amino acids together to form a polypeptide chain?
**Answer:** Amino acids are linked by peptide bonds, formed during a condensation reaction between the carboxyl and amino groups.

**Q203.** The formation of a peptide bond involves a reaction where:
**Answer:** Peptide bond formation is a dehydration synthesis reaction where a water molecule is removed.

**Q204.** The primary structure of a protein refers to its:
**Answer:** The primary structure is the unique linear sequence of amino acids in a protein.

**Q205.** Sickle cell hemoglobin is an example illustrating the significance of:
**Answer:** A single amino acid change in hemoglobin leads to sickle cell disease, showing the importance of amino acid sequence.

**Q206.** Which level of protein structure involves the folding of the polypeptide chain into alpha-helices and beta-pleated sheets, stabilized by hydrogen bonds?
**Answer:** Secondary structure includes localized folding like alpha-helices and beta-sheets, stabilized by hydrogen bonds.

**Q207.** The overall three-dimensional shape of a single polypeptide chain, including all its side chain interactions, defines its:
**Answer:** Tertiary structure is the full 3D structure formed by the folding and interactions of the polypeptide chain.

**Q208.** What type of protein structure results from the association of two or more separate polypeptide chains?
**Answer:** Quaternary structure describes the interaction and arrangement of multiple polypeptide subunits in a protein.

**Q209.** Enzymes, which catalyze biochemical reactions, are primarily what type of biological molecule?
**Answer:** Most enzymes are proteins that catalyze biological reactions.

**Q210.** Which of the following is a characteristic of fibrous proteins?
**Answer:** Fibrous proteins have elongated shapes and provide structural support (e.g., collagen, keratin).

**Q211.** Globular proteins typically have which of the following characteristics?
**Answer:** Globular proteins are spherical, soluble, and perform functional roles in the body.

**Q212.** The breakdown of a protein into smaller polypeptide fragments or individual amino acids is called:
**Answer:** Hydrolysis is the reaction that breaks peptide bonds by adding water, releasing amino acids.

**Q213.** What type of interaction is crucial for maintaining the tertiary and quaternary structures of proteins, often involving the burying of nonpolar side chains away from water?
**Answer:** Hydrophobic interactions drive protein folding by keeping nonpolar side chains away from the aqueous environment.

**Q214.** What is a defining characteristic of lipids?
**Answer:** Lipids are a diverse group of organic compounds characterized by their insolubility in water and solubility in nonpolar organic solvents due to their nonpolar hydrocarbon nature.

**Q215.** Triglycerides (acylglycerols) are formed by the esterification of glycerol with:
**Answer:** Triglycerides are formed when glycerol reacts with three fatty acids via ester bonds in a condensation reaction.

**Q216.** What is the primary biological role of triglycerides?
**Answer:** Triglycerides serve as the main form of long-term energy storage in many organisms.

**Q217.** Phospholipids are crucial components of cell membranes due to their:
**Answer:** Phospholipids are amphipathic, having both a hydrophilic head and hydrophobic tails, enabling them to form cell membranes.

**Q218.** Which of the following is an example of a steroid?
**Answer:** Cholesterol is a steroid composed of a four-ring structure and is a precursor for many hormones.

**Q219.** Waxes are lipids primarily known for their role in:
**Answer:** Waxes are hydrophobic lipids that serve as waterproof coatings on plant leaves, fruits, and animal surfaces.

**Q220.** Terpenes are a diverse group of lipids that include which important biological molecules?
**Answer:** Terpenes include biologically important molecules like vitamins A, D, E, K and pigments like carotenoids.

**Q221.** Which part of a phospholipid molecule is hydrophilic?
**Answer:** The phosphate group is polar and hydrophilic, making it the water-attracting portion of a phospholipid.

**Q222.** The conversion of a fatty acid from a saturated to an unsaturated state involves:
**Answer:** Unsaturated fatty acids contain double bonds, unlike saturated fatty acids which have only single bonds.

**Q223.** Prostaglandins are a group of lipid compounds that act as:
**Answer:** Prostaglandins are eicosanoids that function as local hormones, playing roles in inflammation and other processes.

**Q224.** Which of the following is a key structural difference between RNA and DNA?
**Answer:** RNA differs from DNA by having ribose instead of deoxyribose and uracil instead of thymine. RNA is usually single-stranded; both contain phosphate groups.

**Q225.** What is the primary role of messenger RNA?
**Answer:** Messenger RNA (mRNA) carries genetic code from the DNA to ribosomes, where it is translated into proteins.

**Q226.** Which type of RNA plays a crucial role in protein synthesis by catalyzing peptide bond formation?
**Answer:** Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) is part of ribosomes and catalyzes the formation of peptide bonds during protein synthesis.

**Q227.** What defines a conjugated molecule?
**Answer:** Conjugated molecules consist of two or more different biological components, such as protein and carbohydrate.

**Q228.** Glycoproteins are crucial components of cell membranes, often involved in:
**Answer:** Glycoproteins help in cell communication and recognition by acting as molecular identifiers on cell surfaces.

**Q229.** Which conjugated molecule is primarily responsible for the transport of lipids (like cholesterol and triglycerides) in the bloodstream?
**Answer:** Lipoproteins transport hydrophobic lipids in blood. LDL and HDL are examples of lipoproteins.

**Q230.** Glycolipids are found in cell membranes, particularly abundant in nervous tissue, and contribute to:
**Answer:** Glycolipids are involved in cell recognition and communication, especially in neural tissue.

**Q231.** Which type of conjugated molecule forms structures like chromosomes and ribosomes, playing roles in genetic information storage and protein synthesis?
**Answer:** Nucleoproteins are DNA/RNA-protein complexes found in chromosomes and ribosomes, essential for gene expression and protein production.

**Q232.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

### Biotechnology (61 Questions)

**Q233.** Which of these is a primary method used in biotechnology to produce vaccines?
 **A.** Extracting antibodies from recovered patients
 **B.** Weakening a virus by exposing it to heat
 **C.** Amplifying the entire viral genome using PCR
 **D.** Synthesizing a specific antigen using a cloned gene ✓
**Answer:** Synthesizing a specific antigen using a cloned gene

**Q234.** What marketable vaccine is explicitly mentioned as being a product of transgenic bacteria?
 **A.** Polio vaccine
 **B.** MMR vaccine
 **C.** Tetanus vaccine
 **D.** Hepatitis B vaccine ✓
**Answer:** Hepatitis B vaccine

**Q235.** In vaccine development, what is the role of monoclonal antibodies?
 **A.** To act as the main ingredient in the vaccine
 **B.** To deliver a cloned gene into bacteria
 **C.** To diagnose the disease before vaccination
 **D.** To prepare a pure antigen for the vaccine ✓
**Answer:** To prepare a pure antigen for the vaccine

**Q236.** The creation of an antigen from a cloned gene is a direct application of what technology?
 **A.** Polymerase Chain Reaction
 **B.** Gene Therapy
 **C.** DNA Sequencing
 **D.** Recombinant DNA technology ✓
**Answer:** Recombinant DNA technology

**Q237.** What is the main function of a DNA probe in disease diagnosis?
 **A.** To cut the patient's DNA at specific sites
 **B.** To replicate a segment of DNA millions of times
 **C.** To carry a therapeutic gene into a patient's cells
 **D.** To find and bind with a complementary DNA strand ✓
**Answer:** To find and bind with a complementary DNA strand

**Q238.** According to the text, PCR can be used as a tool to diagnose which conditions?
 **A.** A patient's blood type and pH levels
 **B.** Antibody concentrations after vaccination
 **C.** Blood sugar levels and nutritional deficiencies
 **D.** Viral infections, genetic disorders, and cancer ✓
**Answer:** Viral infections, genetic disorders, and cancer

**Q239.** The unique patterns of DNA fragments used in DNA fingerprinting are known as:
**Answer:** Plasmids

**Q240.** What technique allows for the analysis of a minuscule DNA sample from a crime scene?
 **A.** Gene therapy
 **B.** Anther culture
 **C.** Gel electrophoresis
 **D.** Polymerase Chain Reaction ✓
**Answer:** Polymerase Chain Reaction

**Q241.** How are DNA probes made detectable in a diagnostic test?
 **A.** They are stained with a colored protein dye
 **B.** They naturally glow when bound to DNA
 **C.** They are attached to large metal particles
 **D.** They are made radioactive ✓
**Answer:** They are made radioactive

**Q242.** Which of these is NOT listed as a product of genetic engineering for disease treatment?
 **A.** Human insulin
 **B.** Human growth hormone
 **C.** Antithrombin III
 **D.** Quinine ✓
**Answer:** Quinine

**Q243.** What is the term for using transgenic farm animals to produce pharmaceuticals?
 **A.** Gene therapy
 **B.** Bioleaching
 **C.** Cloning
 **D.** Gene pharming ✓
**Answer:** Gene pharming

**Q244.** What is the most common approach in gene therapy for correcting a faulty gene?
 **A.** Removing the faulty gene entirely from all cells
 **B.** Using specific drugs to reactivate the faulty gene
 **C.** Swapping the abnormal gene with a normal one
 **D.** Inserting a normal gene into a nonspecific location ✓
**Answer:** Inserting a normal gene into a nonspecific location

**Q245.** What enzyme is deficient in patients with SCID who undergo \*ex vivo\* gene therapy?
 **A.** DNA ligase
 **B.** Taq polymerase
 **C.** Restriction endonuclease
 **D.** Adenosine deaminase ✓
**Answer:** Adenosine deaminase

**Q246.** What is a 'vector' in gene therapy?
 **A.** A protein product made by the new gene
 **B.** The bone marrow stem cells that receive the gene
 **C.** The faulty gene that is being replaced
 **D.** A carrier molecule, like a virus, to deliver a gene ✓
**Answer:** A carrier molecule, like a virus, to deliver a gene

**Q247.** What is the purpose of creating 'frost-minus' bacteria?
 **A.** To increase the fertility of frozen soil
 **B.** To act as a novel type of insecticide
 **C.** To clean up oil spills in cold climates
 **D.** To protect plants from ice crystal formation ✓
**Answer:** To protect plants from ice crystal formation

**Q248.** Which tool of biotechnology is known as 'molecular scissors'?
 **A.** DNA ligase
 **B.** Plasmids
 **C.** DNA polymerase
 **D.** Restriction endonucleases ✓
**Answer:** Restriction endonucleases

**Q249.** What is a 'genomic library'?
 **A.** A digital database of all known protein sequences
 **B.** A single chromosome carefully preserved from an organism
 **C.** A stock of a single cloned gene with millions of copies
 **D.** A collection of clones containing an organism's entire genome ✓
**Answer:** A collection of clones containing an organism's entire genome

**Q250.** What are 'sticky ends'?
 **A.** The telomeres at the ends of a chromosome
 **B.** The primers used to start a PCR reaction
 **C.** The circular shape of a bacterial plasmid
 **D.** Complementary single-stranded ends of cut DNA ✓
**Answer:** Complementary single-stranded ends of cut DNA

**Q251.** How is the gene for cystic fibrosis delivered in the \*in vivo\* therapy trial described?
 **A.** By injecting a viral vector into the blood
 **B.** By transplanting genetically modified lung cells
 **C.** By using a particle gun on the respiratory tract
 **D.** By spraying liposomes into the patient's nostrils ✓
**Answer:** By spraying liposomes into the patient's nostrils

**Q252.** Which of these is NOT a stated application of PCR?
 **A.** Diagnosing viral infections
 **B.** Identifying criminals in forensic labs
 **C.** Studying evolutionary history
 **D.** Producing therapeutic proteins like insulin ✓
**Answer:** Producing therapeutic proteins like insulin

**Q253.** Gene therapy for familial hypercholesterolemia targets which problem?
 **A.** A lack of the ADA enzyme in immune cells
 **B.** A faulty CFTR gene in lung cells
 **C.** A defective blood clotting factor gene
 **D.** Liver cells lacking a cholesterol receptor ✓
**Answer:** Liver cells lacking a cholesterol receptor

**Q254.** A gene inserted into corn roots to protect against pests codes for what product?
 **A.** A growth hormone
 **B.** A frost-preventing protein
 **C.** A herbicide resistance enzyme
 **D.** An insect toxin ✓
**Answer:** An insect toxin

**Q255.** What is a 'protoplast'?
 **A.** A mature pollen grain for anther culture
 **B.** An undifferentiated mass of plant cells
 **C.** An encapsulated 'artificial seed'
 **D.** A plant cell with its cell wall removed ✓
**Answer:** A plant cell with its cell wall removed

**Q256.** The Taq polymerase used in PCR is extracted from what organism?
 **A.** A common bacteriophage
 **B.** The intestinal bacterium \*E. coli\*
 **C.** A species of baking yeast
 **D.** The bacterium \*Thermus aquaticus\* ✓
**Answer:** The bacterium \*Thermus aquaticus\*

**Q257.** Besides a plasmid, what else can be used as a vector to insert a gene into bacteria?
 **A.** A liposome
 **B.** A microneedle
 **C.** A plant protoplast
 **D.** DNA from a bacterial virus ✓
**Answer:** DNA from a bacterial virus

**Q258.** What was the main scientific significance of cloning the sheep Dolly from an adult cell?
 **A.** Showing asexual reproduction is more efficient
 **B.** Creating a method to produce industrial bioplastics
 **C.** Confirming any 2n nucleus could be put into a sheep egg
 **D.** Proving adult cell genes can be reprogrammed for full development ✓
**Answer:** Proving adult cell genes can be reprogrammed for full development

**Q259.** What is the benefit of making crops resistant to a broad-spectrum herbicide?
 **A.** It makes the crops grow twice as fast
 **B.** It enhances the flavor of the final food product
 **C.** It makes the crops produce their own fertilizer
 **D.** It allows for killing weeds without harming the crop ✓
**Answer:** It allows for killing weeds without harming the crop

**Q260.** Which of the following was a primary goal of the Human Genome Project?
 **A.** To cure all known genetic diseases
 **B.** To create a perfect human clone
 **C.** To prove all human DNA is identical
 **D.** To construct a genetic map of each chromosome ✓
**Answer:** To construct a genetic map of each chromosome

**Q261.** How is a foreign gene typically introduced when creating a transgenic animal?
 **A.** By injecting it into an adult animal's bloodstream
 **B.** By feeding it to the host mother during pregnancy
 **C.** By adding it to the animal's milk after birth
 **D.** By injecting it into donor eggs before fertilization ✓
**Answer:** By injecting it into donor eggs before fertilization

**Q262.** The pest-resistance gene known as the Bt gene comes from what source?
 **A.** A virus
 **B.** A fungus
 **C.** A different pest-resistant plant
 **D.** A bacterium ✓
**Answer:** A bacterium

**Q263.** What is the function of DNA ligase?
 **A.** It cuts plasmid DNA at specific sites
 **B.** It acts as a carrier for a foreign gene
 **C.** It synthesizes a new strand of DNA
 **D.** It seals foreign DNA into a vector ✓
**Answer:** It seals foreign DNA into a vector

**Q264.** What is a key advantage of using meristem culture for plant propagation?
 **A.** The resulting plants are haploid
 **B.** The resulting plants are always genetically different
 **C.** It is the best method for cereal grains
 **D.** The resulting plants are virus-free ✓
**Answer:** The resulting plants are virus-free

**Q265.** What happened when a tobacco plant was engineered with the firefly luciferase gene?
 **A.** It became resistant to all common insects
 **B.** It grew to three times its normal size
 **C.** It developed the ability to grow in salty soil
 **D.** It glowed when sprayed with luciferin ✓
**Answer:** It glowed when sprayed with luciferin

**Q266.** What does the term 'totipotency' describe?
 **A.** The process of crossing different plant varieties
 **B.** A specific type of palindromic DNA sequence
 **C.** The uptake of foreign DNA by a host cell
 **D.** The ability of a single plant cell to form a whole organism ✓
**Answer:** The ability of a single plant cell to form a whole organism

**Q267.** Why are bacterial cells treated with calcium chloride before introducing a plasmid?
 **A.** To kill any untransformed bacteria
 **B.** To activate the DNA ligase enzyme
 **C.** To make the plasmid radioactive
 **D.** To make their membranes more permeable ✓
**Answer:** To make their membranes more permeable

**Q268.** Which product is used to treat the blood clotting disorder hemophilia?
 **A.** Insulin
 **B.** Human growth hormone
 **C.** Tissue plasminogen activator
 **D.** Haemophilia factor VIII ✓
**Answer:** Haemophilia factor VIII

**Q269.** In DNA analysis, what does gel electrophoresis do?
 **A.** It cuts DNA into many small fragments
 **B.** It makes millions of copies of DNA fragments
 **C.** It transfers DNA fragments onto a membrane
 **D.** It separates DNA fragments based on their length ✓
**Answer:** It separates DNA fragments based on their length

**Q270.** What is the initial chromosomal state of plants produced via anther culture?
 **A.** Virus-free
 **B.** Identical to the parent
 **C.** Herbicide-resistant
 **D.** Haploid ✓
**Answer:** Haploid

**Q271.** Which was the first human chromosome to be fully sequenced?
 **A.** Chromosome 1
 **B.** X chromosome
 **C.** Y chromosome
 **D.** Chromosome 22 ✓
**Answer:** Chromosome 22

**Q272.** What is a 'callus'?
 **A.** The initial tissue piece used in plant culture
 **B.** A plant cell with its cell wall removed
 **C.** A somatic embryo ready for germination
 **D.** An undifferentiated group of dividing plant cells ✓
**Answer:** An undifferentiated group of dividing plant cells

**Q273.** Who is credited with isolating the first restriction enzyme in 1970?
 **A.** Kary B. Mullis
 **B.** Frederick Sanger
 **C.** Gottlieb Haberlandt
 **D.** Hamilton O. Smith ✓
**Answer:** Hamilton O. Smith

**Q274.** Which of these therapeutic products is made by transgenic plants, not bacteria?
 **A.** Insulin
 **B.** Human growth hormone
 **C.** Tissue plasminogen activator
 **D.** Antibodies for treating genital herpes ✓
**Answer:** Antibodies for treating genital herpes

**Q275.** Why are 'suicide genes' sometimes added to bacteria used for bioremediation?
 **A.** To make them work faster under pressure
 **B.** To protect them from environmental toxins
 **C.** To help them reproduce more quickly in the field
 **D.** To make them self-destruct after their job is done ✓
**Answer:** To make them self-destruct after their job is done

**Q276.** How is paternity determined using DNA fingerprinting?
 **A.** The child's DNA bands must be a perfect match to the father's
 **B.** The father must have more DNA bands than the mother
 **C.** The child must have no bands in common with the father
 **D.** The child's DNA contains a mix of bands from both parents ✓
**Answer:** The child's DNA contains a mix of bands from both parents

**Q277.** What is the function of a particle gun in plant biotechnology?
 **A.** To inject therapeutic genes into human patients
 **B.** To rapidly sequence a plant's DNA sample
 **C.** To eliminate agricultural pests using metal pellets
 **D.** To shoot DNA-coated particles into a plant callus ✓
**Answer:** To shoot DNA-coated particles into a plant callus

**Q278.** The gene for which hormone has been used to create larger transgenic fish?
 **A.** Luciferase
 **B.** Antithrombin III
 **C.** An insect toxin
 **D.** Bovine growth hormone ✓
**Answer:** Bovine growth hormone

**Q279.** What ecological concern is mentioned regarding transgenic fish?
 **A.** They are not safe for human consumption
 **B.** They reproduce much too slowly in captivity
 **C.** They require a highly specialized and expensive diet
 **D.** They could disrupt natural ecosystems if they escape ✓
**Answer:** They could disrupt natural ecosystems if they escape

**Q280.** What type of bond initially forms between complementary 'sticky ends'?
 **A.** Covalent bond
 **B.** Ionic bond
 **C.** Disulphide bond
 **D.** Hydrogen bond ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogen bond

**Q281.** What is the potential benefit of using cell suspension cultures to produce chemicals?
 **A.** It allows chemicals to be synthesized from petroleum
 **B.** It is a process that requires no genetic engineering
 **C.** It reduces the need to import chemicals
 **D.** It eliminates the need to farm the source plants ✓
**Answer:** It eliminates the need to farm the source plants

**Q282.** What special nucleotide terminates DNA synthesis in the Sanger sequencing method?
 **A.** Ribonucleotides (NTPs)
 **B.** Deoxyribonucleotides (dNTPs)
 **C.** Radioactive nucleotides
 **D.** Dideoxynucleotides (ddNTPs) ✓
**Answer:** Dideoxynucleotides (ddNTPs)

**Q283.** What is the main benefit of automated DNA sequencing?
 **A.** It requires no enzymes like DNA polymerase
 **B.** It uses significantly less DNA than manual methods
 **C.** It can only be used for sequencing viral DNA
 **D.** It greatly improves the quality and speed of sequencing ✓
**Answer:** It greatly improves the quality and speed of sequencing

**Q284.** When using \*Agrobacterium\* as a vector, where is the foreign gene first placed?
 **A.** Directly into the plant's chromosome
 **B.** Into the plant's mitochondria
 **C.** Into the cell wall of the plant
 **D.** Into the bacterium's plasmid ✓
**Answer:** Into the bacterium's plasmid

**Q285.** To create salt-tolerant plants, a gene for a channel protein was used to sequester Na+ ions into which organelle?
 **A.** The nucleus
 **B.** The chloroplast
 **C.** The cytoplasm
 **D.** The vacuole ✓
**Answer:** The vacuole

**Q286.** What is the natural purpose of restriction enzymes within bacteria?
 **A.** To repair mutations in their own DNA
 **B.** To assist in bacterial conjugation
 **C.** To synthesize messenger RNA
 **D.** To defend against infecting viruses ✓
**Answer:** To defend against infecting viruses

**Q287.** What defines a 'palindromic sequence' in DNA?
 **A.** It consists only of A and T bases
 **B.** It is always over 100 base pairs long
 **C.** It is a sequence that cannot be cut by enzymes
 **D.** Its nucleotides are arranged symmetrically in reverse order ✓
**Answer:** Its nucleotides are arranged symmetrically in reverse order

**Q288.** Why are bone marrow stem cells ideal for \*ex vivo\* gene therapy?
 **A.** They are the easiest cells to remove from the body
 **B.** They are the only cells affected by most genetic diseases
 **C.** They do not have a nucleus, which simplifies the process
 **D.** They divide to create a lasting supply of corrected cells ✓
**Answer:** They divide to create a lasting supply of corrected cells

**Q289.** How did biotechnology improve the production of phenylalanine for aspartame?
 **A.** By engineering plants to produce it in their leaves
 **B.** By using gene therapy to help humans synthesize it
 **C.** By extracting it from the milk of transgenic animals
 **D.** By engineering bacteria for more efficient production ✓
**Answer:** By engineering bacteria for more efficient production

**Q290.** What is complementary DNA (cDNA)?
 **A.** The non-coding strand of a DNA double helix
 **B.** A short piece of DNA used as a PCR primer
 **C.** DNA found exclusively within bacterial plasmids
 **D.** DNA synthesized from an mRNA template ✓
**Answer:** DNA synthesized from an mRNA template

**Q291.** Bioleaching by engineered bacteria is used for what industrial purpose?
 **A.** Producing biodegradable plastics
 **B.** Cleaning up environmental oil spills
 **C.** Removing sulfur from coal before burning
 **D.** Extracting metals from low-grade ore ✓
**Answer:** Extracting metals from low-grade ore

**Q292.** What is the function of the enzyme reverse transcriptase?
 **A.** It cuts DNA at specific palindromic sequences
 **B.** It joins two DNA fragments together
 **C.** It synthesizes RNA from a DNA template
 **D.** It synthesizes DNA from an mRNA template ✓
**Answer:** It synthesizes DNA from an mRNA template
