### Body Fluids And Circulation (87 Questions)

**Q294.** What is the primary function of the human heart?
 **A.** Gas exchange
 **B.** Nutrient absorption
 **C.** Waste filtration
 **D.** Pumping blood ✓
**Answer:** Pumping blood

**Q295.** The human heart is best described as a:
 **A.** Solid, muscular organ
 **B.** Glandular, epithelial organ
 **C.** Spongy, cartilaginous organ
 **D.** Hollow, fibromuscular organ ✓
**Answer:** Hollow, fibromuscular organ

**Q296.** Which of the following is responsible for producing the pressure that makes blood flow through blood vessels?
 **A.** Relaxation of the heart
 **B.** Contraction of blood vessels
 **C.** Forceful contraction of the heart ✓
**Answer:** Forceful contraction of the heart

**Q297.** In which body cavity is the heart primarily located?
 **A.** Abdominal cavity
 **B.** Pelvic cavity
 **C.** Cranial cavity
 **D.** Thoracic cavity ✓
**Answer:** Thoracic cavity

**Q298.** The middle portion of the thoracic cavity, formed by the heart, trachea, esophagus, and associated structures, is called the:
**Answer:** Pleural cavity

**Q299.** What is the function of the pericardial fluid?
 **A.** Lubricates heart valves
 **B.** Nourishes cardiac muscle
 **C.** Prevents blood clot formation
 **D.** Reduces friction ✓
**Answer:** Reduces friction

**Q300.** The groove that separates the atria from the ventricles on the surface of the heart is known as the:
 **A.** InterAtrioventricular groove
 **B.** Interventricular groove
 **C.** Coronary sulcus
 **D.** Atrioventricular groove ✓
**Answer:** Atrioventricular groove

**Q301.** The surface of the heart chambers that allows blood to move easily is the:
 **A.** Epicardium
 **B.** Myocardium
 **C.** Pericardium
 **D.** Endocardium ✓
**Answer:** Endocardium

**Q302.** What is the approximate ratio of the thickness of the right ventricle wall to the left ventricle wall?
 **A.** 1:1
 **B.** 1:2
 **C.** 3:1
 **D.** 1:3 ✓
**Answer:** 1:3

**Q303.** Which of the following veins directly empties deoxygenated blood into the right atrium?
 **A.** Pulmonary vein
 **B.** Aorta
 **C.** Pulmonary artery
 **D.** Superior vena cava ✓
**Answer:** Superior vena cava

**Q304.** The interatrial septum separates which two chambers of the heart?
 **A.** Right atrium and right ventricle
 **B.** Left atrium and left ventricle
 **C.** Two ventricles
 **D.** Two atria ✓
**Answer:** Two atria

**Q305.** Which valve is located between the right atrium and the right ventricle?
 **A.** Bicuspid valve
 **B.** Mitral valve
 **C.** Aortic semilunar valve
 **D.** Tricuspid valve ✓
**Answer:** Tricuspid valve

**Q306.** The bicuspid valve is also known as the:
 **A.** Tricuspid valve
 **B.** Pulmonary valve
 **C.** Aortic valve
 **D.** Mitral valve ✓
**Answer:** Mitral valve

**Q307.** What is the strong connective tissue that attach papillary muscles to the cusps of atrioventricular valves?
 **A.** Tendons
 **B.** Ligaments
 **C.** Myofibrils
 **D.** Chordae tendineae ✓
**Answer:** Chordae tendineae

**Q308.** Which valves prevent blood from flowing back into the ventricles from the aorta and pulmonary trunk?
 **A.** Atrioventricular valves
 **B.** Tricuspid valve
 **C.** Bicuspid valve
 **D.** Semilunar valves ✓
**Answer:** Semilunar valves

**Q309.** The thickest layer of the heart wall, responsible for contraction, is the:
 **A.** Endocardium
 **B.** Epicardium
 **C.** Pericardium
 **D.** Myocardium ✓
**Answer:** Myocardium

**Q310.** Deoxygenated blood from the body first enters which chamber of the heart?
 **A.** Left atrium
 **B.** Left ventricle
 **C.** Right ventricle
 **D.** Right atrium ✓
**Answer:** Right atrium

**Q311.** From the right atrium, blood passes through which valve to enter the right ventricle?
 **A.** Bicuspid valve
 **B.** Mitral valve
 **C.** Aortic semilunar valve
 **D.** Tricuspid valve ✓
**Answer:** Tricuspid valve

**Q312.** Where does the right ventricle pump blood to?
 **A.** Aorta
 **B.** Left atrium
 **C.** Body proper
 **D.** Lungs ✓
**Answer:** Lungs

**Q313.** Oxygenated blood from the lungs first enters which chamber of the heart?
 **A.** Right atrium
 **B.** Right ventricle
 **C.** Left ventricle
 **D.** Left atrium ✓
**Answer:** Left atrium

**Q314.** The left ventricle pumps blood into which major artery to be distributed to the body proper?
 **A.** Pulmonary artery
 **B.** Superior vena cava
 **C.** Pulmonary trunk
 **D.** Aorta ✓
**Answer:** Aorta

**Q315.** What is the term for the contraction phase of a heart chamber?
 **A.** Diastole
 **B.** Refraction
 **C.** Repolarization
 **D.** Systole ✓
**Answer:** Systole

**Q316.** What is the term for the relaxation phase of a heart chamber?
 **A.** Systole
 **B.** Depolarization
 **C.** Contraction
 **D.** Diastole ✓
**Answer:** Diastole

**Q317.** Approximately how long does the atrial systole last during a cardiac cycle?
 **A.** 0.3 second
 **B.** 0.4 second
 **C.** 0.8 second
 **D.** 0.1 second ✓
**Answer:** 0.1 second

**Q318.** What event causes the "Lubb" sound of the heart?
 **A.** Closing of semilunar valves
 **B.** Opening of atrioventricular valves
 **C.** Contraction of atria
 **D.** Closing of atrioventricular valves ✓
**Answer:** Closing of atrioventricular valves

**Q319.** What event causes the "Dubb" sound of the heart?
 **A.** Closing of atrioventricular valves
 **B.** Opening of semilunar valves
 **C.** Contraction of ventricles
 **D.** Closing of semilunar valves ✓
**Answer:** Closing of semilunar valves

**Q320.** Which component of the heart's conducting system is known as the natural pacemaker?
 **A.** Atrioventricular (AV) node
 **B.** Bundle of His
 **C.** Purkinje fibers
 **D.** Sinoatrial (SA) node ✓
**Answer:** Sinoatrial (SA) node

**Q321.** Where is the Sinoatrial node located?
 **A.** Near the tricuspid valve
 **B.** In the wall of the left atrium
 **C.** In the interventricular septum
 **D.** In the upper part of the right atrial wall ✓
**Answer:** In the upper part of the right atrial wall

**Q322.** What is the primary function of the Atrioventricular node?
 **A.** To initiate ventricular contraction directly
 **B.** To send impulses to the atria
 **C.** To regulate blood pressure
 **D.** To delay the impulse from the SA node ✓
**Answer:** To delay the impulse from the SA node

**Q323.** After the AV node, the electrical impulse travels down which structure within the interventricular septum?
 **A.** Purkinje fibers
 **B.** Coronary sinus
 **C.** Moderator band
 **D.** Bundle of His ✓
**Answer:** Bundle of His

**Q324.** Which components rapidly conduct the electrical impulse throughout the ventricular muscle, causing coordinated ventricular contraction?
 **A.** Chordae tendineae
 **B.** Papillary muscles
 **C.** Coronary arteries
 **D.** Purkinje fibers ✓
**Answer:** Purkinje fibers

**Q325.** The inherent rhythmicity of the heartbeat originates from:
 **A.** Nervous stimulation from the brain
 **B.** Hormonal regulation
 **C.** Blood pressure changes
 **D.** Specialized cardiac muscle cells ✓
**Answer:** Specialized cardiac muscle cells

**Q326.** What is the average duration of one cardiac cycle at rest?
 **A.** 0.1 second
 **B.** 0.3 second
 **C.** 0.5 second
 **D.** 0.8 second ✓
**Answer:** 0.8 second

**Q327.** Which part of the autonomic nervous system generally decreases heart rate?
 **A.** Sympathetic nervous system
 **B.** Somatic nervous system
 **C.** Enteric nervous system
 **D.** Parasympathetic nervous system ✓
**Answer:** Parasympathetic nervous system

**Q328.** An increase in body temperature typically has what effect on heart rate?
 **A.** Decreases heart rate
 **B.** No effect on heart rate
 **C.** Causes irregular heart rate
 **D.** Increases heart rate ✓
**Answer:** Increases heart rate

**Q329.** Which hormone can increase heart rate and force of contraction during stressful situations?
 **A.** Insulin
 **B.** Glucagon
 **C.** Thyroxine
 **D.** Adrenaline ✓
**Answer:** Adrenaline

**Q330.** What does an Electrocardiogram record?
 **A.** Blood pressure changes in the heart
 **B.** Mechanical contractions of the heart
 **C.** Volume of blood pumped by the heart
 **D.** Electrical activity of the heart ✓
**Answer:** Electrical activity of the heart

**Q331.** The P wave on an ECG represents:
 **A.** Ventricular depolarization
 **B.** Ventricular repolarization
 **C.** Atrial repolarization
 **D.** Atrial depolarization ✓
**Answer:** Atrial depolarization

**Q332.** The QRS complex on an ECG primarily represents:
 **A.** Atrial repolarization
 **B.** Atrial depolarization
 **C.** Ventricular repolarization
 **D.** Ventricular depolarization ✓
**Answer:** Ventricular depolarization

**Q333.** What event in the heart is indicated by the T wave on an ECG?
 **A.** Atrial contraction
 **B.** Atrial relaxation
 **C.** Ventricular contraction
 **D.** Ventricular relaxation ✓
**Answer:** Ventricular relaxation

**Q334.** The interval between the beginning of the P wave and the beginning of the QRS complex (P-R interval) indicates:
 **A.** Atrial contraction time
 **B.** Ventricular relaxation time
 **C.** Time for ventricular repolarization
 **D.** Time taken for impulse to travel from atria to ventricles ✓
**Answer:** Time taken for impulse to travel from atria to ventricles

**Q335.** A prolonged P-R interval on an ECG could indicate a problem with the:
 **A.** Ventricular muscle
 **B.** SA node activity
 **C.** Aortic valve
 **D.** AV node activity ✓
**Answer:** AV node activity

**Q336.** What might a depressed or elevated S-T segment on an ECG signify?
 **A.** Normal heart rhythm
 **B.** Atrial fibrillation
 **C.** Valve dysfunction
 **D.** Myocardial infarction ✓
**Answer:** Myocardial infarction

**Q337.** An ECG can be used to diagnose which of the following?
 **A.** High cholesterol levels
 **B.** Blood clots in veins
 **C.** High blood pressure
 **D.** Abnormal heart rhythms ✓
**Answer:** Abnormal heart rhythms

**Q338.** The segment between the end of the S wave and the beginning of the T wave (S-T segment) represents:
 **A.** Atrial systole
 **B.** Ventricular repolarization
 **C.** Atrial diastole
 **D.** Ventricular systole plateau phase ✓
**Answer:** Ventricular systole plateau phase

**Q339.** Which part of the ECG is typically hidden by the larger QRS complex?
 **A.** P wave
 **B.** T wave
 **C.** Ventricular depolarization
 **D.** Atrial repolarization ✓
**Answer:** Atrial repolarization

**Q340.** Which type of blood vessel carries blood away from the heart?
 **A.** Veins
 **B.** Venules
 **C.** Capillaries
 **D.** Arteries ✓
**Answer:** Arteries

**Q341.** Which layer of an artery wall is primarily composed of elastic and connective tissues, allowing it to stretch and recoil?
 **A.** Tunica intima
 **B.** Tunica media
 **C.** Endothelium
 **D.** Tunica externa ✓
**Answer:** Tunica externa

**Q342.** The smallest arteries that regulate blood flow into capillary beds are called:
 **A.** Veins
 **B.** Venules
 **C.** Aorta
 **D.** Arterioles ✓
**Answer:** Arterioles

**Q343.** What is the primary function of capillaries?
 **A.** Transporting the blood under high pressure
 **B.** Returning blood to the heart
 **C.** Storing blood
 **D.** Exchange of substances between blood and tissues ✓
**Answer:** Exchange of substances between blood and tissues

**Q344.** Which type of blood vessel has valves?
 **A.** Arteries
 **B.** Arterioles
 **C.** Capillaries
 **D.** Veins ✓
**Answer:** Veins

**Q345.** Where is the rate of blood flow slowest in the circulatory system?
 **A.** Aorta
 **B.** Large arteries
 **C.** Veins
 **D.** Capillaries ✓
**Answer:** Capillaries

**Q346.** What is the main reason for the rapid blood flow in arteries?
 **A.** Broad lumen
 **B.** Large total cross-sectional area
 **C.** Contraction of surrounding muscles
 **D.** Narrow lumen ✓
**Answer:** Narrow lumen

**Q347.** Which vessels are formed when venules merge together?
 **A.** Arterioles
 **B.** Arteries
 **C.** Capillaries
 **D.** Veins ✓
**Answer:** Veins

**Q348.** The smooth inner layer of all blood vessels is called the:
 **A.** Tunica externa
 **B.** Tunica media
 **C.** Elastic lamina
 **D.** Endothelium ✓
**Answer:** Endothelium

**Q349.** What happens to the blood flow rate as blood moves from the aorta to the capillaries?
 **A.** It increases significantly
 **B.** It remains constant
 **C.** It fluctuates unpredictably
 **D.** It decreases significantly ✓
**Answer:** It decreases significantly

**Q350.** What is blood pressure?
 **A.** The force exerted by blood on the heart walls
 **B.** The volume of blood in the arteries
 **C.** The rate of blood flow
 **D.** The force exerted by blood on the vessel walls ✓
**Answer:** The force exerted by blood on the vessel walls

**Q351.** What is the term for the maximum pressure in the arteries during ventricular contraction?
 **A.** Diastolic pressure
 **B.** Pulse pressure
 **C.** Mean arterial pressure
 **D.** Systolic pressure ✓
**Answer:** Systolic pressure

**Q352.** What is the term for the minimum pressure in the arteries during ventricular relaxation?
 **A.** Systolic pressure
 **B.** Pulse pressure
 **C.** Mean arterial pressure
 **D.** Diastolic pressure ✓
**Answer:** Diastolic pressure

**Q353.** A healthy average blood pressure reading for an adult is often cited as:
 **A.** 100/70 mmHg
 **B.** 140/90 mmHg
 **C.** 90/60 mmHg
 **D.** 120/80 mmHg ✓
**Answer:** 120/80 mmHg

**Q354.** Which instrument is commonly used to measure blood pressure?
 **A.** Stethoscope
 **B.** Electrocardiogram (ECG)
 **C.** Pulse oximeter
 **D.** Sphygmomanometer ✓
**Answer:** Sphygmomanometer

**Q355.** Which of the following factors would generally lead to an \*increase\* in blood pressure?
 **A.** Decrease in cardiac output
 **B.** Decrease in peripheral resistance
 **C.** Increased elasticity of arteries
 **D.** Increase in blood volume ✓
**Answer:** Increase in blood volume

**Q356.** How does vasoconstriction affect peripheral resistance and thus blood pressure?
 **A.** Decreases resistance, decreases pressure
 **B.** Increases resistance, decreases pressure
 **C.** Decreases resistance, increases pressure
 **D.** Increases resistance, increases pressure ✓
**Answer:** Increases resistance, increases pressure

**Q357.** Hypertension is defined as persistently high blood pressure, often starting at readings equal to or greater than:
 **A.** 100/70 mmHg
 **B.** 120/80 mmHg
 **C.** 160/90 mmHg
 **D.** 140/90 mmHg ✓
**Answer:** 140/90 mmHg

**Q358.** What effect does increased blood viscosity have on blood pressure?
 **A.** Decreases blood pressure
 **B.** No effect on blood pressure
 **C.** Stabilizes blood pressure
 **D.** Increases blood pressure ✓
**Answer:** Increases blood pressure

**Q359.** When measuring blood pressure, what sound is listened for with a stethoscope that indicates blood flow returning through the artery?
 **A.** Lubb Dubb sounds
 **B.** Murmur sounds
 **C.** Bruit sounds
 **D.** Korotkoff sounds ✓
**Answer:** Korotkoff sounds

**Q360.** What is the term for the formation of a blood clot inside a blood vessel?
 **A.** Hemorrhage
 **B.** Embolism
 **C.** Aneurysm
 **D.** Thrombosis ✓
**Answer:** Thrombosis

**Q361.** A piece of a thrombus that breaks off and travels through the bloodstream is called a/an:
 **A.** Plaque
 **B.** Hematoma
 **C.** Aneurysm
 **D.** Embolus ✓
**Answer:** Embolus

**Q362.** Chest pain caused by insufficient blood flow to the heart muscle is known as:
 **A.** Myocardial infarction
 **B.** Heart failure
 **C.** Arrhythmia
 **D.** Angina pectoris ✓
**Answer:** Angina pectoris

**Q363.** A "heart attack" is medically termed as:
 **A.** Angina pectoris
 **B.** Bradycardia
 **C.** Tachycardia
 **D.** Myocardial infarction ✓
**Answer:** Myocardial infarction

**Q364.** What condition results when the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body's needs?
 **A.** Arrhythmia
 **B.** Angina pectoris
 **C.** Stroke
 **D.** Heart failure ✓
**Answer:** Heart failure

**Q365.** Irregular heart rhythms are collectively known as:
 **A.** Bradycardia
 **B.** Tachycardia
 **C.** Dysrhythmias
 **D.** Arrhythmias ✓
**Answer:** Arrhythmias

**Q366.** Which diagnostic test involves injecting a dye and using X-rays to visualize blood vessels?
 **A.** ECG
 **B.** Echocardiography
 **C.** Stress test
 **D.** Angiography ✓
**Answer:** Angiography

**Q367.** What non-invasive diagnostic tool uses sound waves to create images of the heart's structure and function?
 **A.** ECG
 **B.** Angiography
 **C.** CT scan
 **D.** Echocardiography ✓
**Answer:** Echocardiography

**Q368.** A treadmill test where the heart's electrical activity is monitored during exercise is typically called a/an:
 **A.** Echocardiogram
 **B.** Angiogram
 **C.** ECG
 **D.** Stress test ✓
**Answer:** Stress test

**Q369.** Which surgical procedure uses a balloon-tipped catheter to widen narrowed or blocked coronary arteries?
 **A.** Bypass surgery
 **B.** Valve replacement
 **C.** Pacemaker implantation
 **D.** Angioplasty ✓
**Answer:** Angioplasty

**Q370.** What is the common name for the surgical procedure that reroutes blood around a blocked artery using a healthy blood vessel from another part of the body?
 **A.** Angioplasty
 **B.** Stent placement
 **C.** Atherectomy
 **D.** Bypass surgery ✓
**Answer:** Bypass surgery

**Q371.** A key lifestyle modification for preventing cardiovascular disorders is:
 **A.** Increasing intake of saturated fats
 **B.** Sedentary lifestyle
 **C.** Smoking
 **D.** Regular physical activity ✓
**Answer:** Regular physical activity

**Q372.** What is the medical term for persistently high blood pressure?
 **A.** Hypotension
 **B.** Tachycardia
 **C.** Bradycardia
 **D.** Hypertension ✓
**Answer:** Hypertension

**Q373.** A common symptom of hypotension is:
**Answer:** Severe chest pain

**Q374.** What is a significant risk factor for developing hypertension?
 **A.** Low sodium diet
 **B.** Regular exercise
 **C.** Maintaining a healthy weight
 **D.** High intake of saturated fats ✓
**Answer:** High intake of saturated fats

**Q375.** The fluid that circulates within the lymphatic system is called:
**Answer:** Interstitial fluid

**Q376.** What is the primary function of the lymphatic system?
 **A.** Pumping blood to the lungs
 **B.** Transporting oxygen to tissues
 **C.** Absorbing nutrients from the digestive system
 **D.** Returning excess interstitial fluid to the blood ✓
**Answer:** Returning excess interstitial fluid to the blood

**Q377.** Lymphatic capillaries differ from blood capillaries by being:
 **A.** Impermeable to large molecules
 **B.** Less permeable to fluid
 **C.** Containing red blood cells
 **D.** Closed at one end ✓
**Answer:** Closed at one end

**Q378.** Which organ of the lymphatic system is responsible for filtering blood and removing old red blood cells?
**Answer:** Lymph node

**Q379.** The spleen functions as a reservoir for:
**Answer:** Lymph

### Cell Structure And Function (99 Questions)

**Q381.** Which of the following organelles is primarily responsible for photosynthesis in plant cells?
**Answer:** Chloroplasts are unique to plant cells and are the site where light energy is converted into chemical energy through the process of photosynthesis.

**Q382.** A rigid outer layer found in plant cells but absent in animal cells is the:
**Answer:** The cell wall provides structural support and protection to plant cells, which is not found in animal cells.

**Q383.** Which of these structures is typically much larger and more prominent in mature plant cells compared to animal cells?
**Answer:** Plant cells usually have a large central vacuole that maintains turgor pressure against the cell wall and stores water, nutrients, and waste products.

**Q384.** Centrioles are involved in cell division and are characteristic components of:
**Answer:** Centrioles are part of the centrosome and are crucial for the formation of the spindle fibers during cell division in animal cells.

**Q385.** Which organelle is responsible for synthesizing lipids and detoxifying drugs, found in both animal and plant cells?
**Answer:** The Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER) is involved in lipid synthesis, metabolism of carbohydrates, and detoxification of drugs and poisons.

**Q386.** Both animal and plant cells possess a selectively permeable outer boundary called the:
**Answer:** The plasma membrane is present in both animal and plant cells, regulating the passage of substances in and out of the cell.

**Q387.** The primary function of mitochondria, found in both animal and plant cells, is:
**Answer:** Mitochondria are where cellular respiration occurs to generate ATP (energy) for the cell's activities.

**Q388.** Which organelle modifies, sorts, and packages proteins and lipids for secretion or delivery to other organelles?
**Answer:** The Golgi apparatus functions in processing, packaging, and secreting cellular products.

**Q389.** Lysosomes are more commonly found and prominent in:
**Answer:** Lysosomes are the primary digestive organelles in animal cells, breaking down macromolecules, old organelles, and foreign substances.

**Q390.** The site of protein synthesis in both animal and plant cells is the:
**Answer:** Ribosomes are responsible for protein synthesis and are found in both animal and plant cells.

**Q391.** Which structure is responsible for controlling the cell's activities by housing the genetic material?
**Answer:** The nucleus contains the cell's genetic material and controls all cellular activities.

**Q392.** The network of interconnected membranes involved in protein synthesis and transport, studded with ribosomes, is the:
**Answer:** The Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum is involved in the synthesis and transport of proteins.

**Q393.** A key difference between typical mature plant cells and animal cells is the permanent presence of:
**Answer:** Plastids, including chloroplasts, are responsible for photosynthesis and are not found in animal cells.

**Q394.** What is the primary component of the cell wall in plants?
**Answer:** The plant cell wall is primarily composed of cellulose, a carbohydrate that provides strength and rigidity.

**Q395.** Which of the following is involved in maintaining cell shape and facilitating cell movement?
**Answer:** The cytoskeleton provides structural support and is involved in various forms of cell movement and transport.

**Q396.** Which of the following best describes the primary difference in cellular support between plant and animal cells?
**Answer:** Plant cells have a rigid cell wall for structural support. Animal cells lack a cell wall and instead maintain their shape and organization through their cytoskeleton and the extracellular matrix.

**Q397.** Plasmodesmata are channels that connect adjacent cells, allowing for communication and transport. These are characteristic features of:
**Answer:** Plasmodesmata are small channels that traverse the cell walls of plant cells, enabling direct cytoplasmic connections and intercellular communication.

**Q398.** Which organelle is responsible for storing water, nutrients, and waste products?
**Answer:** The large central vacuole is a prominent feature of mature plant cells, serving multiple functions including storage and maintaining turgor.

**Q399.** A defining characteristic of plant cells that allows them to produce their own food is the presence of:
**Answer:** Chloroplasts are the organelles in plant cells that contain chlorophyll and are the sites of photosynthesis.

**Q400.** Animal cells can change shape and move more readily than plant cells primarily due to the absence of a:
**Answer:** The rigid cell wall in plant cells restricts their shape changes and movement, while the absence of a cell wall in animal cells allows for greater flexibility and motility.

**Q401.** Both plant and animal cells contain ribosomes, indicating their shared need for:
**Answer:** Ribosomes are universal organelles responsible for synthesizing proteins.

**Q402.** Which of the following is a common organelle found in abundance in cells with high energy demands, such as muscle cells in animals and actively growing plant cells?
**Answer:** Mitochondria are the primary sites of ATP production, making them abundant in high-energy-demand cells.

**Q403.** The internal membrane system involved in packaging and secretion of substances in both animal and plant cells is the:
**Answer:** The Golgi apparatus modifies, sorts, and packages proteins and lipids for secretion or delivery.

**Q404.** Which of these structures is found in animal cells and is involved in organizing microtubules, particularly during cell division, but is generally absent in higher plant cells?
**Answer:** Centrosomes, with centrioles, organize microtubules and are crucial in animal cell division.

**Q405.** The tough, flexible outer boundary of an animal cell that controls what enters and leaves the cell is the:
**Answer:** The plasma membrane serves as the outer boundary of animal cells, regulating transport.

**Q406.** Plant cells store excess glucose primarily as starch, and animal cells store it as:
**Answer:** Plants store glucose as starch. Animals store it as glycogen, especially in liver and muscle cells.

**Q407.** Which structure is common to both plant and animal cells and helps in intracellular transport and mechanical support?
**Answer:** The cytoskeleton provides support, maintains cell shape, and helps with movement inside the cell.

**Q408.** What is the main structural component providing rigidity and protection to plant cells?
**Answer:** The cell wall provides rigidity and protection to plant cells and lies outside the plasma membrane.

**Q409.** If a cell contains a cell wall, chloroplasts, and a large central vacuole, it is most likely a:
**Answer:** These features — cell wall, chloroplasts, and large central vacuole — are characteristic of plant cells.

**Q410.** Which of the following is a defining characteristic of eukaryotic cells that is absent in prokaryotic cells?
**Answer:** Eukaryotic cells possess a variety of membrane-bound organelles (like the nucleus, mitochondria, ER, Golgi), which are not found in prokaryotic cells.

**Q411.** The genetic material of a prokaryotic cell is typically found in a region called the:
**Answer:** Prokaryotic cells lack a true nucleus; their genetic material is located in an irregular region called the nucleoid.

**Q412.** Which statement accurately describes the nucleus in prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells?
**Answer:** A key distinction is the presence of a membrane-enclosed nucleus in eukaryotic cells, which is absent in prokaryotic cells.

**Q413.** Ribosomes are present in:
**Answer:** Ribosomes are essential for protein synthesis and are found in both types of cells, though structurally different.

**Q414.** The cell wall of most bacteria is primarily composed of:
**Answer:** Peptidoglycan is the primary component of the bacterial cell wall, providing structural integrity.

**Q415.** Which of the following is characteristic of eukaryotic cell division but not prokaryotic cell division?
**Answer:** Eukaryotic cells divide through mitosis or meiosis, unlike prokaryotes which divide by binary fission.

**Q416.** In terms of size, prokaryotic cells are generally:
**Answer:** Prokaryotic cells are typically much smaller (1–10 µm) than eukaryotic cells (10–100 µm).

**Q417.** Which structure is common to both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells and regulates the passage of substances into and out of the cell?
**Answer:** The plasma membrane is a universal feature that controls the exchange of substances in all cells.

**Q418.** The presence of a cytoskeleton for structural support and intracellular transport is a feature of:
**Answer:** Eukaryotic cells possess a complex cytoskeleton that supports structure and organelle movement.

**Q419.** Which of the following organelles is NOT found in prokaryotic cells?
**Answer:** Prokaryotic cells lack mitochondria and perform respiration using their cell membrane.

**Q420.** The DNA of a prokaryotic cell is typically:
**Answer:** Prokaryotic DNA is circular and not associated with histone proteins.

**Q421.** A feature often present in prokaryotic cells for locomotion is a:
**Answer:** Many prokaryotes move using a simple flagellum that rotates like a propeller.

**Q422.** The primary form of genetic material in eukaryotic cells is:
**Answer:** Eukaryotic cells have their genetic material organized into multiple linear chromosomes, enclosed in a nucleus.

**Q423.** Which type of cell contains a more complex endomembrane system?
**Answer:** The endomembrane system is a hallmark of eukaryotic cells, allowing compartmentalization and specialized functions.

**Q424.** While both cell types have a cell membrane, eukaryotic cell membranes also contain:
**Answer:** Eukaryotic membranes contain sterols(CHOLESTROL) that regulate fluidity, which are typically absent in prokaryotic membranes.

**Q425.** The genetic material of prokaryotic cells is naked, meaning it is not associated with:
**Answer:** Prokaryotic DNA is not associated with histones, unlike eukaryotic DNA which is wrapped around histone proteins.

**Q426.** Which of the following is true regarding the organization of DNA in prokaryotes versus eukaryotes?
**Answer:** Prokaryotic DNA is typically a single circular chromosome; eukaryotic DNA consists of multiple linear chromosomes.

**Q427.** Photosynthesis in cyanobacteria occurs in:
**Answer:** Photosynthetic prokaryotes use internal thylakoid membranes in the cytoplasm, not chloroplasts.

**Q428.** Which type of cell typically has a larger and more complex genome?
**Answer:** Eukaryotic genomes are generally larger and more complex, containing more genes and non-coding regions.

**Q429.** The process of transcription and translation are coupled (occur simultaneously) in:
**Answer:** In prokaryotes, these processes occur together in the cytoplasm due to the absence of a nuclear envelope.

**Q430.** The internal compartmentalization by membranes, allowing for specialized metabolic functions, is extensive in:
**Answer:** Eukaryotic cells have numerous membrane-bound compartments for specialized metabolic processes.

**Q431.** A plasmid is commonly found in:
**Answer:** Plasmids, a small, circular, extrachromosomal DNA molecule, are typical in prokaryotes and provide additional traits like antibiotic resistance.

**Q432.** Which of the following is present in the cytoplasm of eukaryotic cells but generally absent in prokaryotic cells?
**Answer:** Endoplasmic Reticulum is a eukaryotic-specific organelle not found in prokaryotes.

**Q433.** Which of the following best describes the primary function of the nucleus?
**Answer:** The nucleus houses the cell's DNA and controls all cellular functions, including gene expression and cell division.

**Q434.** The nuclear envelope is perforated by:
**Answer:** Nuclear pores regulate the transport of macromolecules between the nucleus and cytoplasm.

**Q435.** The nucleolus is primarily involved in the synthesis of:
**Answer:** The nucleolus synthesizes rRNA and assembles ribosomal subunits.

**Q436.** What is the primary role of the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum (RER)?
**Answer:** The RER is involved in the synthesis and processing of proteins, particularly those for secretion or membrane insertion.

**Q437.** What is one of the main functions of the Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER)?
**Answer:** The SER plays roles in lipid metabolism, detoxification, and calcium storage.

**Q438.** Which organelle receives proteins and lipids from the ER, modifies them, sorts them, and packages them into vesicles?
**Answer:** The Golgi apparatus is responsible for processing and packaging proteins and lipids for delivery.

**Q439.** The Golgi apparatus typically consists of flattened membrane-bound sacs called:
**Answer:** Cisternae are the stacked, membrane-bound structures that make up the Golgi apparatus.

**Q440.** Which organelle is often referred to as the 'powerhouse of the cell'?
**Answer:** Mitochondria are responsible for most of the cell’s ATP production via aerobic respiration.

**Q441.** The inner membrane of the mitochondrion is highly folded into structures called:
**Answer:** Cristae increase the surface area for ATP-producing reactions in the mitochondria.

**Q442.** Where does the citric acid cycle primarily occur within the mitochondrion?
**Answer:** The mitochondrial matrix houses enzymes and substrates for the Krebs cycle (citric acid cycle).

**Q443.** The main function of ribosomes, whether free or attached to the ER, is:
**Answer:** Ribosomes translate mRNA into polypeptide chains (proteins).

**Q444.** Which of the following is synthesized on the ribosomes of the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum?
**Answer:** Proteins synthesized on RER ribosomes are destined for membranes, secretion, or specific organelles.

**Q445.** Which organelle is responsible for breaking down long-chain fatty acids through beta-oxidation?
**Answer:** Peroxisomes carry out beta-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids and neutralize toxic compounds.

**Q446.** Which component of the nucleus is primarily composed of chromatin?
**Answer:** The nucleoplasm is the substance within the nuclear envelope that contains chromatin and the nucleolus.

**Q447.** If a cell is highly active in synthesizing and secreting proteins, which organelle would be extensively developed?
**Answer:** Secretory cells have abundant RER and Golgi apparatus for protein production and export.

**Q448.** Cellular respiration is primarily carried out by which organelle?
**Answer:** Mitochondria convert glucose into ATP through the process of cellular respiration.

**Q449.** Which of the following is a primary function of the nucleoplasm?
**Answer:** The nucleoplasm is the semi-fluid substance within the nuclear envelope, suspending the chromatin and nucleolus.

**Q450.** The outer membrane of the nuclear envelope is continuous with the membrane of which organelle?
**Answer:** The outer nuclear membrane is connected to the Rough ER, showing their functional continuity.

**Q451.** What is the role of ribosomes found attached to the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum?
**Answer:** Ribosomes on the RER synthesize proteins for secretion or membrane integration.

**Q452.** Which face of the Golgi apparatus is typically closest to the Endoplasmic Reticulum and receives vesicles from it?
**Answer:** The cis face of the Golgi apparatus is closest to the ER and receives vesicles from it.

**Q453.** The final modifications and sorting of proteins and lipids occur primarily in the:
**Answer:** The trans face of the Golgi apparatus is the site for final modification and sorting.

**Q454.** What is the primary role of the inner mitochondrial membrane in ATP production?
**Answer:** The inner membrane’s cristae hold the electron transport chain (ETC) critical for ATP synthesis.

**Q455.** Which of the following is a characteristic of mitochondrial DNA?
**Answer:** Mitochondrial DNA is circular and inherited from the mother.

**Q456.** If a cell has a high demand for ATP, which organelle would you expect to be numerous and well-developed?
**Answer:** Cells with high energy needs contain many mitochondria for ATP production.

**Q457.** What type of molecules primarily constitute the nuclear lamina?
**Answer:** The nuclear lamina is made of protein filaments that support the nuclear envelope.

**Q458.** Which of the following pathways best describes the synthesis and secretion of a protein like insulin?
**Answer:** Insulin is synthesized in the RER, processed in the Golgi, then secreted via vesicles.

**Q459.** The cisternal maturation model describes the Golgi apparatus as:
**Answer:** In this model, Golgi cisternae mature and move from cis to trans while carrying cargo.

**Q460.** Besides ATP production, mitochondria also play a crucial role in:
**Answer:** Mitochondria release signals that trigger apoptosis (programmed cell death) during cellular stress or damage.

**Q461.** Which of the following is NOT a direct function of the Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER)?
**Answer:** Protein glycosylation primarily occurs in the RER and Golgi, not the SER.

**Q462.** The presence of abundant free ribosomes in the cytoplasm indicates that the cell is primarily synthesizing proteins for use in the:
**Answer:** Free ribosomes produce proteins that function in the cytosol.

**Q463.** The function of the nuclear pores is to:
**Answer:** Nuclear pores regulate selective exchange between nucleus and cytoplasm.

**Q464.** The primary role of cholesterol in the inner mitochondrial membrane is to:
**Answer:** The inner mitochondrial membrane lacks cholesterol and instead maintains efficiency through a high protein-to-lipid ratio.

**Q465.** Which of the following best describes the function of vesicles budding off from the trans-Golgi network?
**Answer:** Vesicles from the trans-Golgi carry processed proteins and lipids to their specific cellular destinations or outside the cell.

**Q466.** In the nucleus, the genetic material exists as chromatin during interphase. Chromatin is a complex of DNA and:
**Answer:** Chromatin consists of DNA wound around histone and non-histone proteins, aiding in packaging and gene regulation.

**Q467.** The lumen of the Endoplasmic Reticulum (ER) is:
**Answer:** The ER lumen is where proteins fold and undergo initial modifications before transport.

**Q468.** A cell specializing in detoxification, such as a liver cell, would have an abundance of which organelle?
**Answer:** Liver cells have extensive SER to detoxify drugs and metabolize lipids.

**Q469.** The inner mitochondrial membrane is folded into cristae to:
**Answer:** Cristae increase surface area for the proteins involved in the electron transport chain and ATP synthesis.

**Q470.** Which of the following cellular components are produced in the nucleolus?
**Answer:** The nucleolus synthesizes rRNA and assembles ribosomal subunits.

**Q471.** Proteins synthesized on free ribosomes typically function in the:
**Answer:** Free ribosomes produce proteins that stay in the cytosol, such as metabolic enzymes.

**Q472.** The term "oxidative phosphorylation" refers to ATP synthesis that occurs in the mitochondria, coupled with:
**Answer:** Electron transport drives the proton gradient, powering ATP synthesis during oxidative phosphorylation.

**Q473.** Which region of the Golgi apparatus is primarily involved in the initial processing and glycosylation of proteins received from the ER?
**Answer:** The cis face receives proteins from the ER and begins their modification.

**Q474.** The primary function of the nuclear pores is to:
**Answer:** Nuclear pores allow selective transport of RNA, proteins, and small molecules.

**Q475.** Proteins targeted for degradation by lysosomes are typically tagged and then processed through which pathway?
**Answer:** Proteins for lysosomal degradation follow the RER → Golgi → lysosome pathway.

**Q476.** The process of aerobic respiration, which yields a large amount of ATP, occurs mainly in which two parts of the mitochondrion?
**Answer:** The Krebs cycle occurs in the matrix, while oxidative phosphorylation occurs at the inner membrane.

**Q477.** What is the role of vesicles that bud off from the Endoplasmic Reticulum and fuse with the Golgi apparatus?
**Answer:** Transport vesicles move cargo from the ER to the Golgi for further processing.

**Q478.** Which organelle serves as the main control center of a eukaryotic cell?
**Answer:** The nucleus controls gene expression and cellular regulation, making it the control center.

**Q479.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

---

### Coordination And Control (143 Questions)

**Q480.** Which of the following defines nervous coordination?
**Answer:** Nervous coordination is defined as the system of the body that provides coordination through electric signals among different body parts for the response to a particular stimulus.

**Q481.** How do receptors function as transducers?
**Answer:** Receptors act as transducers because they convert one form of energy, such as light energy from a stimulus, into another form, specifically electrochemical energy in the form of a nerve impulse.

**Q482.** Which of the following is an example of a receptor acting as a transducer?
**Answer:** An example of a receptor acting as a transducer is found in the retina of the eye, where rod and cone cells convert light energy into nerve impulses (electrochemical energy).

**Q483.** What are muscles and glands primarily identified as in the context of nervous transmission?
**Answer:** Muscles and glands are identified as the effectors, which carry out the response to a stimulus after receiving commands from the nervous system.

**Q484.** What are the basic structural and functional units of the nervous system?
**Answer:** Neurons are explicitly stated as the basic structural and functional unit of the nervous system.

**Q485.** Which of the following is NOT one of the three basic components of all neurons?
**Answer:** While a myelin sheath may cover some axons, the three basic components found in all neurons are the cell body, dendrites, and an axon.

**Q486.** What is the primary function of dendrites?
**Answer:** The function of the dendrite is to receive stimuli and conduct impulses to the cell body.

**Q487.** What structures are responsible for conducting action potentials from the neuron cell body to the presynaptic terminals?
**Answer:** Axons functionally conduct action potentials from the neuron cell body to the presynaptic terminals, meaning they conduct signals away from the cell body.

**Q488.** What is the fatty substance that acts as an insulator around axons?
**Answer:** Schwann cells are covered by a fatty substance called myelin sheath that acts as an insulator, making these axons myelinated fibers.

**Q489.** What is the non-myelinated part of an axon between two Schwann cells called?
**Answer:** A non-myelinated part of an axon between two Schwann cells is called a node of Ranvier.

**Q490.** Which type of neuron conducts impulses towards the central nervous system from receptors?
**Answer:** Sensory neurons are responsible for conducting impulses towards the central nervous system from the sensory receptors.

**Q491.** Where do interneurons primarily occur?
**Answer:** Interneurons occur entirely within the CNS and convey messages between various parts of the CNS.

**Q492.** Which statement accurately describes motor neurons?
**Answer:** Motor neurons conduct impulses away from the central nervous system, and their terminal branches are connected to an effector.

**Q493.** Which factor contributes to a faster velocity of nerve impulse transmission?
**Answer:** The myelinated axons transmit information much faster than other neurons, and the velocity of nerve impulse is faster in myelinated neuron fibers due to saltatory conduction.

**Q494.** What is saltatory conduction?
**Answer:** Saltatory conduction is the rapid transmission of a nerve impulse along an axon, resulting from the action potential jumping from one node of Ranvier to another, skipping the myelinated regions of membrane.

**Q495.** How does the diameter of a neuron fiber affect the velocity of nerve impulse?
**Answer:** Thick neuron fibers conduct faster impulses than thin fibers because resistance to electrical current flow is inversely proportional to the cross-sectional area of the conductor, meaning increased thickness leads to decreased resistance.

**Q496.** What is the cytoplasm of a neuron cell body characterized by?
**Answer:** The cytoplasm of the cell body is characterized by the presence of Nissl's granules, which are groups of ribosomes and rough ER associated with protein synthesis.

**Q497.** What type of neurons have three or more processes, typically several dendrites and one axon?
**Answer:** Multipolar neurons are characterized by having three or more processes, specifically several dendrites and one axon.

**Q498.** What is the role of Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system?
**Answer:** Schwann cells are neuroglial cells in the peripheral nervous system that support, protect, and nourish the neurons. They typically wrap around axon fibers and are covered by a myelin sheath.

**Q499.** What is a reflex action?
**Answer:** A reflex action is defined as a rapid, involuntary, and unconscious response to a stimulus, occurring almost instantaneously.

**Q500.** In a typical reflex arc, what is the role of the interneuron?
**Answer:** In a reflex arc, the interneuron (also called associative neuron) acts as a link between the sensory neuron and the motor neuron, processing the signal within the CNS.

**Q501.** What happens if the sensory neuron in a reflex arc is damaged?
**Answer:** If the sensory neuron is damaged, the stimulus cannot be successfully received by the CNS and thus no reflex action will occur.

**Q502.** Which component of a reflex arc carries impulses away from the central nervous system to the muscles or glands?
**Answer:** Motor neurons conduct impulses away from the central nervous system to the muscles or glands (effectors).

**Q503.** The withdrawal reflex, where a hand is pulled away from a hot object, involves which type of neurons?
**Answer:** The withdrawal reflex is a classic example of a reflex arc involving all three types of neurons: sensory (detects heat), interneuron (processes in spinal cord), and motor (activates muscles to withdraw).

**Q504.** Where is the cell body of a sensory neuron typically located in a reflex arc?
**Answer:** The cell body of a sensory neuron is generally located in the dorsal root ganglion, which is part of the Peripheral Nervous System, outside the CNS.

**Q505.** Which of the following is an effector in a reflex arc?
**Answer:** Effectors are the structures that respond to the nerve impulse, such as muscles contracting or glands secreting.

**Q506.** A knee-jerk reflex is an example of what type of reflex arc?
**Answer:** The knee-jerk reflex is a monosynaptic reflex, meaning it involves only one synapse between the sensory and motor neuron, without an interneuron.

**Q507.** What is the correct sequence of components in a reflex arc?
**Answer:** The typical pathway of a reflex arc is: Receptor detects stimulus, Sensory neuron transmits to CNS, Interneuron processes (in CNS), Motor neuron transmits to effector, Effector produces response.

**Q508.** What is a nerve impulse defined as?
**Answer:** A nerve impulse is defined as an electrochemical change which travels along the length of a neuron.

**Q509.** What is the state of a neuron membrane when it is at its resting potential?
**Answer:** When a neuron is at its resting potential, it is in a polarized state, meaning there is a difference in electrical charge across the membrane.

**Q510.** Which of the following ions has a higher concentration inside the neuron cell during the resting membrane potential?
**Answer:** During the resting membrane potential, there is a higher concentration of potassium ions (K+) inside the cell compared to the outside.

**Q511.** What is the primary role of the Sodium-Potassium (Na+/K+) pump in maintaining the resting membrane potential?
**Answer:** The Na+/K+ pump actively transports 3 Na+ ions out of the cell and 2 K+ ions into the cell, which significantly contributes to maintaining the negative resting membrane potential.

**Q512.** What contributes to the negative charge inside the neuron during resting potential, in addition to the Na+/K+ pump?
**Answer:** The presence of negatively charged organic ions (proteins, amino acids) inside the cytoplasm, which cannot easily cross the membrane, contributes to the overall negative charge inside.

**Q513.** What is the immediate event that occurs when a stimulus reaches the threshold level, leading to an action potential?
**Answer:** When a stimulus reaches the threshold level, voltage-gated Na+ channels open rapidly, causing Na+ ions to rush into the cell, leading to depolarization.

**Q514.** What is the term for the rapid influx of sodium ions that causes the inside of the neuron membrane to become positive?
**Answer:** Depolarization is the phase during an action potential where the rapid influx of Na+ ions causes the inside of the membrane to become positive relative to the outside.

**Q515.** During the repolarization phase of an action potential, which ion movement is primarily responsible for restoring the negative charge inside the cell?
**Answer:** Repolarization occurs as voltage-gated K+ channels open, allowing K+ ions to rush out of the cell, making the inside of the membrane negative again.

**Q516.** What is hyperpolarization?
**Answer:** Hyperpolarization is a brief period after repolarization where the membrane potential becomes even more negative than the resting potential, due to the slow closing of K+ channels.

**Q517.** What is the minimum strength of a stimulus required to generate an action potential?
**Answer:** A threshold stimulus is the minimum strength of a stimulus required to generate an action potential.

**Q518.** If a stimulus is applied that is too weak to cause a significant change in membrane potential, it is referred to as a:
**Answer:** A sub-threshold stimulus is one that is too weak to reach the threshold potential and therefore does not generate an action potential.

**Q519.** What is the refractory period of a neuron?
**Answer:** The refractory period is a brief period immediately following an action potential during which the neuron is unable to generate another action potential, ensuring one-way propagation.

**Q520.** In which type of neuron fiber does saltatory conduction occur, leading to faster impulse transmission?
**Answer:** Saltatory conduction, which involves the action potential "jumping" between Nodes of Ranvier, occurs only in myelinated fibers, significantly increasing the speed of impulse transmission.

**Q521.** How does the diameter of an axon influence the velocity of nerve impulse conduction?
**Answer:** Larger diameter axons conduct impulses faster because their larger cross-sectional area offers less resistance to the flow of local currents, allowing depolarization to spread more rapidly.

**Q522.** The "All-or-None Law" applies to:
**Answer:** The "All-or-None Law" states that if a stimulus reaches threshold, an action potential will occur with full strength, or it won't occur at all if the stimulus is sub-threshold. The magnitude of the action potential is independent of the stimulus strength once threshold is reached.

**Q523.** Which ion channels are crucial for the repolarization phase of an action potential?
**Answer:** The opening of voltage-gated K+ channels and the subsequent efflux of K+ ions are primarily responsible for the repolarization phase, bringing the membrane potential back towards resting levels.

**Q524.** During which phase of an action potential is the membrane most permeable to sodium ions?
**Answer:** During depolarization, voltage-gated sodium channels open, causing a large influx of Na+ ions, making the membrane highly permeable to sodium.

**Q525.** The initial segment of the axon where action potentials are typically generated is called the:
**Answer:** The axon hillock is the specialized region where the axon originates from the cell body, and it is typically where the summation of graded potentials occurs to trigger an action potential.

**Q526.** What maintains the resting membrane potential of a neuron?
**Answer:** The resting membrane potential is maintained by both the active transport of ions via the Na+/K+ pump (pumping 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in) and the higher permeability of the membrane to K+ ions through leak channels, leading to a net efflux of K+ and a negative internal charge.

**Q527.** What is the specialized junction where a nerve impulse is transmitted from one neuron to another, or from a neuron to an effector cell?
**Answer:** A synapse is the specialized junction or point of contact where an impulse is transmitted from one neuron to another or to an effector organ like a muscle or gland.

**Q528.** Which part of a synapse is located at the end of the presynaptic axon and contains synaptic vesicles?
**Answer:** The synaptic knob (or terminal) is the swollen end of the presynaptic axon, which contains synaptic vesicles filled with neurotransmitters.

**Q529.** What is the narrow gap between the presynaptic and postsynaptic neurons called?
**Answer:** The synaptic cleft is the narrow space that separates the presynaptic membrane from the postsynaptic membrane.

**Q530.** What are the chemical messengers stored in synaptic vesicles and released into the synaptic cleft?
**Answer:** Neurotransmitters are chemical substances stored in synaptic vesicles within the presynaptic terminal, which are released into the synaptic cleft to transmit signals.

**Q531.** What is the essential role of Calcium (Ca2+) ions in synaptic transmission?
**Answer:** When an action potential reaches the presynaptic terminal, voltage-gated Ca2+ channels open, and the influx of Ca2+ ions triggers the exocytosis of synaptic vesicles, releasing neurotransmitters into the cleft.

**Q532.** Upon release, what do neurotransmitters bind to on the postsynaptic membrane?
**Answer:** Neurotransmitters diffuse across the synaptic cleft and bind to specific receptor proteins on the postsynaptic membrane, causing a change in its permeability to ions.

**Q533.** Which type of neurotransmitter increases the likelihood of an action potential in the postsynaptic neuron?
**Answer:** Excitatory neurotransmitters cause a depolarization of the postsynaptic membrane, making it more likely for an action potential to be generated.

**Q534.** Which type of neurotransmitter decreases the likelihood of an action potential in the postsynaptic neuron?
**Answer:** Inhibitory neurotransmitters cause hyperpolarization or stabilization of the resting potential of the postsynaptic membrane, making it less likely for an action potential to be generated.

**Q535.** The process of neurotransmitter removal from the synaptic cleft is crucial for:
**Answer:** Neurotransmitters must be quickly removed from the synaptic cleft (by enzymatic degradation or reuptake) to ensure that the postsynaptic neuron is not continuously stimulated or inhibited, allowing for precise and rapid control over neural signaling.

**Q536.** What is the direct effect of a neurotransmitter binding to its receptor on the postsynaptic membrane?
**Answer:** The binding of a neurotransmitter to its receptor directly causes ion channels to open or close, leading to a change in the postsynaptic membrane potential (either depolarization for excitation or hyperpolarization for inhibition).

**Q537.** The human nervous system is broadly divided into which two main parts?
**Answer:** The human nervous system consists of the central nervous system (CNS) and the peripheral nervous system (PNS).

**Q538.** Which part of the nervous system serves as the coordinating center and lies in the midline of the body?
**Answer:** The CNS is identified as the coordinating center and lies in the midline of the body, comprising the brain and spinal cord.

**Q539.** What is the primary function of the Peripheral Nervous System (PNS)?
**Answer:** The PNS transmits information from receptors to the CNS and transmits orders and commands from the CNS to effectors.

**Q540.** Which division of the human nervous system controls voluntary movements?
**Answer:** The Somatic Nervous System controls voluntary movements.

**Q541.** The "Fight or Flight" response is primarily mediated by which division of the Autonomic Nervous System?
**Answer:** The Sympathetic Division of the Autonomic Nervous System is responsible for the "Fight or Flight" response.

**Q542.** Which division of the Autonomic Nervous System is primarily associated with "Rest and Digest" functions?
**Answer:** The Parasympathetic Division of the Autonomic Nervous System controls involuntary responses and is associated with "Rest and Digest" activities.

**Q543.** What are the two main components of the Central Nervous System (CNS)?
**Answer:** The Central Nervous System (CNS) consists of the Brain and Spinal Cord.

**Q544.** The brain is responsible for all of the following EXCEPT:
**Answer:** The brain receives and processes sensory information, initiates responses, stores memories, and generates thoughts and emotions. Reflex activities are primarily controlled by the spinal cord.

**Q545.** What is the primary function of the spinal cord?
**Answer:** The spinal cord conducts signals to and from the brain and controls reflex activities.

**Q546.** Which of the following best describes the function of cranial and spinal nerves?
**Answer:** Cranial and spinal nerves are components of the Peripheral Nervous System (PNS) that connect the CNS to receptors and effectors throughout the body.

**Q547.** Which of the following is categorized as a narcotic drug affecting the nervous system?
**Answer:** Heroine is a narcotic drug, which affects the nervous system by acting on opiate receptors, producing feelings of euphoria and pain relief.

**Q548.** Which of the following is a common withdrawal symptom associated with alcohol addiction?
**Answer:** Convulsions are explicitly mentioned as a withdrawal symptom of alcohol, indicating the severe impact of alcohol dependence on the nervous system.

**Q549.** Drug addiction is primarily characterized by:
**Answer:** Drug addiction is characterized by both psychological dependence (craving the drug) and physiological dependence (withdrawal symptoms upon cessation), meaning the body has adapted to the drug's presence.

**Q550.** What is drug tolerance?
**Answer:** Drug tolerance refers to the phenomenon where, with repeated exposure, the body adapts to a drug, requiring increasingly larger doses to produce the same initial effect.

**Q551.** Cannabis is classified as:
**Answer:** Cannabis (Marijuana) is classified as a hallucinogen, affecting perception and mood.

**Q552.** Which of the following is a vascular disorder of the nervous system?
**Answer:** Stroke is a vascular disorder of the nervous system, typically caused by a disruption of blood flow to the brain, leading to brain damage.

**Q553.** Which infectious disorder of the nervous system involves inflammation of the brain membranes?
**Answer:** Meningitis is an infectious disease of the nervous system characterized by inflammation of the meninges, the protective membranes surrounding the brain and spinal cord.

**Q554.** Which diagnostic test uses magnetic fields and radio waves to create detailed images of the brain and spinal cord?
**Answer:** Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) is a diagnostic test that utilizes strong magnetic fields and radio waves to produce detailed images of organs and soft tissues, including the brain and spinal cord.

**Q555.** Which neurodegenerative disorder is characterized by progressive memory loss and cognitive decline, primarily affecting older adults?
**Answer:** Alzheimer's disease is a common neurodegenerative disorder primarily affecting older adults, characterized by progressive memory loss, cognitive decline, and changes in behavior.

**Q556.** An Electroencephalogram (EEG) is primarily used to:
**Answer:** An Electroencephalogram (EEG) is a diagnostic tool that measures and records the electrical activity of the brain, useful in diagnosing conditions like epilepsy and sleep disorders.

**Q557.** What is the primary role of hormones in the body?
**Answer:** Hormones are chemical messengers secreted by endocrine glands that travel through the bloodstream to target cells, regulating a wide range of physiological processes.

**Q558.** How does the endocrine system primarily coordinate body cells?
**Answer:** The endocrine system coordinates most body cells by secreting hormones (chemical messengers) directly into the bloodstream, which then travel to target cells.

**Q559.** Which type of gland secretes its products directly into the bloodstream?
**Answer:** Endocrine glands are ductless glands that secrete hormones directly into the bloodstream, which then transports them to target cells or organs.

**Q560.** Which type of gland secretes its products into ducts?
**Answer:** Exocrine glands secrete their products, such as digestive enzymes or sweat, into ducts that carry them to a specific external or internal surface.

**Q561.** Which of the following is an example of an exocrine gland?
**Answer:** Salivary glands are exocrine glands that secrete saliva through ducts into the mouth. The pancreas has both endocrine (insulin, glucagon) and exocrine (digestive enzymes) functions, but for this question, salivary gland is a clear example of a solely exocrine function.

**Q562.** Which of the following hormone types is derived from cholesterol and is lipid-soluble?
**Answer:** Steroid hormones (e.g., testosterone, estrogen) are lipid-soluble hormones derived from cholesterol.

**Q563.** Which chemical nature characterizes hormones like insulin and growth hormone?
**Answer:** Insulin and growth hormone are examples of protein or peptide hormones, which are composed of amino acids.

**Q564.** Which of the following hormones typically utilizes a "second messenger system" to exert its effects on target cells?
**Answer:** Protein (and peptide) hormones, being water-soluble, cannot directly cross the cell membrane. They bind to receptors on the cell surface and activate a second messenger system, such as cAMP, to trigger intracellular responses. Insulin is a protein hormone.

**Q565.** How do steroid hormones typically exert their action on target cells?
**Answer:** Steroid hormones are lipid-soluble and can directly diffuse across the cell membrane. They bind to intracellular receptors (in the cytoplasm or nucleus) and the hormone-receptor complex then acts directly on DNA to activate or repress gene transcription, leading to protein synthesis.

**Q566.** Epinephrine are derived from which type of molecule?
**Answer:** Catecholamines (like epinephrine and norepinephrine) are amine hormones derived from amino acids (specifically tyrosine).

**Q567.** Which hormone characteristic allows it to easily pass through the cell membrane to reach intracellular receptors?
**Answer:** Hormones that are lipid-soluble (like steroid hormones and thyroid hormones) can readily diffuse across the lipid bilayer of the cell membrane to bind to receptors inside the cell.

**Q568.** Cyclic AMP (cAMP) often acts as a second messenger for which type of hormone?
**Answer:** Cyclic AMP (cAMP) is a common second messenger activated when water-soluble protein/peptide hormones bind to their receptors on the cell surface.

**Q569.** When a protein hormone binds to its receptor on the cell membrane, what is the immediate effect inside the cell?
**Answer:** The binding of a protein hormone to its cell surface receptor typically activates an enzyme (like adenylate cyclase) that then produces a second messenger, such as cAMP, inside the cell.

**Q570.** Which of the following is an example of an amino acid derivative hormone?
**Answer:** Thyroxine (T4) is an amine hormone derived from the amino acid tyrosine, similar to catecholamines, but with different synthesis and action mechanisms.

**Q571.** What is the primary advantage of the second messenger system for water-soluble hormones?
**Answer:** The second messenger system provides signal amplification, meaning a single hormone molecule binding to its receptor can lead to the production of many second messenger molecules, resulting in a large and rapid cellular response.

**Q572.** Which part of the brain is considered the "master control center" for the endocrine system?
**Answer:** The hypothalamus is described as the "master control center" because it plays a crucial role in regulating the anterior pituitary gland and influencing various endocrine functions.

**Q573.** The hypothalamus produces releasing and inhibiting factors that primarily control the secretions of which gland?
**Answer:** The hypothalamus produces various releasing hormones (e.g., GnRH, TRH) and inhibiting hormones (e.g., GHIH, PIH) that regulate the secretion of hormones from the anterior pituitary gland.

**Q574.** Which two hormones are produced by the hypothalamus but stored and released by the posterior pituitary gland?
**Answer:** Antidiuretic Hormone (ADH) and Oxytocin are neurohormones produced by neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus, but they are transported down axons and stored in the posterior pituitary gland until their release.

**Q575.** What is the primary function of ADH (Antidiuretic Hormone)?
**Answer:** ADH (Vasopressin) primarily acts on the kidneys to increase water reabsorption, thus conserving body water and concentrating urine.

**Q576.** Which hormone, produced by the hypothalamus and released by the posterior pituitary, plays a key role in uterine contractions during childbirth and milk ejection?
**Answer:** Oxytocin is known for its role in stimulating uterine contractions during labor and promoting milk ejection during breastfeeding.

**Q577.** Where is the pituitary gland primarily located?
**Answer:** The pituitary gland is a pea-sized gland located at the base of the brain, beneath the hypothalamus, to which it is functionally and structurally connected.

**Q578.** How many lobes does the pituitary gland typically have?
**Answer:** The pituitary gland consists of three lobes: the anterior lobe, the posterior lobe, and the median (intermediate) lobe.

**Q579.** Which lobe of the pituitary gland is often referred to as the "master gland"?
**Answer:** The anterior lobe of the pituitary gland is often called the "master gland" because it produces and secretes several hormones that regulate the function of other endocrine glands, such as the thyroid, adrenal cortex, and gonads.

**Q580.** Which hormone secreted by the anterior pituitary stimulates overall body growth, especially bone and muscle?
**Answer:** Growth Hormone (GH), also known as Somatotropic Hormone (STH), is secreted by the anterior pituitary and promotes overall body growth, particularly in bones and muscles.

**Q581.** What is the condition caused by the oversecretion of Growth Hormone (GH) in adults?
**Answer:** Acromegaly is a disorder resulting from the oversecretion of GH in adults, causing abnormal growth of bones in the face, hands, and feet after epiphyseal plates have closed.

**Q582.** Undersecretion of STH (Somatotropic Hormone) in children can lead to which condition?
**Answer:** Undersecretion of Growth Hormone (GH) during childhood leads to dwarfism, characterized by stunted growth.

**Q583.** Which anterior pituitary hormone stimulates the thyroid gland to produce and secrete its hormones?
**Answer:** Thyroid Stimulating Hormone (TSH), also known as thyrotropin, is secreted by the anterior pituitary and stimulates the thyroid gland to produce and release thyroid hormones.

**Q584.** Which two gonadotropic hormones are secreted by the anterior pituitary?
**Answer:** The anterior pituitary secretes two gonadotropic hormones: Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) and Luteinizing Hormone (LH), which regulate the function of the gonads.

**Q585.** What is the primary function of Prolactin secreted by the anterior pituitary?
**Answer:** Prolactin (LTH - Luteotropic Hormone) primarily stimulates and maintains milk production after childbirth.

**Q586.** Which hormone is secreted by the median lobe of the pituitary gland?
**Answer:** The median (intermediate) lobe of the pituitary gland secretes Melanocyte Stimulating Hormone (MSH).

**Q587.** Which of the following hormones is released from the posterior pituitary but is actually produced in the hypothalamus?
**Answer:** ADH (Antidiuretic Hormone) and Oxytocin are produced by neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus and then transported to and stored in the posterior pituitary for release.

**Q588.** Diabetes insipidus is a disorder caused by the undersecretion of which hormone?
**Answer:** Diabetes insipidus is a condition characterized by excessive urination and thirst, caused by the undersecretion of ADH (Vasopressin) from the posterior pituitary, leading to impaired water reabsorption by the kidneys.

**Q589.** What is the primary function of Oxytocin?
**Answer:** Oxytocin's main functions include stimulating strong uterine contractions during labor and promoting milk ejection ("let-down") during breastfeeding.

**Q590.** If a person has abnormally low levels of ADH, what would be a likely symptom?
**Answer:** Low levels of ADH would result in the kidneys reabsorbing less water, leading to increased urine output and potentially dehydration.

**Q591.** What is the general term for the hormones secreted by the anterior pituitary that influence the activity of other endocrine glands?
**Answer:** Many hormones from the anterior pituitary are "tropic hormones" (or tropins) because they target and stimulate other endocrine glands to secrete their own hormones (e.g., TSH on thyroid, ACTH on adrenal cortex).

**Q592.** Where is the thyroid gland located?
**Answer:** The thyroid gland is located in the neck, anterior to the trachea, below the larynx.

**Q593.** Which two hormones, containing iodine, are primarily responsible for regulating the body's metabolic rate?
**Answer:** Thyroxine (T4) and Triiodothyronine (T3) are the two iodine-containing hormones produced by the thyroid gland that play a crucial role in regulating basal metabolic rate.

**Q594.** What is the primary function of Calcitonin, secreted by the thyroid gland?
**Answer:** Calcitonin is involved in calcium homeostasis; it works to decrease blood calcium levels by inhibiting osteoclast activity and promoting calcium deposition in bones.

**Q595.** Graves' disease is a condition characterized by hyperthyroidism. Which of the following symptoms is commonly associated with it?
**Answer:** Graves' disease is associated with hyperthyroidism (overactive thyroid), which leads to symptoms like increased metabolic rate, weight loss, heat intolerance, and nervousness.

**Q596.** Cretinism, a condition characterized by stunted physical and mental growth in children, is caused by:
**Answer:** Cretinism is caused by severe hypothyroidism (underactive thyroid) during infancy or early childhood, leading to impaired physical and mental development.

**Q597.** Where are the parathyroid glands typically located?
**Answer:** The parathyroid glands are usually four small glands embedded in the posterior surface of the thyroid gland.

**Q598.** What is the main hormone secreted by the parathyroid glands?
**Answer:** The parathyroid glands secrete Parathyroid Hormone (PTH), which is the primary regulator of calcium levels in the blood.

**Q599.** What is the primary function of Parathyroid Hormone?
**Answer:** PTH acts to increase blood calcium levels by stimulating osteoclasts to break down bone, enhancing calcium reabsorption in the kidneys, and promoting the synthesis of vitamin D, which aids in intestinal calcium absorption.

**Q600.** The endocrine functions of the pancreas are carried out by specialized clusters of cells called:
**Answer:** The endocrine cells of the pancreas are organized into clusters called Islets of Langerhans, which produce hormones.

**Q601.** Which hormone is secreted by the beta cells of the Islets of Langerhans?
**Answer:** Insulin, secreted by the beta cells of the Islets of Langerhans, acts to lower blood glucose levels by promoting glucose uptake by cells and its conversion to glycogen.

**Q602.** Which hormone raises blood glucose levels?
**Answer:** Glucagon, secreted by the alpha cells of the Islets of Langerhans, raises blood glucose levels primarily by stimulating the liver to release stored glucose (glycogenolysis) and produce new glucose (gluconeogenesis).

**Q603.** When blood glucose levels are high, which hormone is released to bring them back to normal?
**Answer:** Insulin is released in response to high blood glucose levels to facilitate glucose uptake by cells and lower blood sugar.

**Q604.** What type of feedback mechanism controls the secretion of both insulin and glucagon to maintain blood glucose homeostasis?
**Answer:** The secretion of both insulin and glucagon is regulated by a negative feedback mechanism to maintain stable blood glucose levels. When glucose is high, insulin is released to lower it; when glucose is low, glucagon is released to raise it, thus opposing the initial change.

**Q605.** Which two main subdivisions make up the adrenal gland?
**Answer:** The adrenal gland is divided into two main parts: the outer adrenal cortex and the inner adrenal medulla.

**Q606.** Which part of the adrenal gland secretes catecholamines like Epinephrine (Adrenaline) and Norepinephrine (Noradrenaline)?
**Answer:** The adrenal medulla is responsible for secreting the catecholamines Epinephrine and Norepinephrine, which are involved in the "fight or flight" response.

**Q607.** Which hormone, secreted by the adrenal cortex, is a glucocorticoid?
**Answer:** Cortisol is a glucocorticoid hormone produced by the adrenal cortex that plays a vital role in metabolism, immune response, and the body's response to stress.

**Q608.** Which mineralocorticoid hormone, secreted by the adrenal cortex, is crucial for regulating sodium and potassium balance and blood pressure?
**Answer:** Aldosterone, a mineralocorticoid from the adrenal cortex, is essential for maintaining electrolyte balance by regulating sodium and potassium levels in the blood, thereby influencing blood pressure.

**Q609.** Androgens, which are male sex hormones, are also produced in small amounts by which part of the adrenal gland?
**Answer:** The adrenal cortex also produces small amounts of androgens, which are male sex hormones, in both males and females.

**Q610.** What is the primary male sex hormone produced by the testes?
**Answer:** Testosterone is the primary male sex hormone secreted by the interstitial cells of the testes.

**Q611.** Which hormone secreted by the ovaries is primarily responsible for the development of female secondary sex characteristics and the regulation of the menstrual cycle?
**Answer:** Estrogens are the female sex hormones primarily produced by the ovaries, responsible for the development of female secondary sex characteristics and various aspects of the menstrual cycle.

**Q612.** Which ovarian hormone is mainly secreted by the corpus luteum after ovulation and is crucial for preparing and maintaining the uterus for pregnancy?
**Answer:** Progesterone is produced by the corpus luteum (and later the placenta during pregnancy) and is vital for preparing the uterine lining for implantation and maintaining pregnancy.

**Q613.** What is the primary function of Inhibin, produced by both testes and ovaries?
**Answer:** Inhibin, secreted by Sertoli cells in the testes and granulosa cells in the ovaries, acts to inhibit the secretion of Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) from the anterior pituitary.

**Q614.** Undersecretion of progesterone during the menstrual cycle may lead to:
**Answer:** Undersecretion of progesterone during the menstrual cycle decreases the chance of pregnancy and may cause early menstruation or, if pregnancy occurs, may lead to miscarriage.

**Q615.** Which hormone is primarily secreted by the Pineal Gland and helps regulate circadian rhythm?
**Answer:** The pineal gland secretes Melatonin, a hormone that plays a crucial role in regulating the body's circadian rhythms, including sleep-wake cycles.

**Q616.** The Thymus gland secretes hormones called Thymosins, which are vital for the development and maturation of which type of immune cell?
**Answer:** Thymosins, secreted by the thymus gland, are essential for the maturation and differentiation of T lymphocytes (T cells), which are crucial components of the immune system.

**Q617.** The heart produces hormones known as Natriuretic Peptides. What is their primary role?
**Answer:** The heart secretes natriuretic peptides (e.g., ANP), which primarily act to lower blood pressure and blood volume by promoting sodium and water excretion by the kidneys.

**Q618.** Which hormone is produced by the kidneys and stimulates the production of red blood cells in the bone marrow?
**Answer:** Erythropoietin (EPO) is a hormone secreted by the kidneys in response to low oxygen levels, which stimulates the bone marrow to produce more red blood cells.

**Q619.** Which active form of Vitamin D is produced by the kidneys and is essential for calcium absorption in the intestine?
**Answer:** The kidneys convert an inactive form of Vitamin D into its active form, Calcitriol, which is crucial for the absorption of calcium from the digestive tract.

**Q620.** Adipose tissue (fat tissue) secretes a hormone that plays a role in regulating appetite and metabolism. What is this hormone called?
**Answer:** Adipose tissue produces and secretes Leptin, a hormone that signals satiety to the brain, helping to regulate energy balance and metabolism.

**Q621.** The digestive tract produces numerous hormones that regulate digestion and absorption. Which of the following is an example of a hormone produced by the stomach that stimulates gastric acid secretion?
**Answer:** Gastrin is a hormone produced by the stomach that stimulates the secretion of gastric acid (HCl) by the parietal cells, playing a key role in digestion.

**Q649.** The arrival of fats in the duodenum initiates the release of which two hormones?

**Q652.** The bile pigment bilirubin is formed from the breakdown of what molecule?

**Q654.** Which organ synthesizes blood clotting proteins like prothrombin and fibrinogen?

**Q655.** In the liver, toxic ammonia is converted into which less toxic substance?

**Q656.** What is the main function of bicarbonate ions in pancreatic juice?

**Q657.** The inactive enzyme trypsinogen is activated to trypsin by what?

**Q658.** Which pancreatic enzyme continues the digestion of polysaccharides?

**Q659.** Why are proteases like trypsin secreted from the pancreas in an inactive form?

**Q660.** What are the final absorbable products of carbohydrate digestion?
