**Answer:** At the intestinal brush border

**Q686.** Why is defecation an automatic reflex in infants?
 **A.** Lack of control over the internal anal sphincter
 **B.** Lack of control over the ileocecal sphincter
 **C.** Lack of control over the rectal muscles
 **D.** Lack of control over the external anal sphincter ✓
**Answer:** Lack of control over the external anal sphincter

**Q687.** Which substance synthesized by the liver acts as an anticoagulant?
 **A.** Fibrinogen
 **B.** Prothrombin
 **C.** Vitamin K
 **D.** Heparin ✓
**Answer:** Heparin

**Q688.** The Nobel Prize in 2005 recognized the discovery of which bacterium's role in ulcers?
 **A.** Salmonella enterica
 **B.** Campylobacter jejuni
 **C.** Escherichia coli
 **D.** Helicobacter pylori ✓
**Answer:** Helicobacter pylori

**Q689.** What are the three major pairs of salivary glands?
 **A.** Buccal, lingual, and palatine
 **B.** Gastric, duodenal, and intestinal
 **C.** Hepatic, cystic, and pancreatic
 **D.** Parotid, submandibular, and sublingual ✓
**Answer:** Parotid, submandibular, and sublingual

**Q690.** What is the primary function of the gastric folds known as rugae?
 **A.** Increase absorptive area
 **B.** Secrete hydrochloric acid
 **C.** Anchor the stomach wall
 **D.** Allow stomach expansion ✓
**Answer:** Allow stomach expansion

**Q691.** What type of epithelium covers the surface of an intestinal villus?
 **A.** Stratified squamous
 **B.** Simple cuboidal
 **C.** Transitional
 **D.** Simple columnar ✓
**Answer:** Simple columnar

**Q692.** The endocrine function of the pancreas is performed by which structures?
 **A.** Acini cells
 **B.** Kupffer cells
 **C.** Ductal cells
 **D.** Pancreatic islets ✓
**Answer:** Pancreatic islets

**Q693.** The stomach's muscularis layer contains a third layer of what type of muscle?
 **A.** Skeletal muscle
 **B.** Diagonal muscle
 **C.** Transverse muscle
 **D.** Oblique muscle ✓
**Answer:** Oblique muscle

**Q694.** Besides stimulating the pancreas, cholecystokinin (CCK) also causes what action?
 **A.** Stomach acid secretion
 **B.** Hepatic glucose production
 **C.** LES sphincter relaxation
 **D.** Gallbladder contraction ✓
**Answer:** Gallbladder contraction

**Q695.** Immunoglobulin and lysozyme in saliva contribute to what function?
 **A.** Digesting starch
 **B.** Lubricating food
 **C.** Neutralizing acids
 **D.** Preventing bacterial infection ✓
**Answer:** Preventing bacterial infection

**Q696.** How is ingested cellulose handled by the human digestive system?
 **A.** It is digested by pancreatic amylase
 **B.** It is absorbed in the large intestine
 **C.** It is broken down by stomach acid
 **D.** It passes through largely undigested ✓
**Answer:** It passes through largely undigested

**Q697.** The 'brush border' of the small intestine is formed by what structures?
 **A.** Villi
 **B.** Goblet cells
 **C.** Lacteals
 **D.** Microvilli ✓
**Answer:** Microvilli

**Q698.** Anorexia nervosa is a serious eating disorder primarily characterized by what?
 **A.** Binging and purging
 **B.** Excessive food intake
 **C.** Imperfect digestion
 **D.** Fear of becoming obese ✓
**Answer:** Fear of becoming obese

**Q699.** The removal of amino groups from amino acids, or deamination, occurs in which organ?
 **A.** Kidneys
 **B.** Small intestine
 **C.** Stomach
 **D.** Liver ✓
**Answer:** Liver

**Q700.** Which pancreatic cells produce the exocrine secretion known as pancreatic juice?
 **A.** Islets of Langerhans
 **B.** Parietal cells
 **C.** Hepatocytes
 **D.** Acini cells ✓
**Answer:** Acini cells

**Q701.** Gluconeogenesis in the liver refers to the synthesis of glucose from what?
 **A.** The breakdown of glycogen
 **B.** Fructose and galactose
 **C.** The breakdown of starch
 **D.** Non-carbohydrate sources ✓
**Answer:** Non-carbohydrate sources

**Q702.** What is the primary digestive function of the pharynx?
 **A.** Secrete mucus and amylase
 **B.** Begin protein digestion
 **C.** Absorb water and electrolytes
 **D.** Act as a passageway for the bolus ✓
**Answer:** Act as a passageway for the bolus

**Q703.** What is the straight, muscular tube that precedes the anal canal?
 **A.** Sigmoid colon
 **B.** Appendix
 **C.** Descending colon
 **D.** Rectum ✓
**Answer:** Rectum

**Q704.** What are the main products of lipid digestion by pancreatic lipase?
 **A.** Amino acids
 **B.** Monosaccharides
 **C.** Bile salts and cholesterol
 **D.** Free fatty acids and glycerol ✓
**Answer:** Free fatty acids and glycerol

**Q705.** What are the gentle, 20-second interval contractions in the stomach called?
 **A.** Segmentation contractions
 **B.** Mass movements
 **C.** Tonic contractions
 **D.** Mixing waves ✓
**Answer:** Mixing waves

**Q706.** Which group of vitamins is stored in the liver?
 **A.** C and most B vitamins
 **B.** A and C only
 **C.** K and B6 only
 **D.** A, D, E, K, and B12 ✓
**Answer:** A, D, E, K, and B12

**Q707.** Food poisoning from \*Salmonella\* often occurs due to what?
 **A.** Excessive stomach acid
 **B.** An autoimmune reaction
 **C.** Self-induced vomiting
 **D.** Cross-contamination from raw meat ✓
**Answer:** Cross-contamination from raw meat

**Q708.** What is the medical term for having an abnormal amount of body fat?
 **A.** Anorexia nervosa
 **B.** Dyspepsia
 **C.** Obesity ✓
**Answer:** Obesity

**Q709.** Where are the phagocytic Kupffer cells that destroy old red blood cells located?
 **A.** Spleen
 **B.** Pancreas
 **C.** Small intestine
 **D.** Liver ✓
**Answer:** Liver

**Q710.** What is the main role of the lower esophageal sphincter?
 **A.** Regulate stomach emptying
 **B.** Initiate swallowing
 **C.** Mix the bolus with mucus
 **D.** Prevent acid reflux ✓
**Answer:** Prevent acid reflux

**Q711.** What is the term for gases produced by bacterial action in the colon?
**Answer:** Faeces

**Q712.** Which enzymes in pancreatic juice digest DNA and RNA?
 **A.** Amylase and lipase
 **B.** Trypsin and chymotrypsin
 **C.** Pepsin and gastrin
 **D.** Deoxyribonucleases and ribonucleases ✓
**Answer:** Deoxyribonucleases and ribonucleases

**Q713.** Hyperplastic obesity is a condition characterized by what?
 **A.** An increase in fat cell size
 **B.** Uneven fat distribution
 **C.** A decreased metabolic rate
 **D.** A greater number of fat cells ✓
**Answer:** A greater number of fat cells

### Enzymes (72 Questions)

**Q715.** Which of the following is a primary distinguishing characteristic of enzymes?
 **A.** They are consumed during the reaction.
 **B.** They increase the activation energy of a reaction.
 **C.** They are always simple proteins.
 **D.** They are highly specific in their action. ✓
**Answer:** They are highly specific in their action.

**Q716.** Enzymes accelerate biochemical reactions by:
 **A.** Increasing the kinetic energy of reactants.
 **B.** Shifting the equilibrium of the reaction.
 **C.** Increasing the concentration of products.
 **D.** Lowering the activation energy. ✓
**Answer:** Lowering the activation energy.

**Q717.** Which statement best describes the quantity of enzymes required for a biochemical reaction?
 **A.** Large quantities are needed as they are consumed in the reaction.
 **B.** The quantity needed is equal to the quantity of substrate.
 **C.** They are only needed in vitro, not in vivo.
 **D.** They are required in very small quantities and are reusable. ✓
**Answer:** They are required in very small quantities and are reusable.

**Q718.** Enzymes primarily function by:
 **A.** Increasing the activation energy of the reaction.
 **B.** Decreasing the stability of the products.
 **C.** Increasing the temperature of the reaction system.
 **D.** Providing an alternative reaction pathway with lower activation energy. ✓
**Answer:** Providing an alternative reaction pathway with lower activation energy.

**Q719.** Most enzymes are categorized as which type of macromolecule?
**Answer:** Carbohydrates

**Q720.** The specific region on an enzyme where the substrate binds and catalysis occurs is known as the:
 **A.** Allosteric site
 **B.** Regulatory site
 **C.** Inhibitor site
 **D.** Active site ✓
**Answer:** Active site

**Q721.** Enzymes are highly sensitive to changes in environmental conditions, particularly:
 **A.** Light intensity and atmospheric pressure.
 **B.** Humidity and gravitational force.
 **C.** Substrate concentration and product concentration.
 **D.** Temperature and pH. ✓
**Answer:** Temperature and pH.

**Q722.** Which of the following statements about enzyme activity is INCORRECT?
 **A.** Enzymes can catalyze reactions both inside and outside living cells.
 **B.** Enzymes are highly specific for their substrates.
 **C.** Some enzymes require non-protein components for their activity.
 **D.** Enzymes change the equilibrium constant of a reaction. ✓
**Answer:** Enzymes change the equilibrium constant of a reaction.

**Q723.** Which of the following is true regarding enzyme action and activation energy?
 **A.** Enzymes increase the energy required for a reaction to start.
 **B.** Enzymes do not affect the activation energy.
 **C.** Enzymes convert all the activation energy into heat.
 **D.** Enzymes provide an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy. ✓
**Answer:** Enzymes provide an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy.

**Q724.** The tertiary or quaternary structures of enzymes are crucial because they:
 **A.** Allow for their easy transportation across membranes.
 **B.** Determine their solubility in water.
 **C.** Are responsible for their color.
 **D.** Form the specific active site necessary for catalysis. ✓
**Answer:** Form the specific active site necessary for catalysis.

**Q725.** An enzyme's ability to catalyze a specific reaction efficiently is largely due to its:
 **A.** Large molecular weight.
 **B.** Globular shape.
 **C.** Presence of disulfide bonds only.
 **D.** Unique active site structure. ✓
**Answer:** Unique active site structure.

**Q726.** The active site of an enzyme is best described as a:
 **A.** Linear chain of amino acids.
 **B.** Flat surface where any molecule can bind.
 **C.** Storage region for excess substrate molecules.
 **D.** Three-dimensional, charge-bearing cavity. ✓
**Answer:** Three-dimensional, charge-bearing cavity.

**Q727.** According to the Lock and Key Model of enzyme action:
 **A.** The active site is flexible and changes shape upon substrate binding.
 **B.** Multiple enzymes can bind to a single substrate.
 **C.** Substrates induce a conformational change in the enzyme's active site.
 **D.** The enzyme's active site has a rigid, pre-formed shape complementary to the substrate. ✓
**Answer:** The enzyme's active site has a rigid, pre-formed shape complementary to the substrate.

**Q728.** The Induced Fit Model suggests that:
 **A.** The enzyme's active site is permanently rigid.
 **B.** The substrate's shape changes to fit the enzyme.
 **C.** Enzymes are non-regulatory and have absolute specificity.
 **D.** The active site undergoes a conformational change upon substrate binding. ✓
**Answer:** The active site undergoes a conformational change upon substrate binding.

**Q729.** Cofactors are often required for enzyme activity. They are typically:
 **A.** Large protein molecules that assist in denaturation.
 **B.** Products of the enzymatic reaction.
 **C.** Inhibitors that block the active site.
 **D.** Non-protein components that aid in catalysis. ✓
**Answer:** Non-protein components that aid in catalysis.

**Q730.** What is formed immediately after the substrate binds to the active site of an enzyme?
 **A.** Enzyme-product complex
 **B.** Free enzyme and product
 **C.** Denatured enzyme
 **D.** Enzyme-substrate complex ✓
**Answer:** Enzyme-substrate complex

**Q731.** The energy required to initiate a biochemical reaction, even with the presence of an enzyme, is known as:
 **A.** Potential energy
 **B.** Kinetic energy
 **C.** Free energy
 **D.** Activation energy ✓
**Answer:** Activation energy

**Q732.** Within the active site, the amino acids responsible for chemically transforming the substrate are part of the:
 **A.** Binding site
 **B.** Allosteric site
 **C.** Regulatory site
 **D.** Catalytic site ✓
**Answer:** Catalytic site

**Q733.** Which statement correctly describes the effect of an enzyme on a biochemical reaction?
 **A.** It increases the equilibrium constant.
 **B.** It makes an unfavorable reaction favorable.
 **C.** It changes the overall Gibb's free energy (ΔG) of the reaction.
 **D.** It increases the rate at which the reaction reaches equilibrium. ✓
**Answer:** It increases the rate at which the reaction reaches equilibrium.

**Q734.** A holoenzyme is formed when an apoenzyme combines with a:
 **A.** Substrate
 **B.** Product
 **C.** Inhibitor
 **D.** Cofactor ✓
**Answer:** Cofactor

**Q735.** Which model of enzyme action is better suited for explaining the action of regulatory enzymes that can bind to more than one related substrate?
 **A.** Lock and Key Model
 **B.** Allosteric Model
 **C.** Competitive Inhibition Model
 **D.** Induced Fit Model ✓
**Answer:** Induced Fit Model

**Q736.** When an enzyme catalyzes a reaction, it achieves this by:
 **A.** Directly increasing the temperature of the reactants.
 **B.** Forcing reactants to collide more frequently.
 **C.** Consuming the reactants to form an intermediate.
 **D.** Providing a microenvironment that facilitates bond breaking and formation. ✓
**Answer:** Providing a microenvironment that facilitates bond breaking and formation.

**Q737.** Which of the following is NOT a step in the general mechanism of enzyme action?
 **A.** Formation of an enzyme-substrate complex.
 **B.** Conversion of substrate to product.
 **C.** Release of enzyme from the product.
 **D.** Permanent alteration of the enzyme's active site. ✓
**Answer:** Permanent alteration of the enzyme's active site.

**Q738.** The term "transition state" in an enzyme-catalyzed reaction refers to:
 **A.** The initial state of the reactants.
 **B.** The final state of the products.
 **C.** The state where the enzyme is denatured.
 **D.** A high-energy, unstable intermediate formed during the reaction. ✓
**Answer:** A high-energy, unstable intermediate formed during the reaction.

**Q739.** If an enzyme has an optimum pH of 2.0, it is likely to be found in which part of the human body?
 **A.** Small intestine
 **B.** Blood
 **C.** Muscle cells
 **D.** Stomach ✓
**Answer:** Stomach

**Q740.** Which of the following best describes the role of enzymes in metabolic pathways?
 **A.** They provide the energy needed for the reactions.
 **B.** They act as structural components of the pathway.
 **C.** They primarily store genetic information for the pathway.
 **D.** They direct the pathway towards the formation of specific products. ✓
**Answer:** They direct the pathway towards the formation of specific products.

**Q741.** A characteristic common to both the Lock and Key and Induced Fit models of enzyme action is:
 **A.** The active site remains rigid throughout the reaction.
 **B.** The enzyme undergoes denaturation during catalysis.
 **C.** The enzyme is consumed in the reaction.
 **D.** Substrate binding occurs at a specific active site. ✓
**Answer:** Substrate binding occurs at a specific active site.

**Q742.** At what approximate temperature do most human enzymes exhibit their maximum activity?
 **A.** 0°C
 **B.** 25°C
 **C.** 88.6°C
 **D.** 37°C ✓
**Answer:** 37°C

**Q743.** Increasing the temperature of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction typically doubles the reaction rate for every 10°C rise, up to a certain point. This is due to:
 **A.** Enzyme denaturation.
 **B.** Decrease in activation energy.
 **C.** Formation of stronger enzyme-substrate complexes.
 **D.** Increased kinetic energy and collision frequency between enzyme and substrate. ✓
**Answer:** Increased kinetic energy and collision frequency between enzyme and substrate.

**Q744.** What happens to an enzyme's activity when the temperature rises significantly above its optimum?
 **A.** The enzyme becomes more efficient.
 **B.** The enzyme's tertiary structure is maintained.
 **C.** The enzyme forms stronger bonds with the substrate.
 **D.** Denaturation occurs, leading to a loss of catalytic activity. ✓
**Answer:** Denaturation occurs, leading to a loss of catalytic activity.

**Q745.** When an enzyme is exposed to temperatures significantly below its optimum (e.g., near freezing), its activity usually:
 **A.** Increases rapidly.
 **B.** Becomes permanently denatured.
 **C.** Shifts its optimum pH.
 **D.** Is temporarily inactivated but can regain activity upon warming. ✓
**Answer:** Is temporarily inactivated but can regain activity upon warming.

**Q746.** The pH at which an enzyme exhibits its maximum activity is called its:
 **A.** Isoelectric point
 **B.** Neutral point
 **C.** Saturation pH
 **D.** Optimum pH ✓
**Answer:** Optimum pH

**Q747.** Pepsin, an enzyme found in the stomach, has an optimum pH of approximately 2.0. This indicates that:
 **A.** It functions best in a neutral environment.
 **B.** It is inactivated in acidic conditions.
 **C.** It only functions in the presence of high salt concentrations.
 **D.** Its active site is adapted to highly acidic environments. ✓
**Answer:** Its active site is adapted to highly acidic environments.

**Q748.** Extreme changes in pH can lead to irreversible loss of enzyme activity primarily by:
 **A.** Increasing the enzyme's affinity for its substrate.
 **B.** Altering the enzyme's primary amino acid sequence.
 **C.** Reducing the kinetic energy of the enzyme molecules.
 **D.** Disrupting the ionic and hydrogen bonds that maintain the enzyme's tertiary structure. ✓
**Answer:** Disrupting the ionic and hydrogen bonds that maintain the enzyme's tertiary structure.

**Q749.** How does increasing enzyme concentration affect the rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction, assuming substrate is not limiting?
 **A.** It decreases the reaction rate.
 **B.** It has no effect on the reaction rate.
 **C.** It decreases the activation energy further.
 **D.** It directly increases the reaction rate. ✓
**Answer:** It directly increases the reaction rate.

**Q750.** In a reaction with a fixed amount of enzyme, as substrate concentration increases, the reaction rate will initially increase but eventually plateau. This plateau indicates:
 **A.** The enzyme is denatured.
 **B.** The reaction has reached its equilibrium.
 **C.** The temperature is too high.
 **D.** All active sites of the enzyme are saturated with substrate. ✓
**Answer:** All active sites of the enzyme are saturated with substrate.

**Q751.** The maximum velocity (Vmax) of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction occurs when:
 **A.** The enzyme concentration is very low.
 **B.** The substrate concentration is negligible.
 **C.** The pH is extremely acidic or basic.
 **D.** The enzyme is saturated with substrate. ✓
**Answer:** The enzyme is saturated with substrate.

**Q752.** If the graph of reaction rate versus temperature for a human enzyme shows a sharp decrease after the optimum, this is primarily due to:
 **A.** Increased substrate inhibition.
 **B.** Reversible inactivation.
 **C.** Formation of precipitate.
 **D.** Permanent denaturation of the enzyme. ✓
**Answer:** Permanent denaturation of the enzyme.

**Q753.** Which factor's effect on enzyme activity is often depicted by a bell-shaped curve?
 **A.** Enzyme concentration
 **B.** Substrate concentration
 **C.** Product concentration
 **D.** pH ✓
**Answer:** pH

**Q754.** Consider an enzyme from thermophilic bacteria. Compared to a human enzyme, its optimum temperature would likely be:
 **A.** Significantly lower.
 **B.** The same.
 **C.** Dependent on the substrate.
 **D.** Significantly higher. ✓
**Answer:** Significantly higher.

**Q755.** If the rate of an enzymatic reaction is plotted against enzyme concentration, assuming unlimited substrate, the graph would show a:
 **A.** Bell-shaped curve.
 **B.** Hyperbolic curve.
 **C.** Decrease followed by a plateau.
 **D.** Linear increase. ✓
**Answer:** Linear increase.

**Q756.** The effect of pH on enzyme activity is primarily related to its influence on the:
 **A.** Molecular weight of the enzyme.
 **B.** Covalent bonds within the enzyme.
 **C.** Overall concentration of water in the reaction.
 **D.** Ionization state of amino acid residues in the active site. ✓
**Answer:** Ionization state of amino acid residues in the active site.

**Q757.** Why does the rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction decrease at very high substrate concentrations in some?
 **A.** The enzyme becomes denatured.
 **B.** The active sites are all saturated.
 **C.** The product starts inhibiting the enzyme.
 **D.** Excess substrate might bind to an allosteric site and inhibit the enzyme. ✓
**Answer:** Excess substrate might bind to an allosteric site and inhibit the enzyme.

**Q758.** The term "Q10 value" in relation to enzyme activity refers to the factor by which the rate of reaction increases for every:
 **A.** 1°C increase in temperature.
 **B.** 5°C increase in temperature.
 **C.** 1 pH unit change.
 **D.** 10°C increase in temperature. ✓
**Answer:** 10°C increase in temperature.

**Q759.** If an enzyme functions optimally at a pH of 8.0, it is likely to be active in:
 **A.** The stomach
 **B.** The lysosome
 **C.** The vacuole
 **D.** The small intestine ✓
**Answer:** The small intestine

**Q760.** An enzyme's activity is significantly reduced at a non-optimal pH due to:
 **A.** A change in the enzyme's primary structure.
 **B.** Disruption of the enzyme's peptide bonds.
 **C.** Increased covalent bonding within the active site.
 **D.** Alterations in the ionization state of amino acid residues. ✓
**Answer:** Alterations in the ionization state of amino acid residues.

**Q761.** Which graph represents the effect of enzyme concentration on the rate of reaction when substrate is abundant?
 **A.** A bell-shaped curve.
 **B.** A curve that plateaus at Vmax.
 **C.** A curve showing initial increase then decrease.
 **D.** A straight line increasing proportionally. ✓
**Answer:** A straight line increasing proportionally.

**Q762.** What happens to enzyme activity when the pH is slightly altered from the optimum, but not to an extreme level?
 **A.** The enzyme irreversibly denatures.
 **B.** The enzyme's primary structure changes.
 **C.** The enzyme becomes more active.
 **D.** The enzyme's activity is temporarily reduced due to ionization changes. ✓
**Answer:** The enzyme's activity is temporarily reduced due to ionization changes.

**Q763.** An enzyme from a psychrophilic organism would likely have an optimum temperature:
 **A.** Much higher than human enzymes.
 **B.** Similar to human enzymes.
 **C.** That is highly variable.
 **D.** Close to 0°C. ✓
**Answer:** Close to 0°C.

**Q764.** Which statement about the effect of substrate concentration on enzyme activity is correct?
 **A.** Increasing substrate concentration always increases the reaction rate linearly.
 **B.** At very high substrate concentrations, the enzyme activity decreases rapidly.
 **C.** Substrate concentration primarily affects the enzyme's optimal temperature.
 **D.** The reaction rate reaches Vmax when the enzyme becomes saturated with substrate. ✓
**Answer:** The reaction rate reaches Vmax when the enzyme becomes saturated with substrate.

**Q765.** A significant decrease in enzyme activity at very low temperatures is primarily due to:
 **A.** Denaturation of the enzyme.
 **B.** Formation of stronger enzyme-substrate bonds.
 **C.** Competitive inhibition by water molecules.
 **D.** Reduced kinetic energy of molecules, leading to fewer effective collisions. ✓
**Answer:** Reduced kinetic energy of molecules, leading to fewer effective collisions.

**Q766.** The effect of enzyme concentration on reaction rate can be understood as:
 **A.** Each enzyme molecule can only catalyze one reaction.
 **B.** Enzyme concentration affects the activation energy.
 **C.** It is only relevant when substrate is limiting.
 **D.** More enzyme molecules. ✓
**Answer:** More enzyme molecules.

**Q767.** If a graph shows enzyme activity sharply declining below its optimum pH, it suggests that:
 **A.** The enzyme is becoming more stable.
 **B.** The enzyme is becoming saturated.
 **C.** The enzyme is forming aggregates.
 **D.** The active site is gaining more positive charges. ✓
**Answer:** The active site is gaining more positive charges.

**Q768.** Which pair of factors primarily influences the enzyme's three-dimensional structure and, consequently, its active site?
 **A.** Substrate concentration and product concentration.
 **B.** Light intensity and atmospheric pressure.
 **C.** Enzyme concentration and cofactor concentration.
 **D.** Temperature and pH. ✓
**Answer:** Temperature and pH.

**Q769.** The initial rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction is often measured because:
 **A.** Product inhibition becomes significant later.
 **B.** The enzyme is most stable at the beginning.
 **C.** The temperature is constant only at the beginning.
 **D.** Substrate concentration is highest at the start. ✓
**Answer:** Substrate concentration is highest at the start.

**Q770.** A molecule that binds to an enzyme and decreases its activity is called an:
 **A.** Activator
 **B.** Cofactor
 **C.** Substrate
 **D.** Inhibitor ✓
**Answer:** Inhibitor

**Q771.** In competitive inhibition, the inhibitor typically binds to the:
 **A.** Allosteric site
 **B.** Catalytic site
 **C.** Product binding site
 **D.** Active site ✓
**Answer:** Active site

**Q772.** Which characteristic distinguishes a competitive inhibitor from a non-competitive inhibitor?
 **A.** Competitive inhibitors bind irreversibly.
 **B.** Competitive inhibitors are structurally dissimilar to the substrate.
 **C.** Competitive inhibitors bind to an allosteric site.
 **D.** Competitive inhibition can often be overcome by increasing substrate concentration. ✓
**Answer:** Competitive inhibition can often be overcome by increasing substrate concentration.

**Q773.** Non-competitive inhibitors primarily affect enzyme activity by binding to the:
 **A.** Active site.
 **B.** Product.
 **C.** Substrate itself.
 **D.** Allosteric site. ✓
**Answer:** Allosteric site.

**Q774.** Which of the following is an example of a competitive inhibitor mentioned in the provided text?
 **A.** Cyanide
 **B.** Heavy metal salts
 **C.** Threonine
 **D.** Penicillin ✓
**Answer:** Penicillin

**Q775.** Irreversible non-competitive inhibitors typically cause permanent inactivation of an enzyme by:
 **A.** Mimicking the substrate structure.
 **B.** Competing for active sites.
 **C.** Increasing the optimal temperature of the enzyme.
 **D.** Forming strong covalent bonds with the enzyme. ✓
**Answer:** Forming strong covalent bonds with the enzyme.

**Q776.** Feedback inhibition is a regulatory mechanism where the activity of an enzyme in a metabolic pathway is inhibited by:
 **A.** An activator molecule.
 **B.** An intermediate product of the pathway.
 **C.** A competitive substrate.
 **D.** The end product of the pathway. ✓
**Answer:** The end product of the pathway.

**Q777.** In feedback inhibition, the end product typically binds to which site on the enzyme to exert its inhibitory effect?
 **A.** Active site
 **B.** Substrate binding site
 **C.** Catalytic site
 **D.** Allosteric site ✓
**Answer:** Allosteric site

**Q778.** Cyanide is a potent poison because it acts as an irreversible non-competitive inhibitor of:
 **A.** Amylase
 **B.** Pepsin
 **C.** Trypsin
 **D.** Cytochrome oxidase ✓
**Answer:** Cytochrome oxidase

**Q779.** Which type of inhibition is often a normal mechanism for regulating metabolic pathways in living organisms?
 **A.** Irreversible non-competitive inhibition
 **B.** Competitive inhibition by drugs
 **C.** Heavy metal inhibition
 **D.** Feedback inhibition ✓
**Answer:** Feedback inhibition

**Q780.** Malonate is given as an example of an inhibitor of succinate dehydrogenase. It is chemically similar to succinate. What type of inhibition would malonate likely exhibit?
 **A.** Non-competitive inhibition
 **B.** Irreversible inhibition
 **C.** Allosteric inhibition
 **D.** Competitive inhibition ✓
**Answer:** Competitive inhibition

**Q781.** If increasing the substrate concentration can completely reverse the effect of an inhibitor, that inhibitor is most likely:
 **A.** A non-competitive inhibitor.
 **B.** An irreversible inhibitor.
 **C.** An allosteric activator.
 **D.** A competitive inhibitor. ✓
**Answer:** A competitive inhibitor.

**Q782.** Heavy metal ions like Hg++ and Ag+ can act as non-competitive inhibitors by:
 **A.** Binding to the active site.
 **B.** Increasing the enzyme's optimal temperature.
 **C.** Competing with the substrate for binding.
 **D.** Breaking disulfide bridges and causing enzyme denaturation. ✓
**Answer:** Breaking disulfide bridges and causing enzyme denaturation.

**Q783.** An enzyme undergoing feedback inhibition typically has:
 **A.** Only an active site.
 **B.** Only an allosteric site.
 **C.** No specific binding sites.
 **D.** Both an active site and an allosteric site. ✓
**Answer:** Both an active site and an allosteric site.

**Q784.** Sulfa drugs act as inhibitors of bacterial enzyme action by mimicking PABA (para-aminobenzoic acid), a substrate for folic acid synthesis. This mechanism classifies sulfa drugs as:
 **A.** Non-competitive inhibitors.
 **B.** Irreversible inhibitors.
 **C.** Allosteric regulators.
 **D.** Competitive inhibitors. ✓
**Answer:** Competitive inhibitors.

**Q785.** Which type of inhibition involves the inhibitor binding to the enzyme-substrate complex, but not to the free enzyme?
 **A.** Competitive inhibition
 **B.** Non-competitive inhibition
 **C.** Feedback inhibition
 **D.** Uncompetitive inhibition ✓
**Answer:** Uncompetitive inhibition

**Q786.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

### Evolution (56 Questions)

**Q787.** According to evolutionists, approximately how long ago did life begin on Earth?
 **A.** About 1.5 billion years ago
 **B.** About 5 billion years ago
 **C.** About 420 million years ago
 **D.** About 3.5 billion years ago ✓
**Answer:** About 3.5 billion years ago

**Q788.** What was the likely atmospheric condition of early Earth, according to evolutionists?
 **A.** Oxidizing and hot
 **B.** Reduced and oxygen-rich
 **C.** Oxidizing and ozone-rich
 **D.** Reduced and ozone-less ✓
**Answer:** Reduced and ozone-less

**Q789.** The hypothesis suggesting that the first living beings originated in hot water springs among inorganic and organic molecules is called the:
 **A.** Panspermia hypothesis
 **B.** Biochemical evolution hypothesis
 **C.** Spontaneous generation hypothesis
 **D.** Vent hypothesis ✓
**Answer:** Vent hypothesis

**Q790.** What significant change occurred in Earth's atmosphere that allowed life on land to become possible approximately 420 million years ago?
 **A.** Accumulation of carbon dioxide
 **B.** Decrease in ultraviolet radiation
 **C.** Increase in nitrogen levels
 **D.** Buildup of protective ozone ✓
**Answer:** Buildup of protective ozone

**Q791.** Which of the following describes the initial nutritional mode of the earliest prokaryotes according to the text?
 **A.** Photosynthetic autotrophs
 **B.** Absorptive heterotrophs ✓
**Answer:** Absorptive heterotrophs

**Q792.** Eukaryotes are estimated to have appeared how many years ago, according to fossil records?
 **A.** 3.5 billion years ago
 **B.** 420 million years ago
 **C.** 5 billion years ago
 **D.** 1.9 to 2.1 billion years ago ✓
**Answer:** 1.9 to 2.1 billion years ago

**Q793.** Which theory for the evolution of eukaryotes suggests that the prokaryotic cell membrane folded inward to enclose genetic material?
 **A.** Endosymbiosis theory
 **B.** Chemiosmotic theory
 **C.** Vent hypothesis
 **D.** Membrane invagination theory ✓
**Answer:** Membrane invagination theory

**Q794.** Who proposed the Endosymbiosis theory?
 **A.** Charles Darwin
 **B.** Jean-Baptiste Lamarck
 **C.** Alfred Wallace
 **D.** Lynn Margulis ✓
**Answer:** Lynn Margulis

**Q795.** According to the endosymbiosis theory, what did a large anaerobic amoeboid prokaryote ingest that later developed into a mitochondrion?
 **A.** A small photosynthetic bacterium
 **B.** A spirochete
 **C.** A eukaryotic nucleus
 **D.** A small aerobic bacterium ✓
**Answer:** A small aerobic bacterium

**Q796.** Which of the following is cited as evidence supporting the endosymbiosis theory for mitochondria and chloroplasts?
 **A.** They have different genetic codes from bacteria.
 **B.** They are significantly larger than bacteria.
 **C.** They produce all of their own enzymes and proteins independently.
 **D.** They have their own DNA and ribosomes similar to bacteria. ✓
**Answer:** They have their own DNA and ribosomes similar to bacteria.

**Q797.** What was the critical consequence of the emergence of the eukaryotic cell?
 **A.** A decrease in biodiversity
 **B.** The immediate formation of land-based life
 **C.** The end of all prokaryotic life forms
 **D.** An increase in biodiversity ✓
**Answer:** An increase in biodiversity

**Q798.** Which of the following is NOT one of Lamarck's basic assumptions regarding evolution?
 **A.** Use and disuse of organs
 **B.** Inheritance of acquired characteristics
 **C.** Organisms undergo change over time
 **D.** Natural selection of advantageous traits ✓
**Answer:** Natural selection of advantageous traits

**Q799.** According to Lamarck, what happens to organs that are more frequently used by an organism?
 **A.** They diminish and disappear
 **B.** They become vestigial structures
 **C.** They are passed on only if beneficial
 **D.** They develop and become strong ✓
**Answer:** They develop and become strong

**Q800.** What did Lamarck propose about characteristics acquired by an individual during its lifetime?
 **A.** They are rarely passed to offspring.
 **B.** They are only passed if there's a genetic mutation.
 **C.** They are lost in the next generation.
 **D.** They are passed on to the offspring. ✓
**Answer:** They are passed on to the offspring.

**Q801.** Which animal's evolution did Lamarck famously use to illustrate his hypothesis of acquired characteristics?
 **A.** Finches
 **B.** Rabbits
 **C.** Snakes
 **D.** Giraffes ✓
**Answer:** Giraffes

**Q802.** In the Lamarckian explanation of giraffe neck evolution, how did their necks become longer?
 **A.** Offspring were born with mutations for longer necks.
 **B.** Giraffes with naturally longer necks survived better.
 **C.** They evolved through a sudden act of creation.
 **D.** Each generation stretched its neck to reach higher leaves, and this trait was inherited. ✓
**Answer:** Each generation stretched its neck to reach higher leaves, and this trait was inherited.

**Q803.** Lamarck's contribution to science is considered important because he was the first to propose that:
 **A.** Species are individually created by God.
 **B.** Genetic mutations drive evolutionary change.
 **C.** Evolution occurs through survival of the fittest.
 **D.** Organisms undergo change due to natural phenomena. ✓
**Answer:** Organisms undergo change due to natural phenomena.

**Q804.** What is a major drawback of Lamarckism, identified by later scientific understanding?
 **A.** It did not account for environmental conditions.
 **B.** It proposed a 'ladder of life.'
 **C.** It failed to recognize the concept of 'use and disuse.'
 **D.** Acquired characteristics have no genetic basis and cannot be inherited. ✓
**Answer:** Acquired characteristics have no genetic basis and cannot be inherited.

**Q805.** The anatomical, biochemical, and behavioral characteristics an individual displays as it develops through life are known as its:
 **A.** Genotype
 **B.** Allele frequency
 **C.** Acquired character
 **D.** Phenotype ✓
**Answer:** Phenotype

**Q806.** Why was Lamarck criticized in his day?
 **A.** For advocating the idea of evolutionary change.
 **B.** For his belief in special creation.
 **C.** For his lack of observations during voyages.
 **D.** For the mechanism of evolution he proposed. ✓
**Answer:** For the mechanism of evolution he proposed.

**Q807.** Which of the following examples did Lamarck cite to illustrate the 'use and disuse' principle?
 **A.** Darwin's finches
 **B.** Industrial melanism in moths
 **C.** The evolution of giraffes with shorter necks
 **D.** A blacksmith developing a bigger bicep ✓
**Answer:** A blacksmith developing a bigger bicep

**Q808.** What was Charles Darwin's position aboard the HMS Beagle?
 **A.** Captain
 **B.** Physician
 **C.** Geologist
 **D.** Naturalist ✓
**Answer:** Naturalist

**Q809.** Darwin's observations on the Galapagos Islands were most important in the development of his ideas about:
 **A.** Geological formations
 **B.** Human population growth
 **C.** Plant classification
 **D.** Evolution ✓
**Answer:** Evolution

**Q810.** Which concept describes the differential reproductive capacities among individuals of a population, central to Darwin's theory?
 **A.** Overproduction
 **B.** Variations
 **C.** Struggle for existence
 **D.** Natural selection ✓
**Answer:** Natural selection

**Q811.** According to Darwin, what is the consequence of each species having the capacity to produce more offspring than will survive to maturity?
 **A.** Increased variations
 **B.** Reduced competition
 **C.** Instant speciation
 **D.** Overproduction ✓
**Answer:** Overproduction

**Q812.** Which of Darwin's observations states that individuals in a population exhibit differences in their traits?
 **A.** Overproduction
 **B.** Struggle for existence
 **C.** Survival of the fittest
 **D.** Variations ✓
**Answer:** Variations

**Q813.** Who proposed the theory of uniformitarianism, influencing Darwin's understanding of Earth's age?
 **A.** Charles Lyell
 **B.** Thomas Malthus
 **C.** Alfred Wallace
 **D.** James Hutton ✓
**Answer:** James Hutton

**Q814.** Thomas R. Malthus's work, 'An Essay on the Principle of Population,' primarily highlighted the discrepancy between:
 **A.** Genetic variation and environmental stability
 **B.** Species diversity and habitat size
 **C.** Acquired traits and inherited traits
 **D.** Human population growth and food supply ✓
**Answer:** Human population growth and food supply

**Q815.** Who independently conceived a theory of the origin of species by natural selection?
 **A.** Charles Lyell
 **B.** James Hutton
 **C.** Joseph Dalton Hooker
 **D.** Alfred R. Wallace ✓
**Answer:** Alfred R. Wallace

**Q816.** 'Survival of the fittest' means that individuals who are fittest are those that:
 **A.** Are physically strongest
 **B.** Live the longest
 **C.** Can outcompete all other species
 **D.** Reproduce most successfully ✓
**Answer:** Reproduce most successfully

**Q817.** The processes of natural selection cause an increase of which type of alleles within a population?
 **A.** Unfavourable alleles
 **B.** Deleterious alleles
 **C.** Neutral alleles
 **D.** Favourable alleles ✓
**Answer:** Favourable alleles

**Q818.** Darwin's 'Descent with modification' suggests that all organisms are related through:
 **A.** Independent creation events
 **B.** Spontaneous generation
 **C.** Acquired characteristics
 **D.** Descent from some common ancestors ✓
**Answer:** Descent from some common ancestors

**Q819.** What does 'overproduction' imply about a population's potential growth?
 **A.** It grows linearly over time.
 **B.** It remains stable regardless of resources.
 **C.** It always reaches carrying capacity immediately.
 **D.** It grows exponentially over time. ✓
**Answer:** It grows exponentially over time.

**Q820.** Which of the following is an example of an 'environmental' limit on population growth mentioned in the text?
 **A.** Interspecific competition
 **B.** Intraspecific competition
 **C.** Variation in traits
 **D.** Disease causing organisms ✓
**Answer:** Disease causing organisms

**Q821.** How many pages was Darwin's initial brief sketch of his theory, written for himself in 1842?
 **A.** 10 pages
 **B.** 100 pages
 **C.** 230 pages
 **D.** 35 pages ✓
**Answer:** 35 pages

**Q822.** What was the title of Darwin's landmark book published in 1859?
 **A.** The Evolution by Means of Natural Selection
 **B.** An Essay on the Principle of Population
 **C.** Zoological Philosophy
 **D.** The Origin of Species by Means of Natural Selection ✓
**Answer:** The Origin of Species by Means of Natural Selection

**Q823.** Which of the following best describes the belief of supporters of Special Creation?
 **A.** The universe and man are products of innumerable changes from lower to higher forms.
 **B.** Life originated spontaneously from inorganic and organic molecules in hot water springs.
 **C.** Organisms undergo change over time due to natural phenomena.
 **D.** Every species was individually created by God in its present form and is incapable of change. ✓
**Answer:** Every species was individually created by God in its present form and is incapable of change.

**Q824.** According to evolutionists, what is the origin of the universe and man?
 **A.** A sudden, supernatural act at a particular time.
 **B.** An unobservable process that cannot be scientifically proven or disproven.
 **C.** Directly from existing conditions in their present form.
 **D.** The result of innumerable changes from lower to higher forms. ✓
**Answer:** The result of innumerable changes from lower to higher forms.

**Q825.** Scientific truth, concerning creation, is described as tentative, while theological truth to the believer is considered:
 **A.** Tentative
 **B.** Observable
 **C.** Contradictory
 **D.** Absolute ✓
**Answer:** Absolute

**Q826.** What is biogeography?
 **A.** The study of ancient life forms through fossils.
 **B.** The study of the geological distribution of rocks and minerals.
 **C.** The study of the genetic makeup of populations over time.
 **D.** The study of the geographical distribution of plants and animals on Earth. ✓
**Answer:** The study of the geographical distribution of plants and animals on Earth.

**Q827.** Darwin noticed the absence of which animal in South America, despite suitable environmental conditions?
 **A.** Finches
 **B.** Tortoises
 **C.** Llamas
 **D.** Rabbits ✓
**Answer:** Rabbits

**Q828.** Which of the following is an example of a physical barrier inhibiting the distribution of organisms?
 **A.** An unfavourable climate
 **B.** The presence of competing organisms
 **C.** Disease-causing organisms
 **D.** A mountain range ✓
**Answer:** A mountain range

**Q829.** Biogeographic studies reveal that life-forms in different parts of the world possess:
 **A.** Identical evolutionary histories.
 **B.** Similar genetic potentials.
 **C.** Universal distribution patterns.
 **D.** Distinctive evolutionary histories. ✓
**Answer:** Distinctive evolutionary histories.

**Q830.** What is the science of discovery, identification, and interpretation of fossils called?
 **A.** Biogeography
 **B.** Anatomy
 **C.** Molecular biology
 **D.** Paleontology ✓
**Answer:** Paleontology

**Q831.** According to the fossil record, which type of organism represents the oldest known fossils?
 **A.** Eukaryotes
 **B.** Amphibians
 **C.** Fishes
 **D.** Prokaryotes ✓
**Answer:** Prokaryotes

**Q832.** Which of the following represents the correct chronological sequence of vertebrate classes in the fossil record?
 **A.** Amphibians, Fishes, Reptiles, Mammals, Birds
 **B.** Reptiles, Fishes, Amphibians, Birds, Mammals
 **C.** Mammals, Birds, Fishes, Amphibians, Reptiles
 **D.** Fishes, Amphibians, Reptiles, Mammals and Birds ✓
**Answer:** Fishes, Amphibians, Reptiles, Mammals and Birds

**Q833.** The succession of fossil forms provides strong evidence in favor of evolution because it offers a:
 **A.** Theoretical framework for genetic inheritance.
 **B.** Direct observation of ongoing speciation events.
 **C.** Snapshot of life forms at a single point in time.
 **D.** Visual record showing the evolution of a species. ✓
**Answer:** Visual record showing the evolution of a species.

**Q834.** What does Neo-Darwinism refer to?
 **A.** Darwin's original theory without any modifications.
 **B.** A new theory that completely replaced Darwinism.
 **C.** A theory explaining the origin of life before prokaryotes.
 **D.** The reappraisal of natural selection in terms of modern population genetics. ✓
**Answer:** The reappraisal of natural selection in terms of modern population genetics.

**Q835.** The emergence of which scientific field has provided firm support for Darwinian theory, leading to Neo-Darwinism?
 **A.** Paleontology
 **B.** Biogeography
 **C.** Comparative anatomy
 **D.** Population genetics ✓
**Answer:** Population genetics

**Q836.** Which of the following is cited as a source of evidence for evolution?
 **A.** Astrology
 **B.** Creationism
 **C.** Mythology
 **D.** Comparative Anatomy ✓
**Answer:** Comparative Anatomy

**Q837.** The study of similarities and differences in the DNA and proteins of different species provides evidence for evolution through:
 **A.** Biogeography
 **B.** Paleontology
 **C.** Uniformitarianism
 **D.** Molecular Biology ✓
**Answer:** Molecular Biology

**Q838.** Differentiating between convergent and divergent evolution is based on the inheritance of which types of structures?
 **A.** Vestigial and analogous structures
 **B.** Acquired and inherited structures
 **C.** Primitive and advanced structures
 **D.** Homologous and analogous structures ✓
**Answer:** Homologous and analogous structures

**Q839.** Which concept involves organisms that share similar traits due to adapting to similar environments, despite not sharing a recent common ancestor?
 **A.** Divergent evolution
 **B.** Coevolution
 **C.** Parallel evolution
 **D.** Convergent evolution ✓
**Answer:** Convergent evolution

**Q840.** Which principle describes the conditions under which allelic frequencies in a population remain constant from generation to generation?
 **A.** Natural Selection
 **B.** Inheritance of Acquired Characters
 **C.** Endosymbiosis Theory
 **D.** Hardy-Weinberg Principle ✓
**Answer:** Hardy-Weinberg Principle

**Q841.** What is the concept related to random fluctuations in allele frequencies from one generation to the next, especially in small populations?
 **A.** Natural selection
 **B.** Gene flow
 **C.** Genetic drift ✓
**Answer:** Genetic drift

### Hemostasis (59 Questions)

**Q843.** A kangaroo rat is an example of an animal exhibiting anhydrobiosis. What is the primary source of the water it uses for survival?
**Answer:** Kangaroo rats derive about 90% of their water from the cellular oxidation of carbohydrates found in the seeds they eat. This characteristic is called anhydrobiosis.

**Q844.** Which of the following nitrogenous wastes requires the most water for safe excretion and is therefore common in aquatic animals?
**Answer:** Ammonia is highly toxic and requires about 500 ml of water per gram of nitrogen to be diluted to a non-toxic level, making it suitable only for animals with constant access to water.

**Q845.** The blood vessel that carries filtered blood away from the glomerulus is the:
**Answer:** The efferent arteriole arises from the glomeruli and carries blood away from it after filtration has occurred.

**Q846.** What causes the high filtration pressure in the glomerulus?
**Answer:** The diameter of the efferent arteriole is about half that of the afferent arteriole, creating a bottleneck that significantly increases blood pressure within the glomerulus, which drives filtration.

**Q847.** A patient is diagnosed with pyelonephritis. Which part of the urinary tract is infected?
**Answer:** Pyelonephritis is the medical term for an infection of the kidneys. An infection of the urethra is urethritis, and an infection of the bladder is cystitis.

**Q848.** The countercurrent multiplier mechanism primarily serves to:
**Answer:** The countercurrent multiplier mechanism, involving the loop of Henle and vasa recta, achieves the reabsorption of about 99.5% of water from the filtrate by creating a concentration gradient.

**Q849.** A patient with chronic kidney failure undergoes haemodialysis. The dialysate fluid used in the dialyzer should contain:
**Answer:** The dialysate absorbs waste by-products from the blood by creating a concentration gradient. To prevent the loss of essential substances, the fluid must have normal concentrations of minerals and electrolytes, but it contains no waste products.

**Q850.** Which of the following is considered a physiological response to hyperthermia (high body temperature)?
**Answer:** When body temperature rises above 37°C (hyperthermia), one of the main physiological responses is sweating. Evaporation of the sweat produces a cooling effect.

**Q851.** A person has a kidney transplant. To prevent the rejection of the new kidney, they are prescribed medications like cyclosporine. What is the primary function of these medications?
**Answer:** Patients are required to take medications such as cyclosporine to suppress their immune system in order to prevent rejection of the transplanted kidney.

**Q852.** The vasa recta are specialized capillaries associated with which part of the nephron?
**Answer:** The vasa recta are a specialized part of the peritubular capillaries that course into the medulla along with the loops of Henle.

**Q853.** The ESWL procedure treats kidney stones by:
**Answer:** In ESWL, an instrument called a lithotripter generates shock waves from outside the patient's body, which are focused on the stone to break it into small pieces.

**Q854.** A person is dehydrated. Which hormonal changes would you expect to see?
**Answer:** During dehydration, anti-diuretic hormone (ADH) promotes water reabsorption from the descending limb and collecting duct, while aldosterone promotes the transport of sodium chloride out of the ascending limb, which also aids water retention.

**Q855.** Tubular secretion is a vital process for:
**Answer:** Tubular secretion is the process by which certain substances, like ammonium and hydrogen ions, are secreted by tubular epithelial cells into the filtrate to maintain the pH of urine.

**Q856.** An animal that produces metabolic heat at a low level and relies on the environment to regulate its body temperature is called a(n):
**Answer:** Ectotherms produce metabolic heat at a low level and rely more on heat derived from the environment to raise their body temperature.

**Q857.** The glomerular filtrate has a chemical composition most similar to:
**Answer:** The glomerular filtrate has a chemical composition similar to that of blood plasma, but it lacks larger molecules like proteins, red blood cells, and platelets.

**Q858.** The functional unit of the kidney is the:
**Answer:** The nephron is the functional unit of the kidney, and it consists of a renal corpuscle and a renal tubule.

**Q859.** Why are urinary tract infections (UTIs) significantly more common in women than in men?
**Answer:** The higher risk of UTIs in women is mostly due to the shortness of the female urethra (1.5 inches compared to 8 inches in men), which allows bacteria to be more easily transferred to the bladder.

**Q860.** A urine test shows a persistently acidic pH. This patient is at a higher risk for which type of kidney stone?
**Answer:** When urine is persistently acidic, there is a higher risk of forming uric acid type stones. Calcium phosphate stones tend to form in alkaline urine.

**Q861.** The thermoregulatory center of the human body, acting as a thermostat, is located in the:
**Answer:** The thermoregulatory centre in the human body is located in the hypothalamus, which acts as a thermostat, detecting the temperature of the blood passing through it.

**Q862.** The primary function that distinguishes juxtamedullary nephrons is their ability to:
**Answer:** Juxtamedullary nephrons have long loops that extend deep into the medulla. This makes juxtamedullary nephrons key for water retention when water is in short supply.

**Q863.** During peritoneal dialysis, where is the dialysate fluid introduced?
**Answer:** In peritoneal dialysis, catheters are surgically inserted into the abdominal cavity, through which the dialysate enters and leaves.

**Q864.** The process where substances are moved from the filtrate back into the blood is called:
**Answer:** Selective reabsorption is the process by which certain substances that have been filtered out of the blood are reabsorbed back into it.

**Q865.** Which of the following is NOT a cause of chronic renal failure?
**Answer:** Severe hemorrhage due to trauma is a cause of acute renal failure. Chronic renal failure may be caused by bacterial infections, high blood pressure, and diabetes mellitus.

**Q866.** The funnel-shaped structure within the kidney that collects urine before it passes into the ureter is the:
**Answer:** The ureter begins as the funnel-shaped renal pelvis, which collects urine within the kidney.

**Q867.** Which structure carries urine from the urinary bladder to the outside of the body?
**Answer:** The urethra is a tube that carries urine from the urinary bladder to the outside of the body.

**Q868.** The main purpose of secreting hydrogen ions (H+) into the filtrate is to:
**Answer:** The main purpose of secreting substances like hydrogen ions is to maintain the pH of the urine, which normally ranges from 4.8 to 7.5.

**Q869.** A homeotherm is an animal that:
**Answer:** Homeotherms, like birds and mammals, are able to maintain a fairly constant body temperature by using physiological mechanisms.

**Q870.** All of the following are typically found in the glomerular filtrate EXCEPT:
**Answer:** Larger molecules like proteins, as well as red blood cells and platelets, are left behind in the blood and do not pass into the glomerular filtrate.

**Q871.** Hypercalciuria is a condition that directly contributes to the formation of which type of kidney stone?
**Answer:** Increased calcium in the blood (hypercalcemia) causes high calcium in the urine (hypercalciuria), which along with high oxalate levels (hyperoxaluria) causes calcium oxalate stones.

**Q872.** Which part of the renal tubule is responsible for reabsorbing the majority of water, ions, and all organic nutrients like glucose?
**Answer:** The proximal convoluted tubule is where the reabsorption of water, ions, and all organic nutrients occurs.

**Q873.** Shivering warms the body primarily through:
**Answer:** Shivering is the spasmodic contraction of muscles, and this contraction produces heat which helps to raise the body temperature.

**Q874.** What is the primary role of the urinary bladder?
**Answer:** The urinary bladder is a hollow and distensible muscular organ that serves as a urine reservoir.

**Q875.** An animal that excretes urea as its primary nitrogenous waste is described as:
**Answer:** Animals which excrete urea as their major nitrogenous waste product are called ureotelic.

**Q876.** The tough, fibrous connective tissue layer that encloses and protects each kidney is called the:
**Answer:** A fibrous connective tissue layer, called the fibrous capsule, encloses each kidney.

**Q877.** Besides excretion, the kidneys play a crucial osmoregulatory role by:
**Answer:** As osmoregulatory organs, the kidneys are concerned with the formation of diluted urine during flooding and concentrated urine during dehydration.

**Q878.** Which hormone, secreted by the posterior pituitary, promotes the reabsorption of water from the collecting ducts?
**Answer:** The movement of water out of the descending limb and collecting duct is promoted by anti-diuretic hormone (ADH), which is secreted from the posterior lobe of the pituitary.

**Q879.** The cone-shaped tissues within the renal medulla are called:
**Answer:** The renal medulla consists of a number of cone-shaped renal pyramids.

**Q880.** Piloerection helps to warm the body by:
**Answer:** Piloerection, or the 'erection of skin hair', traps air in the erected hair, which acts as an insulator for heat.

**Q881.** A key factor in a successful kidney transplant is matching the donor and recipient's ABO blood group and which other factor?
**Answer:** ABO blood group compatibility is essential for a kidney transplant, and it is also usual to select donor kidneys on the basis of human leucocytes antigen (HLA) matching to improve graft survival.

**Q882.** In humans, the small amount of uric acid excreted daily is primarily a breakdown product of:
**Answer:** Humans excrete small quantities of uric acid, but this is produced from the breakdown of nucleic acids, not proteins.

**Q883.** The final part of the nephron, which receives filtrate from several distal convoluted tubules, is the:
**Answer:** The distal convoluted tubule is connected to the collecting duct, which receives filtrate from many nephrons.

**Q884.** A patient with end-stage renal disease (ESRD) might have a condition called uremia, which is characterized by:
**Answer:** Chronic kidney failure can progress to end-stage renal disease (ESRD) and uremia, which is fatal unless treated with dialysis or a transplant.

**Q885.** Which of these is a behavioral, rather than physiological, response to thermoregulation?
**Answer:** A behavioral response, such as changing clothing, is voluntary and often corrects a temperature imbalance without the need for a physiological response like shivering or sweating.

**Q886.** The medial depression of the kidney where the renal artery, renal vein, and ureter connect is called the:
**Answer:** The medial side of the kidney is concave, and the entrance to the hollow chamber (renal sinus) is termed the hilum, where the renal artery and nerves enter, and the renal vein and ureter exit.

**Q887.** In peritoneal dialysis, the exchange of waste products occurs across which membrane?
**Answer:** Peritoneal dialysis involves the use of a natural membrane in the body, the peritoneum, which encloses the abdominal cavity.

**Q888.** The conversion of highly toxic ammonia to the less toxic urea occurs in which organ?
**Answer:** The liver supports the excretory role of the kidney by producing ammonia, urea, and uric acid from the nitrogen of amino acids.

**Q889.** Which of the following would be a cause of acute, rather than chronic, renal failure?
**Answer:** Acute renal failure may be caused by an obstruction of the ureters, bladder, or urethra by a kidney stone. Diabetes and high blood pressure are causes of chronic renal failure.

**Q890.** Vasodilation helps to cool the body by:
**Answer:** Vasodilation is the expansion of blood capillaries beneath the skin, which increases blood flow and allows heat to be carried away from the blood through the skin.

**Q891.** Urea is approximately how many times less toxic than ammonia?
**Answer:** Organisms invest energy to convert ammonia into urea, which is 100,000 times less toxic than ammonia.

**Q892.** The science concerned with the structure, function, and diseases of the kidney is called:
**Answer:** The science concerned with the structure, functions, and diseases of the kidneys is called nephrology.

**Q893.** Which hormone acts on the ascending limb of the loop of Henle to control the active transport of sodium chloride?
**Answer:** The movement of sodium chloride out of the ascending limb is controlled by the hormone aldosterone.

**Q894.** Which of the following best describes the path of filtrate through the nephron after leaving the Bowman's capsule?
**Answer:** The renal tubule leads away from the Bowman's capsule and becomes the proximal convoluted tubule (PCT), which then forms the loop of Henle, followed by the distal convoluted tubule (DCT), and finally connects to the collecting duct.

**Q895.** The PCNL procedure accesses a kidney stone:
**Answer:** In PCNL, a tube is inserted from the patient's back into the kidney to create a tract, through which a scope and equipment can be inserted to break up and remove the stone.

**Q896.** The main difference between poikilotherms and homeotherms is based on:
**Answer:** Animals are classified as poikilotherms if they are unable to maintain their body temperature within narrow limits, and as homeotherms if they are able to maintain a fairly constant body temperature.

**Q897.** If a person's kidneys were failing, what would you expect to happen to the concentration of urea in their blood?
**Answer:** Kidney failure is a decline in kidney function, particularly the efficiency of the filtering process. This leads to a buildup of nitrogenous wastes like urea in the blood.

**Q898.** The expansion of blood capillaries just beneath the skin to increase heat loss is known as:
**Answer:** Vasodilation is the expansion of blood capillaries which lie just beneath the epidermis of the skin, leading to more blood flow and subsequent heat loss.

**Q899.** A staghorn stone is a large kidney stone that is notable for its:
**Answer:** A branching stone that can fill the renal pelvis is called a staghorn stone.

**Q900.** Which of these animals would be classified as a homeothermic endotherm?
**Answer:** Homeotherms (maintaining constant temperature) include birds and mammals. Endotherms (producing their own heat) also include birds and mammals. Humans are mammals.

**Q901.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)
