### Immunity (100 Questions)

**Q902.** The ability of the body to resist damage from foreign substances like microorganisms and harmful chemicals is defined as:
**Answer:** Immunity is specifically defined as the ability to resist damage from foreign substances such as microorganisms and harmful chemicals.

**Q903.** The body's response to foreign molecules, characterized by the production of antibodies against a specific antigen, is known as:
**Answer:** An immune response is the body's reaction to foreign molecules, including the production of antibodies against a specific antigen.

**Q904.** The term "immune" is derived from:
**Answer:** Word immune is derived from The Latin word "immunis" that means "safe" or "free of burden."

**Q905.** Which Greek historian observed that individuals who recovered from a disease often became resistant to subsequent infections of the same disease?
**Answer:** Thucydides, a Greek historian, observed this phenomenon more than 2000 years ago.

**Q906.** The English country doctor who hypothesized that cowpox conferred protection against smallpox in 1796 was:
**Answer:** Edward Jenner hypothesized this protective effect of cowpox against smallpox in 1796.

**Q907.** Which of the following is considered the first line of defense against microbial attack in the human body?
**Answer:** The first line of defense consists of external barriers that prevent microbes from entering the body.

**Q908.** These two serve as the first line of defense:
**Answer:** The skin and the mucous membranes of the digestive and respiratory tracts are the two main external barriers.

**Q909.** The two primary layers that make up the human skin are:
**Answer:** The skin is composed of two layers: the dermis and the epidermis.

**Q910.** Which layer of the skin is described as dense, irregular connective tissue containing nerve endings, hair follicles, glands, and lymphatics?
**Answer:** The dermis is characterized by dense, irregular connective tissue with various embedded structures.

**Q911.** The most abundant cells in the epidermis, responsible for producing keratin, are called:
**Answer:** Keratinocytes are the primary cells of the epidermis and produce keratin.

**Q912.** Which cells in the epidermis contribute to skin color?
**Answer:** Melanocytes are responsible for skin pigmentation.

**Q913.** The sebaceous glands are located in:
**Answer:** The sebaceous glands, located in the dermis, produce an oily, white substance rich in lipids called sebum, which oils the hair and skin surface.

**Q914.** The secretions from sweat glands and sebaceous glands contribute to the skin's defense by containing:
**Answer:** These secretions, such as lactic acid, inhibit the growth of bacteria and fungi.

**Q915.** In the stomach, hydrochloric acid is secreted by which cells to kill microorganisms?
**Answer:** Oxyntic or parietal cells in the stomach are responsible for secreting hydrochloric acid.

**Q916.** What is the primary function of the hairs lining the vestibule of the nasal cavity?
**Answer:** Hairs in the nasal vestibule serve to trap large particles of dust from the inhaled air.

**Q917.** The mucous membrane lining the nasal cavity consists of pseudostratified ciliated columnar epithelium with what type of cells that secrete mucus?
**Answer:** Goblet cells in the nasal mucous membrane secrete mucus to trap debris.

**Q918.** How are trapped debris and mucus swept posteriorly from the nasal cavity to the pharynx for elimination?
**Answer:** Cilia on the surface of the mucous membrane sweep mucus and trapped debris towards the pharynx.

**Q919.** What is the approximate maximum diameter of particles that typically do NOT enter the lungs through the nose due to the nasal turbulence mechanism?
**Answer:** The nasal turbulence mechanism is highly effective, preventing particles larger than 6 micrometers from entering the lungs via the nose.

**Q920.** Particles between 1 and 5 micrometers in diameter that manage to bypass the nasal cavity often settle out in the small bronchioles due to:
**Answer:** Smaller particles settle in the bronchioles due to gravity.

**Q921.** What happens to particles smaller than 0.5 micrometers that remain suspended in alveolar air?
**Answer:** Very small particles are expelled from the lungs during exhalation.

**Q922.** Which type of white blood cell is characterized by its large size and ability to leave the bloodstream to become macrophages in tissues?
**Answer:** Monocytes are granular leukocytes that develop into macrophages once they exit the bone marrow and enter tissues.

**Q923.** Monocytes originate in the:
**Answer:** Monocytes are formed in the bone marrow before circulating in the bloodstream.

**Q924.** Once in tissues, monocytes differentiate into:
**Answer:** Monocytes mature into macrophages when they migrate from the blood into various tissues.

**Q925.** Macrophages are essential for defense due to their ability to:
**Answer:** Macrophages are highly phagocytic cells, meaning they engulf and digest cellular debris, foreign substances, and microbes.

**Q926.** After engulfing particles, what do macrophages typically do with the residual products?
**Answer:** Macrophages are known to extrude the residual products from their cellular cytoplasm after digestion.

**Q927.** Macrophages secrete a protein that aids in the maturation of monocytes into macrophages and plays a role in the inflammatory response. This protein is:
**Answer:** Macrophages secrete Interleukin-1, which promotes monocyte maturation and contributes to inflammation and fever.

**Q928.** The lifespan of monocytes circulating in the blood is approximately:
**Answer:** Monocytes circulate in the blood for about 10-20 days before migrating into tissues.

**Q929.** Fixed macrophages are those that:
**Answer:** Macrophages can be either free-moving or fixed in tissues, where they perform their phagocytic functions.

**Q930.** Which of the following describes neutrophils?
**Answer:** Neutrophils are characterized as granular leukocytes with a multi-lobed nucleus.

**Q931.** What is the approximate lifespan of neutrophils in circulation?
**Answer:** Neutrophils typically have a short lifespan in circulation, often only a few days.

**Q932.** What is the primary function of neutrophils in the immune response?
**Answer:** Neutrophils are highly motile phagocytes that ingest bacteria and dead cells.

**Q933.** Neutrophils are particularly adept at:
**Answer:** Neutrophils are highly mobile and use pseudopodia to move and engulf targets.

**Q934.** Natural Killer (NK) cells are a type of lymphocyte that primarily targets:
**Answer:** NK cells are specialized to recognize and kill cells that have been infected by viruses or have become cancerous.

**Q935.** How do Natural Killer (NK) cells destroy their target cells?
**Answer:** NK cells secrete proteins that insert into the plasma membrane of the target cell, forming pores and leading to its destruction.

**Q936.** What is the primary advantage of Natural Killer (NK) cells over specific immune cells?
**Answer:** NK cells are part of the innate immune system and provide rapid, non-specific defense against infected or cancerous cells without requiring prior exposure to an antigen.

**Q937.** The complement system consists of a group of about 20 proteins found in:
**Answer:** Complement proteins are found in the plasma, which is the fluid component of blood.

**Q938.** The activation of the complement system leads to the formation of a protein complex that inserts into the cell membrane of microorganisms, causing them to burst. This complex is called:
**Answer:** The Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) is a key component of the complement system that creates holes in microbial membranes.

**Q939.** In addition to direct lysis, the complement system also amplifies the:
**Answer:** Activated complement proteins contribute to the inflammatory response by attracting phagocytes and promoting vasodilation.

**Q940.** Interferons are a group of small proteins that are secreted by cells infected with:
**Answer:** Interferons are produced and released by host cells in response to the presence of viruses.

**Q941.** What is the primary action of interferons in combating viral infections?
**Answer:** Interferons bind to uninfected cells, stimulating them to produce proteins that inhibit viral protein synthesis, thus preventing viral replication.

**Q942.** Which type of interferon is known for enhancing the activity of Natural Killer (NK) cells?
**Answer:** Gamma interferon (Type II interferon) stimulates the activity of NK cells and macrophages.

**Q943.** Interferons are generally classified as a type of:
**Answer:** Interferons are a group of signaling proteins, specifically a type of cytokine, that mediate communication between cells.

**Q944.** Which of the following is a key mechanism by which interferons limit the spread of a virus?
**Answer:** Interferons warn neighboring healthy cells, making them resistant to viral infection by prompting the production of antiviral proteins.

**Q945.** The overall function of the complement system and interferons is to provide:
**Answer:** Both the complement system and interferons are components of the innate immune system, offering rapid and general defense mechanisms.

**Q946.** A key difference between the action of the complement system and interferons is that:
**Answer:** The complement system primarily causes direct destruction (lysis) of pathogens, whereas interferons act by preventing viral replication in host cells.

**Q947.** The inflammatory response is a localized tissue response to:
**Answer:** The inflammatory response is triggered by various forms of tissue injury or infection.

**Q948.** A systemic inflammatory response involves changes that occur throughout the body, such as:
**Answer:** A systemic inflammatory response can include widespread effects like an increase in white blood cell count (leukocytosis), particularly neutrophils.

**Q949.** During inflammation, which substance is released by basophils, causing vasodilation and increased vascular permeability?
**Answer:** Histamine is a key mediator released by basophils, leading to the characteristic signs of inflammation.

**Q950.** The increased blood flow and plasma leakage into the injured tissue during inflammation are responsible for which two cardinal signs of inflammation?
**Answer:** Increased blood flow causes redness and warmth, while increased vascular permeability leads to fluid leakage, causing swelling (edema).

**Q951.** One of the main purposes of the inflammatory response is to:
**Answer:** The inflammatory response brings immune cells and antimicrobial substances to the site of injury to eliminate pathogens and clear debris.

**Q952.** The accumulation of dead neutrophils, bacteria, and tissue debris at an infection site forms a thick, yellowish fluid called:
**Answer:** Pus is a common outcome of bacterial infections, consisting of dead immune cells, bacteria, and necrotic tissue.

**Q953.** A fever is an abnormally high body temperature that can be caused by:
**Answer:** Besides infections, brain abnormalities, like tumors, can also affect the thermoregulatory center and cause fever.

**Q954.** Substances that cause fever by acting on the hypothalamus to raise the body's temperature set-point are known as:
**Answer:** Pyrogens (e.g., bacterial lipopolysaccharide, toxins, or endogenous pyrogens like IL-1) reset the hypothalamic thermostat to a higher temperature.

**Q955.** Which medication is a common antipyretic that reduces fever by inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis in the hypothalamus?
**Answer:** Aspirin is a non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) that acts as an antipyretic by blocking prostaglandin synthesis.

**Q956.** One way fever helps the body fight infection is by:
**Answer:** Fever makes iron less available to bacteria, which require it for growth, and also enhances interferon production, boosting antiviral defense.

**Q957.** The third line of defense, or adaptive immunity, is highly dependent on the cells of the:
**Answer:** The lymphatic system, including its cells (lymphocytes), is central to the specific (adaptive) immune response.

**Q958.** The effectiveness of the third line of defense relies on the ability of immune cells to:
**Answer:** Adaptive immunity is characterized by its specificity, where immune cells target particular antigens.

**Q959.** Foreign substances that elicit an immune response, such as the production of antibodies, are called:
**Answer:** Antigens are molecules, often foreign, that are recognized by the immune system and can trigger an immune response.

**Q960.** A small molecule that is not antigenic by itself but can become antigenic when bound to a larger carrier molecule is called a:
**Answer:** Haptens are small molecules that require conjugation to a carrier protein to become immunogenic.

**Q961.** The immune system primarily distinguishes between 'self' and 'non-self' based on:
**Answer:** The immune system identifies foreignness by recognizing specific antigenic determinants on molecules.

**Q962.** Which of the following is an example of an exogenous antigen?
**Answer:** Exogenous antigens are those that enter the body from the outside, such as bacterial toxins.

**Q963.** The type of specific immunity that involves the production of antibodies by B cells is known as:
**Answer:** Humoral immunity relies on antibodies circulating in the body fluids (humors).

**Q964.** Which type of specific immunity primarily involves T lymphocytes directly attacking infected cells or regulating other immune cells?
**Answer:** Cell-mediated immunity involves T cells directly engaging with target cells or orchestrating immune responses.

**Q965.** Humoral immunity is most effective against:
**Answer:** Antibodies are effective at neutralizing pathogens and toxins circulating in body fluids before they can infect cells.

**Q966.** Cell-mediated immunity is crucial for combating:
**Answer:** Cell-mediated immunity, particularly cytotoxic T cells, is essential for identifying and eliminating cells that have become abnormal (e.g., infected or cancerous).

**Q967.** Which cells of the immune system are primarily responsible for producing antibodies?
**Answer:** B cells differentiate into plasma cells, which are the primary antibody-producing cells.

**Q968.** T lymphocytes mature in the:
**Answer:** T cells undergo maturation and selection in the thymus gland.

**Q969.** What is the primary role of Helper T cells in the adaptive immune response?
**Answer:** Helper T cells play a central role in orchestrating both humoral and cell-mediated immune responses by secreting cytokines that activate other immune cells.

**Q970.** Cytotoxic T cells primarily recognize and kill target cells that display:
**Answer:** Cytotoxic T cells recognize processed endogenous antigens presented on MHC class I molecules, typically found on all nucleated cells.

**Q971.** Memory cells are a crucial component of adaptive immunity because they:
**Answer:** Memory cells provide immunological memory, allowing for a more rapid and robust response upon re-exposure to the same pathogen.

**Q972.** Which type of lymphocyte is responsible for immunological surveillance against cancer cells?
**Answer:** Cytotoxic T cells recognize and eliminate tumor cells expressing abnormal antigens.

**Q973.** Which of the following cells presents antigens to Helper T cells?
**Answer:** Macrophages, dendritic cells, and B cells can function as Antigen-Presenting Cells (APCs) to activate Helper T cells by presenting antigens on MHC class II molecules.

**Q974.** Eosinophils are granulocytes primarily involved in defense against:
**Answer:** Eosinophils are associated with allergic responses and defense against helminthic (worm) infections.

**Q975.** Basophils play a key role in allergic reactions by releasing:
**Answer:** These cells degranulate and release histamine, which contributes to the symptoms of allergic reactions.

**Q976.** The human lymphatic system is composed of lymph vessels, lymphoid masses, lymph nodes, and:
**Answer:** The lymphatic system consists of vessels, lymphoid organs, nodes, and the fluid called lymph that flows within it.

**Q977.** In addition to the blood circulatory system, what other system is responsible for the transport of materials and returns substances from tissues to blood?
**Answer:** The lymphatic system works in parallel with the blood circulatory system to transport materials and maintain fluid balance.

**Q978.** The branches of lymph capillaries found in the villi of the small intestine, responsible for absorbing fatty acids and glycerol, are called:
**Answer:** Lacteals are specialized lymphatic capillaries in the intestinal villi that absorb digested fats.

**Q979.** Fatty acids and glycerol are absorbed into the epithelial cells of villi, where they form triglycerides, which are then coated with proteins to form:
**Answer:** Chylomicrons are lipoprotein particles formed in the enterocytes that transport dietary lipids to the body.

**Q980.** The lymphatic system begins with small vessels called lymph capillaries, which have blind endings in the:
**Answer:** Lymph capillaries originate as blind-ended tubules in the Extracellular fluid (interstitial fluid), collecting excess fluid from tissues.

**Q981.** Compared to blood capillaries, lymph capillaries are:
**Answer:** Lymph capillaries have highly permeable walls, allowing larger molecules and excess fluid to enter easily.

**Q982.** When interstitial fluid enters lymph capillaries, it is then referred to as:
**Answer:** Once interstitial fluid enters the lymphatic capillaries, it becomes lymph.

**Q983.** Lymph capillaries join to form larger lymphatic vessels, which eventually converge to form two main lymph ducts: the right lymphatic duct and the:
**Answer:** The thoracic duct is the largest lymphatic vessel, draining lymph from most of the body.

**Q984.** The right lymphatic duct and the thoracic duct both open into:
**Answer:** These major lymphatic ducts return lymph to the bloodstream by emptying into the subclavian veins.

**Q985.** Which of the following mechanisms helps maintain the flow of lymph through the lymphatic vessels?
**Answer:** Lymph flow is largely facilitated by skeletal muscle contractions compressing the vessels and one-way valves preventing backflow.

**Q986.** One of the key functions of the lymphatic system is to return excess fluid and proteins from the interstitial spaces to the:
**Answer:** The lymphatic system collects excess interstitial fluid and plasma proteins that have leaked from capillaries and returns them to the circulation, maintaining blood volume and pressure.

**Q987.** The lymphatic system plays a crucial role in the body's defense by:
**Answer:** Lymph nodes filter lymph, and lymphoid organs produce lymphocytes, which are key immune cells.

**Q988.** Small, bean-shaped structures found along lymphatic vessels that filter lymph and contain lymphocytes and macrophages are called:
**Answer:** Lymph nodes are strategically located to filter lymph and initiate immune responses.

**Q989.** Lymph nodes are typically present in clusters in regions such as the neck, axilla (armpit), and:
**Answer:** Common locations for lymph node clusters include the neck, axilla, and groin.

**Q990.** What happens to lymph as it passes through a lymph node?
**Answer:** Lymph nodes act as filters, removing pathogens and debris from lymph and exposing them to immune cells.

**Q991.** Which of the following is considered one of the larger lymphoid masses in the body?
**Answer:** The spleen is a major lymphoid organ, alongside the thymus, tonsils, and adenoids.

**Q992.** The spleen is primarily responsible for:
**Answer:** The spleen functions to filter blood, remove old red blood cells, and store blood components.

**Q993.** Which lymphoid mass is crucial for the maturation of T lymphocytes?
**Answer:** The thymus is the primary lymphoid organ where T cells mature and differentiate.

**Q994.** Tonsils and adenoids are examples of lymphoid masses that primarily produce:
**Answer:** Tonsils and adenoids, like other lymphoid tissues, contain and produce lymphocytes, contributing to local immune defense.

**Q995.** The collection of lymph nodes, lymph vessels, and lymphoid masses constitutes the system primarily involved in:
**Answer:** The lymphatic system's main roles are to maintain fluid homeostasis and provide immune defense.

**Q996.** Rheumatoid arthritis is an autoimmune disorder characterized by the immune system attacking:
**Answer:** Rheumatoid arthritis is a chronic inflammatory disorder that primarily affects the lining of the joints.

**Q997.** In rheumatoid arthritis, the body produces autoantibodies that target:
**Answer:** A hallmark of rheumatoid arthritis is the presence of rheumatoid factor, which are autoantibodies against the Fc portion of IgG.

**Q998.** Which immune cells are found in the synovial fluid of affected joints in rheumatoid arthritis and contribute to the inflammation?
**Answer:** The synovial fluid in rheumatoid arthritis is infiltrated with various immune cells, including T cells, plasma cells (producing autoantibodies), and macrophages.

**Q999.** The inflammation and joint damage in rheumatoid arthritis are largely mediated by the release of:
**Answer:** Pro-inflammatory cytokines, such as TNF-alpha and IL-6, play a critical role in the pathogenesis of rheumatoid arthritis.

**Q1000.** Rheumatoid arthritis is classified as an autoimmune disease because:
**Answer:** Autoimmune diseases occur when the immune system loses its ability to distinguish between self and non-self, leading to an attack on the body's own components.

**Q1001.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

### Inheritance (92 Questions)

**Q1002.** Who is famously known as the 'Father of Genetics' for his foundational work on heredity?
 **A.** Charles Darwin
 **B.** James Watson
 **C.** Francis Crick
 **D.** Gregor Mendel ✓
**Answer:** Gregor Mendel

**Q1003.** In a monohybrid cross, if a purebred tall pea plant (TT) is crossed with a purebred short pea plant (tt), what will be the genotype of the F1 generation?
**Answer:** A mix of TT and tt

**Q1004.** The observable physical characteristics of an organism, determined by its genetic makeup, are called its:
**Answer:** Allele

**Q1005.** If the genotype of an organism is 'Aa', it is considered to be:
 **A.** Homozygous dominant
 **B.** Homozygous recessive
 **C.** Heterozygous ✓
**Answer:** Heterozygous

**Q1006.** The Law of Segregation states that:
 **A.** Alleles for different traits assort independently.
 **B.** Dominant alleles always mask recessive alleles.
 **C.** Genes located on the same chromosome are always inherited together.
 **D.** Each individual has two alleles for a trait, which separate during gamete formation. ✓
**Answer:** Each individual has two alleles for a trait, which separate during gamete formation.

**Q1007.** A recessive allele expresses its trait:
 **A.** Only if present with a dominant allele.
 **B.** Only if in a heterozygous condition.
 **C.** Either in heterozygous or homozygous form.
 **D.** Only if in a homozygous condition. ✓
**Answer:** Only if in a homozygous condition.

**Q1008.** What is the genotypic ratio observed in the F2 generation of a monohybrid cross involving complete dominance?
 **A.** 3:1
 **B.** 9:3:3:1
 **C.** 1:1
 **D.** 1:2:1 ✓
**Answer:** 1:2:1

**Q1009.** In a dihybrid cross, how many different phenotypic combinations are expected in the F2 generation under independent assortment?
 **A.** 2
 **B.** 3
 **C.** 9
 **D.** 4 ✓
**Answer:** 4

**Q1010.** The phenotypic ratio of 9:3:3:1 is characteristic of which type of cross?
 **A.** Monohybrid cross
 **B.** Test cross
 **C.** Back cross
 **D.** Dihybrid cross ✓
**Answer:** Dihybrid cross

**Q1011.** Mendel's Law of Independent Assortment states that:
 **A.** Alleles for the same trait segregate independently.
 **B.** Dominant alleles are always expressed.
 **C.** Traits are always inherited together.
 **D.** Genes located on different chromosomes assort independently during gamete formation. ✓
**Answer:** Genes located on different chromosomes assort independently during gamete formation.

**Q1012.** Which of the following is a limitation of the Law of Independent Assortment?
 **A.** It only applies to monohybrid crosses.
 **B.** It does not account for dominant alleles.
 **C.** It only applies to purebred organisms.
 **D.** It does not apply to linked genes. ✓
**Answer:** It does not apply to linked genes.

**Q1013.** Dihybrid crosses demonstrate that the inheritance of one trait:
 **A.** Always influences the inheritance of another trait.
 **B.** Is completely dependent on the inheritance of another trait.
 **C.** Is only possible if traits are recessive.
 **D.** Does not affect the inheritance of another trait, if genes are unlinked. ✓
**Answer:** Does not affect the inheritance of another trait, if genes are unlinked.

**Q1014.** The usefulness of the Law of Independent Assortment lies in explaining:
 **A.** Why all offspring are identical.
 **B.** How recessive traits disappear from a population.
 **C.** The process of natural selection.
 **D.** The production of new combinations of traits in offspring. ✓
**Answer:** The production of new combinations of traits in offspring.

**Q1015.** In a cross between two pea plants heterozygous for seed shape (Rr) and seed color (Yy), how many unique gametes can each parent produce?
 **A.** 1
 **B.** 2
 **C.** 3
 **D.** 4 ✓
**Answer:** 4

**Q1016.** What is the phenotype ratio in the F2 generation of a dihybrid cross?
 **A.** 1:1:1:1
 **B.** 3:1
 **C.** 4:3
 **D.** 9:3:3:1 ✓
**Answer:** 9:3:3:1

**Q1017.** If a parent has the genotype AABb, what are the possible gametes it can produce?
 **A.** AA, Bb
 **B.** A, B
 **C.** AABB, AAbb
 **D.** AB, Ab ✓
**Answer:** AB, Ab

**Q1018.** The scope of independent assortment in variation refers to its role in:
 **A.** Reducing genetic diversity.
 **B.** Increasing the number of identical offspring.
 **C.** Ensuring all offspring are purebred.
 **D.** Creating new combinations of alleles on different chromosomes. ✓
**Answer:** Creating new combinations of alleles on different chromosomes.

**Q1019.** What is the probability of a coin landing on heads twice in a row?
 **A.** 1/2
 **B.** 1/8
 **C.** 1
 **D.** 1/4 ✓
**Answer:** 1/4

**Q1020.** In a cross between two individuals, Aa x Aa, what is the probability of producing an 'AA' offspring?
 **A.** 1/2
 **B.** 3/4
 **C.** 1
 **D.** 1/4 ✓
**Answer:** 1/4

**Q1021.** If the probability of inheriting allele 'A' is 1/2 and the probability of inheriting allele 'B' is 1/2, what is the probability of inheriting both 'A' and 'B' (assuming independent assortment)?
 **A.** 1/2
 **B.** 1/8
 **C.** 1
 **D.** 1/4 ✓
**Answer:** 1/4

**Q1022.** A pedigree chart is a diagram that shows:
 **A.** The dietary habits of a family.
 **B.** The migratory patterns of a species.
 **C.** The evolutionary timeline of an organism.
 **D.** The inheritance of traits across generations in a family. ✓
**Answer:** The inheritance of traits across generations in a family.

**Q1023.** If a couple has three children, what is the probability that all three will be girls?
 **A.** 1/2
 **B.** 1/4
 **C.** 1/6
 **D.** 1/8 ✓
**Answer:** 1/8

**Q1024.** In incomplete dominance, the heterozygous genotype results in a phenotype that is:
 **A.** Identical to one of the homozygous parents.
 **B.** A combination of both parental phenotypes appearing distinctly.
 **C.** Completely new and unrelated to either parent.
 **D.** Identical to neither homozygous parent, but an intermediate blend. ✓
**Answer:** Identical to neither homozygous parent, but an intermediate blend.

**Q1025.** Which of the following is a classic example of incomplete dominance?
 **A.** Human ABO blood groups
 **B.** Human MN blood groups
 **C.** Pea plant height
 **D.** Flower color in 4 O'clock plants ✓
**Answer:** Flower color in 4 O'clock plants

**Q1026.** If a red-flowered 4 O'clock plant (RR) is crossed with a white-flowered 4 O'clock plant (WW), what is the phenotype of the F1 generation?
 **A.** All red flowers
 **B.** All white flowers
 **C.** A mix of red and white flowers
 **D.** All pink flowers ✓
**Answer:** All pink flowers

**Q1027.** What is the genotypic ratio expected in the F2 generation of a cross between two pink-flowered 4 O'clock plants?
 **A.** 3:1
 **B.** 9:3:3:1
 **C.** 1:1
 **D.** 1:2:1 ✓
**Answer:** 1:2:1

**Q1028.** In incomplete dominance, the phenotypic ratio of the F2 generation for a monohybrid cross is typically:
 **A.** 3:1
 **B.** 9:3:3:1
 **C.** 1:1
 **D.** 1:2:1 ✓
**Answer:** 1:2:1

**Q1029.** A plant with pink flowers is crossed with a white-flowered plant. If pink is heterozygous (RW) and white is homozygous (WW), what percentage of the offspring will have white flowers?
 **A.** 0%
 **B.** 25%
 **C.** 100%
 **D.** 50% ✓
**Answer:** 50%

**Q1030.** In incomplete dominance, if a character has alleles R (red) and W (white), an individual with genotype RW would exhibit which phenotype?
 **A.** Red
 **B.** White
 **C.** Red and white patches
 **D.** Pink ✓
**Answer:** Pink

**Q1031.** Co-dominance is characterized by:
 **A.** The blending of parental phenotypes in heterozygotes.
 **B.** The complete masking of one allele by another.
 **C.** The expression of a trait only in homozygous recessive individuals.
 **D.** The simultaneous and distinct expression of both alleles in the heterozygote. ✓
**Answer:** The simultaneous and distinct expression of both alleles in the heterozygote.

**Q1032.** Which of the following human blood group systems is a classic example of co-dominance?
 **A.** Rh blood group
 **B.** MN blood group
 **C.** ABO blood group
 **D.** Both MN and ABO blood groups ✓
**Answer:** Both MN and ABO blood groups

**Q1033.** In the human MN blood group system, an individual with the genotype LMLN will express:
 **A.** Only M antigen
 **B.** Only N antigen
 **C.** Neither M nor N antigens
 **D.** Both M and N antigens ✓
**Answer:** Both M and N antigens

**Q1034.** What is a key difference between incomplete dominance and co-dominance?
 **A.** In incomplete dominance, only one allele is expressed in phenotype; in co-dominance, both phenotypes are distinctly expressed.
 **B.** In incomplete dominance, dominant alleles are stronger; in co-dominance, they are weaker.
 **C.** Incomplete dominance involves multiple genes; co-dominance involves only one.
 **D.** In incomplete dominance, a blend of phenotypes occurs; in co-dominance, both phenotypes are distinctly expressed. ✓
**Answer:** In incomplete dominance, a blend of phenotypes occurs; in co-dominance, both phenotypes are distinctly expressed.

**Q1035.** If two individuals, both with MN blood type, have children, what are the possible genotypes of their offspring?
 **A.** Only LMLN
 **B.** Only LMLM and LNLN
 **C.** Only LMLM and LMLN
 **D.** LMLM, LMLN, and LNLN ✓
**Answer:** LMLM, LMLN, and LNLN

**Q1036.** Which genetic interaction results in a third, distinct phenotype that is not a blend, but shows traits of both alleles?
 **A.** Complete dominance
 **B.** Incomplete dominance
 **C.** Epistasis
 **D.** Co-dominance ✓
**Answer:** Co-dominance

**Q1037.** The presence of more than two alleles for a gene in a population is known as:
 **A.** Incomplete dominance
 **B.** Co-dominance
 **C.** Polygenic inheritance
 **D.** Multiple alleles ✓
**Answer:** Multiple alleles

**Q1038.** The best-known example of multiple alleles in humans is the inheritance of:
 **A.** Eye color
 **B.** Height
 **C.** Sex-linked traits
 **D.** ABO blood groups ✓
**Answer:** ABO blood groups

**Q1039.** How many alleles are responsible for determining the ABO blood group in humans at the population level?
 **A.** 1
 **B.** 2
 **C.** 4
 **D.** 3 ✓
**Answer:** 3

**Q1040.** An individual with blood type A can have which of the following genotypes?
 **A.** IBIB or IBi
 **B.** IAIB
 **C.** ii
 **D.** IAIA or IAi ✓
**Answer:** IAIA or IAi

**Q1041.** A person with AB blood type has the genotype:
 **A.** IAIA
 **B.** IBIB
 **C.** ii
 **D.** IAIB ✓
**Answer:** IAIB

**Q1042.** If a child has O blood type (genotype ii), and the mother has A blood type (genotype IAi), what must be the father's blood type genotype?
 **A.** IAIA
 **B.** IBIB
 **C.** ii
 **D.** It must contain at least one 'i' allele (e.g., IAi, IBi, or ii). ✓
**Answer:** It must contain at least one 'i' allele (e.g., IAi, IBi, or ii).

**Q1043.** Which allele in the ABO blood group system is recessive to both IA and IB alleles?
 **A.** IA
 **B.** IB
 **C.** All are co-dominant
 **D.** i ✓
**Answer:** i

**Q1044.** Which of the following antigens are present on the surface of red blood cells in an individual with B blood type?
 **A.** A antigens
 **B.** Both A and B antigens
 **C.** Neither A nor B antigens
 **D.** B antigens ✓
**Answer:** B antigens

**Q1045.** A person with AB blood type has which antibodies in their plasma?
 **A.** Anti-A antibodies
 **B.** Anti-B antibodies
 **C.** Both Anti-A and Anti-B antibodies
 **D.** Neither Anti-A nor Anti-B antibodies ✓
**Answer:** Neither Anti-A nor Anti-B antibodies

**Q1046.** An individual with O blood type produces which of the following antibodies?
 **A.** Anti-A only
 **B.** Anti-B only
 **C.** Neither Anti-A nor Anti-B
 **D.** Both Anti-A and Anti-B ✓
**Answer:** Both Anti-A and Anti-B

**Q1047.** Why is O-negative blood considered the "universal donor"?
 **A.** It has both A and B antigens.
 **B.** It has A, B, and Rh antibodies.
 **C.** It only contains plasma.
 **D.** It lacks A, B, and Rh antigens. ✓
**Answer:** It lacks A, B, and Rh antigens.

**Q1048.** Why is AB-positive blood considered the "universal recipient"?
 **A.** It lacks all major antigens.
 **B.** It has a unique type of hemoglobin.
 **C.** It is very rare.
 **D.** It has no antibodies against A, B, or Rh antigens. ✓
**Answer:** It has no antibodies against A, B, or Rh antigens.

**Q1049.** If a person with A blood type receives a transfusion of B blood type, what will likely happen?
 **A.** The blood will mix without issue.
 **B.** The donor's antibodies will attack the recipient's cells.
 **C.** The recipient's blood type will change to AB.
 **D.** The recipient's antibodies will attack donor's cells. ✓
**Answer:** The recipient's antibodies will attack donor's cells.

**Q1050.** Which of the following genotypes represents a person with B blood type?
 **A.** I A I A
 **B.** I A I B
 **C.** ii
 **D.** I B I B or I B i ✓
**Answer:** I B I B or I B i

**Q1051.** A couple has blood types A and B. Can they have a child with O blood type?
 **A.** No, never.
 **B.** Yes, if the father is homozygous dominant.
 **C.** Only if father or mother is O.
 **D.** Yes, if both parents are heterozygous. ✓
**Answer:** Yes, if both parents are heterozygous.

**Q1052.** Which blood type lacks both A and B antigens on the surface of red blood cells?
 **A.** A
 **B.** B
 **C.** AB
 **D.** O ✓
**Answer:** O

**Q1053.** The presence or absence of which antigen determines if a person's blood type is positive or negative within the Rh system?
 **A.** A antigen
 **B.** B antigen
 **C.** M antigen
 **D.** D antigen (Rh factor) ✓
**Answer:** D antigen (Rh factor)

**Q1054.** An Rh-negative individual will develop anti-Rh antibodies if exposed to:
 **A.** Rh-negative blood
 **B.** Plasma
 **C.** White blood cells
 **D.** Rh-positive blood ✓
**Answer:** Rh-positive blood

**Q1055.** What is the primary concern for a second Rh-positive fetus carried by an Rh-negative mother if no preventative measures are taken?
 **A.** The fetus will develop Rh-negative blood.
 **B.** The mother will become Rh-positive.
 **C.** The fetus will spontaneously abort due to ABO incompatibility.
 **D.** Maternal anti-Rh antibodies may cross the placenta and destroy fetal red blood cells. ✓
**Answer:** Maternal anti-Rh antibodies may cross the placenta and destroy fetal red blood cells.

**Q1056.** Hemolytic Disease of the Newborn (Erythroblastosis Fetalis) is most likely to occur when:
 **A.** Rh-positive mother carries her second or subsequent Rh-negative fetus.
 **B.** Rh-negative mother carries her first Rh-positive fetus.
 **C.** Rh-positive mother receives Rh-negative blood.
 **D.** Rh-negative mother carries her second or subsequent Rh-positive fetus. ✓
**Answer:** Rh-negative mother carries her second or subsequent Rh-positive fetus.

**Q1057.** A common preventative measure to avoid Erythroblastosis Fetalis is the administration of:
 **A.** Antibiotics to the mother.
 **B.** Vitamin K to the newborn.
 **C.** Blood transfusion to the mother during pregnancy.
 **D.** Rh immunoglobulin (RhoGAM) to the Rh-negative mother. ✓
**Answer:** Rh immunoglobulin (RhoGAM) to the Rh-negative mother.

**Q1058.** Which of the following is NOT a typical symptom or consequence of severe Erythroblastosis Fetalis in a newborn?
 **A.** Anemia
 **B.** Jaundice
 **C.** Enlarged spleen and liver
 **D.** Increased red blood cell production ✓
**Answer:** Increased red blood cell production

**Q1059.** The Rh factor is an antigen found on the surface of:
 **A.** White blood cells
 **B.** Platelets
 **C.** Plasma proteins
 **D.** Red blood cells ✓
**Answer:** Red blood cells

**Q1060.** If a father is Rh-positive (homozygous dominant RR) and the mother is Rh-negative (rr), what is the likelihood of their first child being Rh-positive?
 **A.** 0%
 **B.** 25%
 **C.** 50%
 **D.** 100% ✓
**Answer:** 100%

**Q1061.** Besides ABO and Rh, which other blood group system is an example of co-dominance?
 **A.** Duffy blood group
 **B.** Kidd blood group
 **C.** Kell blood group
 **D.** MN blood group ✓
**Answer:** MN blood group

**Q1062.** Minor blood group systems, other than ABO and Rh, generally:
 **A.** Cause severe complications in all blood transfusions.
 **B.** Are only found in very rare individuals.
 **C.** Determine blood type compatibility more than ABO and Rh.
 **D.** Usually do not complicate routine blood transfusions. ✓
**Answer:** Usually do not complicate routine blood transfusions.

**Q1063.** The fact that there are more than two hundred minor blood groups indicates:
 **A.** That only ABO and Rh are important.
 **B.** That blood transfusions are rarely safe.
 **C.** That blood types are constantly changing.
 **D.** A high level of genetic diversity in human blood characteristics. ✓
**Answer:** A high level of genetic diversity in human blood characteristics.

**Q1064.** Which of the following best defines polygenic inheritance?
 **A.** A single gene affecting multiple traits.
 **B.** Genes located on sex chromosomes.
 **C.** Genes that blend their effects.
 **D.** Multiple genes affecting a single trait. ✓
**Answer:** Multiple genes affecting a single trait.

**Q1065.** Human skin color is a classic example of a trait governed by:
 **A.** Monogenic inheritance
 **B.** Co-dominance
 **C.** Sex-linked inheritance
 **D.** Polygenic inheritance ✓
**Answer:** Polygenic inheritance

**Q1066.** Which of the following is a characteristic of polygenic traits?
 **A.** They exhibit discrete phenotypic categories.
 **B.** They are always controlled by a single dominant allele.
 **C.** They show clear Mendelian ratios (e.g., 3:1).
 **D.** Their expression is often influenced by environmental factors. ✓
**Answer:** Their expression is often influenced by environmental factors.

**Q1067.** If genes A, B, and C all contribute to the same trait additively, this is an example of:
 **A.** Epistasis
 **B.** Incomplete dominance
 **C.** Pleiotropy
 **D.** Polygenic inheritance ✓
**Answer:** Polygenic inheritance

**Q1068.** The inheritance of grain color in wheat, which can show a range of shades from white to dark red, is an example of:
 **A.** Mendelian inheritance
 **B.** Sex-linked inheritance
 **C.** Lethal alleles
 **D.** Polygenic inheritance ✓
**Answer:** Polygenic inheritance

**Q1069.** What is epistasis?
 **A.** When one gene affects multiple, unrelated traits.
 **B.** When two alleles present at different locus are expressed simultaneously.
 **C.** When a gene has more than two alleles in a population.
 **D.** When one gene's expression is masked by another gene at a different locus. ✓
**Answer:** When one gene's expression is masked by another gene at a different locus.

**Q1070.** The coat color in Labrador retrievers, where the E/e gene determines pigment deposition and the B/b gene determines black/brown, is a classic example of:
 **A.** Incomplete dominance
 **B.** Co-dominance
 **C.** Polygenic inheritance
 **D.** Epistasis ✓
**Answer:** Epistasis

**Q1071.** In epistasis, the gene that masks or modifies the expression of another gene is called the:
 **A.** Hypostatic gene
 **B.** Recessive gene
 **C.** Modifier gene
 **D.** Epistatic gene ✓
**Answer:** Epistatic gene

**Q1072.** A common modified Mendelian phenotypic ratio observed in recessive epistasis (e.g., in sweet peas for pigment) is:
 **A.** 9:3:3:1
 **B.** 12:3:1
 **C.** 1:2:1
 **D.** 9:3:4 ✓
**Answer:** 9:3:4

**Q1073.** In the pigment production in sweet peas, if a recessive genotype (cc) prevents the production of color regardless of the other gene's alleles (A/a), this is an example of:
 **A.** Dominant epistasis
 **B.** Complementary gene action
 **C.** Inhibitory gene action
 **D.** Recessive epistasis ✓
**Answer:** Recessive epistasis

**Q1074.** The exchange of genetic material between homologous chromosomes during meiosis is called:
 **A.** Independent assortment
 **B.** Segregation
 **C.** Linkage
 **D.** Crossing over ✓
**Answer:** Crossing over

**Q1075.** How does crossing over affect genetic variation?
 **A.** It reduces genetic variation by keeping genes linked.
 **B.** It ensures that all offspring are identical to parents.
 **C.** It prevents mutation.
 **D.** It creates new combinations of alleles on chromosomes. ✓
**Answer:** It creates new combinations of alleles on chromosomes.

**Q1076.** If two genes are located very far apart on the same chromosome, their inheritance pattern will most closely resemble that of:
 **A.** Incomplete dominance.
 **B.** Complete linkage.
 **C.** Co-dominance.
 **D.** Independent assortment. ✓
**Answer:** Independent assortment.

**Q1077.** Which organism is famously used as a model to study gene linkage and crossing over, particularly concerning traits like wing length and eye color?
 **A.** Pisum sativum
 **B.** Homo sapiens
 **C.** Escherichia coli
 **D.** Drosophila melanogaster ✓
**Answer:** Drosophila melanogaster

**Q1078.** In the XX-XY system of sex determination, which chromosome carries the primary genes responsible for maleness?
 **A.** X chromosome
 **B.** Autosomes
 **C.** Both X and Y chromosomes
 **D.** Y chromosome ✓
**Answer:** Y chromosome

**Q1079.** In the XX-XO system of sex determination, What is the genotype for males?
 **A.** XY
 **B.** YY
 **C.** XXY
 **D.** XO ✓
**Answer:** XO

**Q1080.** Which term refers to any chromosome that is not a sex chromosome?
 **A.** Allosome
 **B.** Holo chromosome
 **C.** Heterosome
 **D.** Autosome ✓
**Answer:** Autosome

**Q1081.** A trait that is inherited on a sex chromosome (usually the X chromosome) is called:
 **A.** Autosomal dominant
 **B.** Autosomal recessive
 **C.** Sex-limited
 **D.** Sex-linked ✓
**Answer:** Sex-linked

**Q1082.** Which gender is typically more affected by X-linked recessive disorders in humans?
**Answer:** Females

**Q1083.** Color blindness in humans is an example of a(n):
 **A.** Autosomal dominant disorder
 **B.** Autosomal recessive disorder
 **C.** Y-linked disorder
 **D.** X-linked recessive disorder ✓
**Answer:** X-linked recessive disorder

**Q1084.** Genes that are located only on the Y chromosome and are passed directly from father to son are called:
 **A.** X-linked genes
 **B.** Autosomal genes
 **C.** Sex-influenced genes
 **D.** Holandric genes ✓
**Answer:** Holandric genes

**Q1085.** A trait that appears in both genders but is expressed differently or more frequently in one gender due to hormonal influence is:
**Answer:** Sex-limited

**Q1086.** A trait that is expressed only in one gender, despite being present in both genders' genotypes, is:
 **A.** Sex-linked
 **B.** Sex-influenced
 **C.** Sex-limited ✓
**Answer:** Sex-limited

**Q1087.** Haemophilia, a blood clotting disorder, is a well-known example of a(n):
 **A.** Autosomal dominant disorder
 **B.** Autosomal recessive disorder
 **C.** Y-linked disorder
 **D.** X-linked recessive disorder ✓
**Answer:** X-linked recessive disorder

**Q1088.** If a carrier female for an X-linked recessive disorder (X A X a) has children with an unaffected male (X A Y), what is the probability that a son will be affected?
 **A.** 0%
 **B.** 25%
 **C.** 100%
 **D.** 50% ✓
**Answer:** 50%

**Q1089.** Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) is an X-linked recessive disorder. If a mother is a carrier and the father is unaffected, what is the probability that their daughter will be a carrier?
 **A.** 0%
 **B.** 25%
 **C.** 100%
 **D.** 50% ✓
**Answer:** 50%

**Q1090.** In the ZZ-ZW system of sex determination (e.g., in birds), who is having ZW?
 **A.** Males
 **B.** Both males and females
 **C.** Neither one
 **D.** Females ✓
**Answer:** Females

**Q1091.** Which of the following is true regarding sex-linked traits?
 **A.** They are always found on the Y chromosome.
 **B.** They are inherited independently of sex.
 **C.** They only affect reproductive organs.
 **D.** Their expression pattern can differ between males and females. ✓
**Answer:** Their expression pattern can differ between males and females.

**Q1092.** If a trait is controlled by a gene on an autosome, but its expression is only in males, it is an example of a:
 **A.** Sex-linked trait
 **B.** Sex-influenced trait
 **C.** Holandric trait
 **D.** Sex-limited trait ✓
**Answer:** Sex-limited trait

**Q1093.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

---

### Plants (81 Questions)

**Q1094.** Which of the following elements is classified as a macronutrient required by plants?
**Answer:** Macronutrients are required in relatively large quantities (greater than 0.05% of dry weight). Potassium is a macronutrient, while zinc, molybdenum, and boron are micronutrients, needed in trace amounts.

**Q1095.** The technique most useful for determining if an element is essential for plant growth is:
**Answer:** Hydroponics allows plants to be grown in a controlled environment with specific mineral solutions, enabling precise determination of an element's essentiality by observing growth in its absence.

**Q1096.** A plant showing stunted growth and purplish discoloration of leaves, particularly older ones, is likely deficient in:
**Answer:** Phosphorus deficiency often results in stunted growth and a distinctive purplish coloration, especially in older leaves, due to its role in energy transfer and nucleic acid synthesis.

**Q1097.** Which micronutrient is essential for chlorophyll synthesis but is not a constituent of the chlorophyll molecule itself?
**Answer:** Iron is crucial for the synthesis of chlorophyll, but it does not become a part of the chlorophyll molecule's structure. Magnesium, in contrast, is a central atom in the chlorophyll molecule.

**Q1098.** Carnivorous plants, like the pitcher plant, primarily obtain which nutrient from consuming insects?
**Answer:** Carnivorous plants typically thrive in nitrogen-poor soils. They supplement their nitrogen requirements by trapping and digesting insects and other small organisms.

**Q1099.** Which of these is a typical characteristic of a plant suffering from nitrogen deficiency?
**Answer:** Nitrogen is a mobile element in plants. When deficient, it is re-mobilized from older leaves to support new growth, leading to a general yellowing or chlorosis of the older foliage first.

**Q1100.** Which element is a component of amino acids and nucleic acids, and its deficiency leads to stunted growth and chlorosis?
**Answer:** Sulfur is an essential component of several amino acids (methionine, cysteine) and is therefore crucial for protein synthesis. Its deficiency can cause stunted growth and yellowing of younger leaves.

**Q1101.** The primary purpose of growing plants in hydroponic solutions is to:
**Answer:** Hydroponics provides a controlled environment where the precise composition of mineral nutrients supplied to the plant roots can be manipulated, allowing for experimental determination of nutrient requirements.

**Q1102.** During the daytime, which gas primarily enters the plant leaf for the process of photosynthesis?
**Answer:** Plants take in carbon dioxide from the atmosphere through stomata for use as a reactant in photosynthesis during daylight hours.

**Q1103.** Which of the following structures is responsible for regulating the opening and closing of stomata?
**Answer:** Guard cells are specialized epidermal cells that surround each stoma. Their turgor changes regulate the size of the stomatal pore, thereby controlling gas exchange.

**Q1104.** The primary site for gaseous exchange in the submerged parts of aquatic plants is typically through their:
**Answer:** Submerged aquatic plants often lack stomata or lenticels in their submerged parts, and gaseous exchange occurs directly through the diffusion across their general body surface.

**Q1105.** Which process in plants releases oxygen as a by-product?
**Answer:** Photosynthesis utilizes carbon dioxide and water to produce glucose and oxygen. Oxygen is released into the atmosphere as a by-product of the light-dependent reactions.

**Q1106.** Stomata are typically more numerous on the lower epidermis of a dicot leaf. This arrangement primarily helps to:
**Answer:** Placing more stomata on the lower surface, which is less exposed to direct sunlight and wind, helps to minimize water loss through transpiration while still allowing for sufficient gas exchange.

**Q1107.** The opening of stomata is primarily regulated by the influx and efflux of which ion into and out of the guard cells?
**Answer:** The active transport of potassium ions into guard cells increases their solute concentration, leading to water uptake by osmosis, increased turgor pressure, and subsequent stomatal opening.

**Q1108.** During periods of water stress, plants typically:
**Answer:** Under water stress, plants close their stomata to reduce transpiration and prevent excessive water loss, even though this limits carbon dioxide uptake for photosynthesis.

**Q1109.** Small pores on the woody stems and roots of plants that allow for some gaseous exchange are called:
**Answer:** Lenticels are small, raised pores found on the bark of woody stems and some roots, facilitating gaseous exchange between the internal tissues and the atmosphere. Stomata are on leaves, hydathodes release water, and cuticles are a protective layer.

**Q1110.** Which of the following conditions generally promotes the closing of stomata?
**Answer:** When a plant experiences a water deficit (e.g., drought conditions), abscisic acid is produced, which signals the guard cells to lose turgor and close the stomata, conserving water.

**Q1111.** What is the primary role of the intercellular air spaces within the mesophyll layer of a leaf?
**Answer:** The extensive network of intercellular air spaces within the spongy mesophyll allows for efficient diffusion of carbon dioxide from the stomata to the photosynthetic cells and the diffusion of oxygen and water vapor out of the leaf.

**Q1112.** The primary structures in roots responsible for the absorption of water and minerals are:
**Answer:** Root hairs are slender extensions of epidermal cells that greatly increase the surface area of the root, making them highly efficient at absorbing water and dissolved minerals from the soil.

**Q1113.** Water tends to move from an area of:
**Answer:** Water movement in plants, including absorption by roots, occurs down a water potential gradient, meaning from an area where water potential is higher (less negative) to an area where it is lower (more negative).

**Q1114.** In the apoplast pathway, water moves primarily through the:
**Answer:** The apoplast pathway involves water moving through the non-living parts of the root, specifically the cell walls and the intercellular spaces, without crossing any cell membranes until it reaches the endodermis.

**Q1115.** The symplast pathway involves water movement through the:
**Answer:** In the symplast pathway, water moves from cell to cell by passing through the cytoplasm of cells, connected via microscopic channels called plasmodesmata.

**Q1116.** Which part of the root acts as a selective barrier, forcing water and solutes to enter the symplast pathway before reaching the vascular cylinder?
**Answer:** The endodermis, with its Casparian strips, forms a waterproof barrier that forces water and dissolved solutes to move from the apoplast into the symplast before they can enter the stele (vascular cylinder).

**Q1117.** Casparian strips are composed mainly of:
**Answer:** Casparian strips are waxy bands within the cell walls of endodermal cells, impregnated with hydrophobic substances like lignin and suberin, which make them impermeable to water.

**Q1118.** The movement of water directly across the cell membranes and cytoplasm of individual cells, not relying on plasmodesmata or cell walls, is known as the:
**Answer:** The transmembrane pathway involves water repeatedly entering and exiting cells by crossing the plasma membranes, thereby moving through the cell cytoplasm and vacuoles.

**Q1119.** A high concentration of solutes inside root hair cells compared to the soil water leads to water absorption primarily by:
**Answer:** Root hair cells maintain a lower (more negative) water potential than the surrounding soil water due to a higher concentration of solutes. This water potential gradient drives the passive movement of water into the root hairs via osmosis.

**Q1120.** Which factor contributes to the water potential of a plant cell?
**Answer:** Water potential (Ψ) is a measure of the free energy of water. It is determined by the combined effects of solute potential (Ψs, due to dissolved solutes) and pressure potential (Ψp, due to turgor pressure).

**Q1121.** The process by which mineral ions are taken up by root cells against their concentration gradient is:
**Answer:** Mineral ions are often present in lower concentrations in the soil than inside root cells. To absorb these ions, plants utilize active transport, which requires energy (ATP) to move ions against their concentration gradient.

**Q1122.** The upward movement of water and dissolved minerals from the roots to the aerial parts of the plant is called:
**Answer:** Ascent of sap specifically refers to the transport of water and inorganic solutes upwards through the xylem from the roots to the leaves. Translocation is for organic solutes in phloem.

**Q1123.** Which of the following contributes to root pressure?
**Answer:** Root pressure is generated by the active transport of mineral ions into the xylem of roots, which lowers the water potential within the xylem, causing water to move in by osmosis and build up pressure.

**Q1124.** The primary driving force for the ascent of sap in tall trees, according to the cohesion-tension theory, is:
**Answer:** The cohesion-tension theory proposes that the evaporation of water from leaves (transpiration) creates a negative pressure or 'pull' that draws water upwards through the xylem vessels due to the cohesive properties of water molecules.

**Q1125.** The property of water molecules to stick to each other through hydrogen bonds is called:
**Answer:** Cohesion refers to the attractive forces between like molecules. In water, these are strong hydrogen bonds that allow water molecules to form a continuous column in the xylem.

**Q1126.** The property of water molecules to stick to the inner surfaces of xylem vessels is called:
**Answer:** Adhesion refers to the attractive forces between different types of molecules. Water molecules adhere to the hydrophilic (water-attracting) walls of the xylem vessels, helping to counteract the pull of gravity.

**Q1127.** Which type of xylem cell is narrower and longer, with tapered ends and pits, contributing to water transport and support?
**Answer:** Tracheids are elongated, narrow, dead cells with tapered ends and pits in their walls, forming continuous pathways for water transport in xylem. Sieve tubes and companion cells are part of phloem.

**Q1128.** Xylem vessels are characterized by:
**Answer:** Xylem vessels are formed from a series of dead, hollow cells (vessel elements) arranged end-to-end, forming continuous tubes. Their walls are typically lignified, providing structural support.

**Q1129.** The transpiration stream is driven by:
**Answer:** The evaporation of water from the moist surfaces within the leaf (through stomata) creates a negative pressure (tension) that pulls the continuous column of water up through the xylem, establishing the transpiration stream.

**Q1130.** The opening of stomata is primarily caused by an increase in:
**Answer:** When guard cells absorb water, their turgor pressure increases, causing them to bow outwards and open the stomatal pore.

**Q1131.** Which of the following ions actively accumulate in guard cells, leading to stomatal opening?
**Answer:** The active influx of potassium ions into guard cells significantly lowers their water potential, drawing water into the cells by osmosis and increasing turgor, which leads to stomatal opening.

**Q1132.** Light typically promotes stomatal opening because it triggers:
**Answer:** Light drives photosynthesis, which consumes CO2. A decrease in internal CO2 concentration signals the stomata to open to allow more CO2 uptake. Light also directly promotes potassium ion uptake.

**Q1133.** During stomatal closing, guard cells primarily:
**Answer:** Stomatal closing occurs when guard cells lose water, leading to a decrease in their turgor pressure. This happens as potassium ions move out of the guard cells, causing water to follow by osmosis.

**Q1134.** Which hormone plays a significant role in promoting stomatal closure under water stress conditions?
**Answer:** Abscisic Acid (ABA) is a stress hormone that is produced in response to water deficit. It signals guard cells to close stomata rapidly to conserve water.

**Q1135.** The uneven thickening of the cell walls of guard cells contributes to:
**Answer:** Guard cells have unevenly thickened walls, with the inner wall (facing the stoma) being thicker. This structural feature causes them to bow outwards when turgid, facilitating stomatal opening.

**Q1136.** The primary sugar transported by the phloem is:
**Answer:** Sucrose is the most common form of sugar transported in the phloem because it is a non-reducing sugar, making it less reactive and more stable for long-distance transport.

**Q1137.** Which of the following phloem cells are enucleated at maturity and primarily responsible for conducting sugars?
**Answer:** Sieve tube elements are living cells that form the continuous tubes of the phloem, but they lose their nucleus at maturity, allowing for efficient sugar transport.

**Q1138.** The 'source' in the pressure flow hypothesis refers to the plant part where:
**Answer:** A source is typically a photosynthesizing leaf or a storage organ (like a root tuber) that is exporting sugars because it has an excess.

**Q1139.** According to the pressure flow hypothesis, sugar is actively loaded into sieve tubes at the source, which leads to:
**Answer:** Active loading of sucrose into the sieve tube elements at the source increases their solute concentration, lowering water potential and causing water to move in from the xylem by osmosis, thus building up turgor pressure.

**Q1140.** Which cells are closely associated with sieve tube elements and provide metabolic support for loading and unloading sugars?
**Answer:** Companion cells are specialized parenchyma cells that are metabolically very active and are intimately associated with sieve tube elements, playing a crucial role in loading and unloading sugars into and out of the sieve tubes.

**Q1141.** The 'sink' in the pressure flow hypothesis refers to a plant part where:
**Answer:** A sink is a non-photosynthetic part of the plant or a storage organ (e.g., growing root, fruit, flower) that requires sugars and actively removes them from the phloem.

**Q1142.** The process of water loss in the form of water vapor from the aerial parts of a plant, primarily through stomata, is called:
**Answer:** Transpiration is the evaporative loss of water from plants, mainly from leaves through stomata, and it is a major process of water regulation.

**Q1143.** The exudation of xylem sap, often seen as droplets on leaf margins in humid conditions, is known as:
**Answer:** Guttation is the process where water is released as liquid droplets from the hydathodes (specialized pores) on leaf margins, typically occurring when transpiration rates are low and root pressure is high.

**Q1144.** Which of the following is a primary way plants regulate their temperature?
**Answer:** Similar to sweating in animals, the evaporation of water during transpiration dissipates heat from the leaf surface, effectively cooling the plant.

**Q1145.** Plants store many waste products, such as oxalic acid crystals, in which cellular component to prevent harm?
**Answer:** Plant cells often isolate and store various metabolic waste products, including toxic substances and crystals (like calcium oxalate), within their large central vacuoles, effectively compartmentalizing them away from active metabolism.

**Q1146.** Which adaptation helps xerophytes (plants in dry environments) to reduce water loss?
**Answer:** Sunken stomata create a humid microenvironment that reduces the water potential gradient between the leaf and the outside air, while thick cuticles provide a waxy, impermeable barrier, both significantly reducing water loss.

**Q1147.** The closing of stomata on hot, dry days is an example of a plant's response to maintain:
**Answer:** Stomatal closure in response to dry conditions is a critical homeostatic mechanism that allows the plant to conserve water, even at the cost of reduced photosynthesis.

**Q1148.** In deciduous trees, the shedding of leaves in autumn can be considered a mechanism for:
**Answer:** Many plants accumulate metabolic waste products, sometimes in high concentrations, within their leaves. By shedding leaves seasonally, deciduous trees effectively remove these accumulated wastes from their system.

**Q1149.** In herbaceous plants, the primary means of providing mechanical support is:
**Answer:** Turgor pressure, the pressure exerted by the cell contents against the cell wall, keeps herbaceous plant cells firm and provides rigidity, preventing wilting.

**Q1150.** The rigid outer layer of plant cells that provides structural support and protection is the:
**Answer:** The plant cell wall, primarily composed of cellulose, is a strong, rigid layer outside the cell membrane that provides structural support, maintains cell shape, and protects the cell.

**Q1151.** Which plant tissue, characterized by highly lignified cell walls, provides significant mechanical strength and support to the plant body?
**Answer:** Sclerenchyma tissue is composed of cells with thick, lignified secondary walls that provide strong structural support, particularly in mature plant parts.

**Q1152.** The dead, hollow cells with lignified walls that contribute to both water transport and mechanical support in plants are primarily found in:
**Answer:** Xylem tissue contains vessel elements and tracheids, which are dead at maturity, hollow, and have lignified walls. These features enable them to transport water efficiently and provide substantial structural support to the plant.

**Q1153.** Loss of turgor pressure in a plant cell will most directly lead to:
**Answer:** When plant cells lose water, their turgor pressure drops, and they become flaccid. This reduction in rigidity causes the non-woody parts of the plant, like leaves and herbaceous stems, to droop or wilt.

**Q1154.** The primary meristematic tissue responsible for increasing the length of a plant's roots and shoots is the:
**Answer:** Apical meristems are located at the tips of roots and shoots and are responsible for primary growth, which is the increase in length of the plant body.

**Q1155.** Which type of growth results in an increase in the girth or thickness of a plant stem or root?
**Answer:** Secondary growth, characteristic of woody plants, leads to an increase in diameter and is facilitated by lateral meristems such as the vascular cambium and cork cambium.

**Q1156.** The vascular cambium is a type of lateral meristem responsible for producing:
**Answer:** The vascular cambium is a cylindrical meristem that produces secondary xylem (wood) to the inside and secondary phloem to the outside, contributing to the thickening of stems and roots.

**Q1157.** Which tissue system is produced by the cork cambium during secondary growth?
**Answer:** The cork cambium (phellogen) is a lateral meristem that produces the periderm, which replaces the epidermis as the protective outer layer in plants undergoing secondary growth. The periderm includes cork (phellem) and phelloderm.

**Q1158.** Primary growth in plants occurs at:
**Answer:** Primary growth, which involves elongation, occurs at the apical meristems found at both the tips of the roots and the tips of the shoots (stems).

**Q1159.** Growth rings in tree stems are formed due to the seasonal activity of the:
**Answer:** The vascular cambium's activity fluctuates with seasons, producing larger, thin-walled xylem cells in spring wood and smaller, thick-walled xylem cells in summer wood, leading to the formation of visible annual growth rings.

**Q1160.** The irreversible increase in the size of a plant organ or the entire plant is defined as:
**Answer:** Growth in plants refers to an irreversible increase in size, typically involving an increase in cell number, cell size, and accumulation of dry mass.

**Q1161.** The process by which cells become specialized in structure and function is called:
**Answer:** Differentiation is the process where unspecialized cells (e.g., from meristems) develop into specialized cell types with distinct structures and functions (e.g., xylem, phloem, epidermal cells).

**Q1162.** The sum of all changes that an organism undergoes from its origin to its maturity, including growth and differentiation, is termed:
**Answer:** Development in plants encompasses all the progressive and irreversible changes that occur during its life cycle, from germination through maturity and reproduction, including growth, differentiation, and various responses to environmental cues.

**Q1163.** Which of the following is an example of a developmental process in plants, rather than solely growth?
**Answer:** While growth is involved, the transformation of a vegetative bud into a reproductive structure like a flower involves complex changes in gene expression and cell differentiation, which falls under the broader concept of development. The other options are primarily examples of growth.

**Q1164.** Which plant hormone is primarily responsible for promoting cell elongation in shoots and plays a key role in phototropism and gravitropism?
**Answer:** Auxins are a class of plant hormones that promote cell elongation, particularly in shoots, and are crucial for various growth responses like phototropism (growth towards light) and gravitropism (growth in response to gravity).

**Q1165.** Which plant hormone is known for breaking seed dormancy and promoting stem elongation and fruit development?
**Answer:** Gibberellins are plant hormones that play significant roles in breaking dormancy in seeds and buds, promoting stem elongation, and influencing fruit development and flowering.

**Q1166.** Which plant hormone primarily promotes cell division and differentiation, often found in actively growing tissues like roots and embryos?
**Answer:** Cytokinins are a class of plant hormones that stimulate cell division (cytokinesis) and differentiation, working in conjunction with auxins to regulate various aspects of plant growth and development.

**Q1167.** The plant hormone often associated with stress responses, dormancy, and stomatal closure is:
**Answer:** Abscisic Acid (ABA) is a plant hormone that primarily acts as a growth inhibitor, playing crucial roles in initiating and maintaining dormancy in seeds and buds, and promoting stomatal closure under water stress.

**Q1168.** Which gaseous plant hormone promotes fruit ripening and senescence in plants?
**Answer:** Ethylene is a unique plant hormone that exists as a gas. It is well-known for its role in promoting fruit ripening, leaf abscission (shedding), and senescence (aging).

**Q1169.** The apical dominance, where the growth of lateral buds is inhibited by the apical bud, is primarily maintained by which hormone produced in the shoot tip?
**Answer:** Auxin produced by the apical meristem in the shoot tip is responsible for maintaining apical dominance, suppressing the growth of lateral (axillary) buds.

**Q1170.** The growth response of a plant part towards or away from a light source is called:
**Answer:** Phototropism is the directional growth of a plant organ in response to a light stimulus, typically seen as shoots growing towards light and roots growing away from it.

**Q1171.** The positive gravitropic response of roots ensures that they grow:
**Answer:** Gravitropism (also called geotropism) is growth in response to gravity. Roots exhibit positive gravitropism, meaning they grow downwards, anchoring the plant and seeking water and nutrients.

**Q1172.** A plant that flowers only when the day length is shorter than a critical period is known as a:
**Answer:** Short-day plants (SDP) initiate flowering when the night period is longer than a critical length, meaning the day length is shorter than a critical period.

**Q1173.** The requirement of a cold period to stimulate flowering in some plants is known as:
**Answer:** Vernalization is the process by which a plant's flowering is promoted or accelerated by exposure to a period of low temperatures (cold treatment), often required for temperate zone plants.

**Q1174.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)
