### Reproduction (48 Questions)

**Q1175.** What is the primary significance of reproduction for a species?
 **A.** Ensures individual survival.
 **B.** Enables growth and development.
 **C.** Maintains homeostasis within the organism.
 **D.** Prevents the species from ceasing to exist. ✓
**Answer:** Prevents the species from ceasing to exist.

**Q1176.** Which of the following is a unique characteristic of the human reproductive system compared to other body systems?
 **A.** It is functional at birth.
 **B.** It maintains constant functionality throughout life.
 **C.** It exhibits minimal differences between males and females.
 **D.** It becomes functional at puberty. ✓
**Answer:** It becomes functional at puberty.

**Q1177.** In terms of structural differences, how does the reproductive system compare to other organ systems in males and females?
 **A.** All organ systems show significant differences between sexes.
 **B.** Only the digestive system shows major differences between sexes.
 **C.** All organ systems are largely identical in males and females.
 **D.** The reproductive system is quite different, while others show slight differences. ✓
**Answer:** The reproductive system is quite different, while others show slight differences.

**Q1178.** A single cell undergoes meiosis. How many daughter cells are produced, and what is their ploidy level?
 **A.** Two diploid cells.
 **B.** Four diploid cells.
 **C.** Two haploid cells.
 **D.** Four haploid cells. ✓
**Answer:** Four haploid cells.

**Q1179.** Which of the following events characterizes Prophase I of meiosis?
 **A.** Sister chromatids separate.
 **B.** Chromosome pairs line up at the equator.
 **C.** DNA is copied.
 **D.** Homologous chromosomes pair up and may exchange DNA. ✓
**Answer:** Homologous chromosomes pair up and may exchange DNA.

**Q1180.** During which stage of meiosis do homologous chromosome pairs align at the center of the cell?
 **A.** Prophase I
 **B.** Anaphase I
 **C.** Telophase I
 **D.** Metaphase I ✓
**Answer:** Metaphase I

**Q1181.** What crucial event distinguishes Anaphase I of meiosis from Anaphase of mitosis?
 **A.** Sister chromatids separate.
 **B.** Chromosomes condense.
 **C.** Nuclear membrane reforms.
 **D.** Homologous chromosomes are pulled apart. ✓
**Answer:** Homologous chromosomes are pulled apart.

**Q1182.** What is the final outcome of meiosis in males?
 **A.** One egg cell and three polar bodies.
 **B.** Four genetically identical sperm cells.
 **C.** Two diploid spermatids.
 **D.** Four haploid sperm cells. ✓
**Answer:** Four haploid sperm cells.

**Q1183.** Which hormone, released from the hypothalamus, controls the release of anterior pituitary gonadotropins?
 **A.** FSH
 **B.** LH
 **C.** Testosterone
 **D.** GnRH ✓
**Answer:** GnRH

**Q1184.** What is the primary role of Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) in male reproduction?
 **A.** Stimulates Leydig cells to release testosterone.
 **B.** Inhibits sperm production.
 **C.** Causes growth of germinal epithelium.
 **D.** Stimulates Sertoli cells to complete sperm development. ✓
**Answer:** Stimulates Sertoli cells to complete sperm development.

**Q1185.** Luteinizing Hormone (LH) in males primarily stimulates which cells to release testosterone?
 **A.** Sertoli cells
 **B.** Germinal epithelial cells
 **C.** Spermatogonia
 **D.** Leydig cells ✓
**Answer:** Leydig cells

**Q1186.** Which hormone, produced by Sertoli cells, helps to control the rate of spermatogenesis?
 **A.** Testosterone
 **B.** LH
 **C.** GnRH
 **D.** Inhibin ✓
**Answer:** Inhibin

**Q1187.** How does testosterone primarily influence LH and FSH production in the anterior pituitary?
 **A.** It stimulates their production.
 **B.** It has no effect on their production.
 **C.** It directly releases them.
 **D.** It inhibits their production. ✓
**Answer:** It inhibits their production.

**Q1188.** The female reproductive cycle is primarily divided into which two phases?
 **A.** Ovulation and Menstruation.
 **B.** Follicular phase and Luteal phase.
 **C.** Proliferative phase and Secretory phase.
 **D.** Ovarian cycle and Uterine cycle. ✓
**Answer:** Ovarian cycle and Uterine cycle.

**Q1189.** What is the name of the process where the ripening follicle ejects its oocyte from the ovary each month?
 **A.** Oogenesis
 **B.** Fertilization
 **C.** Menstruation
 **D.** Ovulation ✓
**Answer:** Ovulation

**Q1190.** What structure is formed from the ruptured follicle after ovulation?
 **A.** Primordial follicle
 **B.** Mature follicle
 **C.** Secondary oocyte
 **D.** Corpus luteum ✓
**Answer:** Corpus luteum

**Q1191.** At what stage is the secondary oocyte arrested before ovulation?
 **A.** Prophase I
 **B.** Metaphase I
 **C.** Prophase II
 **D.** Metaphase II ✓
**Answer:** Metaphase II

**Q1192.** What happens to an ovulated secondary oocyte if it is not penetrated by sperm?
 **A.** It completes meiosis II and becomes an ovum.
 **B.** It is implanted into the uterus.
 **C.** It transforms into a corpus luteum.
 **D.** It simply deteriorates. ✓
**Answer:** It simply deteriorates.

**Q1193.** What is the primary function of the male reproductive system?
 **A.** Produce and maintain female gametes.
 **B.** Regulate body temperature.
 **C.** Produce digestive enzymes.
 **D.** Produce and maintain sperms. ✓
**Answer:** Produce and maintain sperms.

**Q1194.** Which of the following is NOT an accessory duct of the male reproductive system?
 **A.** Epididymis
 **B.** Rete testis
 **C.** Vas deferens
 **D.** Seminiferous tubule ✓
**Answer:** Seminiferous tubule

**Q1195.** Why are the testes located outside the abdominal cavity in the scrotum?
 **A.** To protect them from injury.
 **B.** To facilitate blood circulation.
 **C.** To allow for easier sperm release.
 **D.** To maintain a lower temperature. ✓
**Answer:** To maintain a lower temperature.

**Q1196.** Where does spermatogenesis primarily take place within the testes?
 **A.** Epididymis
 **B.** Rete testis
 **C.** Ductus deferens
 **D.** Seminiferous tubules ✓
**Answer:** Seminiferous tubules

**Q1197.** Which structure serves as the site for sperm maturation and storage?
 **A.** Vas deferens
 **B.** Ejaculatory duct
 **C.** Urethra
 **D.** Epididymis ✓
**Answer:** Epididymis

**Q1198.** What is the approximate daily production rate of sperms in a healthy adult male?
 **A.** 50 million
 **B.** 100 million
 **C.** 200 million
 **D.** 400 million ✓
**Answer:** 400 million

**Q1199.** Which accessory gland contributes to semen by forming the ejaculatory duct with the ductus deferens?
 **A.** Prostate gland
 **B.** Bulbourethral gland
 **C.** Cowper's gland
 **D.** Seminal vesicle ✓
**Answer:** Seminal vesicle

**Q1200.** Which gland encircles the urethra just inferior to the bladder in males?
 **A.** Seminal vesicle
 **B.** Bulbourethral gland
 **C.** Epididymis
 **D.** Prostate gland ✓
**Answer:** Prostate gland

**Q1201.** What is the role of Sertoli cells in spermatogenesis?
 **A.** Produce testosterone.
 **B.** Store mature sperms.
 **C.** Initiate meiosis I.
 **D.** Nourish the spermatids. ✓
**Answer:** Nourish the spermatids.

**Q1202.** What is the typical volume of semen ejaculated during intercourse?
 **A.** 0.5 - 1 ml
 **B.** 1 - 2 ml
 **C.** 5 - 10 ml
 **D.** 2 - 5 ml ✓
**Answer:** 2 - 5 ml

**Q1203.** Which part of the male reproductive system is also known as the urinogenital duct?
 **A.** Vas deferens
 **B.** Ejaculatory duct
 **C.** Epididymis
 **D.** Urethra ✓
**Answer:** Urethra

**Q1204.** The copulatory organ of the male reproductive system is the:
 **A.** Scrotum
 **B.** Testis
 **C.** Epididymis
 **D.** Penis ✓
**Answer:** Penis

**Q1205.** Which of the following is a component of semen besides sperms?
 **A.** Blood plasma
 **B.** Gastric juice
 **C.** Lymph fluid
 **D.** Secretions from accessory glands ✓
**Answer:** Secretions from accessory glands

**Q1206.** What process involves the elongation, shedding of excess cytoplasm, and formation of a tail in spermatids?
 **A.** Spermatogenesis
 **B.** Spermiation
 **C.** Meiosis
 **D.** Spermiogenesis ✓
**Answer:** Spermiogenesis

**Q1207.** What is the correct sequence of sperm passage through the accessory ducts after leaving the seminiferous tubules?
 **A.** Epididymis -> Rete testis -> Vas deferens -> Urethra
 **B.** Vas deferens -> Epididymis -> Rete testis -> Urethra
 **C.** Efferent ductules -> Vas deferens -> Rete testis -> Epididymis
 **D.** Rete testis -> Efferent ductules -> Epididymis -> Vas deferens ✓
**Answer:** Rete testis -> Efferent ductules -> Epididymis -> Vas deferens

**Q1208.** Which of the following is NOT a main component of the female reproductive system?
 **A.** Ovaries
 **B.** Oviducts
 **C.** Uterus
 **D.** Prostate gland ✓
**Answer:** Prostate gland

**Q1209.** What is the primary function of the ovaries?
 **A.** To transport the ovum to the uterus.
 **B.** To serve as the site of fertilization.
 **C.** To receive and nourish a fertilized ovum.
 **D.** To produce ova and release hormones. ✓
**Answer:** To produce ova and release hormones.

**Q1210.** What is contained within each ovarian follicle?
 **A.** A mature ovum
 **B.** A corpus luteum
 **C.** Sperms
 **D.** An immature egg ✓
**Answer:** An immature egg

**Q1211.** Where does fertilization typically occur in the female reproductive system?
 **A.** Uterus
 **B.** Vagina
 **C.** Ovary
 **D.** Oviducts ✓
**Answer:** Oviducts

**Q1212.** Which part of the uterus is the narrow opening leading into the vagina?
 **A.** Fundus
 **B.** Body
 **C.** Endometrium
 **D.** Cervix ✓
**Answer:** Cervix

**Q1213.** What is the outermost layer of the uterine wall?
 **A.** Myometrium
 **B.** Endometrium
 **C.** Epithelium
 **D.** Perimetrium ✓
**Answer:** Perimetrium

**Q1214.** Which layer of the uterus is responsible for rhythmic contractions during childbirth?
 **A.** Perimetrium
 **B.** Endometrium
 **C.** Cervix
 **D.** Myometrium ✓
**Answer:** Myometrium

**Q1215.** What is the primary function of the endometrium?
 **A.** To produce hormones.
 **B.** To transport the ovum.
 **C.** To prevent sperm entry.
 **D.** To serve as the site of embryo implantation. ✓
**Answer:** To serve as the site of embryo implantation.

**Q1216.** What is another name for the vagina?
 **A.** Uterine tube
 **B.** Womb
 **C.** Ovarian tube
 **D.** Birth canal ✓
**Answer:** Birth canal

**Q1217.** At what stage is the primary oocyte arrested during oogenesis until puberty?
 **A.** Metaphase I
 **B.** Anaphase II
 **C.** Telophase II
 **D.** Prophase I ✓
**Answer:** Prophase I

**Q1218.** What is the significance of the unequal cytoplasmic division during oogenesis?
 **A.** To produce four equally sized ova.
 **B.** To facilitate sperm penetration.
 **C.** To reduce the chromosome number equally.
 **D.** To ensure the fertilized egg has ample nutrients. ✓
**Answer:** To ensure the fertilized egg has ample nutrients.

**Q1219.** When does a secondary oocyte complete meiosis II?
 **A.** Before ovulation.
 **B.** At birth.
 **C.** During puberty.
 **D.** Only if penetrated by sperm. ✓
**Answer:** Only if penetrated by sperm.

**Q1220.** What happens to the first and second polar bodies produced during oogenesis?
 **A.** They develop into ova.
 **B.** They participate in fertilization.
 **C.** They fuse to form a new cell.
 **D.** They degenerate and die. ✓
**Answer:** They degenerate and die.

**Q1221.** Which of the following is NOT a main responsibility of the female reproductive system?
 **A.** Produce female gametes.
 **B.** Nurture a developing embryo.
 **C.** Provide a site for fertilization.
 **D.** Produce seminal fluid. ✓
**Answer:** Produce seminal fluid.

### Respiration (119 Questions)

**Q1223.** What is the primary characteristic of cellular respiration?
 **A.** Uptake of oxygen from the environment
 **B.** Exhaling of carbon dioxide into the environment
 **C.** Exchange of gases in capillaries
 **D.** Breakdown of complex organic compounds to release energy ✓
**Answer:** Breakdown of complex organic compounds to release energy

**Q1224.** Which of the following processes are collectively referred to as cellular respiration?
 **A.** Breathing and gas exchange
 **B.** Inhaling and exhaling
 **C.** Oxygen uptake and carbon dioxide disposal
 **D.** Glycolysis, Krebs cycle, and electron transport chain ✓
**Answer:** Glycolysis, Krebs cycle, and electron transport chain

**Q1225.** What does the term "external respiration" refer to?
 **A.** Energy production within cells
 **B.** Breakdown of glucose molecules
 **C.** Transport of gases by blood
 **D.** Uptake of oxygen from the environment and disposal of carbon dioxide ✓
**Answer:** Uptake of oxygen from the environment and disposal of carbon dioxide

**Q1226.** Which of the following is NOT a characteristic of cellular respiration?
 **A.** It is a catabolic process.
 **B.** It occurs at the cellular level.
 **C.** It releases energy.
 **D.** It involves the inhaling of oxygen. ✓
**Answer:** It involves the inhaling of oxygen.

**Q1227.** Which property is essential for a respiratory surface to allow effective gas diffusion?
 **A.** It must be thick.
 **B.** It must be dry.
 **C.** It must have a small surface area.
 **D.** It must be permeable. ✓
**Answer:** It must be permeable.

**Q1228.** Why must a respiratory surface be thin for efficient diffusion?
 **A.** To increase its surface area.
 **B.** To prevent water loss.
 **C.** To ensure a good blood supply.
 **D.** Diffusion is only efficient over short distances. ✓
**Answer:** Diffusion is only efficient over short distances.

**Q1229.** What is the significance of a large surface area for a respiratory surface?
 **A.** It reduces the need for ventilation.
 **B.** It prevents the entry of foreign particles.
 **C.** It helps to maintain its structural support.
 **D.** It allows sufficient amounts of gases to be exchanged. ✓
**Answer:** It allows sufficient amounts of gases to be exchanged.

**Q1230.** What is the role of a good ventilation mechanism across the respiratory surface?
 **A.** To keep the surface moist.
 **B.** To provide structural support.
 **C.** To filter the air.
 **D.** To maintain a steep diffusion gradient. ✓
**Answer:** To maintain a steep diffusion gradient.

**Q1231.** Why is it advantageous for the respiratory surface to be located internally in air-breathing vertebrates?
 **A.** To increase the surface area for gas exchange.
 **B.** To protect it from physical damage.
 **C.** To enhance the blood supply.
 **D.** To prevent the surface from losing water to the atmosphere. ✓
**Answer:** To prevent the surface from losing water to the atmosphere.

**Q1232.** Air becomes saturated with water vapor before reaching the respiratory surface due to its passage through a branched tubular way. What is the benefit of this?
 **A.** It helps in filtering foreign particles.
 **B.** It warms the air to body temperature.
 **C.** It increases the oxygen concentration.
 **D.** It prevents the respiratory surface from becoming too dry. ✓
**Answer:** It prevents the respiratory surface from becoming too dry.

**Q1233.** Diffusion of gases across the respiratory surface is most effective over a distance of:
 **A.** 5 mm or less
 **B.** 10 mm or less
 **C.** 2 mm or less
 **D.** 1 mm or less ✓
**Answer:** 1 mm or less

**Q1234.** Which of the following is NOT a property of an ideal respiratory surface?
 **A.** Moist and permeable
 **B.** Large surface area
 **C.** Good blood supply
 **D.** Thick and dry ✓
**Answer:** Thick and dry

**Q1235.** The structural support of a respiratory surface ensures that:
 **A.** It can expand and contract easily.
 **B.** It can filter incoming air.
 **C.** It is well-ventilated.
 **D.** It remains open and does not collapse. ✓
**Answer:** It remains open and does not collapse.

**Q1236.** The main function of the hair and mucus lining the vestibules of the nasal cavity is:
 **A.** To detect odors.
 **B.** To warm the inhaled air.
 **C.** To moisten the air.
 **D.** To filter out dust and foreign particles. ✓
**Answer:** To filter out dust and foreign particles.

**Q1237.** Which of the following is responsible for warming the inhaled air as it passes through the nasal cavity?
 **A.** Nasal septum
 **B.** Olfactory receptors
 **C.** Blood capillaries ✓
**Answer:** Blood capillaries

**Q1238.** The nasal cavity is lined with a ciliated mucous membrane primarily for:
 **A.** Detecting smells.
 **B.** Adding moisture to the air.
 **C.** Providing structural support to the nose.
 **D.** Propelling trapped particles towards the pharynx. ✓
**Answer:** Propelling trapped particles towards the pharynx.

**Q1239.** The part of the pharynx that connects with the nasal cavity is the:
 **A.** Oropharynx
 **B.** Laryngopharynx
 **C.** Epiglottis
 **D.** Nasopharynx ✓
**Answer:** Nasopharynx

**Q1240.** The pharynx serves as a passageway for both:
 **A.** Air and blood
 **B.** Food and water
 **C.** Blood and lymph
 **D.** Air and food ✓
**Answer:** Air and food

**Q1241.** Which of the following structures is commonly known as the voice box?
 **A.** Pharynx
 **B.** Trachea
 **C.** Bronchi
 **D.** Larynx ✓
**Answer:** Larynx

**Q1242.** The opening of the larynx is called the:
 **A.** Epiglottis
 **B.** Trachea
 **C.** Pharynx
 **D.** Glottis ✓
**Answer:** Glottis

**Q1243.** What is the primary function of the epiglottis during swallowing?
 **A.** To produce sound.
 **B.** To warm the air.
 **C.** To filter foreign particles from the air.
 **D.** To prevent food and liquid from entering the trachea. ✓
**Answer:** To prevent food and liquid from entering the trachea.

**Q1244.** The vocal cords are located within the:
 **A.** Pharynx
 **B.** Nasal cavity
 **C.** Trachea
 **D.** Larynx ✓
**Answer:** Larynx

**Q1245.** Which part of the nasal cavity contains hair that traps large dust particles?
 **A.** Olfactory region
 **B.** Respiratory region
 **C.** Nasal conchae
 **D.** Vestibules ✓
**Answer:** Vestibules

**Q1246.** The sense of smell is primarily associated with which part of the upper respiratory tract?
 **A.** Pharynx
 **B.** Larynx
 **C.** Trachea
 **D.** Nasal cavity ✓
**Answer:** Nasal cavity

**Q1247.** Which of the following is NOT a division of the pharynx?
 **A.** Nasopharynx
 **B.** Oropharynx
 **C.** Laryngopharynx
 **D.** Tracheopharynx ✓
**Answer:** Tracheopharynx

**Q1248.** What is the main characteristic of the nasal mucous membrane that helps in moistening the air?
 **A.** Presence of vocal cords
 **B.** Rich blood supply
 **C.** Presence of cartilage
 **D.** Secretion of mucus ✓
**Answer:** Secretion of mucus

**Q1249.** The structure that prevents food from entering the trachea during swallowing is part of the:
 **A.** Pharynx
 **B.** Nasal cavity
 **C.** Oral cavity
 **D.** Larynx ✓
**Answer:** Larynx

**Q1250.** Which of the following statements about the nasal cavity's function is INCORRECT?
 **A.** It filters inhaled air.
 **B.** It moistens inhaled air.
 **C.** It warms inhaled air.
 **D.** It cools inhaled air. ✓
**Answer:** It cools inhaled air.

**Q1251.** The primary cartilage that forms the Adam's apple and is part of the larynx is the:
 **A.** Epiglottis
 **B.** Arytenoid cartilage
 **C.** Cricoid cartilage
 **D.** Thyroid cartilage ✓
**Answer:** Thyroid cartilage

**Q1252.** If the epiglottis fails to function correctly, what might be the consequence?
 **A.** Difficulty in speaking
 **B.** Air entering the esophagus
 **C.** Impaired sense of smell
 **D.** Food entering the trachea ✓
**Answer:** Food entering the trachea

**Q1253.** Which part of the pharynx serves as a passageway only for air?
 **A.** Oropharynx
 **B.** Laryngopharynx
 **C.** All parts serve both
 **D.** Nasopharynx ✓
**Answer:** Nasopharynx

**Q1254.** The two bands of smooth muscles within the larynx that vibrate to produce sound are known as:
 **A.** Ligaments
 **B.** Cartilages
 **C.** Tracheal rings
 **D.** Vocal cords ✓
**Answer:** Vocal cords

**Q1255.** Which feature of the nasal cavity helps in trapping fine dust particles and microorganisms?
 **A.** The presence of vibrissae (hairs)
 **B.** The rich blood supply
 **C.** Its connection to the pharynx
 **D.** The ciliated mucous membrane ✓
**Answer:** The ciliated mucous membrane

**Q1256.** The trachea is commonly known as the:
 **A.** Voice box
 **B.** Food pipe
 **C.** Bronchial tree
 **D.** Windpipe ✓
**Answer:** Windpipe

**Q1257.** What is the primary function of the C-shaped cartilaginous rings in the trachea?
 **A.** To facilitate gas exchange.
 **B.** To warm the inhaled air.
 **C.** To produce mucus.
 **D.** To prevent its collapse. ✓
**Answer:** To prevent its collapse.

**Q1258.** The inner lining of the trachea is composed of:
 **A.** Smooth muscle tissue.
 **B.** Squamous epithelium.
 **C.** Cartilaginous tissue.
 **D.** Ciliated mucous membrane. ✓
**Answer:** Ciliated mucous membrane.

**Q1259.** The trachea divides into two primary branches called the:
 **A.** Bronchioles
 **B.** Alveolar ducts
 **C.** Terminal bronchioles
 **D.** Primary bronchi ✓
**Answer:** Primary bronchi

**Q1260.** How many secondary bronchi typically branch off to supply the right lung?
 **A.** Two
 **B.** Four
 **C.** Five
 **D.** Three ✓
**Answer:** Three

**Q1261.** Which of the following respiratory structures lacks cartilage in its walls?
 **A.** Trachea
 **B.** Primary bronchi
 **C.** Secondary bronchi
 **D.** Bronchioles ✓
**Answer:** Bronchioles

**Q1262.** The smallest branches of the bronchi that lead to alveolar ducts are called:
 **A.** Tertiary bronchi
 **B.** Segmental bronchi

**Q1263.** The tiny air-filled sacs where gas exchange occurs in the lungs are called:
 **A.** Bronchioles
 **B.** Alveolar ducts
 **C.** Tracheal rings

**Answer:** Alveoli

**Q1264.** Approximately how many alveoli are present in the human lungs?
 **A.** 50 million
 **B.** 200 million
 **C.** 1 billion
 **D.** 700 million ✓
**Answer:** 700 million

**Q1265.** What is the approximate thickness of the alveolar walls, crucial for efficient gas exchange?
 **A.** 1 µm
 **B.** 10 µm
 **C.** 0.5 mm
 **D.** 0.1 µm ✓
**Answer:** 0.1 µm

**Q1266.** The substance that lines the alveoli and lowers surface tension, preventing their collapse, is:
 **A.** Mucus
 **B.** Plasma
 **C.** Lymph
 **D.** Surfactant ✓
**Answer:** Surfactant

**Q1267.** The principal organs of the respiratory system are the:
 **A.** Trachea and bronchi
 **B.** Larynx and pharynx
 **C.** Diaphragm and intercostal muscles
 **D.** Lungs ✓
**Answer:** Lungs

**Q1268.** How many lobes does the left human lung typically have?
 **A.** One
 **B.** Three
 **C.** Four
 **D.** Two ✓
**Answer:** Two

**Q1269.** The right human lung typically has how many lobes?
 **A.** Two
 **B.** Four
 **C.** Five
 **D.** Three ✓
**Answer:** Three

**Q1270.** The double-layered membrane covering the lungs is called the:
 **A.** Pericardium
 **B.** Peritoneum
 **C.** Meninges
 **D.** Pleura ✓
**Answer:** Pleura

**Q1271.** The space between the visceral and parietal pleura is known as the:
 **A.** Mediastinum
 **B.** Pericardial cavity
 **C.** Alveolar space
 **D.** Pleural cavity ✓
**Answer:** Pleural cavity

**Q1272.** What is the function of the fluid within the pleural cavity?
 **A.** To facilitate gas exchange.
 **B.** To produce surfactant.
 **C.** To warm the inhaled air.

**Answer:** To lubricate the surfaces.

**Q1273.** The hilum of the lung is the region where:
 **A.** Gas exchange primarily occurs.
 **B.** The lung lobes are separated.
 **C.** Surfactant is produced.
 **D.** Bronchi, blood vessels, and nerves enter and exit the lung. ✓
**Answer:** Bronchi, blood vessels, and nerves enter and exit the lung.

**Q1274.** Which structure forms the walls of the alveolar ducts and leads to the alveoli?
 **A.** Tertiary bronchi
 **B.** Primary bronchi
 **C.** Trachea
 **D.** Terminal bronchioles ✓
**Answer:** Terminal bronchioles

**Q1275.** The dense network of capillaries surrounding each alveolus is crucial for:
 **A.** Maintaining alveolar shape.
 **B.** Secreting mucus.
 **C.** Producing sound.
 **D.** Effective transport of respiratory gases. ✓
**Answer:** Effective transport of respiratory gases.

**Q1276.** The main difference between bronchi and bronchioles is that bronchioles:
 **A.** Are larger in diameter.
 **B.** Have cartilaginous rings.
 **C.** Are directly connected to the trachea.
 **D.** Lack cartilaginous support. ✓
**Answer:** Lack cartilaginous support.

**Q1277.** Which part of the lower respiratory tract is primarily responsible for conducting air to the respiratory zone?
 **A.** Alveoli
 **B.** Alveolar ducts
 **C.** Lungs
 **D.** Bronchi ✓
**Answer:** Bronchi

**Q1278.** The visceral pleura is the layer of the pleura that:
 **A.** Lines the thoracic cavity.
 **B.** Forms the mediastinum.
 **C.** Contains the pleural fluid.
 **D.** Covers the surface of the lungs. ✓
**Answer:** Covers the surface of the lungs.

**Q1279.** The parietal pleura is the layer of the pleura that:
 **A.** Covers the surface of the lungs.
 **B.** Is responsible for gas exchange.
 **C.** Produces surfactant.
 **D.** Lines the inner wall of the thoracic cavity. ✓
**Answer:** Lines the inner wall of the thoracic cavity.

**Q1280.** Which of the following statements about the lungs is TRUE?
 **A.** They are rigid, non-elastic organs.
 **B.** The left lung is typically larger than the right lung.
 **C.** They are directly attached to the ribs.
 **D.** They are spongy due to the presence of millions of alveoli. ✓
**Answer:** They are spongy due to the presence of millions of alveoli.

**Q1281.** The walls of the alveoli are composed of a moist squamous epithelium because:
 **A.** It provides strength and rigidity.
 **B.** It secretes protective enzymes.
 **C.** It filters out impurities from the air.
 **D.** It facilitates rapid diffusion of gases. ✓
**Answer:** It facilitates rapid diffusion of gases.

**Q1282.** What is the consequence if a baby is born with insufficient surfactant in its lungs?
 **A.** The baby will have difficulty forming vocal sounds.
 **B.** The trachea will become blocked.
 **C.** The lungs will be unable to warm inhaled air.
 **D.** The alveoli will tend to collapse, making breathing difficult. ✓
**Answer:** The alveoli will tend to collapse, making breathing difficult.

**Q1283.** The cartilage rings of the trachea are C-shaped rather than complete rings to allow for:
 **A.** Greater flexibility during neck movements.
 **B.** Easier passage of air.
 **C.** More efficient gas exchange.
 **D.** The esophagus to expand into the tracheal space during swallowing. ✓
**Answer:** The esophagus to expand into the tracheal space during swallowing.

**Q1284.** Which structures are responsible for increasing the surface area for gas exchange in the lungs?
 **A.** Trachea and bronchi
 **B.** Bronchioles and alveolar ducts
 **C.** Pleural membranes
 **D.** Alveoli ✓
**Answer:** Alveoli

**Q1285.** The movement of foreign particles trapped in the trachea's lining is primarily due to the action of:
 **A.** Smooth muscle contractions.
 **B.** Cartilage rings.
 **C.** Mucous glands.
 **D.** Cilia. ✓
**Answer:** Cilia.

**Q1286.** Which of the following muscles contracts during normal inspiration?
 **A.** Internal intercostal muscles
 **B.** Abdominal muscles
 **C.** Pectoralis minor
 **D.** Diaphragm ✓
**Answer:** Diaphragm

**Q1287.** What happens to the thoracic cavity's volume during inspiration?
 **A.** It decreases.
 **B.** It remains unchanged.
 **C.** It first decreases, then increases.
 **D.** It increases. ✓
**Answer:** It increases.

**Q1288.** During inspiration, the pressure inside the lungs:
 **A.** Increases above atmospheric pressure.
 **B.** Becomes equal to atmospheric pressure.
 **C.** Fluctuates rapidly.
 **D.** Decreases below atmospheric pressure. ✓
**Answer:** Decreases below atmospheric pressure.

**Q1289.** Normal expiration at rest is generally considered a:
 **A.** Active process
 **B.** Forceful process
 **C.** Muscular contraction process
 **D.** Passive process ✓
**Answer:** Passive process

**Q1290.** Which muscles relax during normal quiet expiration?
 **A.** Internal intercostal muscles and abdominal muscles
 **B.** Sternocleidomastoid muscles
 **C.** Pectoralis major muscles
 **D.** Diaphragm and external intercostal muscles ✓
**Answer:** Diaphragm and external intercostal muscles

**Q1291.** What happens to the diaphragm during expiration?
 **A.** It contracts and flattens.
 **B.** It moves upward and forward.
 **C.** It remains stationary.
 **D.** It relaxes and becomes dome-shaped. ✓
**Answer:** It relaxes and becomes dome-shaped.

**Q1292.** Which part of the brain is primarily responsible for initiating and controlling the rhythm of breathing?
 **A.** Cerebellum
 **B.** Cerebrum
 **C.** Pons
 **D.** Medulla oblongata ✓
**Answer:** Medulla oblongata

**Q1293.** The amount of air inhaled or exhaled during a normal, quiet breath is called the:
 **A.** Residual volume
 **B.** Vital capacity
 **C.** Total lung capacity
 **D.** Tidal volume ✓
**Answer:** Tidal volume

**Q1294.** What is the approximate average tidal volume for an adult?
 **A.** 100 mL
 **B.** 1500 mL
 **C.** 3000 mL
 **D.** 500 mL ✓
**Answer:** 500 mL

**Q1295.** The extra volume of air that can be inhaled with maximum effort beyond a normal tidal inhalation is the:
 **A.** Expiratory reserve volume
 **B.** Residual volume
 **C.** Vital capacity
 **D.** Inspiratory reserve volume ✓
**Answer:** Inspiratory reserve volume

**Q1296.** The total lung capacity (TLC) of an average adult is approximately:
 **A.** 2000 mL
 **B.** 3500 mL
 **C.** 4500 mL
 **D.** 6000 mL ✓
**Answer:** 6000 mL

**Q1297.** When the external intercostal muscles contract, the rib cage moves:
 **A.** Downward and inward
 **B.** Sideways only
 **C.** Remains stationary
 **D.** Upward and forward ✓
**Answer:** Upward and forward

**Q1298.** In forced expiration, which muscles contract?
 **A.** Diaphragm and external intercostals
 **B.** Pectoralis major and minor
 **C.** Sternocleidomastoid and scalenes
 **D.** Internal intercostals and abdominal muscles ✓
**Answer:** Internal intercostals and abdominal muscles

**Q1299.** An increase in the pressure inside the lungs causes air to move:
 **A.** Into the lungs
 **B.** Into the blood
 **C.** Out of the blood
 **D.** Out of the lungs ✓
**Answer:** Out of the lungs

**Q1300.** Which of the following describes the correct sequence of events during inspiration?
 **A.** Diaphragm relaxes, thoracic volume decreases, pressure increases, air flows out.
 **B.** Diaphragm contracts, thoracic volume decreases, pressure increases, air flows in.
 **C.** Diaphragm relaxes, thoracic volume increases, pressure decreases, air flows out.
 **D.** Diaphragm contracts, thoracic volume increases, pressure decreases, air flows in. ✓
**Answer:** Diaphragm contracts, thoracic volume increases, pressure decreases, air flows in.

**Q1301.** The respiratory center that controls breathing is primarily located in the:
 **A.** Cerebral cortex
 **B.** Hypothalamus
 **C.** Cerebellum
 **D.** Medulla oblongata ✓
**Answer:** Medulla oblongata

**Q1302.** When the respiratory center sends impulses to the diaphragm and intercostal muscles, it leads to:
 **A.** Relaxation of these muscles.
 **B.** Inhibition of breathing.
 **C.** Forced expiration.
 **D.** Contraction of these muscles. ✓
**Answer:** Contraction of these muscles.

**Q1303.** What is the effect of the relaxation of the inspiratory muscles during normal expiration?
 **A.** Increase in thoracic volume
 **B.** Decrease in thoracic volume
 **C.** Increased lung pressure
 **D.** Both b and c ✓
**Answer:** Both b and c

**Q1304.** The volume of air remaining in the lungs even after a maximal expiratory effort is known as the:
 **A.** Tidal volume
 **B.** Vital capacity
 **C.** Inspiratory reserve volume
 **D.** Residual volume ✓
**Answer:** Residual volume

**Q1305.** A person takes a deep breath in, beyond their normal quiet inhalation. This additional volume is called the:
 **A.** Expiratory reserve volume
 **B.** Tidal volume
 **C.** Functional residual capacity
 **D.** Inspiratory reserve volume ✓
**Answer:** Inspiratory reserve volume

**Q1306.** How is the majority of oxygen transported in the blood?
 **A.** Dissolved directly in blood plasma.
 **B.** As bicarbonate ions.
 **C.** Bound to plasma proteins.
 **D.** Bound to hemoglobin in red blood cells. ✓
**Answer:** Bound to hemoglobin in red blood cells.

**Q1307.** What is the name of the complex formed when oxygen binds to hemoglobin?
 **A.** Carboxyhemoglobin
 **B.** Methemoglobin
 **C.** Deoxyhemoglobin
 **D.** Oxyhemoglobin ✓
**Answer:** Oxyhemoglobin

**Q1308.** In which form is a small amount of oxygen transported in the blood?
 **A.** Bound to white blood cells.
 **B.** As carbaminohemoglobin.
 **C.** Within platelets.
 **D.** Dissolved in blood plasma. ✓
**Answer:** Dissolved in blood plasma.

**Q1309.** What is the approximate partial pressure of oxygen (PO2) in the alveoli, which facilitates oxygen loading onto hemoglobin?
 **A.** 40 mm Hg
 **B.** 70 mm Hg
 **C.** 159 mm Hg
 **D.** 105 mm Hg ✓
**Answer:** 105 mm Hg

**Q1310.** Which of the following factors would cause hemoglobin to release oxygen more readily to the tissues?
 **A.** Increased pH
 **B.** Decreased temperature
 **C.** Increased partial pressure of oxygen
 **D.** Increased carbon dioxide concentration ✓
**Answer:** Increased carbon dioxide concentration

**Q1311.** The Bohr effect describes the phenomenon where:
 **A.** Hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen increases with increasing temperature.
 **B.** Myoglobin stores oxygen in muscles.
 **C.** Carbon dioxide is transported as bicarbonate ions.
 **D.** Hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen decreases with decreasing pH. ✓
**Answer:** Hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen decreases with decreasing pH.

**Q1312.** How does an increase in body temperature affect oxygen transport by hemoglobin?
 **A.** It increases hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen.
 **B.** It has no effect on oxygen affinity.
 **C.** It prevents oxygen from binding to hemoglobin.
 **D.** It decreases hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen. ✓
**Answer:** It decreases hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen.

**Q1313.** What is the color of deoxyhemoglobin?
 **A.** Bright red
 **B.** Green
 **C.** Yellow
 **D.** Dark red ✓
**Answer:** Dark red

**Q1314.** In what form is the largest percentage of carbon dioxide transported in the blood?
 **A.** Dissolved in plasma.
 **B.** Bound to hemoglobin as carbaminohemoglobin.
 **C.** As carbonic acid.
 **D.** As bicarbonate ions. ✓
**Answer:** As bicarbonate ions.

**Q1315.** Carbon dioxide moves from body tissues into the blood because:
 **A.** The partial pressure of CO2 is lower in the tissues than in the blood.
 **B.** Hemoglobin has a stronger affinity for CO2 in the blood.
 **C.** Oxygen is abundant in the tissues.
 **D.** The partial pressure of CO2 is higher in the tissues than in the blood. ✓
**Answer:** The partial pressure of CO2 is higher in the tissues than in the blood.

**Q1316.** Which enzyme facilitates the conversion of carbon dioxide and water into carbonic acid in red blood cells?
 **A.** Amylase
 **B.** Pepsin
 **C.** Lipase
 **D.** Carbonic anhydrase ✓
**Answer:** Carbonic anhydrase

**Q1317.** The transport of carbon dioxide as bicarbonate ions helps to:
 **A.** Increase the pH of the blood.
 **B.** Directly increase oxygen binding to hemoglobin.
 **C.** Reduce the partial pressure of oxygen.
 **D.** Maintain the acid-base balance (pH) of the blood. ✓
**Answer:** Maintain the acid-base balance (pH) of the blood.

**Q1318.** When carbon dioxide binds directly to hemoglobin, it forms:
 **A.** Oxyhemoglobin
 **B.** Deoxyhemoglobin
 **C.** Methemoglobin
 **D.** Carbaminohemoglobin ✓
**Answer:** Carbaminohemoglobin

**Q1319.** Which statement about carbon dioxide transport is TRUE?
 **A.** All CO2 is transported dissolved in plasma.
 **B.** CO2 only binds to hemoglobin when oxygen is absent.
 **C.** CO2 is transported primarily by white blood cells.
 **D.** The majority of CO2 is transported in the form of bicarbonate ions. ✓
**Answer:** The majority of CO2 is transported in the form of bicarbonate ions.

**Q1320.** What is the effect of high CO2 levels on blood pH?
 **A.** Increases pH (becomes more alkaline).
 **B.** Has no effect on pH.
 **C.** Fluctuates pH unpredictably.
 **D.** Decreases pH (becomes more acidic). ✓
**Answer:** Decreases pH (becomes more acidic).

**Q1321.** Where does oxygen primarily unload from hemoglobin to enter the body cells?
 **A.** In the lungs.
 **B.** In the heart chambers.
 **C.** In the pulmonary arteries.
 **D.** In the systemic capillaries. ✓
**Answer:** In the systemic capillaries.

**Q1322.** Which of the following conditions would shift the oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve to the right, favoring oxygen release?
 **A.** Decreased temperature
 **B.** Increased pH
 **C.** Increased partial pressure of O2
 **D.** Increased acidity ✓
**Answer:** Increased acidity

**Q1323.** Why does carbon dioxide move from the blood into the alveoli in the lungs?
 **A.** The partial pressure of CO2 is higher in the alveoli.
 **B.** Hemoglobin has a stronger affinity for CO2 in the lungs.
 **C.** Oxygen is actively transported into the blood.
 **D.** The partial pressure of CO2 is lower in the alveoli. ✓
**Answer:** The partial pressure of CO2 is lower in the alveoli.

**Q1324.** When oxyhemoglobin reaches the tissues, what causes it to release oxygen?
 **A.** Higher partial pressure of oxygen in the tissues.
 **B.** Higher pH in the tissues.
 **C.** Lower temperature in the tissues.
 **D.** Lower partial pressure of oxygen in the tissues. ✓
**Answer:** Lower partial pressure of oxygen in the tissues.

**Q1325.** Which form of carbon dioxide transport plays a significant role in acid-base buffering in the blood?
 **A.** Dissolved CO2
 **B.** Carbaminohemoglobin
 **C.** Carbonic acid directly
 **D.** Bicarbonate ions ✓
**Answer:** Bicarbonate ions

**Q1326.** What is the primary function of hemoglobin in the blood?
 **A.** Transporting carbon dioxide only.
 **B.** Storing oxygen in muscles.
 **C.** Fighting infections.
 **D.** Transporting oxygen from the lungs to the tissues. ✓
**Answer:** Transporting oxygen from the lungs to the tissues.

**Q1327.** A hemoglobin molecule is capable of carrying how many molecules of oxygen (O2)?
 **A.** One
 **B.** Two
 **C.** Three
 **D.** Four ✓
**Answer:** Four

**Q1328.** Which of the following statements best describes myoglobin?
 **A.** It is the primary oxygen transport protein in blood.
 **B.** It has a lower binding affinity for oxygen compared to hemoglobin.
 **C.** It transports carbon dioxide.
 **D.** It is an oxygen-storing protein found in muscle cells. ✓
**Answer:** It is an oxygen-storing protein found in muscle cells.

**Q1329.** How many oxygen molecules can a single myoglobin molecule bind?
 **A.** Two
 **B.** Four
 **C.** One ✓
**Answer:** One

**Q1330.** Compared to hemoglobin, myoglobin has a:
 **A.** Lower binding affinity for oxygen.
 **B.** Similar binding affinity for oxygen.
 **C.** Variable binding affinity for oxygen.
 **D.** Higher binding affinity for oxygen. ✓
**Answer:** Higher binding affinity for oxygen.

**Q1331.** An infection of the sinuses, often characterized by headache and facial pain, is known as:
**Answer:** Otitis Media

**Q1332.** Which of the following is an infection of the middle ear, commonly occurring as a complication of upper respiratory infections?
 **A.** Lung cancer
 **B.** Otitis Media ✓
**Answer:** Otitis Media

**Q1333.** A serious infection that inflames the air sacs in one or both lungs, which may fill with fluid or pus, is called:
 **A.** Emphysema
 **B.** Sinusitis
 **C.** Pneumonia ✓
**Answer:** Pneumonia

**Q1334.** Pulmonary tuberculosis is a chronic bacterial infection primarily affecting the:
 **A.** Pharynx
 **B.** Larynx
 **C.** Nasal cavity
 **D.** Lungs ✓
**Answer:** Lungs

**Q1335.** Which lung disorder is characterized by the destruction of alveolar walls, leading to enlarged air spaces and reduced surface area for gas exchange?
 **A.** Asthma
 **B.** Bronchitis
 **C.** Pneumonia
 **D.** Emphysema ✓
**Answer:** Emphysema

**Q1336.** Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) commonly includes which two conditions?
 **A.** Sinusitis and Otitis Media
 **B.** Pneumonia and Tuberculosis
 **C.** Asthma and allergies
 **D.** Chronic bronchitis and emphysema ✓
**Answer:** Chronic bronchitis and emphysema

**Q1337.** The leading cause of lung cancer is:
**Answer:** Air pollution

**Q1338.** What is a primary effect of smoking on the respiratory system?
 **A.** It increases the number of cilia.
 **B.** It enhances lung elasticity.
 **C.** It increases oxygen absorption capacity.
 **D.** It paralyzes and destroys cilia. ✓
**Answer:** It paralyzes and destroys cilia.

**Q1339.** In mouth-to-mouth artificial respiration (CPR), forcing air into the victim's lungs is done to achieve:
 **A.** Active exhalation
 **B.** Passive inhalation
 **C.** Direct gas exchange in the rescuer's mouth
 **D.** Passive exhalation ✓
**Answer:** Passive exhalation

**Q1340.** Before performing mouth-to-mouth artificial respiration, what is a crucial initial step to ensure effective ventilation?
 **A.** Administering chest compressions immediately.
 **B.** Checking the victim's pulse.
 **C.** Applying pressure to the sternum.
 **D.** Tilting the head back and lifting the chin to open the airway. ✓
**Answer:** Tilting the head back and lifting the chin to open the airway.

**Q1341.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

---

### Support And Movement (98 Questions)

**Q1342.** Which of the following best describes the primary need for support in living organisms?
**Answer:** Support in living organisms is necessary to uphold and sustain the body against gravity and other external forces.

**Q1343.** What was a significant evolutionary development that increased the need for greater support in living organisms?
**Answer:** As living organisms increased in size through evolution, the need for support became greater, especially when they colonized land.

**Q1344.** What is the scientific study of bones and cartilage called?
**Answer:** The study of bones and cartilage is called osteology.

**Q1345.** An individual bone is composed of a variety of tissues. Which of the following is NOT typically found in an individual bone?
**Answer:** An individual bone is composed of bone tissue, cartilage, fibrous connective tissue, blood, and nerve tissue.

**Q1346.** In a long bone, the broad terminal parts are called the:
**Answer:** The terminal broad parts of a long bone are called the epiphysis.

**Q1347.** Which part of a long bone is also known as the shaft and contains a central cavity filled with yellow bone marrow?
**Answer:** The middle part along the length of the bone is called the diaphysis or shaft, which contains a central cavity filled by yellow bone marrow.

**Q1348.** The outer connective tissue layer around a bone is known as the:
**Answer:** The outer connective tissue around the bone is called periosteum.

**Q1349.** Which of the following bone structures primarily contains red bone marrow in its spaces?
**Answer:** The red bone marrow is found in the spaces of spongy bone.

**Q1350.** What is the primary function of osteoblasts?
**Answer:** Osteoblasts are bone-forming cells that synthesize and secrete unmineralized ground substance.

**Q1351.** What do osteoblasts become once they are surrounded by the bone matrix?
**Answer:** Once the osteoblasts are surrounded by matrix, they become osteocytes.

**Q1352.** What is a key function of osteocytes in healthy bone tissue?
**Answer:** Osteocytes maintain healthy bone tissue by secreting enzymes and influencing bone mineral content. They also regulate calcium release from bone tissue to blood.

**Q1353.** Which type of bone cell is responsible for bone resorption?
**Answer:** Osteoclasts are bone-destroying cells that perform bone resorption, breaking down bone and depositing calcium and phosphate into the blood.

**Q1354.** The work of osteoclasts is important for which of the following processes?
**Answer:** The work of osteoclasts is important to the growth and repair of bone.

**Q1355.** How does cartilage compare to bone in terms of strength?
**Answer:** Cartilage is not as strong as bone.

**Q1356.** The flexibility of cartilage is primarily due to its matrix being:
**Answer:** Cartilage is more flexible than bone because its matrix is gel-like and contains many collagenous and elastic fibers.

**Q1357.** What is the dense layer of collagen fibers covering the cartilage matrix called?
**Answer:** The cartilage matrix is covered by a dense layer of collagen fibers called perichondrium.

**Q1358.** The living cells of cartilage are known as:
**Answer:** The living cells of cartilage are called chondrocytes.

**Q1359.** How do chondrocytes primarily receive their nutrient supply?
**Answer:** Cartilage does not contain blood vessels, and the chondrocytes are supplied by diffusion.

**Q1360.** Why does cartilage heal very slowly?
**Answer:** Cartilage heals very slowly because it does not contain blood vessels.

**Q1361.** Which type of cartilage is found at the ends of long bones, in the nose, larynx, and trachea?
**Answer:** Hyaline cartilage is found at the ends of long bones and in the nose, at the larynx and trachea.

**Q1362.** Where is fibrocartilage typically found in the adult human body?
**Answer:** Fibrocartilage is found in the disks located between the vertebrae and the cartilage of the knee.

**Q1363.** In comparison to cartilage, collagen fibers in bone are:
**Answer:** collagen in bone is densely packed, while in cartilage it is loosely packed.

**Q1364.** Which of the following cell types is characteristic of cartilage but not bone?
**Answer:** chondrocytes are the cell types found in cartilage, while bone has osteoblasts, osteocytes, and osteoclasts.

**Q1365.** What is a key difference regarding blood vessels between bone and cartilage?
**Answer:** blood vessels are present in bone but absent in cartilage.

**Q1366.** Which substance is deposited in bone but not in cartilage?
**Answer:** bone deposits minerals such as calcium, carbonates, and phosphates, while there is no deposition of minerals in cartilage.

**Q1367.** What is the external covering of bone called, which differs from that of cartilage?
**Answer:** Table 16.1 indicates that bone is covered by periosteum, while cartilage is covered by perichondrium.

**Q1368.** How many bones are typically found in the human skeletal system?
**Answer:** The human skeletal system consists of 206 bones.

**Q1369.** The human skeletal system is primarily divided into two main divisions. What are they?
**Answer:** The human skeletal system is primarily divided into axial skeleton and appendicular skeleton.

**Q1370.** Which of the following is included in the axial skeleton?
**Answer:** Axial skeleton includes skeletal parts along the central axis of the body, such as the skull, vertebral column, and rib cage.

**Q1371.** How many bones are present in the human head?
**Answer:** The head contains 29 bones.

**Q1372.** How many cranial bones form the cranium?
**Answer:** There are 8 cranial bones that form the cranium (brain box).

**Q1373.** Which of the following are paired cranial bones?
**Answer:** Out of the 8 cranial bones, two are paired: parietal bones (left and right) and temporal bones (left and right).

**Q1374.** Which of the following is an unpaired cranial bone?
**Answer:** Four cranial bones are unpaired: frontal bone, occipital bone, ethmoid bone, and sphenoid bone.

**Q1375.** How many facial bones are there in the human skull?
**Answer:** Facial bones are fourteen in number.

**Q1376.** Which of the following is a paired facial bone?
**Answer:** The paired bones of the face include lacrimal, zygomatic, nasal bones, inferior nasal concha, maxilla, and palatine.

**Q1377.** Which of the following is an unpaired facial bone?
**Answer:** The unpaired bones of the face are mandible and vomer.

**Q1378.** The three pairs of middle ear ossicles are:
**Answer:** Three pairs of middle ear ossicles are malleus, incus, and stapes.

**Q1379.** What is a unique characteristic of the hyoid bone?
**Answer:** The hyoid bone is a small single bone that lies at the base of the skull below the tongue and does not articulate with any other bone of the head.

**Q1380.** How many vertebrae typically make up the human vertebral column?
**Answer:** The vertebral column in human beings consists of thirty-three vertebrae.

**Q1381.** How many cervical vertebrae are there in the human vertebral column?
**Answer:** There are 7 cervical vertebrae.

**Q1382.** Which of the following is the first cervical vertebra?
**Answer:** The atlas is the first cervical vertebra.

**Q1383.** Which characteristic is typical of thoracic vertebrae?
**Answer:** Thoracic vertebrae are rib-carrying vertebrae having large spinous processes and are found in the chest region.

**Q1384.** How many lumbar vertebrae are typically found in the human vertebral column?
**Answer:** There are 5 lumbar vertebrae.

**Q1385.** The sacrum is formed by the fusion of how many vertebrae?
**Answer:** Sacral vertebrae are five fused vertebrae forming the sacrum.

**Q1386.** Which bones articulate with the sacrum to form the back of the pelvis?
**Answer:** The sacrum articulates with the iliac bones of the hip bone to form the back of the pelvis.

**Q1387.** How many coccygeal vertebrae are fused in adults to form the coccyx?
**Answer:** Coccygeal vertebrae or coccyx are four vertebrae fused in adults.

**Q1388.** How many pairs of ribs make up the rib cage?
**Answer:** The rib cage consists of twelve pairs of ribs.

**Q1389.** Which ribs attach directly to the sternum?
**Answer:** The seven pairs of ribs that attach directly to the sternum are called true ribs.

**Q1390.** Which rib pairs are classified as false ribs?
**Answer:** The 8th, 9th, and 10th pairs are called false ribs, as these three pairs of ribs are attached to the sternum by means of common costal cartilage.

**Q1391.** Which rib pairs are known as floating ribs?
**Answer:** The 11th and 12th pairs of ribs are known as floating ribs because they do not attach to the sternum.

**Q1392.** The appendicular skeleton includes skeletal parts present in:
**Answer:** The appendicular skeleton includes those skeletal parts which are present in appendages (arms and legs).

**Q1393.** The pectoral girdle consists of a pair of clavicles and a pair of:
**Answer:** Pectoral girdle consists of a pair of clavicles and a pair of scapula.

**Q1394.** Which of the following bones is NOT part of the upper limb (forelimb)?
**Answer:** The upper limb or forelimb consists of humerus, ulna, radius, carpals, metacarpals, and phalanges. Femur is a lower limb bone.

**Q1395.** The spherical head of the humerus fits into which cavity?
**Answer:** The humerus is a long bone, the end of which has a spherical head, which fits into the glenoid cavity.

**Q1396.** The radius bone is located on which side of the forearm?
**Answer:** The radius is a long, outer bone of the forearm (on the thumb side).

**Q1397.** How many short bones make up the carpals, forming the wrist?
**Answer:** Carpals consist of two rows of eight short bones forming the wrist.

**Q1398.** Which bones make up the palm of the hand?
**Answer:** Metacarpals consist of five bones making up the palm of the hand.

**Q1399.** How many phalanges does the human thumb possess, compared to other fingers?
**Answer:** Each finger possesses three phalanges except the thumb which comprises two phalanges.

**Q1400.** The pelvic girdle is made up of three units that form the coxa. These units are the ileum, ischium, and:
**Answer:** The pelvic girdle is made up of three units: the ileum, ischium, and pubis, which form the coxa.

**Q1401.** Which of the following bones is part of the hind limb?
**Answer:** The lower limb or hind limb consists of femur, patella, tibia, fibula, tarsal, metatarsal, and phalanges.

**Q1402.** What is the definition of a joint or articulation?
**Answer:** A joint or articulation is a place where two bones or bone and cartilage come together.

**Q1403.** What is the scientific study of the structure and function of joints called?
**Answer:** The scientific study of the structure and function of joints is called arthrology.

**Q1404.** Which characteristic defines fibrous joints?
**Answer:** In fibrous joints, adjacent bones are directly connected by fibrous connective tissue, and there is no joint cavity between them.

**Q1405.** An example of a fibrous joint is found in the:
**Answer:** Fibrous joints are found between most bones of the skull, called sutures.

**Q1406.** Which statement is true regarding cartilaginous joints?
**Answer:** At a cartilaginous joint, the adjacent bones are united by cartilage, and these types of joints lack a joint cavity.

**Q1407.** What type of movement do cartilaginous joints generally allow?
**Answer:** Cartilaginous joints allow little movement.

**Q1408.** Which of the following is an example of a cartilaginous joint?
**Answer:** Examples of cartilaginous joints include costal cartilages that attach ribs to the sternum, and the pubic symphysis and intervertebral disc.

**Q1409.** What type of movement is characteristic of synovial joints?
**Answer:** Synovial joints are freely moveable joints.

**Q1410.** What is secreted by the inner lining of the synovial membrane within a synovial joint capsule?
**Answer:** The inner lining of the synovial membrane secretes synovial fluid.

**Q1411.** Which of the following is an example of a synovial joint?
**Answer:** Examples of synovial joints include hinge joint, pivot joint, ball-and-socket joint, and gliding joint.

**Q1412.** What is the medical term for joint replacement?
**Answer:** Joint replacement is called arthroplasty.

**Q1413.** Artificial joints are commonly composed of metal in combination with modern:
**Answer:** Artificial joints are usually composed of metal (e.g., stainless steel, titanium alloys) in combination with modern plastics (e.g., high-density polythene, silastic or elastomer).

**Q1414.** How do the skeleton and muscular systems together maintain bipedal posture?
**Answer:** The skeleton and muscular systems maintain bipedal posture, with certain muscles acting as posture muscles working in opposition to gravity, and the pelvis structure also permitting upright posture.

**Q1415.** Each intervertebral disc consists of an inner semifluid part called the nucleus pulposus and an outer ring of fibrocartilage called the:
**Answer:** Each intervertebral disc consists of nucleus pulposus and annulus fibrosus, which is the strong outer ring of fibrocartilage.

**Q1416.** What is the primary function of the nucleus pulposus in an intervertebral disc?
**Answer:** The nucleus pulposus is an inner semifluid which acts as a rubber ball to give a disc its elasticity and compressibility.

**Q1417.** The strong outer ring of fibrocartilage that holds together successive vertebrae is the:
**Answer:** Annulus fibrosus is the strong outer ring of fibrocartilage, which holds together successive vertebrae.

**Q1418.** What is spondylosis characterized by?
**Answer:** Spondylosis is the immobility and fusion of a vertebral joint.

**Q1419.** Sciatica refers to symptoms such as pain, weakness, numbness, or tingling in the leg. What is its direct cause?
**Answer:** Sciatica is caused by injury to or pressure on the sciatic nerve.

**Q1420.** Which of the following is a common cause of sciatica?
**Answer:** Common causes of sciatica include a slipped disc, pelvic injury or fracture, and tumors.

**Q1421.** What are typical symptoms of arthritis?
**Answer:** The typical symptoms of arthritis include pain after walking (which may later occur even at rest), creaking sounds in the joint, and difficulty in getting up from a chair.

**Q1422.** Which type of arthritis is a progressive disease where articular cartilages gradually soften and disintegrate?
**Answer:** Osteoarthritis is a progressive disease in which the articular cartilages gradually soften and disintegrate.

**Q1423.** Osteoarthritis commonly affects which joints?
**Answer:** Osteoarthritis affects the knee, hip, and intervertebral joints.

**Q1424.** What is the underlying cause of rheumatoid arthritis?
**Answer:** Rheumatoid arthritis is the result of an autoimmune disorder in which the synovial membrane becomes inflamed due to a faulty immune system.

**Q1425.** Gouty arthritis results from a metabolic disorder leading to the deposition of which crystals in the joints?
**Answer:** Gouty arthritis results from a metabolic disorder in which sodium urate crystals are deposited in the joints.

**Q1426.** Which joint is most commonly affected by gouty arthritis?
**Answer:** The most common joint affected by gouty arthritis is the joint of the big toe.

**Q1427.** Osteoporosis is characterized by:
**Answer:** Osteoporosis is a common disease characterized by reduced bone mass and an increased risk of fracture.

**Q1428.** Why is adequate calcium intake in teenage years and twenties a preventive action against osteoporosis?
**Answer:** Adequate calcium intake in teenage years and twenties increases peak bone mass, offering greater tolerance for bone loss later in life, because a greater bone mass before the onset of osteoporosis provides more resilience.

**Q1429.** What is the medical term for a broken bone?
**Answer:** A fracture is the medical term for a broken bone.

**Q1430.** Bone fractures occur when the physical force exerted on the bone is:
**Answer:** Fractures occur when the physical force exerted on the bone is stronger than the bone itself.

**Q1431.** In which type of fracture does the skin remain intact, without any penetration by the bone ends?
**Answer:** Simple fracture or closed fractures are those in which the skin is intact.

**Q1432.** What characterizes a compound fracture?
**Answer:** If the bone ends penetrate the skin and form a wound, it is called a compound fracture or open fracture.

**Q1433.** When a fracture damages adjacent internal organs, it is classified as a:
**Answer:** When a fracture damages the adjacent organs, it is called a complicated fracture.

**Q1434.** What is the very first step in the repair process of a simple bone fracture?
**Answer:** The repair process of a simple fracture takes place in four major steps, starting with haematoma or clot formation.

**Q1435.** During haematoma formation at a fracture site, what happens to the blood vessels in the bone and surrounding tissues?
**Answer:** When a bone breaks, blood vessels in the bone, and perhaps in surrounding tissues, are torn and haemorrhage.

**Q1436.** Following the formation of a haematoma at a fracture site, what happens to the bone cells deprived of nutrition?
**Answer:** Soon after haematoma formation, bone cells deprived of nutrition die.

**Q1437.** After a fracture and haematoma formation, the tissue at the fracture site typically becomes:
**Answer:** As a result of the haematoma and dying bone cells, the tissue at the site becomes swollen, painful, and inflamed.

**Q1438.** A mass of clotted blood that forms at a fracture site is specifically called a:
**Answer:** As a result of torn blood vessels and haemorrhage, a haematoma, a mass of clotted blood, forms at the fracture site.

**Q1439.** Want to look through some MCQs for your Upcoming tests, take a look at the following Grammar tests:
**Answer:** [Acellular Life MCQs](https://www.mcquniverse.pk/biology-mcqs/acellular-life-mcqs/)

**Answer:** -OH attached to a carbon bonded to two other carbons.

**Q1444.** According to IUPAC rules, what is the parent hydrocarbon for naming an alcohol?

**Answer:** The longest chain of carbon atoms containing the hydroxyl group.

**Q1447.** When more than one -OH group is attached to a carbon chain, how are they indicated in the IUPAC name?

**Answer:** By appropriate suffix such as diol, triol, etc.

**Q1448.** In unsaturated alcohols, how is the carbon chain numbered according to IUPAC rules?

**Answer:** The hydroxyl group gets the lower number.

 **A.** Ni/Al₂O₃

 **C.** Raney Nickel
 **D.** Palladium chloride catalyst ✓
**Answer:** Palladium chloride catalyst

**Q1532.** Acetone is prepared by the dry distillation of:
 **A.** Calcium formate
 **B.** Calcium propanoate
 **C.** Calcium oxalate
 **D.** Calcium acetate ✓
**Answer:** Calcium acetate

**Q1533.** The industrial method for formaldehyde production uses a temperature of 500 °C, while the laboratory method uses:
 **A.** 100 °C
 **B.** 200 °C
 **C.** 400 °C
 **D.** 300 °C ✓
**Answer:** 300 °C

**Q1534.** In the preparation of formaldehyde in the laboratory, what is used to draw air through methyl alcohol?
 **A.** A condenser
 **B.** A heating mantle
 **C.** A dropping funnel
 **D.** A suction pump ✓
**Answer:** A suction pump

**Q1535.** The reaction: 2CH₃OH + O₂ —Pt-asbestos, 300°C→ 2H−C=O + 2H₂O represents the laboratory preparation of:
 **A.** Acetaldehyde
 **B.** Acetone
 **C.** Ethanol
 **D.** Formaldehyde ✓
**Answer:** Formaldehyde

**Q1536.** The carbonyl group possesses a σ-bond and a π-bond, allowing it to primarily undergo which type of reactions?
 **A.** Elimination reactions
 **B.** Substitution reactions
 **C.** Rearrangement reactions
 **D.** Addition reactions ✓
**Answer:** Addition reactions

**Q1537.** Why is the carbonyl group considered a polar group?
 **A.** Carbon is more electronegative than oxygen.
 **B.** The double bond restricts electron movement.
 **C.** It has a linear geometry.
 **D.** Oxygen tends to attract electrons to itself, creating partial charges. ✓
**Answer:** Oxygen tends to attract electrons to itself, creating partial charges.

**Q1538.** In the carbonyl group (C=O), which atom has a partial positive charge and is electrophilic?
 **A.** Oxygen atom
 **B.** Both carbon and oxygen atoms
 **C.** Neither carbon nor oxygen atom
 **D.** Carbon atom ✓
**Answer:** Carbon atom

**Q1539.** The characteristic reactions of carbonyl compounds are primarily:
 **A.** Electrophilic substitution reactions
 **B.** Free radical addition reactions
 **C.** Acid-base reactions
 **D.** Nucleophilic addition reactions ✓
**Answer:** Nucleophilic addition reactions

**Q1540.** In nucleophilic addition reactions of aldehydes and ketones, which part of the reagent typically combines with the electrophilic carbon of the carbonyl group?
 **A.** The positive part
 **B.** The neutral part
 **C.** Both positive and negative parts simultaneously
 **D.** The negative part ✓
**Answer:** The negative part

**Q1541.** How does a base catalyst affect nucleophilic addition reactions of carbonyl compounds?
 **A.** It decreases the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon.
 **B.** It increases the positive character of the carbonyl carbon.
 **C.** It facilitates the removal of the positive part of the reagent.
 **D.** It increases the nucleophilic character of the reagent. ✓
**Answer:** It increases the nucleophilic character of the reagent.

**Q1542.** How does an acid catalyst promote nucleophilic attack in carbonyl compounds?
 **A.** By generating a strong nucleophile
 **B.** By decreasing the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon
 **C.** By stabilizing the negative charge on oxygen.
 **D.** By increasing the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon atom. ✓
**Answer:** By increasing the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon atom.

**Q1543.** In nucleophilic addition reactions of aldehydes and ketones, the positive part of the reagent, which is usually hydrogen, goes to which atom?
 **A.** The carbon atom of the carbonyl group
 **B.** An adjacent alkyl group
 **C.** The solvent
 **D.** The oxygen atom of the carbonyl group ✓
**Answer:** The oxygen atom of the carbonyl group

**Q1544.** Which statement accurately compares the adduct formed in base-catalysed versus acid-catalysed nucleophilic addition reactions?
 **A.** The adduct is different for each catalyst.
 **B.** Base-catalysed reactions form a more stable adduct.
 **C.** Acid-catalysed reactions form a more stable adduct.
 **D.** The adduct is the same for both catalysts. ✓
**Answer:** The adduct is the same for both catalysts.

**Q1545.** The first step in a base-catalysed nucleophilic addition reaction involves:
 **A.** Protonation of the carbonyl oxygen.
 **B.** Attack of an electrophile on the carbonyl carbon.
 **C.** Formation of a carbocation.
 **D.** The base reacting with the reagent to generate the nucleophile. ✓
**Answer:** The base reacting with the reagent to generate the nucleophile.

**Q1546.** What is the general characteristic reaction of carbonyl compounds like aldehydes and ketones?
 **A.** Electrophilic substitution
 **B.** Free radical addition
 **C.** Elimination
 **D.** Nucleophilic addition ✓
**Answer:** Nucleophilic addition

**Q1547.** In nucleophilic addition reactions, which atom of the carbonyl group is attacked by the nucleophile?
 **A.** Oxygen atom
 **B.** Both carbon and oxygen atoms simultaneously
 **C.** Hydrogen atom (if present)
 **D.** Carbon atom ✓
**Answer:** Carbon atom

**Q1548.** Which of the following describes the role of a base catalyst in nucleophilic addition reactions?
 **A.** It protonates the carbonyl oxygen.
 **B.** It increases the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon.
 **C.** It removes water from the reaction.
 **D.** It increases the nucleophilic character of the reagent. ✓
**Answer:** It increases the nucleophilic character of the reagent.

**Q1549.** How does an acid catalyst facilitate nucleophilic addition reactions?
 **A.** By decreasing the electrophilic character of the carbonyl carbon.
 **B.** By generating a stronger nucleophile.
 **C.** By stabilizing the negative charge on the attacking nucleophile.
 **D.** By increasing the positive character of the carbonyl carbon atom. ✓
**Answer:** By increasing the positive character of the carbonyl carbon atom.

**Q1550.** The addition of hydrogen cyanide (HCN) to aldehydes and ketones results in the formation of:
 **A.** Alkenes
 **B.** Carboxylic acids
 **C.** Amines
 **D.** Cyanohydrins ✓
**Answer:** Cyanohydrins

**Q1551.** In the addition of HCN to a carbonyl compound, the reaction is usually catalysed by:
 **A.** Strong acid
 **B.** Lewis acid
 **C.** Neutral salt
 **D.** Strong base ✓
**Answer:** Strong base

**Q1552.** Which of the following is a key step in the base-catalysed addition of HCN?
 **A.** Protonation of the carbonyl oxygen.
 **B.** Attack by H⁺ on the carbonyl carbon.
 **C.** Elimination of water.
 **D.** Reaction of the base with HCN to generate CN⁻. ✓
**Answer:** Reaction of the base with HCN to generate CN⁻.

**Q1553.** What type of compounds are formed when Grignard reagents react with aldehydes and ketones, followed by hydrolysis?
 **A.** Carboxylic acids
 **B.** Alkanes
 **C.** Ethers
 **D.** Alcohols ✓
**Answer:** Alcohols

**Q1554.** Reaction of formaldehyde with a Grignard reagent, followed by hydrolysis, gives a:
 **A.** Secondary alcohol
 **B.** Tertiary alcohol
 **C.** Ether
 **D.** Primary alcohol ✓
**Answer:** Primary alcohol

**Q1555.** Reaction of any aldehyde (other than formaldehyde) with a Grignard reagent, followed by hydrolysis, gives a:
 **A.** Primary alcohol
 **B.** Tertiary alcohol
 **C.** Alkene
 **D.** Secondary alcohol ✓
**Answer:** Secondary alcohol

**Q1556.** Reaction of a ketone with a Grignard reagent, followed by hydrolysis, gives a:
 **A.** Primary alcohol
 **B.** Secondary alcohol
 **C.** Aldehyde
 **D.** Tertiary alcohol ✓
**Answer:** Tertiary alcohol

**Q1557.** What is the product formed when sodium bisulphite adds to an aldehyde or ketone?
 **A.** An insoluble solid
 **B.** An alkene
 **C.** A carboxylic acid
 **D.** A bisulphite addition product ✓
**Answer:** A bisulphite addition product

**Q1558.** The bisulphite addition product is generally crystalline and water-soluble. How can the original aldehyde or ketone be regenerated from it?
 **A.** By oxidation
 **B.** By reduction
 **C.** By reaction with HCN
 **D.** By heating with acid or alkali ✓
**Answer:** By heating with acid or alkali

**Q1559.** The addition of sodium bisulphite is reversible. For which type of carbonyl compounds is this addition particularly useful for purification?
 **A.** Only large ketones
 **B.** Only aromatic aldehydes
 **C.** High molecular weight aldehydes only
 **D.** Low molecular weight aldehydes and ketones ✓
**Answer:** Low molecular weight aldehydes and ketones

**Q1560.** What type of reaction involves the joining of two molecules with the elimination of a small molecule like water?
 **A.** Substitution reaction
 **B.** Addition reaction
 **C.** Rearrangement reaction
 **D.** Condensation reaction ✓
**Answer:** Condensation reaction

**Q1561.** What kind of compounds are formed when aldehydes and ketones react with ammonia derivatives?
 **A.** Ethers
 **B.** Alcohols
 **C.** Carboxylic acids
 **D.** Compounds containing the C=N-G group and water ✓
**Answer:** Compounds containing the C=N-G group and water

**Q1562.** The reaction of aldehydes and ketones with ammonia derivatives is considered a condensation reaction because:
 **A.** It requires high pressure.
 **B.** It forms a larger molecule.
 **C.** It is reversible.
 **D.** Water is lost after addition occurs. ✓
**Answer:** Water is lost after addition occurs.

**Q1563.** The reaction of aldehydes and ketones with ammonia derivatives is typically catalysed by:
 **A.** Base
 **B.** Light
 **C.** Heat
 **D.** Acid ✓
**Answer:** Acid

**Q1564.** Why are aldehydes generally more reactive than ketones towards nucleophilic addition reactions?
 **A.** Aldehydes are more sterically hindered.
 **B.** The carbonyl carbon in aldehydes is less electrophilic.
 **C.** Aldehydes have stronger C=O bonds.
 **D.** Ketones have two bulky alkyl groups increasing steric hindrance and decreasing electrophilicity. ✓
**Answer:** Ketones have two bulky alkyl groups increasing steric hindrance and decreasing electrophilicity.

**Q1565.** Which of the following compounds would be most reactive towards nucleophilic addition?
 **A.** Acetone
 **B.** Acetaldehyde
 **C.** Diethyl ketone
 **D.** Formaldehyde ✓
**Answer:** Formaldehyde

**Q1566.** Aldehydes can be reduced to which type of alcohol?
 **A.** Tertiary alcohol
 **B.** Secondary alcohol
 **C.** Dihydric alcohol
 **D.** Primary alcohol ✓
**Answer:** Primary alcohol

**Q1567.** Ketones can be reduced to which type of alcohol?
 **A.** Primary alcohol
 **B.** Tertiary alcohol
 **C.** Trihydric alcohol
 **D.** Secondary alcohol ✓
**Answer:** Secondary alcohol

**Q1568.** Which of the following is a common reducing agent used for the reduction of aldehydes and ketones to alcohols?
 **A.** KMnO₄
 **B.** K₂Cr₂O₇
 **C.** Tollens' reagent
 **D.** NaBH₄ ✓
**Answer:** NaBH₄

**Q1569.** The reduction of aldehydes and ketones using hydrogen in the presence of a catalyst like nickel or platinum is known as:
 **A.** Oxidation
 **B.** Hydration
 **C.** Dehydration
 **D.** Hydrogenation ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogenation

**Q1570.** What type of reaction is the reduction of a carbonyl group to a hydroxyl group?
 **A.** Oxidation
 **B.** Substitution reaction
 **C.** Elimination reaction
 **D.** Addition reaction ✓
**Answer:** Addition reaction

**Q1571.** Which of the following reducing agents is generally more powerful and used for a wider range of reductions, including carboxylic acids?
 **A.** NaBH₄
 **B.** Zn-Hg/HCl
 **C.** Na/C₂H₅OH
 **D.** LiAlH₄ ✓
**Answer:** LiAlH₄

**Q1572.** When 2-propanone (acetone) is reduced, what is the product formed?
 **A.** 1-propanol
 **B.** Propanal
 **C.** Propane
 **D.** 2-propanol ✓
**Answer:** 2-propanol

**Q1573.** The reduction of ethanal will yield:
 **A.** Methane
 **B.** Ethanoic acid
 **C.** Ethane
 **D.** Ethanol ✓
**Answer:** Ethanol

**Q1574.** The general equation for the reduction of an aldehyde to a primary alcohol can be represented as:
 **A.** R-CHO + [O] → R-COOH
 **B.** R-C(=O)-R' + 2[H] → R-CH(OH)-R'
 **C.** R-CH₂OH → R-CHO + H₂
 **D.** R-CHO + 2[H] → R-CH₂OH ✓
**Answer:** R-CHO + 2[H] → R-CH₂OH

**Q1575.** Which reduction method for aldehydes and ketones involves the use of sodium and ethanol?
 **A.** Catalytic hydrogenation
 **B.** Clemmensen reduction
 **C.** Wolff-Kishner reduction
 **D.** Bouveault-Blanc reduction ✓
**Answer:** Bouveault-Blanc reduction

**Q1576.** Aldehydes are generally oxidized to which class of compounds?
 **A.** Alcohols
 **B.** Alkenes
 **C.** Esters
 **D.** Carboxylic acids ✓
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids

**Q1577.** Why are aldehydes more easily oxidized than ketones?
 **A.** Because of the presence of an alkyl group.
 **B.** Because of steric hindrance.
 **C.** Because ketones have stronger C=O bonds.
 **D.** Because of the presence of a hydrogen atom directly attached to the carbonyl carbon. ✓
**Answer:** Because of the presence of a hydrogen atom directly attached to the carbonyl carbon.

**Q1578.** Which of the following is a strong oxidizing agent used to oxidize aldehydes to carboxylic acids?
 **A.** NaBH₄
 **B.** LiAlH₄
 **C.** Sodium bisulphite
 **D.** Acidified potassium dichromate ✓
**Answer:** Acidified potassium dichromate

**Q1579.** Which reagent is commonly used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones due to its ability to oxidize aldehydes but not most ketones?
 **A.** Grignard reagent
 **B.** Sodium borohydride
 **C.** Hydrogen cyanide
 **D.** Tollens' reagent ✓
**Answer:** Tollens' reagent

**Q1580.** What is the characteristic visible change observed when an aldehyde reacts with Tollens' reagent?
 **A.** Formation of a yellow precipitate.
 **B.** Evolution of a gas.
 **C.** Disappearance of purple color.
 **D.** Formation of a silver mirror or black precipitate. ✓
**Answer:** Formation of a silver mirror or black precipitate.

**Q1581.** Tollens' reagent is an ammoniacal solution of:
 **A.** Cupric oxide
 **B.** Ferric chloride
 **C.** Lead acetate
 **D.** Silver nitrate ✓
**Answer:** Silver nitrate

**Q1582.** When an aldehyde is oxidized by Fehling's solution, what characteristic change is observed?
 **A.** Formation of a silver mirror.
 **B.** Evolution of carbon dioxide.
 **C.** Formation of a yellow solution.
 **D.** Disappearance of blue color and formation of a red precipitate. ✓
**Answer:** Disappearance of blue color and formation of a red precipitate.

**Q1583.** Fehling's solution consists of two solutions, Fehling's solution A and Fehling's solution B. What is Fehling's solution B?
 **A.** Aqueous copper sulfate
 **B.** Ammoniacal silver nitrate
 **C.** Dilute nitric acid
 **D.** Alkaline solution of sodium potassium tartrate ✓
**Answer:** Alkaline solution of sodium potassium tartrate

**Q1584.** Ketones can be oxidized under vigorous conditions (strong oxidizing agents and high temperature) resulting in the cleavage of C-C bonds adjacent to the carbonyl group. What is the usual outcome regarding the number of carbon atoms in the products?
 **A.** Products with more carbon atoms than the original ketone.
 **B.** Products with the same number of carbon atoms as the original ketone.
 **C.** Alkenes with fewer carbon atoms.
 **D.** A mixture of carboxylic acids with fewer carbon atoms than the original ketone. ✓
**Answer:** A mixture of carboxylic acids with fewer carbon atoms than the original ketone.

**Q1585.** The Haloform reaction is a specific oxidation reaction that detects the presence of:
 **A.** All aldehydes
 **B.** All ketones
 **C.** Carboxylic acids
 **D.** secondary alcohols ✓
**Answer:** secondary alcohols

### Alkyl Halides (60 Questions)

**Q1586.** An alkyl halide molecule (RX) consists of which two main parts?
 **A.** An alkyl group with a partial negative charge and a halide atom with a partial positive charge.
 **B.** An alkyl group with a full positive charge and a halide atom with a full negative charge.
 **C.** An alkyl group with no charge and a halogen atom with a negative charge.
 **D.** An alkyl group with a partial positive charge on the carbon atom attached to the halogen atom, and the halide atom with a partial negative charge. ✓
**Answer:** An alkyl group with a partial positive charge on the carbon atom attached to the halogen atom, and the halide atom with a partial negative charge.

**Q1587.** What two main factors govern the reactivity of the R-X bond in alkyl halides?
 **A.** Steric hindrance and solvent polarity
 **B.** Temperature and concentration
 **C.** Presence of catalysts and light
 **D.** C-X bond energy and C-X bond polarity ✓
**Answer:** C-X bond energy and C-X bond polarity

**Q1588.** According to experimental evidence, which factor is the main determinant of alkyl halide reactivity?
 **A.** C-X bond polarity
 **B.** Electronegativity difference
 **C.** Size of the halogen atom
 **D.** Strength of the C-X bond ✓
**Answer:** Strength of the C-X bond

**Q1589.** What is the correct order of reactivity of alkyl halides for a particular alkyl group?
 **A.** Fluoride > Chloride > Bromide > Iodide
 **B.** Chloride > Bromide > Iodide > Fluoride
 **C.** Bromide > Iodide > Fluoride > Chloride
 **D.** Iodide > Bromide > Chloride > Fluoride ✓
**Answer:** Iodide > Bromide > Chloride > Fluoride

**Q1590.** Which type of alkyl halide is generally the least reactive due to the strength of its C-X bond?
 **A.** Alkyl iodides
 **B.** Alkyl bromides
 **C.** Alkyl chlorides
 **D.** Alkyl fluorides ✓
**Answer:** Alkyl fluorides

**Q1591.** Which type of alkyl halide is generally the most reactive?
 **A.** Alkyl fluorides
 **B.** Alkyl chlorides
 **C.** Alkyl bromides
 **D.** Alkyl iodides ✓
**Answer:** Alkyl iodides

**Q1592.** What is the bond energy of the C-I bond in alkyl halides?
 **A.** 467 kj/mole
 **B.** 346 kj/mole
 **C.** 290 kj/mole
 **D.** 228 kj/mole ✓
**Answer:** 228 kj/mole

**Q1593.** What is the bond energy of the C-F bond in alkyl halides?
 **A.** 228 kj/mole
 **B.** 290 kj/mole
 **C.** 346 kj/mole
 **D.** 467 kj/mole ✓
**Answer:** 467 kj/mole

**Q1594.** The greatest electronegativity difference exists between carbon and which halogen atom in alkyl halides?
 **A.** Iodine
 **B.** Bromine
 **C.** Chlorine
 **D.** Fluorine ✓
**Answer:** Fluorine

**Q1595.** If an electrophile is the attacking reagent, which alkyl halides would be the most reactive?
 **A.** Alkyl iodides
 **B.** Alkyl bromides
 **C.** Alkyl chlorides
 **D.** Alkyl fluorides ✓
**Answer:** Alkyl fluorides

**Q1596.** The C-Cl bond energy in alkyl halides is:
 **A.** 467 kj/mole
 **B.** 413 kj/mole
 **C.** 290 kj/mole
 **D.** 346 kj/mole ✓
**Answer:** 346 kj/mole

**Q1597.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH(Br)CH(CH₃)CH₂CH₃?
 **A.** 3-Methyl-2-bromopentane
 **B.** 2-Bromo-3-ethylbutane
 **C.** 2-Bromo-3,4-dimethylbutane
 **D.** 2-Bromo-3-methylpentane ✓
**Answer:** 2-Bromo-3-methylpentane

**Q1598.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH₂CH(Cl)CH₂CH(CH₃)₂?
 **A.** 2-Chloro-4-methylhexane
 **B.** 3-Chloro-5-methylhexane
 **C.** 3-Chloro-2,2-dimethylpentane
