**Answer:** 4-Chloro-2-methylhexane

**Q1599.** The C-Br bond energy in alkyl halides is:
 **A.** 467 kj/mole
 **B.** 346 kj/mole
 **C.** 228 kj/mole
 **D.** 290 kj/mole ✓
**Answer:** 290 kj/mole

**Q1600.** What is the electronegativity of Chlorine (Cl)?
 **A.** 4.0
 **B.** 2.8
 **C.** 2.5
 **D.** 3.0 ✓
**Answer:** 3.0

**Q1601.** What is the electronegativity of Iodine (I)?
 **A.** 4.0
 **B.** 3.0
 **C.** 2.8
 **D.** 2.5 ✓
**Answer:** 2.5

**Q1602.** If alkyl iodides were to be considered based purely on electronegativity difference (if an electrophile is the attacking reagent), how would their reactivity compare to other alkyl halides?
 **A.** Most reactive
 **B.** Similar reactivity to bromides
 **C.** Intermediate reactivity
 **D.** Least reactive ✓
**Answer:** Least reactive

**Q1603.** What are the two main processes involved in nucleophilic substitution reactions on alkyl halides?
 **A.** Oxidation and Reduction
 **B.** Addition and Elimination
 **C.** Rearrangement and Isomerization

**Answer:** Breakage of C-X bond and formation of C-Nu bond

**Q1604.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃Cl?
 **A.** Methyl chloride
 **B.** Methane chloride
 **C.** Monochloromethane
 **D.** Chloromethane ✓
**Answer:** Chloromethane

**Q1605.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH₂CH₂CH₂Cl?
 **A.** n-Butylchloride
 **B.** Butyl chloride
 **C.** Chlorobutane
 **D.** 1-Chlorobutane ✓
**Answer:** 1-Chlorobutane

**Q1606.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH₂CH(Cl)CH₃?
 **A.** sec-Butyl chloride
 **B.** Butyl chloride
 **C.** 1-Chlorobutane
 **D.** 2-Chlorobutane ✓
**Answer:** 2-Chlorobutane

**Q1607.** What is the IUPAC name for (CH₃)₂CHCH₂Cl?
 **A.** Isobutyl chloride
 **B.** 2-Methyl-1-chloropropane
 **C.** 1-Chloroisobutane
 **D.** 1-Chloro-2-methylpropane ✓
**Answer:** 1-Chloro-2-methylpropane

**Q1608.** What is the IUPAC name for (CH₃)₃CCl?
 **A.** tert-Butyl chloride
 **B.** 2-Methyl-2-chloropropane
 **C.** Trimethylchloromethane
 **D.** 2-Chloro-2-methylpropane ✓
**Answer:** 2-Chloro-2-methylpropane

**Q1609.** The mechanism of nucleophilic substitution reactions depends upon the timing of which two processes?
 **A.** Nucleophile strength and leaving group ability
 **B.** Substrate concentration and temperature
 **C.** Solvent polarity and steric hindrance
 **D.** Bond breaking and bond formation ✓
**Answer:** Bond breaking and bond formation

**Q1610.** If the two processes (bond breakage and bond formation) occur simultaneously, the mechanism is called:
 **A.** SN1
 **B.** E1
 **C.** E2
 **D.** SN2 ✓
**Answer:** SN2

**Q1611.** If the C-X bond breaks first, followed by the formation of a new bond, the mechanism is called:
 **A.** SN2
 **B.** E2
 **C.** E1
 **D.** SN1 ✓
**Answer:** SN1

**Q1612.** Which type of nucleophilic substitution reaction is a single-step mechanism?
 **A.** SN1
 **B.** E1
 **C.** E2
 **D.** SN2 ✓
**Answer:** SN2

**Q1613.** In an SN2 mechanism, from which side does the attacking nucleophile approach the electrophilic carbon?
 **A.** The same side as the leaving group
 **B.** Any side, randomly
 **C.** It attacks the leaving group first
 **D.** The side opposite to the leaving group ✓
**Answer:** The side opposite to the leaving group

**Q1614.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH₂Br?
 **A.** Ethyl bromide
 **B.** Ethane bromide
 **C.** Monobromoethane
 **D.** Bromoethane ✓
**Answer:** Bromoethane

**Q1615.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH₂CH₂Cl?
 **A.** n-Propyl chloride
 **B.** Propyl chloride
 **C.** Chloropropane
 **D.** 1-Chloropropane ✓
**Answer:** 1-Chloropropane

**Q1616.** What is the IUPAC name for CH₃CH(Cl)CH₃?
 **A.** Isopropyl chloride
 **B.** Propane chloride
 **C.** Chloroisopropane
 **D.** 2-Chloropropane ✓
**Answer:** 2-Chloropropane

**Q1617.** During an SN2 reaction, what is the change in the hybridization state of the substrate carbon atom bearing the halogen?
 **A.** From sp to sp²
 **B.** From sp² to sp³
 **C.** Remains sp³
 **D.** From sp³ to planar sp² ✓
**Answer:** From sp³ to planar sp²

**Q1618.** What happens to the configuration of the alkyl halide molecule during an SN2 reaction?
 **A.** It remains the same
 **B.** It undergoes retention of configuration
 **C.** It undergoes racemization
 **D.** It gets inverted ✓
**Answer:** It gets inverted

**Q1619.** How is the molecularity of an SN2 reaction defined?
 **A.** The number of molecules participating in the slow step
 **B.** The total number of molecules involved in the reaction
 **C.** The number of steps in the mechanism
 **D.** The number of molecules taking part in the rate determining step ✓
**Answer:** The number of molecules taking part in the rate determining step

**Q1620.** What is the molecularity of an SN2 reaction?
 **A.** One
 **B.** Three
 **C.** Zero
 **D.** Two ✓
**Answer:** Two

**Q1621.** What is the molecularity of an SN1 reaction?
 **A.** Two
 **B.** Three
 **C.** Zero
 **D.** One ✓
**Answer:** One

**Q1622.** What is the order of reactivity for SN2 reactions among primary, secondary, and tertiary alkyl halides?
 **A.** Tertiary > Secondary > Primary
 **B.** Secondary > Primary > Tertiary
 **C.** Tertiary = Secondary = Primary
 **D.** Primary > Secondary > Tertiary ✓
**Answer:** Primary > Secondary > Tertiary

**Q1623.** Which of the following alkyl halides would be most reactive in an SN2 reaction?
 **A.** (CH₃)₃C-Br
 **B.** CH₃CH₂CH(Br)CH₃
 **C.** CH₃CH₂CH₂Br
 **D.** CH₃Br ✓
**Answer:** CH₃Br

**Q1624.** Which of the following is typically a characteristic of an SN1 reaction?
 **A.** Proceeds with inversion of configuration
 **B.** Bimolecular rate-determining step
 **C.** Strong nucleophile required
 **D.** Formation of a carbocation intermediate ✓
**Answer:** Formation of a carbocation intermediate

**Q1625.** What is the order of reactivity for SN1 reactions among primary, secondary, and tertiary alkyl halides?
 **A.** Primary > Secondary > Tertiary
 **B.** Secondary > Primary > Tertiary
 **C.** Tertiary = Secondary = Primary
 **D.** Tertiary > Secondary > Primary ✓
**Answer:** Tertiary > Secondary > Primary

**Q1626.** Which of the following factors favors an SN1 reaction mechanism?
 **A.** Strong nucleophile
 **B.** Aprotic solvent
 **C.** Less stable carbocation
 **D.** Polar protic solvent ✓
**Answer:** Polar protic solvent

**Q1627.** What type of nucleophile generally favors an SN2 reaction?
 **A.** Weak nucleophile
 **B.** Neutral nucleophile
 **C.** Bulky nucleophile
 **D.** Strong nucleophile ✓
**Answer:** Strong nucleophile

**Q1628.** What is the effect of steric hindrance on the rate of SN2 reactions?
 **A.** Increases the rate
 **B.** Has no effect on the rate
 **C.** Only affects the product type
 **D.** Decreases the rate ✓
**Answer:** Decreases the rate

**Q1629.** What is racemization, and in which type of nucleophilic substitution reaction can it occur?
 **A.** Inversion of configuration; SN2
 **B.** Retention of configuration; SN2
 **C.** Elimination of a leaving group; E1
 **D.** Formation of equal amounts of enantiomers; SN1 ✓
**Answer:** Formation of equal amounts of enantiomers; SN1

**Q1630.** Which of the following is an example of a nucleophile?
 **A.** H⁺
 **B.** CH₃⁺
 **C.** BF₃
 **D.** H₂O ✓
**Answer:** H₂O

**Q1631.** What is the common name for β-elimination reactions in alkyl halides?
 **A.** Substitution reactions
 **B.** Addition reactions
 **C.** Rearrangement reactions
 **D.** Dehydrohalogenation reactions ✓
**Answer:** Dehydrohalogenation reactions

**Q1632.** Which two mechanisms are possible for β-elimination reactions?
 **A.** SN1 and SN2
 **B.** Addition and Substitution
 **C.** Oxidation and Reduction
 **D.** E1 and E2 ✓
**Answer:** E1 and E2

**Q1633.** In an E2 elimination reaction, what is the role of the base?
 **A.** It acts as a leaving group.
 **B.** It attacks the α-carbon.
 **C.** It forms a carbocation.
 **D.** It removes a proton from the β-carbon. ✓
**Answer:** It removes a proton from the β-carbon.

**Q1634.** What is the molecularity of an E2 reaction?
 **A.** One
 **B.** Three
 **C.** Zero
 **D.** Two ✓
**Answer:** Two

**Q1635.** What is the characteristic feature of the transition state in an E2 reaction?
 **A.** Formation of a carbocation
 **B.** Full positive charge on carbon
 **C.** Full negative charge on carbon
 **D.** Partial breaking of C-H and C-X bonds, and partial formation of C=C bond ✓
**Answer:** Partial breaking of C-H and C-X bonds, and partial formation of C=C bond

**Q1636.** For an E2 reaction to occur efficiently, the hydrogen on the β-carbon and the halogen on the α-carbon must be in which relative orientation?
 **A.** Syn-periplanar
 **B.** Gauche
 **C.** Eclipsed
 **D.** Anti-periplanar ✓
**Answer:** Anti-periplanar

**Q1637.** Which type of alkyl halide is most reactive in an E2 reaction?
 **A.** Primary
 **B.** Secondary
 **C.** Methyl
 **D.** Tertiary ✓
**Answer:** Tertiary

**Q1638.** What is the rate-determining step in an E1 elimination reaction?
 **A.** Abstraction of a proton by a base
 **B.** Formation of a double bond
 **C.** Attack of a nucleophile
 **D.** Ionization of the alkyl halide to form a carbocation ✓
**Answer:** Ionization of the alkyl halide to form a carbocation

**Q1639.** How many steps are involved in an E1 elimination mechanism?
 **A.** One
 **B.** Three
 **C.** Four
 **D.** Two ✓
**Answer:** Two

**Q1640.** What kind of solvent typically favors an E1 reaction?
 **A.** Aprotic solvent
 **B.** Non-polar solvent
 **C.** Basic solvent
 **D.** Polar protic solvent ✓
**Answer:** Polar protic solvent

**Q1641.** Which type of alkyl halide is most reactive in an E1 reaction?
 **A.** Primary
 **B.** Secondary
 **C.** Methyl
 **D.** Tertiary ✓
**Answer:** Tertiary

**Q1642.** What kind of base typically favors an E1 reaction?
 **A.** Strong, bulky base
 **B.** Strong, non-bulky base
 **C.** No base required
 **D.** Weak base ✓
**Answer:** Weak base

**Q1643.** Which rule predicts the major product of an elimination reaction when more than one alkene can be formed?
 **A.** Markovnikov's Rule
 **B.** Hofmann's Rule
 **C.** Hund's Rule
 **D.** Zaitsev's Rule ✓
**Answer:** Zaitsev's Rule

**Q1644.** What conditions generally favor elimination (E1/E2) over substitution (SN1/SN2)?
 **A.** Low temperature and weak base/nucleophile
 **B.** High temperature and weak acid
 **C.** Low temperature and strong acid
 **D.** High temperature and strong base ✓
**Answer:** High temperature and strong base

**Q1645.** If a bulky base is used with a secondary alkyl halide, which pathway is likely to be favored?
 **A.** SN1
 **B.** SN2
 **C.** E1
 **D.** E2 ✓
**Answer:** E2

### Carboxylic Acid (75 Questions)

**Q1678.** Which of the following functional groups defines a carboxylic acid?
**Answer:** The functional group for carboxylic acids is the carboxyl group, which is written as -COOH.

**Q1679.** The carboxyl group is a combination of which two groups?
**Answer:** The carboxyl group is made up of a carbonyl group (>C=O) and a hydroxyl group (-OH).

**Q1680.** What is the IUPAC name for formic acid?
**Answer:** Formic acid is also known as Methanoic acid in IUPAC nomenclature.

**Q1681.** Acetic acid was first isolated from which source?
**Answer:** Acetic acid was first isolated from vinegar.

**Q1682.** What is the common name for CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH?
**Answer:** CH₃CH₂CH₂COOH is commonly known as Butyric acid.

**Q1683.** Which of the following is an example of an aliphatic dicarboxylic acid?
**Answer:** Oxalic acid (Ethanedioic acid) is an example of an aliphatic dicarboxylic acid.

**Q1684.** How many carboxyl groups are present in a tricarboxylic acid?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids are classified based on the number of carboxyl groups; 'tri' indicates three.

**Q1685.** The IUPAC names of saturated monocarboxylic acids are derived from alkanes by changing the ending 'e' to:
**Answer:** The IUPAC names of saturated monocarboxylic acids are alkanoic acids, ending with -oic acid.

**Q1686.** Which of the following is the general formula for an aliphatic carboxylic acid?
**Answer:** The general formula is R-COOH or R-C(=O)OH, where R is H or an alkyl group.

**Q1687.** What type of carboxylic acid is benzoic acid?
**Answer:** Benzoic acid is an aromatic monocarboxylic acid due to the benzene ring.

**Q1688.** Primary alcohols and aldehydes can be oxidized to carboxylic acids using which of the following reagents?
**Answer:** Potassium dichromate in acidic medium is a strong oxidizing agent for this purpose.

**Q1689.** The hydrolysis of alkanenitriles to carboxylic acids requires boiling with:
**Answer:** Boiling with mineral acids or alkalis hydrolyzes nitriles to carboxylic acids.

**Q1690.** How many carbon atoms does the carboxylic acid produced from an alkyl halide via alkanenitrile hydrolysis have, compared to the original alkyl halide?
**Answer:** Cyanide adds one carbon atom during the reaction, increasing the chain length.

**Q1691.** Carboxylic acids can be prepared from Grignard reagents by reacting them with:
**Answer:** Grignard reagents react with CO₂ followed by acidification to form carboxylic acids.

**Q1692.** In the preparation of carboxylic acids from Grignard reagents, the addition product is reacted with:
**Answer:** The addition product is acidified with a mineral acid to yield the acid.

**Q1693.** The hydrolysis of esters to form carboxylic acids is typically done by boiling with:
**Answer:** This forms a salt which on acidification gives carboxylic acid.

**Q1694.** Symmetrical alkenes when heated with alkaline KMnO₄ are cleaved at the double bond to form:
**Answer:** Alkaline KMnO₄ cleaves double bonds oxidatively to yield carboxylic acids.

**Q1695.** What is the smell characteristic of the first three aliphatic acids (formic, acetic, propionic acid)?
**Answer:** They are colorless liquids with a characteristic pungent odor.

**Q1696.** Why are the first four members of aliphatic acids very soluble in water?
**Answer:** Hydrogen bonding with water molecules makes them highly soluble.

**Q1697.** Carboxylic acids have relatively high boiling points due to:
**Answer:** Hydrogen bonding between carboxylic acid molecules leads to higher boiling points.

**Q1698.** In non-polar solvents like benzene, carboxylic acids exist as:
**Answer:** In non-polar solvents, two acid molecules dimerize through hydrogen bonding.

**Q1699.** What is the melting point trend observed for carboxylic acids with an even number of carbon atoms compared to their odd-numbered counterparts?
**Answer:** Even-numbered acids pack more efficiently in solids, leading to higher melting points.

**Q1700.** Which of the following is a laboratory method for preparing acetic acid?
**Answer:** Ethyl alcohol is oxidized to acetic acid in laboratory synthesis.

**Q1701.** The hydrolysis of ethanenitrile to acetic acid proceeds through which intermediate?
**Answer:** The intermediate formed is acetamide, which hydrolyzes to acetic acid.

**Q1702.** What is 'glacial acetic acid' characterized by?
**Answer:** Pure acetic acid solidifies at 17 °C, resembling ice — hence the term 'glacial'.

**Q1703.** Which of the following statements about the carboxyl group's reactivity is true?
**Answer:** The carboxyl group displays the chemistry of both the carbonyl and the hydroxyl groups.

**Q1704.** In most reactions of carboxylic acids, which part of the molecule is typically retained?
**Answer:** In most reactions of carboxylic acids, the carboxyl group is retained.

**Q1705.** The reactivity of carboxylic acid molecules is primarily a consequence of the presence of which group?
**Answer:** The reactivity of these molecules is a consequence of the presence of the carbonyl group.

**Q1706.** Carboxylic acids are considered weaker acids compared to:
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids are weaker acids than mineral acids.

**Q1707.** When dissolved in water, carboxylic acids furnish which ion?
**Answer:** They furnish H⁺ when dissolved in water.

**Q1708.** What is formed when carboxylic acids react with bases like NaOH or KOH?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with bases (NaOH, KOH) to form salts.

**Q1709.** Which gas is evolved when carboxylic acids decompose carbonates and bicarbonates?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids decompose carbonates and bicarbonates evolving carbon dioxide gas with effervescence.

**Q1710.** The reaction of acetic acid with sodium carbonate produces sodium acetate, water, and:
**Answer:** 2CH₃COOH + Na₂CO₃ → 2CH₃COONa + CO₂ + H₂O

**Q1711.** When carboxylic acids react with active metals (Na, K, Ca, Mg), what is evolved along with the formation of their salts?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with active metals (Na, K, Ca, Mg etc.) to form their salts with the evolution of hydrogen gas.

**Q1712.** The -OH group of carboxylic acids can be replaced by which of the following to form acid derivatives?
**Answer:** The -OH group can thus be replaced by X, OR and NH₂ to form halides, esters and amides, respectively.

**Q1713.** What type of reaction occurs when a nucleophile attacks the carboxyl group, followed by the displacement of the -OH group?
**Answer:** The addition of a nucleophile to the carboxyl group is always followed by the displacement of the -OH group by some other group, producing a carboxylic acid derivative.

**Q1714.** The reaction of CH₃COOH with PCl₅ produces CH₃COCl, POCl₃, and:
**Answer:** CH₃COOH + PCl₅ → CH₃COCl + POCl₃ + HCl

**Q1715.** Heating carboxylic acids with alcohols in the presence of concentrated H₂SO₄ leads to the formation of:
**Answer:** When carboxylic acids are heated with alcohols in the presence of concentrated H₂SO₄, esters are formed.

**Q1716.** What is the characteristic smell of esters, often leading to their use as artificial flavors?
**Answer:** Esters have a fruity smell and are used as artificial flavors.

**Q1717.** What are ammonium salts formed when carboxylic acids react with ammonia?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with ammonia to form ammonium salts which on heating produce acid amides.

**Q1718.** Which reagent is used to dehydrate carboxylic acids on strong heating to form acid anhydrides?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids are dehydrated on heating strongly in the presence of phosphorus pentoxide.

**Q1719.** What is the product of the partial reduction of carboxylic acids with lithium aluminum hydride (LiAlH₄)?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids on reaction with lithium aluminum hydride (LiAlH₄) are reduced to alcohols.

**Q1720.** What are carboxylic acids completely reduced to when reacted with HI and red phosphorus?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids on reduction with HI and red phosphorus give alkanes.

**Q1721.** Which of the following is true regarding the reactivity of carboxylic acids based on the carbonyl group?
**Answer:** The reactivity of these molecules is a consequence of the presence of the carbonyl group.

**Q1722.** When CH₃COOH reacts with SOCl₂, what are the products?
**Answer:** The reaction of CH₃COOH with SOCl₂ produces acetyl chloride (CH₃COCl), sulfur dioxide (SO₂), and hydrochloric acid (HCl).

**Q1723.** What happens to the proton (H⁺) in the presence of water (H₂O) when a carboxylic acid dissociates?
**Answer:** In the presence of water (H₂O), the proton breaks away as H₃O⁺ ion.

**Q1724.** What type of functional group is formed when the -OH group of a carboxylic acid is replaced by −NH₂?
**Answer:** The -OH group can be replaced by X, OR, and NH₂ to form halides, esters, and amides, respectively.

**Q1725.** Which of the following is a key step in the mechanism of ester formation from carboxylic acids and alcohols?
**Answer:** The first step in the mechanism of ester formation (esterification) is the protonation of the carboxylic acid.

**Q1726.** What is the product when ammonium acetate (CH₃COONH₄) is heated?
**Answer:** Ammonium salts formed by the reaction of carboxylic acids with ammonia produce acid amides upon heating (CH₃COONH₄ → CH₃CONH₂ + H₂O).

**Q1727.** The overall process by which carboxylic acids react with alcohols to form esters and water is known as:
**Answer:** The formation of an ester when carboxylic acids are heated with alcohols in the presence of concentrated H₂SO₄ is known as esterification.

**Q1728.** What happens to the -OH group of a carboxylic acid when it forms a derivative like an acyl halide, ester, or amide?
**Answer:** The addition of a nucleophile to the carboxyl group is always followed by the displacement of the -OH group by some other group, producing a carboxylic acid derivative.

**Q1729.** Which of the following is NOT a common carboxylic acid derivative discussed in the text?
**Answer:** The -OH group can be replaced by X, OR and NH₂ to form halides, esters and amides, respectively.

**Q1730.** What is the product when acetic acid (CH₃COOH) reacts with PCl₅?
**Answer:** The reaction of CH₃COOH with PCl₅ yields CH₃COCl (Acetyl chloride), POCl₃, and HCl.

**Q1731.** Which reagent is used to convert carboxylic acids into acyl chlorides, along with SO₂ and HCl as by-products?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with SOCl₂ to form acyl chlorides, SO₂, and HCl.

**Q1732.** The formation of an ester from a carboxylic acid and an alcohol in the presence of concentrated H₂SO₄ is known as:
**Answer:** When carboxylic acids are heated with alcohols in the presence of concentrated H₂SO₄, esters are formed.

**Q1733.** Which of the following compounds has a fruity smell and is used as an artificial flavor?
**Answer:** Esters have a fruity smell and are used as artificial flavours.

**Q1734.** What is the product formed when carboxylic acids react with NH₃ and are subsequently heated?
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with ammonia to form ammonium salts which on heating produce acid amides.

**Q1735.** The dehydration of carboxylic acids using phosphorus pentoxide (P₂O₅) on strong heating leads to the formation of:
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids are dehydrated on heating strongly in the presence of phosphorus pentoxide to form acid anhydrides.

**Q1736.** Which of the following is a common step in the mechanism of forming acyl chlorides from carboxylic acids using SOCl₂?
**Answer:** The mechanism for the reaction of CH₃COOH with SOCl₂ shows the formation of an intermediate where sulfur is bonded to the carboxyl oxygen and a chlorine atom.

**Q1737.** What is the product of the reaction between ethanoic acid (CH₃COOH) and ethanol (C₂H₅OH) in the presence of H₂SO₄?
**Answer:** CH₃COOH + C₂H₅OH → CH₃COOC₂H₅ + H₂O.

**Q1738.** The conversion of a carboxylic acid into an acyl halide involves the replacement of the -OH group by a:
**Answer:** The -OH group can be replaced by X (halogen) to form halides.

**Q1739.** The reaction of CH₃COOH with NH₃ initially forms an ammonium salt (CH₃COONH₄). This is an example of a reaction involving:
**Answer:** Carboxylic acids react with bases (like ammonia) to form salts by removing the hydrogen atom of the carboxyl group.

**Q1740.** What is the name of the derivative formed when the -OH group of a carboxylic acid is replaced by an -OR group?
**Answer:** The -OH group can be replaced by OR to form esters.

**Q1741.** Which reagent is used to convert acyl chlorides to carboxylic acids?
**Answer:** Acyl halides are readily hydrolyzed by water to form their corresponding carboxylic acids.

**Q1742.** What is the product when an acid anhydride reacts with an alcohol?
**Answer:** Acid anhydrides react with alcohols to form esters and a carboxylic acid.

**Q1743.** How can an ester be converted back to its parent carboxylic acid?
**Answer:** Esters can be hydrolyzed back to carboxylic acids and alcohols. When done with strong bases, it's called saponification.

**Q1744.** What is the product when an amide is hydrolyzed in the presence of an acid or a base?
**Answer:** Amides undergo hydrolysis in the presence of acid or base to yield a carboxylic acid and an amine (or an ammonium salt).

**Q1745.** Which of the following reactions is an example of an interconversion between carboxylic acid derivatives?
**Answer:** All options represent interconversion reactions of carboxylic acid derivatives.

**Q1746.** The reaction of an acyl chloride with an alcohol yields:
**Answer:** Acyl chlorides are highly reactive and react with alcohols to form esters.

**Q1747.** What product is formed when an acyl chloride reacts with ammonia?
**Answer:** Acyl chlorides react with ammonia to form amides.

**Q1748.** Which derivative can be used to synthesize an amide from a carboxylic acid without going through the ammonium salt intermediate?
**Answer:** Acyl halides react directly with ammonia or amines to form amides without needing an intermediate salt.

**Q1749.** The reaction of an acid anhydride with an amine produces:
**Answer:** Acid anhydrides react with amines to form amides and a carboxylic acid (or its salt).

**Q1750.** Which of the following reagents is most suitable for the reduction of an ester to an alcohol?
**Answer:** Esters can be reduced to alcohols using strong reducing agents like LiAlH₄.

**Q1751.** What is the primary characteristic that makes acyl halides highly reactive among carboxylic acid derivatives?
**Answer:** Halide ions are excellent leaving groups, making acyl halides very reactive.

**Q1752.** The conversion of an acid anhydride to a carboxylic acid can be achieved by:
**Answer:** Acid anhydrides react with water (HYDROLYSIS) to form two molecules of carboxylic acid.

### Chemical Bonding (33 Questions)

**Q1753.** Which of the following best defines a chemical bond?
**Answer:** A chemical bond is a general term for the attractive force that holds two or more atoms or ions together to form a stable chemical entity (a molecule or compound). While options B and C describe specific types of chemical bonds (covalent and ionic, respectively), option A provides the most comprehensive and accurate definition of a chemical bond. Option D is incorrect as it describes forces within an atom, not between atoms.

**Q1754.** Noble gases generally show little chemical reactivity primarily because:
**Answer:** Noble gases (except Helium) have a complete octet in their outermost valence shell (ns2 np6), which is a highly stable electronic configuration. This stability means they have little tendency to gain, lose, or share electrons, thus exhibiting low chemical reactivity. Options A, B, and D are incorrect; their atomic size doesn't dictate their reactivity in this manner, their configurations are stable, and while some are diatomic, it's not the primary reason for their inertness.

**Q1755.** The "octet rule" primarily states that atoms tend to react in order to achieve:
**Answer:** The octet rule is a chemical rule of thumb that states that atoms tend to combine in such a way that each atom has eight electrons in its valence (outermost) shell, giving it the same electronic configuration as a noble gas. This stable configuration makes them less reactive. Options A, B, and D are incorrect descriptions of the octet rule.

**Q1756.** Which of the following elements *typically* forms compounds by gaining electrons to achieve an octet?
**Answer:** Oxygen (O) is in Group 16 and needs two electrons to complete its octet (from 6 valence electrons to 8), so it typically gains electrons. Sodium (Na) is an alkali metal (Group 1) and tends to lose one electron. Carbon (C) often shares electrons to form four covalent bonds. Neon (Ne) is a noble gas and already has a stable octet, so it generally does not react to gain or lose electrons.

**Q1757.** When two isolated atoms form a chemical bond, what typically happens to the potential energy of the system?
**Answer:** : When atoms form a chemical bond, the potential energy of the system typically decreases because the bonded state is more stable than the isolated atoms.

**Q1758.** On a potential energy curve for bond formation between two atoms, the most stable bond corresponds to the point where:
**Answer:** The most stable bond, which represents the equilibrium bond length, occurs at the minimum point on the potential energy curve. At this point, the attractive and repulsive forces between the nuclei and electrons are balanced, leading to the lowest possible potential energy and greatest stability for the bonded system.

**Q1759.** Which factor is primarily responsible for the *increase* in atomic radius down a group in the periodic table?
**Answer:** As we move down a group in the periodic table, new principal electron shells are added with each successive period. These additional shells are further away from the nucleus, and the inner electrons effectively shield the outer electrons from the nuclear charge, leading to an increase in the overall size of the atom (atomic radius).

**Q1760.** Covalent radius is typically defined as half the distance between the nuclei of two identical atoms joined by a:
**Answer:** Anion is always larger than its parent atom because \_\_\_.

**Q1761.** Which of the following pairs of atoms would have the largest difference in their atomic radii?
**Answer:** Li (Period 2, Group 1) and Na (Period 3, Group 1) are in the same group, and Na has an additional electron shell compared to Li, leading to a significant increase in size.

**Q1762.** Which element would typically have the highest first ionization energy?
**Answer:** Neon (Ne) is a noble gas with a complete octet in its valence shell (2s22p6). This stable electronic configuration makes it very difficult to remove an electron, resulting in a very high first ionization energy compared to the other elements listed, which are in the same period but are metals or metalloids.

**Q1763.** Which group of elements generally has the most negative (most exothermic) electron affinities?
**Answer:** Halogens (Group 17) have seven valence electrons and are one electron short of a stable noble gas configuration. They have a strong attraction for an additional electron to complete their octet, thus they readily accept an electron and release a significant amount of energy, leading to highly negative (exothermic) electron affinities.

**Q1764.** According to Pauling's scale, which element has the highest electronegativity value?
**Answer:** Fluorine (F) is the most electronegative element on the Pauling scale, with a value of 3.98. It has the strongest ability to attract shared electrons in a covalent bond due to its small size and high effective nuclear charge.

**Q1765.** A large difference in electronegativity between two bonding atoms typically indicates the formation of:
**Answer:** A large difference (usually > 1.7-2.0 on the Pauling scale) in electronegativity between two bonding atoms leads to significant electron transfer from the less electronegative atom to the more electronegative atom, resulting in the formation of an ionic bond. A small difference (0-0.4) indicates a non-polar covalent bond, and an intermediate difference (0.4-1.7) indicates a polar covalent bond.

**Q1766.** Given the electronegativity values: H (2.20), O (3.44), C (2.55), which bond would be the *most* polar?
**Answer:** The polarity of a bond is determined by the difference in electronegativity between the two bonded atoms.

**Q1767.** Which of the following best describes the formation of an ionic bond?
**Answer:** An ionic bond is formed by the complete transfer of one or more electrons from a metal atom (which tends to lose electrons and form cations) to a nonmetal atom (which tends to gain electrons and form anions). The resulting oppositely charged ions are then held together by strong electrostatic forces. Options A and C describe covalent bonds, and option D is generally incorrect as noble gases are unreactive.

**Q1768.** Which type of bond is characterized by a large electronegativity difference between the bonded atoms?
**Answer:** A large difference in electronegativity (typically greater than 1.7 or 2.0 on the Pauling scale) between two atoms indicates that one atom has a much stronger pull on the bonding electrons than the other, leading to a complete transfer of electrons and the formation of an ionic bond. Non-polar and pure covalent bonds have small or zero electronegativity differences.

**Q1769.** The formation of a double bond between two atoms involves the sharing of how many electrons?
**Answer:** A single bond involves the sharing of two electrons (one pair). A double bond involves the sharing of two pairs of electrons, totaling four electrons, between two atoms. A triple bond involves the sharing of three pairs of electrons, totaling six electrons.

**Q1770.** According to VSEPR theory, the geometry around a central atom with two bonding pairs and no lone pairs is:
**Answer:** For a central atom with two bonding pairs and no lone pairs (AB2 type molecule, e.g., BeCl2​, CO2​), the electron pairs arrange themselves as far apart as possible, leading to a linear geometry with a bond angle of 180°.

**Q1771.** What is the molecular shape of CH4​ (Methane) according to VSEPR theory?
**Answer:** In methane (CH4​), the central carbon atom has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs. To minimize repulsion, these four electron pairs arrange themselves in a tetrahedral geometry around the central carbon atom, resulting in a tetrahedral molecular shape with bond angles of 109.5°.

**Q1772.** Which of the following molecules has a bent (or V-shaped) molecular geometry due to the presence of lone pairs on the central atom?
**Answer:** Sulfur dioxide (SO2​) has a central sulfur atom with two bonding pairs and one lone pair of electrons. The three electron domains (two bonding, one lone pair) arrange in a trigonal planar electron geometry, but the lone pair exerts more repulsion, distorting the molecular shape to bent or V-shaped. CO2​ is linear, BF3​ is trigonal planar, and PCl5​ is trigonal bipyramidal.

**Q1773.** The bond angle in ammonia (NH₃) is approximately 107.5∘. This deviation from the ideal tetrahedral angle (109.5∘) is best explained by:
**Answer:** In ammonia (NH3​), the central nitrogen atom has three bonding pairs and one lone pair of electrons. According to VSEPR theory, lone pair-bonding pair repulsions are stronger than bonding pair-bonding pair repulsions (LP-BP > BP-BP). This stronger repulsion from the lone pair pushes the bonding pairs closer together, reducing the bond angle from the ideal tetrahedral 109.5∘ to approximately 107.5∘.

**Q1774.** What is the electron domain geometry around the central atom in H₂​O?
**Answer:** In H2​O, the central oxygen atom has two bonding pairs and two lone pairs of electrons. The total number of electron domains is four. According to VSEPR theory, these four electron domains (two bonding, two lone pairs) arrange themselves in a tetrahedral electron geometry to minimize repulsion. The molecular shape, however, is bent due to the lone pairs.

**Q1775.** A molecule with a central atom having three bonding pairs and one lone pair would have which molecular geometry?
**Answer:** A molecule with three bonding pairs and one lone pair (AB₃E type, e.g., NH₃) has a tetrahedral electron geometry, but the lone pair occupies a position and exerts stronger repulsion. This pushes the three bonding pairs downwards, resulting in a trigonal pyramidal molecular geometry.

**Q1776.** What is the electron geometry and molecular geometry for PCl₅​ (Phosphorus Pentachloride)?
**Answer:** In PCl5​, the central phosphorus atom has five bonding pairs and no lone pairs. According to VSEPR theory, five electron domains arrange themselves in a trigonal bipyramidal electron geometry to minimize repulsion. Since there are no lone pairs, the molecular geometry is also trigonal bipyramidal.

**Q1777.** A sigma (σ) bond is formed by the:
**Answer:** A sigma (σ) bond is the strongest type of covalent bond formed by the direct, head-on, or axial overlap of atomic orbitals along the internuclear axis. This can involve s-s, s-p, or p-p orbital overlap. Sideways overlap (A) forms pi bonds.

**Q1778.** Which type of molecular orbital has higher energy and destabilizes the molecule?
**Answer:** Antibonding molecular orbitals (ABMOs or σ∗ and π∗) are formed when atomic orbitals combine destructively. Electrons in these orbitals have higher energy than the original atomic orbitals and tend to destabilize the molecule because they reduce the electron density between the nuclei. Bonding molecular orbitals (A) have lower energy and stabilize the molecule.

**Q1779.** Which factor is generally inversely proportional to bond length?
**Answer:** Bond order (single, double, triple) is generally inversely proportional to bond length. As bond order increases (e.g., from single to double to triple bond), the number of shared electron pairs increases, leading to stronger attraction between the nuclei and a shorter bond length.

**Q1780.** Which of the following bonds would have the shortest bond length?
**Answer:** A triple bond (C≡C) has the highest bond order among the carbon-carbon bonds listed (order 3), meaning it involves the sharing of six electrons. This strong attraction pulls the nuclei closest together, resulting in the shortest bond length. Double bonds are shorter than single bonds, and C-H bond lengths are different from C-C.

**Q1781.** A molecule with a zero dipole moment typically indicates that the molecule is:
**Answer:** A zero (or negligible) dipole moment for a molecule implies that the bond dipoles within the molecule cancel each other out due to the molecule's symmetrical structure, resulting in no net separation of charge across the molecule.

**Q1782.** What information can be obtained from the dipole moment of a diatomic molecule like HF?
**Answer:** For a diatomic molecule with a polar covalent bond, the experimental dipole moment can be compared to the theoretical dipole moment for a purely ionic bond to calculate the percentage ionic character of the bond. This indicates the extent of charge separation within the bond.

**Q1783.** If the bond energy of a C-C single bond is approximately 348 kJ/mol, what does this value represent?
**Answer:** Bond energy (or bond dissociation energy) is defined as the energy required to break a specific bond. Therefore, 348 kJ/mol represents the energy needed to break one mole of C-C single bonds. Energy is released when bonds form (A), but bond energy specifically refers to the breaking process.

**Q1784.** Ionic compounds are generally soluble in:
**Answer:** Ionic compounds, being composed of charged ions, are generally soluble in polar solvents (like water) due to the "like dissolves like" principle.

**Q1785.** The directional nature of covalent bonds is responsible for the phenomenon of:
**Answer:** hello this is experiment

**Q1786.** Which statement correctly describes a reversible reaction?
 **A.** Only the forward reaction occurs; the reverse reaction does not take place.
 **B.** Both forward and reverse reactions occur, but under different conditions.
 **C.** Forward and reverse reactions take place at different times and under different conditions.
 **D.** Forward and reverse reactions occur simultaneously under the same conditions. ✓

**Answer:** Forward and reverse reactions occur simultaneously under the same conditions.

**Answer:** Supersaturated

 **B.** [CH₃COOH] = 1.333, [CH₃COOCH₂CH₃] = 0.666, [CH₃CH₂OH] = 0.333, [H₂O] = 1.666
 **C.** [CH₃COOH] = 0.333, [CH₃COOCH₂CH₃] = 1.333, [CH₃CH₂OH] = 0.333, [H₂O] = 1.333

### Electrochemistry (46 Questions)

**Q1854.** What does the oxidation number represent?
**Answer:** The oxidation number is defined as the apparent charge on an atom of an element in a molecule or an ion, which can be positive, negative, or zero, as stated in the document.

**Q1855.** What is the oxidation number of an element in its free or uncombined state?
**Answer:** According to the rules for assigning oxidation numbers, the oxidation number of all elements in their free or uncombined state is zero.

**Q1856.** What is the usual oxidation number of hydrogen in most of its compounds?
**Answer:** The oxidation number of hydrogen is usually +1, except in metal hydrides where it is -1.

**Q1857.** What is the usual oxidation number of oxygen in most of its compounds?
**Answer:** The oxidation number of oxygen is usually -2, except in peroxides (-1), OF₂ (+2), and superoxides (-1/2).

**Q1858.** What is the oxidation number of Manganese (Mn) in KMnO₄?
**Answer:** In KMnO₄, potassium (K) has an oxidation number of +1, and oxygen (O) has -2. Let the oxidation number of Mn be x. The sum of oxidation numbers in a neutral molecule is zero: 1 + x + 4(-2) = 0, which simplifies to 1 + x - 8 = 0, so x = +7.

**Q1859.** What is the oxidation number of Sulfur (S) in the sulfate ion (SO₄²⁻)?
**Answer:** In SO₄²⁻, oxygen (O) has an oxidation number of -2. Let the oxidation number of S be x. The sum of oxidation numbers in a polyatomic ion equals its charge: x + 4(-2) = -2, which simplifies to x - 8 = -2, so x = +6.

**Q1860.** In the ion-electron method for an acidic medium, how are oxygen atoms balanced?
**Answer:** In an acidic medium, oxygen atoms are balanced by adding water molecules (H₂O) to the side of the half-reaction that needs oxygen.

**Q1861.** In the ion-electron method for a basic medium, how are oxygen atoms balanced?
**Answer:** In a basic medium, oxygen atoms are balanced by adding H₂O molecules to the side lacking oxygen, and then adding an equal number of OH⁻ ions to the opposite side.

**Q1862.** For a polyatomic ion, what is the sum of the oxidation numbers of all atoms?
**Answer:** The sum of the oxidation numbers of all atoms in a polyatomic ion is equal to the net charge of that ion.

**Q1863.** What is the usual oxidation number of halogens (e.g., Cl, Br, I) in their compounds with less electronegative elements (like metals)?
**Answer:** Halogens generally exhibit an oxidation number of -1 in most of their compounds, especially when combined with metals or less electronegative elements. (Note: Fluorine always has -1).

**Q1864.** Which of the following half-reactions represents an oxidation process?
**Answer:** Oxidation is defined as an increase in oxidation number or loss of electrons. In option C, the oxidation number of Iron (Fe) increases from +2 to +3, indicating oxidation.

**Q1865.** Which of the following half-reactions represents a reduction process?
**Answer:** Reduction is defined as a decrease in oxidation number or gain of electrons. In option C, Chromium (Cr) in Cr₂O₇²⁻ has an oxidation number of +6, which decreases to +3 in Cr³⁺, indicating reduction.

**Q1866.** When balancing a redox reaction using the ion-electron method, what is the primary role of H₂O molecules?
**Answer:** In both acidic and basic mediums, water molecules (H₂O) are added to the half-reactions to balance the oxygen atoms.

**Q1867.** In which state do electrolytes typically conduct electricity due to the movement of ions?
**Answer:** Electrolytic conduction relies on the mobility of ions, which is achieved when the electrolyte is in a molten (fused) state or dissolved in a solvent to form an aqueous solution.

**Q1868.** In an electrolytic cell, towards which electrode do cations migrate?
**Answer:** Cations are positively charged ions, and in an electrolytic cell, they are attracted to and move towards the negatively charged cathode.

**Q1869.** What are the main products obtained at the electrodes during the electrolysis of molten NaCl using inert electrodes?
**Answer:** During the electrolysis of molten NaCl, sodium ions (Na⁺) are reduced to sodium metal (Na) at the cathode, and chloride ions (Cl⁻) are oxidized to chlorine gas (Cl₂) at the anode.

**Q1870.** The Hall-Heroult process is an industrially important electrolytic method for the extraction of which metal?
**Answer:** The Hall-Heroult process is a widely used electrolytic method for the large-scale production of aluminum from its ore, alumina.

**Q1871.** Which industrially important chemical is produced in the Nelson cell via an electrolytic process?
**Answer:** The Nelson cell is an industrial electrolytic cell used for the production of caustic soda (sodium hydroxide, NaOH) and chlorine gas from brine (aqueous sodium chloride).

**Q1872.** What is the electrolytic process used to form a protective oxide layer on aluminum, increasing its corrosion resistance?
**Answer:** Anodizing is an electrolytic passivation process used to increase the thickness of the natural oxide layer on the surface of aluminum parts, thereby enhancing their corrosion resistance and wear resistance.

**Q1873.** What is the primary function of a galvanic (voltaic) cell?
**Answer:** A galvanic cell is designed to convert chemical energy, released from a spontaneous oxidation-reduction reaction, into electrical energy.

**Q1874.** What is the main purpose of a salt bridge in a galvanic cell?
**Answer:** The salt bridge allows for the migration of ions between the two half-cells, which maintains electrical neutrality in the solutions and completes the electrical circuit, allowing current to flow.

**Q1875.** In a standard zinc-copper galvanic cell, electrons flow from which electrode to which electrode in the external circuit?
**Answer:** Zinc is oxidized at the anode (loses electrons), and these electrons then flow through the external circuit to the copper cathode, where reduction occurs.

**Q1876.** What is the overall spontaneous reaction occurring in a standard zinc-copper galvanic cell?
**Answer:** In the zinc-copper cell, zinc metal is oxidized to zinc ions (Zn → Zn²⁺ + 2e⁻) and copper ions are reduced to copper metal (Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu). The sum of these half-reactions gives the overall spontaneous reaction.

**Q1877.** How can a galvanic cell be made to operate as an electrolytic cell?
**Answer:** A galvanic cell's spontaneous reaction can be reversed by applying an external electrical potential that is greater than its own cell potential and in the opposite direction, forcing the non-spontaneous reaction to occur, thus operating as an electrolytic cell.

**Q1878.** When a galvanic cell is reversed to function as an electrolytic cell, what is the energy conversion?
**Answer:** When a galvanic cell is reversed to act as an electrolytic cell, it consumes electrical energy to drive a non-spontaneous chemical reaction, thereby converting electrical energy into chemical energy.

**Q1879.** In a galvanic cell, the electrodes provide surfaces for:
**Answer:** The electrodes in a galvanic cell serve as the sites where the oxidation and reduction half-reactions take place, facilitating the electron transfer.

**Q1880.** What is the approximate standard cell potential (E°cell) of a standard zinc-copper galvanic cell, as mentioned in the document?
**Answer:** The document explicitly states that the standard cell potential of the zinc-copper galvanic cell is 1.1 V

**Q1881.** What are the standard conditions for measuring standard electrode potential (E°)?
**Answer:** Standard electrode potential is measured at a temperature of 298 K (25°C), with the concentration of ions in the solution being 1.0 M (and 1 atm pressure for gases).

**Q1882.** Why is the Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE) used as a reference electrode?
**Answer:** The Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE) is assigned an arbitrary potential of exactly zero volts under standard conditions, serving as a universal reference point for measuring other electrode potentials.

**Q1883.** Which components are essential for a Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE)?
**Answer:** The SHE consists of a platinum electrode immersed in a 1 M aqueous solution of H⁺ ions (e.g., HCl), with hydrogen gas at 1 atm pressure bubbled around the platinum electrode.

**Q1884.** How is the electrode potential of a given electrode measured?
**Answer:** The potential of a single electrode cannot be measured directly. It is determined by coupling it with a standard reference electrode (like the SHE) to form a galvanic cell and then measuring the overall potential difference (voltage) of that cell.

**Q1885.** When an electrode potential is expressed as a reduction potential under standard conditions, it is called:
**Answer:** Standard electrode potential is conventionally reported as the standard reduction potential, which indicates the tendency of a species to gain electrons (be reduced) under standard conditions.

**Q1886.** If the standard oxidation potential of Zn is +0.76 V, what is its standard reduction potential?
**Answer:** The standard reduction potential for a half-reaction is equal in magnitude but opposite in sign to its standard oxidation potential.

**Q1887.** What drives the flow of electrons from the anode to the cathode in an external circuit of a galvanic cell?
**Answer:** The difference in electrode potentials between the two half-cells creates an electromotive force (EMF) or potential difference, which drives the flow of electrons through the external circuit.

**Q1888.** A positive standard reduction potential indicates:
**Answer:** A positive standard reduction potential signifies that the species has a greater tendency to undergo reduction (gain electrons) compared to hydrogen under standard conditions.

**Q1889.** A negative standard reduction potential indicates:
**Answer:** A negative standard reduction potential suggests that the species has a greater tendency to undergo oxidation (lose electrons) compared to hydrogen under standard conditions

**Q1890.** Which industrial process utilizes an electrolytic cell to extract sodium metal?
**Answer:** The Down's cell is a specific electrolytic method used for the industrial production of sodium metal from molten sodium chloride.

**Q1891.** What is the oxidation number of oxygen in peroxides (e.g., H₂O₂)?
**Answer:** While oxygen commonly has an oxidation number of -2, in peroxides, its oxidation number is -1.

**Q1892.** Which type of electrochemical cell converts chemical energy into electrical energy?
**Answer:** Galvanic or voltaic cells are designed to generate electrical energy from spontaneous chemical (oxidation-reduction) reactions.

**Q1893.** In balancing a redox equation in a basic medium using the ion-electron method, which species is added to balance hydrogen atoms?
**Answer:** In a basic medium, hydrogen atoms are balanced by adding water molecules (H₂O) to the side deficient in hydrogen, and an equal number of hydroxide ions (OH⁻) to the opposite side.

**Q1894.** What is the arbitrarily assigned standard electrode potential of the Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE)?
**Answer:** The Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE) serves as the reference electrode, and its standard electrode potential is, by convention, set to exactly zero volts.

**Q1895.** What is the primary purpose of anodized aluminum preparation?
**Answer:** Anodizing is an electrolytic process that forms a thicker, more durable, and highly corrosion-resistant oxide layer on the surface of aluminum.

**Q1896.** What is the oxidation number of oxygen in OF₂?
**Answer:** In oxygen difluoride (OF₂), fluorine is more electronegative than oxygen. Therefore, oxygen exhibits a positive oxidation number of +2 to balance the two -1 oxidation states of fluorine.

**Q1897.** Which of the following is an example of an electrolytic conductor?
**Answer:** Electrolytic conductors are substances that conduct electricity through the movement of ions when they are in a molten (fused) state or dissolved in a solvent (aqueous solution). Fused sodium chloride fits this description.

**Q1898.** The chemical reaction that takes place in a galvanic cell is always:
**Answer:** Galvanic cells convert chemical energy into electrical energy through a spontaneous oxidation-reduction reaction that inherently proceeds without external energy input.

**Q1899.** The potential of a single electrode cannot be measured directly. It is determined by forming a galvanic cell with a reference electrode and measuring the:
**Answer:** To determine the potential of a single electrode, it is coupled with a reference electrode (like the SHE) to create a complete electrochemical cell, and the overall potential difference (voltage) of this cell is measured.

### Fundamental Principles Of Organic Chemistry (74 Questions)

**Q1900.** Which theory stated that organic compounds could only be obtained from living organisms due to a mysterious 'vital force'?
**Answer:** The Vital Force Theory proposed that organic compounds could only be synthesized within living organisms, requiring a special 'vital force' unique to life.

**Q1901.** Who was the chemist credited with disproving the Vital Force Theory by synthesizing urea from inorganic compound?
**Answer:** Friedrich Wöhler's synthesis of urea from ammonium cyanate in 1828 was a groundbreaking experiment that demonstrated organic compounds could be synthesized without a vital force, thus disproving the Vital Force Theory.

**Q1902.** Modern organic chemistry is primarily defined as the study of compounds containing which element as their backbone?
**Answer:** Organic chemistry is the branch of chemistry that studies carbon-containing compounds, particularly hydrocarbons and their derivatives.

**Q1903.** Which unique property of carbon allows it to form long chains, branched structures, and rings?
**Answer:** Catenation is the unique ability of carbon atoms to link together to form stable chains and rings, which is fundamental to the structural diversity of organic compounds.

**Q1904.** Compared to inorganic reactions, organic reactions are generally characterized by:
**Answer:** Organic reactions are typically molecular, involving the breaking and forming of covalent bonds, which often leads to slower reaction rates and lower yields compared to ionic inorganic reactions.

**Q1905.** Which of the following is a major natural source of organic compounds, formed from the decay of ancient plants and animals over millions of years?
**Answer:** Petroleum, along with coal and natural gas, is a primary fossil fuel formed from decayed organic matter over geological time, serving as a vast reservoir of organic compounds.

**Q1906.** The majority of organic compounds are generally insoluble in water but dissolve readily in:
**Answer:** Organic compounds are typically non-polar or have low polarity, making them largely insoluble in polar solvents like water but soluble in non-polar organic solvents such as benzene or petroleum ether.

**Q1907.** Which of the following vital biological molecules are examples of organic compounds essential for life?
**Answer:** Proteins, carbohydrates, lipids, nucleic acids, and vitamins are all organic compounds that play crucial roles in biological processes and are essential for life.

**Q1908.** Which of the following is NOT typically considered a peculiar feature of organic compounds?
**Answer:** Organic compounds generally have lower melting and boiling points compared to inorganic compounds due to the weaker intermolecular forces between their covalent molecules.

**Q1909.** Why are organic reactions generally considered to be slower and have lower yields compared to inorganic reactions?
**Answer:** Organic reactions often require significant energy to break existing covalent bonds and form new ones, leading to slower reaction rates and often lower yields.

**Q1910.** Which of the following classes of compounds is primarily composed of carbon and hydrogen atoms?
**Answer:** Hydrocarbons are the simplest organic compounds, consisting solely of carbon and hydrogen atoms, and they form the fundamental backbone of many other organic compounds.

**Q1911.** Which of the following is a key reason for the immense importance of organic chemistry in our daily lives?
**Answer:** Organic chemistry is crucial because it underlies the structure and function of all living systems, and it is the basis for many synthetic products vital to modern society.

**Q1912.** Coal, petroleum, and natural gas are formed from the decomposition of biomass over geological periods. These are collectively known as:
**Answer:** Coal, petroleum, and natural gas are fossil fuels, formed from the anaerobic decomposition of ancient organic matter and serving as primary energy sources and raw materials for organic compounds.

**Q1913.** Natural gas is primarily composed of which simple hydrocarbon?
**Answer:** Methane (CH₄) is the principal component of natural gas, making up a significant majority of its volume.

**Q1914.** The process of refining crude oil to separate it into various useful fractions based on their boiling points is known as:
**Answer:** Crude oil is separated into different fractions (like gasoline, kerosene, diesel) through fractional distillation, a process that exploits the varying boiling points of its components.

**Q1915.** Organic compounds that contain carbon atoms joined together to form an open chain are known as:
**Answer:** Acyclic compounds, also known as open-chain compounds, have carbon atoms connected in a straight or branched chain, rather than forming a ring.

**Q1916.** Which of the following describes an organic compound where the carbon atoms are arranged in a continuous, unbranched sequence?
**Answer:** A straight-chain compound is a type of acyclic compound where the carbon atoms are linked consecutively without any branching.

**Q1917.** Compounds in which the carbon atoms form a ring are classified as:
**Answer:** Cyclic compounds (also known as closed-chain or ring compounds) are organic compounds where the carbon atoms are arranged in a ring structure.

**Q1918.** What is the primary characteristic that distinguishes homocyclic compounds from heterocyclic compounds?
**Answer:** Homocyclic compounds have rings composed exclusively of carbon atoms, whereas heterocyclic compounds have at least one atom other than carbon (e.g., oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur) as part of the ring structure.

**Q1919.** Which type of homocyclic compound contains a benzene ring or a similar delocalized pi-electron system?
**Answer:** Aromatic compounds are a special class of cyclic compounds that typically contain a benzene ring and exhibit enhanced stability due to delocalized pi electrons.

**Q1920.** Cyclohexane is an example of which type of organic compound?
**Answer:** Alicyclic compounds are cyclic organic compounds that behave similarly to open-chain aliphatic compounds rather than aromatic ones. Cyclohexane, with its six-membered carbon ring, is a prime example.

**Q1921.** Which of the following organic compounds contains a ring structure with at least one atom other than carbon?
**Answer:** Pyridine is a heterocyclic compound because its six-membered ring contains five carbon atoms and one nitrogen atom. Benzene and naphthalene are aromatic homocyclic, and cyclopropane is alicyclic homocyclic.

**Q1922.** A branched-chain organic compound is a type of:
**Answer:** Branched-chain compounds are a subclass of acyclic (open-chain) compounds where the carbon chain has one or more branches.

**Q1923.** If an organic compound's carbon atoms are arranged in a ring and that ring exclusively contains carbon atoms, it is best described as a:
**Answer:** When the ring consists only of carbon atoms, the compound is homocyclic (carbocyclic). These can be further divided into alicyclic (saturated or unsaturated, but not aromatic) and aromatic compounds (containing a benzene ring or similar system).

**Q1924.** Furan is an example of a:
**Answer:** Furan is a heterocyclic compound because its ring structure includes an oxygen atom in addition to carbon atoms.

**Q1925.** Which of the following would be classified as a straight-chain acyclic compound?
**Answer:** Butane (CH₃CH₂CH₂CH₃) is an unbranched, saturated hydrocarbon, making it a straight-chain acyclic compound.

**Q1926.** An organic compound with the molecular formula C₄H₁₀ can exist as both a straight-chain and a branched-chain isomer. The branched-chain isomer is known as:
**Answer:** For C₄H₁₀, n-butane is the straight-chain isomer, while isobutane (2-methylpropane) is the branched-chain isomer.

**Q1927.** If a cyclic organic compound behaves like an aliphatic compound and does not possess aromatic character, it is termed as:
**Answer:** Alicyclic compounds are cyclic, but they lack the delocalized pi-electron system characteristic of aromatic compounds and exhibit properties similar to open-chain alkanes or alkenes.

**Q1928.** Which of these compounds is an example of an aromatic homocyclic compound?
**Answer:** Toluene consists of a benzene ring with a methyl group attached, making it an aromatic homocyclic compound. Cyclohexanol is alicyclic, tetrahydrofuran and pyrrole are heterocyclic.

**Q1929.** A compound containing a ring of atoms where at least one is a nitrogen atom is specifically a/an:
**Answer:** A heterocyclic compound is defined by having atoms other than carbon (like N, O, S) as part of its ring structure.

**Q1930.** Naphthalene is classified as a/an:
**Answer:** Naphthalene is an aromatic compound because it contains fused benzene rings, and it is homocyclic because its rings are made up exclusively of carbon atoms.

**Q1931.** Which structural classification best describes an organic molecule with a carbon backbone arranged in a branched manner, but no rings?
**Answer:** This describes an open-chain compound that has one or more carbon branches extending from the main chain.

**Q1932.** Consider an organic compound whose structure is a six-membered ring containing five carbon atoms and one oxygen atom. This compound would be classified as:
**Answer:** The presence of an oxygen atom within the ring structure makes it a heterocyclic compound.

**Q1933.** Which of the following organic compounds does NOT contain a ring structure?
**Answer:** 2-Methylpropane (isobutane) is a branched-chain acyclic compound, meaning it has an open, non-cyclic structure. The others are all cyclic.

**Q1934.** The term 'carbocyclic' is synonymous with which of the following classifications?
**Answer:** Homocyclic and carbocyclic are interchangeable terms, both referring to cyclic compounds where the ring is composed entirely of carbon atoms.

**Q1935.** What is the primary role of a functional group in an organic molecule?
**Answer:** A functional group is an atom or a group of atoms within a molecule that is responsible for the characteristic chemical reactions of that molecule.

**Q1936.** Which of the following functional groups characterizes an alcohol?
**Answer:** The hydroxyl group (-OH) is the characteristic functional group of alcohols.

**Q1937.** The functional group -CHO is found in which class of organic compounds?
**Answer:** The aldehyde functional group consists of a carbonyl group (C=O) bonded to a hydrogen atom and an alkyl or aryl group.

**Q1938.** What type of organic compound contains a carbonyl group (C=O) where the carbon atom is bonded to two alkyl or aryl groups?
**Answer:** Ketones are characterized by a carbonyl functional group (C=O) situated within a carbon chain, meaning the carbonyl carbon is bonded to two other carbon atoms.

**Q1939.** The functional group -COOH is characteristic of:
**Answer:** The carboxyl group (-COOH), consisting of a carbonyl group and a hydroxyl group, is the defining functional group of carboxylic acids.

**Q1940.** Which functional group is present in the compound CH3-O-CH3?
**Answer:** The ether functional group consists of an oxygen atom bonded to two alkyl or aryl groups (-O-).

**Q1941.** The presence of a specific functional group in an organic compound leads to:
**Answer:** Functional groups are sites of chemical reactivity; their presence determines how an organic molecule will react in various chemical environments.

**Q1942.** Which of the following functional groups contains a nitrogen atom?
**Answer:** Amines are organic compounds derived from ammonia, characterized by a nitrogen atom with a lone pair of electrons, bonded to one or more alkyl or aryl groups.

**Q1943.** Which class of organic compounds is characterized by the presence of a carbonyl group (C=O) bonded to at least one hydrogen atom and an alkyl or aryl group, but not another carbon?
**Answer:** Aldehydes have a carbonyl group where the carbon is bonded to at least one hydrogen atom and a carbon chain, differentiating them from ketones where the carbonyl carbon is bonded to two other carbons.

**Q1944.** The functional group found in an ester typically contains which linkage?
**Answer:** The ester functional group is characterized by a carbonyl group directly bonded to an oxygen atom, which is then bonded to another carbon chain, forming the -COO- linkage.

**Q1945.** Identify the functional group present in CH3CH2NH2:
**Answer:** The -NH2 group is the amino functional group, characteristic of amines.

**Q1946.** What class of compounds is represented by the general formula R-X, where R is an alkyl group and X is a halogen ?
**Answer:** Alkyl halides, also known as haloalkanes, are organic compounds containing at least one halogen atom bonded to an alkyl group.

**Q1947.** C=C bond represents:
**Answer:** The carbon-carbon double bond (C=C) is the defining functional group for alkenes.

**Q1948.** Which functional group is found in both aldehydes and ketones?
**Answer:** Both aldehydes and ketones contain the carbonyl functional group (C=O). The difference lies in what else is bonded to the carbonyl carbon.

**Q1949.** A compound with a C≡C bond belongs to:
**Answer:** Alkynes are hydrocarbons that contain at least one carbon-carbon triple bond.

**Q1950.** Which of the following organic compounds is an example of an alcohol?
**Answer:** Ethanol (CH₃CH₂OH) contains the hydroxyl (-OH) functional group, which is characteristic of alcohols. CH3CHO is an aldehyde, CH₃COCH₃ is a ketone, and CH₃COOH is a carboxylic acid.

**Q1951.** The phenomenon where two or more compounds have the same molecular formula but different structural formulas or spatial arrangements of atoms is known as:
**Answer:** Isomerism is the existence of compounds with the same molecular formula but different arrangements of atoms.

**Q1952.** Which type of isomerism arises due to the difference in the arrangement of carbon atoms in the main chain?
**Answer:** Chain isomerism occurs when compounds with the same molecular formula differ in the way their carbon atoms are linked to form the main chain (e.g., straight-chain vs. branched-chain).

**Q1953.** CH₃CH₂CH₂OH and CH₃CH(OH)CH₃ are examples of which type of isomerism?
**Answer:** Position isomerism occurs when isomers have the same carbon skeleton and functional group, but the functional group (or substituent) is located at a different position on the carbon chain.

**Q1954.** Which pair of compounds represents functional group isomers?
**Answer:** Functional group isomers have the same molecular formula but different functional groups, leading to different classes of compounds. Ethanol (CH3CH2OH) is an alcohol, and dimethyl ether (CH3OCH3) is an ether, both having the molecular formula C2H6O.

**Q1955.** Metamerism is characterized by:
**Answer:** Metamerism occurs when isomers have the same functional group but different alkyl groups attached to the polyvalent atom (e.g., oxygen in ethers, nitrogen in secondary amines) within the functional group.

**Q1956.** Which of the following conditions is essential for geometric isomerism to occur?
**Answer:** Cis-trans isomerism arises due to restricted rotation about a bond, typically a carbon-carbon double bond or within a rigid ring structure, preventing interconversion between spatial arrangements.

**Q1957.** Consider the compound 2-butene. It can exhibit which type of isomerism?
**Answer:** 2-butene has a carbon-carbon double bond with different groups attached to each carbon of the double bond (a methyl group and a hydrogen on each side), allowing for cis and trans geometric isomers.

**Q1958.** Tautomerism is a special type of functional group isomerism involving:
**Answer:** Tautomerism involves the rapid interconversion of two isomers that differ in the position of a proton (hydrogen atom) and a double bond (often an enol-keto tautomerism).

**Q1959.** Which type of structural isomerism is responsible for the difference between n-pentane, isopentane, and neopentane?
**Answer:** n-pentane, isopentane (2-methylbutane), and neopentane (2,2-dimethylpropane) all have the molecular formula C5H12 but differ in the branching of their carbon chains, illustrating chain isomerism.

**Q1960.** How many structural isomers are possible for the molecular formula C₄H₉Br?
**Answer:** The four structural isomers for C₄H₉Br are: 1-Bromobutane, 2-Bromobutane, 1-Bromo-2-methylpropane (isobutyl bromide), and 2-Bromo-2-methylpropane (tert-butyl bromide).

**Q1961.** Which of the following compounds can exhibit cis-trans isomerism?
**Answer:** For cis-trans isomerism to occur, there must be restricted rotation around a carbon-carbon double bond, and each carbon of the double bond must be attached to two different groups. In 1,2-dichloroethene, each carbon of the double bond is attached to a hydrogen and a chlorine, allowing for cis and trans forms.

**Q1962.** The isomers that differ in the spatial arrangement of atoms but have the same connectivity of atoms are called:
**Answer:** Stereoisomers have the same molecular formula and sequence of bonded atoms but differ in the three-dimensional orientation of their atoms in space.

**Q1963.** Which type of isomerism is exemplified by the difference between an enol form and a keto form?
**Answer:** Tautomerism is a specific type of functional group isomerism involving the rapid interconversion of two isomers through the migration of a hydrogen atom and a rearrangement of pi bonds, most commonly seen in keto-enol tautomerism.

**Q1964.** Which of the following processes is used to break down larger hydrocarbon molecules into smaller, more valuable hydrocarbons, typically done at high temperatures or with catalysts?
**Answer:** Cracking is the process of breaking larger, less valuable hydrocarbon molecules into smaller, more useful ones (like gasoline components) through heat (thermal cracking) or catalysts (catalytic cracking).

**Q1965.** The process that converts straight-chain hydrocarbons into branched-chain and cyclic hydrocarbons to improve the octane number of gasoline is known as:
**Answer:** Reforming is a process used to convert straight-chain alkanes, which have low octane numbers, into branched-chain alkanes and cyclic aromatic compounds, which have higher octane numbers, thereby improving fuel quality.

**Q1966.** What is the primary product obtained from the destructive distillation of coal?
**Answer:** Destructive distillation of coal yields coke, coal gas, ammoniacal liquor, and coal tar. Coal tar is a complex mixture of organic compounds and is a significant source of various chemicals.

**Q1967.** Which statement about sigma (σ) and pi (π) bonds in organic molecules is correct?
**Answer:** A single bond consists of one sigma bond. A double bond consists of one sigma and one pi bond. A triple bond consists of one sigma and two pi bonds.

**Q1968.** The hybridization of carbon in an alkane (containing only single bonds) is:
**Answer:** In alkanes, each carbon atom forms four single covalent bonds and is sp³ hybridized, resulting in a tetrahedral geometry around the carbon atom.

**Q1969.** In a molecule with a carbon-carbon triple bond (e.g., ethyne), the hybridization of each carbon atom is:
**Answer:** Carbon atoms involved in a triple bond are sp hybridized, leading to a linear geometry around the triple bond.

**Q1970.** Which of the following organic compounds is known to cause knocking in internal combustion engines and was historically used as an antiknock agent despite its environmental disadvantages?
**Answer:** Tetraethyl lead (TEL) was widely used as an antiknock additive in gasoline to improve its octane rating, but its use has been phased out due to environmental concerns about lead pollution.

**Q1971.** The main component of natural gas, which is a low-boiling hydrocarbon, is:
**Answer:** Methane (CH₄) is the simplest hydrocarbon and constitutes the largest proportion of natural gas.

**Q1972.** The term "petroleum" primarily refers to:
**Answer:** Petroleum is a broad term encompassing both crude oil, which is directly extracted from the earth, and the various refined products obtained from it, including gasoline, kerosene, diesel, etc.

**Q1973.** Apart from serving as a fuel, natural gas is also significantly used in Pakistan for:
**Answer:** Natural gas is a crucial raw material and energy source for various industries, including the production of fertilizers (e.g., urea) and cement.
