### Hydrocarbons (66 Questions)

**Q1976.** The IUPAC name for CH₂=CH-CH₂-CH₃ is:
 **A.** Either a or b ✓
**Answer:** Either a or b

**Q1977.** Which type of hydrocarbon is characterized by a general formula of CₙH₂ₙ?
**Answer:** Aromatic hydrocarbons

**Q1978.** What is the correct IUPAC name for HC≡C-CH₂-CH₃?
 **A.** Both a and b ✓
**Answer:** Both a and b

**Q1979.** The term "aliphatic" in organic chemistry primarily refers to hydrocarbons that are:
 **A.** Cyclic and conjugated
 **B.** Containing a benzene ring
 **C.** Only straight-chain compounds
 **D.** Open-chain or cyclic, but not aromatic ✓
**Answer:** Open-chain or cyclic, but not aromatic

**Q1980.** What type of bond cleavage occurs during the initiation step of a free radical halogenation of alkanes?
 **A.** Heterolytic cleavage
 **B.** Ionic cleavage
 **C.** Polar cleavage
 **D.** Homolytic cleavage ✓
**Answer:** Homolytic cleavage

**Q1981.** In the free radical chlorination of methane, which species is responsible for abstracting a hydrogen atom from methane?
 **A.** Chlorine molecule (Cl₂)
 **B.** Methyl radical (•CH₃)
 **C.** Hydrogen chloride (HCl)
 **D.** Chlorine radical (Cl•) ✓
**Answer:** Chlorine radical (Cl•)

**Q1982.** Which of the following conditions is typically required to initiate the free radical halogenation of alkanes?
 **A.** High pressure
 **B.** Presence of a strong acid
 **C.** Presence of a noble gas
 **D.** UV light or high temperature ✓
**Answer:** UV light or high temperature

**Q1983.** The reaction of ethane with chlorine in the presence of UV light primarily produces:
 **A.** Ethylene
 **B.** Ethyne
 **C.** Benzene
 **D.** Chloroethane ✓
**Answer:** Chloroethane

**Q1984.** In the free radical mechanism for the halogenation of alkanes, what is the role of UV light or heat?
 **A.** To break C-H bonds heterolytically
 **B.** To accelerate the termination steps
 **C.** To make the alkane more polar
 **D.** To provide energy for the homolytic cleavage of the halogen molecule ✓
**Answer:** To provide energy for the homolytic cleavage of the halogen molecule

**Q1985.** Which of the following best describes a free radical?
 **A.** An ion with a positive charge
 **B.** A molecule with a lone pair of electrons
 **C.** An atom with a full octet of electrons
 **D.** A species with an unpaired electron ✓
**Answer:** A species with an unpaired electron

**Q1986.** The general mechanism of free radical substitution in alkanes involves three main steps. What is the correct order of these steps?
 **A.** Propagation, Initiation, Termination
 **B.** Initiation, Termination, Propagation
 **C.** Termination, Propagation, Initiation
 **D.** Initiation, Propagation, Termination ✓
**Answer:** Initiation, Propagation, Termination

**Q1987.** Why are alkanes generally unreactive towards polar reagents but readily undergo free radical substitution?
 **A.** Because they are highly polar molecules.
 **B.** Because they have delocalized pi electrons.
 **C.** Because they readily form carbocations.
 **D.** Because C-C and C-H bonds are non-polar ✓
**Answer:** Because C-C and C-H bonds are non-polar

**Q1988.** What is the approximate bond angle around each carbon atom in ethene (C₂H₄)?
 **A.** 109.5°
 **B.** 180°
 **C.** 90°
 **D.** 120° ✓
**Answer:** 120°

**Q1989.** The double bond in ethene consists of which types of bonds?
 **A.** Two sigma (σ) bonds
 **B.** Two pi (π) bonds
 **C.** One sigma (σ) and two pi (π) bonds
 **D.** One sigma (σ) and one pi (π) bond ✓
**Answer:** One sigma (σ) and one pi (π) bond

**Q1990.** Which statement best describes the reactivity of alkenes compared to alkanes?
 **A.** Alkenes are less reactive due to their stable single bonds.
 **B.** Alkenes are more reactive due to the presence of a strong pi (π) bond.
 **C.** Alkenes have similar reactivity to alkanes.
 **D.** Alkenes are more reactive due to the exposed and weaker pi (π) bond. ✓
**Answer:** Alkenes are more reactive due to the exposed and weaker pi (π) bond.

**Q1991.** Compounds that have the same molecular formula but different structural formulas are known as:
 **A.** Homologs
 **B.** Allotropes
 **C.** Polymers
 **D.** Isomers ✓
**Answer:** Isomers

**Q1992.** Which type of isomerism involves differences in the arrangement of atoms within the molecule due to restricted rotation around a double bond?
 **A.** Chain isomerism
 **B.** Position isomerism
 **C.** Functional group isomerism
 **D.** Geometric (cis-trans) isomerism ✓
**Answer:** Geometric (cis-trans) isomerism

**Q1993.** Dehydration of ethanol (CH₃CH₂OH) using concentrated H₂SO₄ primarily yields:
 **A.** Ethane
 **B.** Ethyne
 **C.** Dimethyl ether
 **D.** Ethene ✓
**Answer:** Ethene

**Q1994.** Dehydrohalogenation of an alkyl halide (RX) involves the removal of:
 **A.** Hydrogen and oxygen
 **B.** Halogen only
 **C.** Hydrogen only
 **D.** Hydrogen and a halogen ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogen and a halogen

**Q1995.** The addition of hydrogen to ethene in the presence of a catalyst like Ni or Pt is called:
 **A.** Halogenation
 **B.** Hydration
 **C.** Polymerization
 **D.** Hydrogenation ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogenation

**Q1996.** According to Markovnikov's Rule, when an unsymmetrical reagent like HBr adds to an unsymmetrical alkene, the hydrogen atom attaches to the carbon atom that:
 **A.** Has fewer hydrogen atoms already
 **B.** Is more substituted
 **C.** Is bonded to the bromine atom
 **D.** Has more hydrogen atoms already ✓
**Answer:** Has more hydrogen atoms already

**Q1997.** The reaction of ethene with water in the presence of an acid catalyst (H₂SO₄) to form ethanol is known as:
 **A.** Hydrogenation
 **B.** Halogenation
 **C.** Polymerization
 **D.** Hydration ✓
**Answer:** Hydration

**Q1998.** What is the product formed when ethene reacts with bromine water (Br₂/H₂O)?
 **A.** 1,2-Dibromoethane
 **B.** Bromoethane
 **C.** Ethane
 **D.** 2-Bromoethanol ✓
**Answer:** 2-Bromoethanol

**Q1999.** The polymerization of ethene leads to the formation of:
 **A.** Polypropylene
 **B.** Polystyrene
 **C.** Polyvinyl chloride
 **D.** Polyethylene ✓
**Answer:** Polyethylene

**Q2000.** Which of the following is an example of a structural isomer of but-1-ene?
 **A.** Butane
 **B.** Cyclobutane
 **C.** But-2-ene
 **D.** 2-Methylpropene ✓
**Answer:** 2-Methylpropene

**Q2001.** When ethene undergoes halogenation with Cl₂, what is the primary product?
 **A.** Chloroethane
 **B.** 1,1-Dichloroethane
 **C.** 2-Chloroethanol
 **D.** 1,2-Dichloroethane ✓
**Answer:** 1,2-Dichloroethane

**Q2002.** The addition of HCl to ethene yields:
 **A.** Ethane
 **B.** Chloroethene
 **C.** 1,2-Dichloroethane
 **D.** Chloroethane ✓
**Answer:** Chloroethane

**Q2003.** Why do alkenes typically undergo addition reactions rather than substitution reactions?
 **A.** Because they are saturated compounds.
 **B.** Because the π bond is strong and resistant to cleavage.
 **C.** Because they readily form free radicals.
 **D.** Because the π electrons are exposed and easily attacked by electrophiles. ✓
**Answer:** Because the π electrons are exposed and easily attacked by electrophiles.

**Q2004.** Which of the following catalysts is commonly used for the hydrogenation of alkenes?
 **A.** Zinc
 **B.** Sodium hydroxide
 **C.** Sulfuric acid
 **D.** Platinum or Palladium or Nickel ✓
**Answer:** Platinum or Palladium or Nickel

**Q2005.** The structural isomerism exhibited by but-1-ene and 2-methylpropene is an example of:
 **A.** Position isomerism
 **B.** Functional group isomerism
 **C.** Metamerism
 **D.** Chain isomerism ✓
**Answer:** Chain isomerism

**Q2006.** What type of reaction is the formation of ethanol from ethene and steam in the presence of an acid catalyst?
 **A.** Dehydrogenation
 **B.** Dehydration
 **C.** Substitution
 **D.** Hydration ✓
**Answer:** Hydration

**Q2007.** Which reagent is used for the preparation of an alkene from a vicinal dihalide?
 **A.** Aqueous KOH
 **B.** Alcoholic KOH
 **C.** Conc. H₂SO₄
 **D.** Zinc dust ✓
**Answer:** Zinc dust

**Q2008.** According to molecular orbital theory, the benzene molecule consists of a planar hexagonal ring formed by:
 **A.** sp³ hybridized carbon atoms
 **B.** sp hybridized carbon atoms
 **C.** Unhybridized carbon atoms
 **D.** sp² hybridized carbon atoms ✓
**Answer:** sp² hybridized carbon atoms

**Q2009.** The delocalization of pi electrons in benzene results in a special stability known as:
 **A.** Inductive effect
 **B.** Hyperconjugation
 **C.** Aromaticity
 **D.** Resonance energy ✓
**Answer:** Resonance energy

**Q2010.** Compared to alkenes, benzene primarily undergoes:
 **A.** Addition reactions
 **B.** Elimination reactions
 **C.** Rearrangement reactions
 **D.** Substitution reactions ✓
**Answer:** Substitution reactions

**Q2011.** Which of the following is an example of an addition reaction of benzene?
 **A.** Nitration
 **B.** Sulfonation
 **C.** Halogenation
 **D.** Hydrogenation ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogenation

**Q2012.** The mechanism of electrophilic substitution in benzene involves the initial attack of an electrophile on the benzene ring, forming a:
 **A.** Carbanion
 **B.** Free radical
 **C.** Zwitterion
 **D.** Carbocation ✓
**Answer:** Carbocation

**Q2013.** In the nitration of benzene, the electrophile that attacks the benzene ring is:
 **A.** NO₂
 **B.** NO₃⁻
 **C.** HNO₃
 **D.** NO₂⁺ ✓
**Answer:** NO₂⁺

**Q2014.** What is the catalyst commonly used in the electrophilic halogenation of benzene (e.g., chlorination or bromination)?
 **A.** Ni or Pt
 **B.** Conc. H₂SO₄
 **C.** UV light
 **D.** FeCl₃ ✓
**Answer:** FeCl₃

**Q2015.** The Friedel-Crafts alkylation reaction of benzene introduces an alkyl group. What is the typical catalyst for this reaction?
 **A.** NaOH
 **B.** KMnO₄
 **C.** H₂O
 **D.** Anhydrous AlCl₃ ✓
**Answer:** Anhydrous AlCl₃

**Q2016.** The sulfonation of benzene involves the introduction of a -SO₃H group. What is the active electrophile in this reaction?
 **A.** SO₃²⁻
 **B.** H₂SO₄
 **C.** HSO₃⁻
 **D.** SO₃ ✓
**Answer:** SO₃

**Q2017.** Which of the following statements about benzene is FALSE?
 **A.** All C-C bond lengths in benzene are identical.
 **B.** Benzene is more stable than a hypothetical cyclohexatriene.
 **C.** Benzene has a planar structure.
 **D.** Benzene readily undergoes addition reactions. ✓
**Answer:** Benzene readily undergoes addition reactions.

**Q2018.** When methylbenzene (toluene) undergoes nitration, the incoming nitro group (-NO₂) is directed primarily to which positions?
 **A.** Meta positions
 **B.** Only ortho position
 **C.** Only para position
 **D.** Ortho and para positions ✓
**Answer:** Ortho and para positions

**Q2019.** What is the product formed when benzene reacts with an acyl chloride (RCOCl) in the presence of anhydrous AlCl₃?
 **A.** Alkylbenzene
 **B.** Carboxylic acid
 **C.** Phenol
 **D.** Phenylketone ✓
**Answer:** Phenylketone

**Q2020.** Which of the following groups is a deactivating and meta-directing group in electrophilic aromatic substitution?
 **A.** -OH (hydroxyl)
 **B.** -NH₂ (amino)
 **C.** -CH₃ (methyl)
 **D.** -NO₂ (nitro) ✓
**Answer:** -NO₂ (nitro)

**Q2021.** When benzene undergoes Friedel-Crafts acylation, what is the key advantage over alkylation in terms of product control?
 **A.** Acylation leads to multiple substitutions easily.
 **B.** Acylation products can be easily deacylated.
 **C.** Acylation is reversible.
 **D.** Acylation does not lead to carbocation rearrangements. ✓
**Answer:** Acylation does not lead to carbocation rearrangements.

**Q2022.** Which of the following compounds will undergo electrophilic substitution faster than benzene?
 **A.** Nitrobenzene
 **B.** Chlorobenzene
 **C.** Benzoic acid
 **D.** Toluene ✓
**Answer:** Toluene

**Q2023.** The conversion of benzene to cyclohexane under specific conditions is an example of:
 **A.** Electrophilic substitution
 **B.** Free radical substitution
 **C.** Oxidation
 **D.** Catalytic hydrogenation ✓
**Answer:** Catalytic hydrogenation

**Q2024.** What is the product of the reaction between benzene and Cl₂ in the presence of UV light and no catalyst?
 **A.** Chlorobenzene
 **B.** 1,2-Dichlorobenzene
 **C.** Benzyl chloride
 **D.** Hexachlorocyclohexane ✓
**Answer:** Hexachlorocyclohexane

**Q2025.** Which of the following is an ortho/para directing group and an activating group?
 **A.** -COOH
 **B.** -CN
 **C.** -COR
 **D.** -OCH₃ ✓
**Answer:** -OCH₃

**Q2026.** The sulfonation of benzene is reversible. What conditions favor the desulfonation ?
 **A.** Reaction with strong base
 **B.** Reaction with oxidizing agent
 **C.** Reaction with reducing agent
 **D.** Heating with dilute acid ✓
**Answer:** Heating with dilute acid

**Q2027.** When an activating group is present on the benzene ring, the rate of electrophilic substitution reaction:
 **A.** Decreases
 **B.** Remains unchanged
 **C.** Becomes zero
 **D.** Increases ✓
**Answer:** Increases

**Q2028.** Which of the following reagents is typically used for the Friedel-Crafts acylation of benzene?
 **A.** An alkyl halide and AlCl₃

 **C.** Concentrated H₂SO₄
 **D.** An acyl halide and AlCl₃ ✓
**Answer:** An acyl halide and AlCl₃

**Q2029.** The resonance hybrid structure of benzene explains that:
 **A.** It has alternating single and double bonds.
 **B.** It exists as two rapidly interconverting Kekulé structures.
 **C.** It is a saturated compound.
 **D.** All carbon-carbon bonds are identical and intermediate between single and double bonds. ✓
**Answer:** All carbon-carbon bonds are identical and intermediate between single and double bonds.

**Q2030.** Which of the following reactions of benzene is also an electrophilic substitution reaction?
 **A.** Reaction with H₂/Ni
 **B.** Reaction with H₂O/H₂SO₄
 **C.** Reaction with Cl₂/UV light
 **D.** Reaction with Br₂/FeBr₃ ✓
**Answer:** Reaction with Br₂/FeBr₃

**Q2031.** A strong deactivating group like -COOH (carboxyl) on a benzene ring will direct an incoming electrophile to which position?
 **A.** Ortho
 **B.** Para
 **C.** Ortho and para
 **D.** Meta ✓
**Answer:** Meta

**Q2032.** The oxidation of benzene under typical conditions is difficult due to its:
 **A.** High molecular weight
 **B.** Planar structure
 **C.** Non-polar nature
 **D.** Aromatic stability ✓
**Answer:** Aromatic stability

**Q2033.** Which of the following compounds is generally the most reactive towards electrophilic addition reactions?
 **A.** Ethane
 **B.** Ethyne
 **C.** Benzene
 **D.** Ethene ✓
**Answer:** Ethene

**Q2034.** The preparation of alkynes commonly involves which type of reaction?
 **A.** Addition reaction
 **B.** Substitution reaction
 **C.** Rearrangement reaction
 **D.** Elimination reaction ✓
**Answer:** Elimination reaction

**Q2035.** Which statement best describes the acidity of terminal alkynes?
 **A.** They are less acidic than alkanes and alkenes.
 **B.** They are highly acidic, comparable to strong mineral acids.
 **C.** They are neutral compounds.
 **D.** They are more acidic than alkanes and alkenes. ✓
**Answer:** They are more acidic than alkanes and alkenes.

**Q2036.** The complete hydrogenation of acetylene in the presence of a catalyst like Ni or Pt yields:
 **A.** Ethene
 **B.** Methane
 **C.** Butane
 **D.** Ethane ✓
**Answer:** Ethane

**Q2037.** Partial hydrogenation of alkynes to form cis-alkenes is achieved using which catalyst?
 **A.** Na in liquid NH₃
 **B.** Lindlar's catalyst ✓
**Answer:** Lindlar's catalyst

**Q2038.** The hydration of alkynes (e.g., ethyne) in the presence of H₂SO₄ and HgSO₄ typically forms:
 **A.** Carboxylic acids
 **B.** Alkenes
 **C.** Aldehydes or ketones ✓
**Answer:** Aldehydes or ketones

**Q2039.** Which of the following alkynes can react with ammoniacal silver nitrate solution (Tollens' reagent) to form a white precipitate?
 **A.** CH₃-C≡C-CH₃ (2-Butyne)
 **B.** CH₂=CH-C≡CH (Vinylacetylene)
 **C.** CH₂=CH-CH=CH₂ (1,3-Butadiene)
 **D.** CH₃-CH₂-C≡CH (1-Butyne) ✓
**Answer:** CH₃-CH₂-C≡CH (1-Butyne)

**Q2040.** A reaction in which an atom or group of atoms is replaced by another atom or group of atoms in a molecule is known as a:
 **A.** Addition reaction
 **B.** Elimination reaction
 **C.** Rearrangement reaction
 **D.** Substitution reaction ✓
**Answer:** Substitution reaction

**Q2041.** Which type of reaction involves the joining of two or more molecules to form a single larger molecule, typically by breaking a pi bond?
 **A.** Substitution reaction
 **B.** Elimination reaction
 **C.** Polymerization reaction
 **D.** Addition reaction ✓
**Answer:** Addition reaction

### Industrial Chemistry (75 Questions)

**Q2042.** Which component in a dye is primarily responsible for its ability to bind to a fabric?
**Answer:** Auxochromes are functional groups that intensify color and also help the dye to fix onto the fabric by interacting with it.

**Q2043.** Bismarck Brown, used for dyeing wool and in boot polishes, belongs to which class of dyes based on its structure?
**Answer:** Bismarck Brown contains two azo groups (-N=N-) linking three aromatic rings, making it an azo dye.

**Q2044.** What is the color of the indicator phenolphthalein in a basic medium?
**Answer:** In a basic solution, the structure of phenolphthalein changes to a quinoid form, which imparts a distinct red or pink color.

**Q2045.** Vat dyes, such as Indigo, are applied to fabric in a special way. What is the key first step in the vat dyeing process?
**Answer:** Vat dyes are insoluble, so they must be chemically reduced (often with sodium hydrosulphide) to a soluble, colorless form that can penetrate the fibers.

**Q2046.** Malachite Green is an example of a triarylmethane dye. What is the characteristic structural feature of this class?
**Answer:** This accurately describes the core structure of triarylmethane dyes, where a central carbon is bonded to three aromatic rings, one of which is in the quinoid form.

**Q2047.** Which type of dye is applied to a fabric with the help of salts of aluminum or chromium, which form an insoluble complex?
**Answer:** Mordants are metallic salts that are applied to the fabric to form a coordination complex, which in turn binds the dye to the fabric.

**Q2048.** Congo Red is prepared by coupling tetrazotised benzidine with two molecules of which compound?
**Answer:** The structure of Congo Red confirms that two molecules of naphthionic acid are coupled with the tetrazotised benzidine.

**Q2049.** Azoic dyeing, also known as ingrain dyeing, is a method that involves:
**Answer:** In this process, the fabric is soaked in a coupling agent and then in a solution of a diazotized amine, forming the final dye 'in-grain' (inside the fabric).

**Q2050.** Which of the following is an example of a Nitro dye, containing the -NO₂ group as a chromophore?
**Answer:** The structure of Naphthol Yellow S clearly shows nitro groups attached to the naphthalene ring system.

**Q2051.** The primary chromophore in azo dyes is the:
**Answer:** This diazo linkage (-N=N-) is the defining chromophore for the entire class of azo dyes.

**Q2052.** The polymerization of ethylene to form polyethylene is an example of what type of polymerization?
**Answer:** In this reaction, ethylene monomers add to each other in such a way that the polymer contains all the atoms of the monomer unit. The double bond breaks to form new single bonds.

**Q2053.** What is the defining characteristic of a condensation polymer?
**Answer:** This is the hallmark of condensation polymerization, where monomers join together by 'condensing out' a small molecule, such as water.

**Q2054.** Nylon-6,6 is a polyamide formed by the condensation reaction between which two monomers?
**Answer:** Nylon-6,6 derives its name from its two six-carbon monomers: adipic acid (a dicarboxylic acid) and hexamethylene diamine (a diamine).

**Q2055.** Which of the following is a key characteristic of a thermosetting polymer?
**Answer:** Heating causes extensive cross-linking between polymer chains, forming a rigid, infusible solid that cannot be softened by reheating.

**Q2056.** The reaction by which monomers are converted into polymers is known as:
**Answer:** This is the general term for the process of joining many small molecules (monomers) together to form a very large molecule (polymer).

**Q2057.** A polymer synthesized from more than one kind of monomer is called a:
**Answer:** The prefix 'co-' indicates that it is formed from two or more different monomers working together.

**Q2058.** During the addition polymerization of an alkene monomer, what happens to its double bond?
**Answer:** The electrons from the weaker pi bond are used to form the strong sigma bonds that constitute the polymer chain.

**Q2059.** Which of the following is a common use for PVC?
**Answer:** PVC is a versatile and durable plastic used in a wide range of building and consumer products, including floor covering, roofing, and imitation leather.

**Q2060.** A polymer that softens on heating and becomes hard again on cooling is known as:
**Answer:** The prefix 'thermo-' refers to heat, and 'plastic' refers to its ability to be molded. These polymers can be repeatedly softened by heat and hardened by cooling.

**Q2061.** Adhesives that are made from sources like vegetable matter, starch, or animal skin are classified as:
**Answer:** These adhesives are derived from biological or inorganic mineral sources and are often referred to as bioadhesives.

**Q2062.** How do drying adhesives, such as white glue and rubber cement, harden?
**Answer:** These adhesives consist of a polymer dissolved or suspended in a solvent. The bond forms as the solvent evaporates.

**Q2063.** Hot glue, which is applied with a glue gun, is an example of which type of adhesive?
**Answer:** Also known as hot melt adhesives, these are thermoplastics that are melted, applied, and then harden as they cool to room temperature.

**Q2064.** Elastomers, thermoplastics, and thermosetting adhesives are all examples of what broad category of adhesives?
**Answer:** These are all classifications of adhesives created through chemical synthesis.

**Q2065.** UV and light curing adhesives are composed of low or medium molecular weight:
**Answer:** These adhesives consist essentially of low or medium molecular weight resins that polymerize upon exposure to light.

**Q2066.** What is the primary function of an adhesive?
**Answer:** This is the fundamental definition of an adhesive – a compound that adheres or bonds two items together.

**Q2067.** An example of a natural adhesive derived from starch is:
**Answer:** Dextrin is a type of adhesive made from starch, which is a natural vegetable matter.

**Q2068.** Which type of adhesive is popular for crafts due to its ease of use with a wide range of materials?
**Answer:** Hot melt adhesives have become popular for crafts because of their ease of use and the wide range of common materials to which they can adhere.

**Q2069.** The term 'bioadhesives' often refers to which category of adhesives?
**Answer:** Natural adhesives derived from biological sources are often referred to as bioadhesives.

**Q2070.** The preparation of Methyl Orange involves diazotising which compound first?
**Answer:** The synthesis of Methyl Orange starts with the diazotization of sulphanilic acid.

**Q2071.** Which of the following dyes is also well-known as an acid-base indicator?
**Answer:** Although classified as a triarylmethane dye, phenolphthalein is better known for its use as an acid-base indicator.

**Q2072.** Disperse dyes are primarily used for which type of fabric?
**Answer:** Disperse dyes are used with modern synthetic fabrics such as nylon, orlon, polyester, and cellulose acetate.

**Q2073.** The para quinoid structure is the characteristic chromophore in which class of dyes?
**Answer:** The para quinoid chromophore is present in anthracene-type dyes, such as Alizarin.

**Q2074.** What is the function of an auxochrome like -OH or -NH₂ in a dye molecule?
**Answer:** The polar auxochrome makes the dye water-soluble and binds it to the fabric by interacting with oppositely charged groups.

**Q2075.** Which of the following is NOT a characteristic of an addition polymer?
**Answer:** Addition polymerization is a chain-growth process. Step-growth is characteristic of condensation polymerization.

**Q2076.** The monomers adipic acid and hexamethylene diamine each contain how many carbon atoms?
**Answer:** Nylon-6,6 derives its name from its two starting materials, both of which have six carbons.

**Q2077.** What type of chemical bond is formed between the monomers in Nylon-6,6?
**Answer:** Nylon-6,6 is a polyamide, formed by the reaction between a carboxylic acid and an amine to create an amide linkage (-CONH-).

**Q2078.** A polymer made from a single type of monomer, such as polyethylene from ethylene, is called a:
**Answer:** Polymers which are synthesized from only one kind of monomer are called homopolymers.

**Q2079.** The synthesis of vinyl chloride monomer can be achieved by reacting acetylene with which substance in the presence of HgCl₂?
**Answer:** Vinyl chloride is obtained from acetylene by treatment with HCl in the presence of HgCl₂.

**Q2080.** Which of these is NOT a typical use for Nylon-6,6?
**Answer:** Nylon-6,6 is used for fibers, filaments, bristles, and molded objects. Floor covering and gramophone records are uses for PVC.

**Q2081.** The primary difference between a thermoplastic and a thermosetting polymer lies in their:
**Answer:** A thermoplastic softens on heating, while a thermosetting polymer becomes hard on heating and cannot be softened again.

**Q2082.** Addition polymerization is also known as:
**Answer:** Addition polymers are also referred to as Chain-Growth Polymers.

**Q2083.** Condensation polymerization is also known as:
**Answer:** Condensation polymers are also referred to as step-growth polymers.

**Q2084.** Which of these adhesive types works by a physical process rather than a chemical one?
**Answer:** Hot melt adhesives are thermoplastics that are applied hot and simply harden as they cool, which is a physical change of state.

**Q2085.** An adhesive made from inorganic mineral sources would be classified as:
**Answer:** Natural adhesives are made from inorganic mineral sources or biological sources.

**Q2086.** White glue, a common household and school adhesive, belongs to which class?
**Answer:** White glue and rubber cements are members of the drying adhesive family, which harden as their solvent evaporates.

**Q2087.** What is a major advantage of modern synthetic adhesives over many traditional natural adhesives?
**Answer:** Some modern adhesives are extremely strong and are becoming increasingly important in modern construction and industry.

**Q2088.** The process of using a glue gun involves which type of adhesive?
**Answer:** The glue gun melts the solid hot melt adhesive and allows the liquid to pass through onto the material where it solidifies.

**Q2089.** Mordant Green 4 is a dye that contains which chromophore?
**Answer:** The -NO group is the chromophore in Nitroso dyes, and Mordant Green 4 is given as an example.

**Q2090.** Para Red was the first azo dye prepared. Its synthesis involves the reaction of diazotized p-nitroaniline with what coupling compound?
**Answer:** Para red is obtained by the reaction of diazotized p-nitroaniline with β-naphthol.

**Q2091.** The color of Methyl Orange changes from yellow in base to red in acid due to:
**Answer:** The change in color of Methyl Orange is due to the change in the structure of the ions.

**Q2092.** What happens to the colorless, soluble form of Indigo ('Indigo White') when the dyed cloth is hung in the air?
**Answer:** When the cloth is hung in air, the colorless compound is oxidized back to the insoluble colored dye.

**Q2093.** The synthesis of phenolphthalein involves heating phthalic anhydride with two moles of what substance?
**Answer:** Phenolphthalein is prepared by heating phthalic anhydride (1 mole) and phenol (two moles).

**Q2094.** Direct dyes, like Martius Yellow, can be applied directly to fibers like wool and silk because:
**Answer:** A direct dye contains an acidic or basic auxochrome which combines with the opposite polar group present in the chemical structure of the fiber.

**Q2095.** Which of the following is NOT a main type of dye when classified by structure according to the provided text?
**Answer:** Azo, Anthraquinone, and Indigo dyes are all classifications by structure. Vat Dyes are a classification based on the method of application.

**Q2096.** The chemical stability of a dye, meaning it won't wash out with soap or fade in sunlight, is referred to as its:
**Answer:** The dye must be 'fast' or chemically stable so that the color will not wash out with soap and water, or fade on exposure to sunlight.

**Q2097.** The structure of Alizarin is based on which parent aromatic hydrocarbon?
**Answer:** Alizarin is a typical anthraquinone dye, which is an anthracene-type dye.

**Q2098.** The 'lake' formed during mordant dyeing is:
**Answer:** The mordant forms an insoluble coordination complex between the fiber and the dye, which appears in the form of lakes.

**Q2099.** What is the fundamental difference between homopolymers and copolymers?
**Answer:** Homopolymers are synthesized from only one kind of monomer, whereas copolymers are prepared from more than one kind.

**Q2100.** The process of forming polyethylene can be catalyzed by Ziegler catalysts, which consist of trialkyl aluminum and what other compound?
**Answer:** Ziegler Catalysts consist of a trialkyl aluminum and titanium tetrachloride.

**Q2101.** In the abbreviated formula for an addition polymer like polyethylene, -[CH₂-CH₂]-n, what does the 'n' represent?
**Answer:** 'n' is the degree of polymerization, indicating that the monomer unit in the brackets is repeated many times to form the long chain polymer.

**Q2102.** Which of these is NOT a condensation polymer?
**Answer:** PVC is formed by the addition polymerization of vinyl chloride monomers. Nylon and polyesters are explicitly mentioned as condensation polymers.

**Q2103.** The key difference in the final structure of thermosetting polymers compared to thermoplastic polymers is the presence of:
**Answer:** Thermosetting polymers become hard on heating and cannot be softened, which is due to the formation of a rigid, cross-linked 3D network structure.

**Q2104.** To create a thermosetting polymer, it is beneficial to use:
**Answer:** To form a cross-linked network instead of linear chains, at least one of the monomers must have more than two functional groups to allow for branching. This leads to a thermoset structure.

**Q2105.** What is the structural formula of adipic acid, a monomer of Nylon-6,6?
**Answer:** Adipic acid is listed as a reactant for Nylon-6,6 with the formula HOOC(CH₂)₄COOH.

**Q2106.** What is the structural formula of hexamethylene diamine, a monomer of Nylon-6,6?
**Answer:** Hexamethylene diamine is listed as a reactant for Nylon-6,6 with the formula H₂N(CH₂)₆NH₂.

**Q2107.** The repeating unit in polyethylene is:
**Answer:** The ethylene monomer (CH₂=CH₂) polymerizes to form the repeating unit -CH₂-CH₂-.

**Q2108.** The repeating unit in polyvinyl chloride is:
**Answer:** The vinyl chloride monomer (CH₂=CHCl) polymerizes to form the repeating unit -CH₂-CH-Cl.

**Q2109.** An adhesive that is a thermoplastic applied in a molten state is known as a:
**Answer:** 'Hot melt' adhesives are thermoplastics that are applied hot and allowed to harden as they cool.

**Q2110.** Which of the following is an example of a synthetic adhesive?
**Answer:** Elastomers, thermoplastics, and thermosetting adhesives are examples of synthetic adhesives.

**Q2111.** The hardening of rubber cement is an example of which process?
**Answer:** Rubber cement is a member of the drying adhesive family, which hardens as the solvent evaporates.

**Q2112.** Which of these is NOT listed as a category of synthetic adhesives?
**Answer:** Elastomers, thermoplastics, and thermosetting adhesives are listed as synthetic. Bioadhesives are referred to as natural adhesives.

**Q2113.** What is the key functional group that gives an auxochrome its ability to bind to a protein fiber like wool?
**Answer:** The polar auxochrome binds the dye to the fabric by interaction with the oppositely charged groups of the fiber structure, such as the -NH₂ group of wool.

**Q2114.** The reaction to form Nylon-6,6 eliminates a small molecule. What is this molecule?
**Answer:** Condensation polymers are formed with the elimination of simple molecules such as H₂O. The reaction image for Nylon-6,6 explicitly shows -H₂O.

**Q2115.** If you mix two different types of monomers, one with two acid groups and one with two alcohol groups, what type of polymer will you form?
**Answer:** Polyesters are a main type of condensation polymer. The reaction between an acid and an alcohol forms an ester linkage. Since two different monomers are used, it will be a copolymer.

**Q2116.** The main purpose of a mordant in dyeing is to:
**Answer:** The mordant forms an insoluble coordination complex between the fiber and the dye and binds the two.

### Liquids (37 Questions)

**Q2117.** Why does CHCl₃ (Chloroform) exist in a liquid state?
 **A.** Dipole-dipole and London dispersion forces
 **B.** Dipole-induced dipole forces
 **C.** Hydrogen bonding
 **D.** dipole-dipole forces ✓
 **E.** Which of the following has the highest boiling point?
 **F.** NH₃
 **G.** CHCl₃
 **H.** Xe
 **I.** Br₂
**Answer:** dipole-dipole forces

**Q2118.** The dominant Van der Waals force in NH₃ is:
 **A.** London dispersion forces
 **B.** Dipole-induced dipole forces
 **C.** Dipole-dipole forces
 **D.** Hydrogen bonding ✓
**Answer:** Hydrogen bonding

**Q2119.** Liquids have no definite shape because:
 **A.** The intermolecular forces in liquids are weaker than in solids
 **B.** Liquid molecules have kinetic energy less than gases
 **C.** All statements are correct
 **D.** The molecules of liquid are in constant motion, sliding over each other ✓
**Answer:** The molecules of liquid are in constant motion, sliding over each other

**Q2120.** Why is propanone (acetone) miscible in water?
 **A.** Both are polar molecules
 **B.** Hydrogen bonding occurs between them
 **C.** Dipole-dipole attraction exists between them
 **D.** All of these ✓
**Answer:** All of these

**Q2121.** Strong dipole-dipole forces among liquid molecules are responsible for:
 **A.** Very low boiling point
 **B.** Very low heat of vaporization
 **C.** All are correct
 **D.** Very high heat of vaporization ✓
**Answer:** Very high heat of vaporization

**Q2122.** Which of the following is the correct order of increasing intermolecular interactions?
 **A.** Hydrogen bonding, London forces, Dipole-dipole
 **B.** London forces, Hydrogen bonding, Dipole-dipole
 **C.** Dipole-dipole, London forces, Hydrogen bonding
 **D.** London forces, Dipole-dipole, Hydrogen bonding ✓
**Answer:** London forces, Dipole-dipole, Hydrogen bonding

**Q2123.** Which of the following has the strongest intermolecular forces?
 **A.** Hydrogen (H₂)
 **B.** Chlorine (Cl₂)
 **C.** Methane (CH₄)
 **D.** Iodine (I₂) ✓
**Answer:** Iodine (I₂)

**Q2124.** What type of force exists between ions and water molecules?
 **A.** Dipole-induced dipole forces
 **B.** Dipole-dipole forces
 **C.** London dispersion forces
 **D.** Ion-dipole forces ✓
**Answer:** Ion-dipole forces

**Q2125.** Which forces are present between all types of atoms and molecules?
 **A.** Hydrogen bonding
 **B.** Dipole-dipole forces
 **C.** Dipole-induced dipole forces
 **D.** London dispersion forces ✓
**Answer:** London dispersion forces

**Q2126.** The attractive forces between the partial positive end of one molecule and the partial negative end of another molecule are called:
 **A.** Ion-dipole forces
 **B.** London dispersion forces
 **C.** Debye forces
 **D.** Dipole-dipole forces ✓
**Answer:** Dipole-dipole forces

**Q2127.** Why do liquids take the shape of their container?
 **A.** Liquids do not have a definite volume
 **B.** Liquids are highly compressible
 **C.** Liquid molecules can slide over each other
 **D.** Liquids do not have a definite shape ✓
**Answer:** Liquids do not have a definite shape

**Q2128.** Nature of bonding affects the properties like:
 **A.** Solubility
 **B.** Melting, boiling points and isomerism
 **C.** Reaction kinetics
 **D.** All of these ✓
**Answer:** All of these

**Q2129.** Vapour pressure of water at 0°C is 4.579 torr, and at 10°C it is 9.209 torr, so increase in vapour pressure from 0 C to 10 C is 4.630 torr  What will be the change in vapour pressure from 90°C to 100°C?
 **A.** 4.630 torr
 **B.** < 4.630 torr
 **C.** v.p remains same at all temperatures
 **D.** > 4.630 torr ✓
**Answer:** > 4.630 torr

**Q2130.** Which of the following has the highest vapour pressure?
 **A.** Water
 **B.** Mercury
 **C.** Glycerol
 **D.** Isopentane ✓
**Answer:** Isopentane

**Q2131.** The boiling point of water would be highest at:
 **A.** Murree
 **B.** Mount Everest
 **C.** Siachin
 **D.** Gwadar ✓
**Answer:** Gwadar

**Q2132.** Which of the following is false for evaporation?
 **A.** surface phenomenon
 **B.** continuous
 **C.** causes cooling
 **D.** exothermic ✓
**Answer:** exothermic

**Q2133.** The vapour pressure of water at 100°C is:
 **A.** 55 mm Hg
 **B.** 355 mm Hg
 **C.** 1489 mm Hg
 **D.** 760 mm Hg ✓
**Answer:** 760 mm Hg

**Q2134.** The conversion of vapours back to liquid is called:
 **A.** Crystallization
 **B.** Evaporation
 **C.** Vapourization
 **D.** Condensation ✓
**Answer:** Condensation

**Q2135.** Which of the following has highest volatility?
 **A.** Ethyl alcohol
 **B.** Water
 **C.** Ethylene glycol
 **D.** Diethyl ether ✓
**Answer:** Diethyl ether

**Q2136.** To maintain the boiling point of water at 110°C, the pressure should be:
 **A.** Between 200 torr and 760 torr
 **B.** 765 torr
 **C.** Any pressure value
 **D.** Between 760 torr and 1200 torr ✓
 **E.** If we provide very high amount of heat to a liquid its boiling point will
 **F.** Increase
 **G.** Decrease
 **H.** There will be no boiling
 **I.** Remains constant
 **J.** Evaporation is designated as a cooling process because of the reason
 **K.** It is a surface phenomenon
 **L.** It is exothermic process
 **M.** All of the above
 **N.** High energy molecules leave behind the low energy molecules and cause cooling
**Answer:** Between 760 torr and 1200 torr

**Q2137.** Vapour pressure of a liquid depends on:
 **A.** Surface area and temperature only
 **B.** Volume of the liquid
 **C.** Humidity of the liquid in the air
 **D.** Temperature and intermolecular forces ✓
**Answer:** Temperature and intermolecular forces

**Q2138.** At 1 atm pressure, liquid 1 has a boiling point lower than liquid 2. What can we predict about both liquids?
 **A.** Liquid 1 has a higher vapour pressure than liquid 2
 **B.** Liquid 1 has weaker intermolecular forces of attraction than liquid 2
 **C.** Liquid 1 is more volatile than liquid 2
 **D.** All of the above ✓
**Answer:** All of the above

**Q2139.** The distillation of a solution under reduced pressure is called:
 **A.** Fractional distillation
 **B.** Distillation
 **C.** Destructive distillation
 **D.** Vacuum distillation ✓
**Answer:** Vacuum distillation

**Q2140.** A pressure cooker reduces cooking time because:
 **A.** A large flame is used
 **B.** Heat is uniformly distributed
 **C.** Vapour pressure of the liquid decreases
 **D.** The boiling point of water rises ✓
**Answer:** The boiling point of water rises

**Q2141.** Molar heat of vaporization of water is:
 **A.** 140.6 kJ mol⁻¹
 **B.** 14.6 kJ mol⁻¹
 **C.** Zero
 **D.** 40.6 kJ mol⁻¹ ✓
**Answer:** 40.6 kJ mol⁻¹

**Q2142.** The rate of evaporation of a liquid does not depend on:
 **A.** Surface area of the liquid
 **B.** Temperature
 **C.** Intermolecular forces
 **D.** All of these ✓
**Answer:** All of these

**Q2143.** Which one of the following would cause severe burning?
 **A.** Boiling water at 90°C
 **B.** Boiling water at 80°C
 **C.** Water at 20°C
 **D.** Steam at 100°C ✓
**Answer:** Steam at 100°C

**Q2144.** The boiling point of a liquid is the temperature at which:
 **A.** The vapour pressure of the liquid is less than the atmospheric pressure
 **B.** The vapour pressure of the liquid is greater than the atmospheric pressure
 **C.** The vapour pressure of the liquid is equal to the intermolecular forces of the liquid molecules
 **D.** The vapour pressure of the liquid is equal to the atmospheric pressure ✓
**Answer:** The vapour pressure of the liquid is equal to the atmospheric pressure

**Q2145.** The strength of hydrogen bonding is:

 **C.** 20 times more than an ionic bond
 **D.** 20 times less than a covalent bond ✓

**Q2146.** When ammonia is dissolved in water, the number of hydrogen bonds formed by ammonia is:
 **A.** 2
 **B.** 3
 **C.** 4
 **D.** 1 ✓
**Answer:** 1

**Q2147.** Lower alcohols are soluble in water because:
 **A.** Dipole-induced dipole forces
 **B.** Low electronegativity difference between C and H
 **C.** All of the above
 **D.** Intermolecular hydrogen bonding ✓
**Answer:** Intermolecular hydrogen bonding

**Q2148.** The strongest hydrogen bond is:
 **A.** O—H
 **B.** O—F
 **C.** N—H
 **D.** F—H ✓
**Answer:** F—H

**Q2149.** When two ice cubes are pressed against each other, they stick due to:
 **A.** Covalent attraction

**Answer:** Hydrogen bond formation

**Q2150.** Which of the following can form hydrogen bonding among its molecules more prominently?

**Q2151.** Hydrogen bonding is involved in:

 **F.** H₂O > HF > HCl > NH₃
 **G.** HF > H₂O > HCl > NH₃
 **H.** HF > H₂O > NH₃ > HCl
 **I.** H₂O > HF > NH₃ > HCl
**Answer:** Empty space present in the structure of ice

### Macromolecules (71 Questions)

**Q2154.** Which of the following elements is always present in proteins in addition to carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen?
**Answer:** All proteins contain the elements carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, and nitrogen. Other elements like phosphorus, iron, copper, iodine, manganese, and sulfur may also be present in traces.

**Q2155.** Proteins are high molecular weight macromolecules that yield what upon complete hydrolysis?
**Answer:** Proteins are very high molecular weight macromolecules. All proteins yield amino acids upon complete hydrolysis.

**Q2156.** Simple proteins are characterized by yielding only which of the following upon hydrolysis?
**Answer:** Simple proteins on hydrolysis yield only amino acids or their derivatives.

**Q2157.** Which of the following is an example of a simple protein?
**Answer:** Examples of simple proteins mentioned include albumins, globulins, legumin, and collagen. Lactoglobulin is specifically stated to be found in muscles.

**Q2158.** Compound or conjugated proteins are formed when the protein part is attached to a:
**Answer:** In compound or conjugated proteins, the protein is attached or conjugated to some non-protein groups which are called prosthetic groups.

**Q2159.** A lipoprotein is an example of a conjugated protein where the protein is conjugated with:
**Answer:** Lipoproteins are conjugated with lipid substances like lecithin, cholesterol, and fatty acids.

**Q2160.** Which class of protein includes substances that are derived from simple and conjugated proteins, such as proteoses and peptones?
**Answer:** This class of protein includes substances which are derived from simple and conjugated proteins. For example, proteoses, enzymes, peptones, oligopeptides, polypeptides, etc.

**Q2161.** The sequence of amino acids combined in a peptide chain is referred to as the:
**Answer:** The sequence of the amino acids combined in a peptide chain is referred to as the primary structure.

**Q2162.** What is the regular coiling or zigzagging of polypeptide chains, primarily caused by hydrogen bonding, known as?
**Answer:** The secondary structure of a protein is a regular coiling or zigzagging of polypeptide chains caused by hydrogen bonding between NH and C=O groups of amino acids near each other in the chains.

**Q2163.** The three-dimensional twisting and folding of the polypeptide chain results in which level of protein structure?
**Answer:** The three-dimensional twisting and folding of the polypeptide chain results in the tertiary structure of proteins.

**Q2164.** What is the most familiar example of protein denaturation, where the change is irreversible?
**Answer:** The most familiar example of denaturation is the change that takes place in albumin, the principal component of egg white, when it is cooked. In this particular case the change is irreversible.

**Q2165.** The structure of proteins can be easily disrupted by all of the following except:
**Answer:** The structure of proteins can be disrupted easily by heat, change in pH and under strongly oxidizing or reducing conditions. Freezing to 0°C is generally not listed as a primary denaturing agent.

**Q2166.** Proteins that serve as carriers of heredity from one generation to the other are complexes of proteins with nucleic acids, known as:
**Answer:** Nucleoproteins which are complexes of proteins with nucleic acids serve as carriers of heredity from one generation to the other.

**Q2167.** Which of the following is a function of proteins, as stated in the text?
**Answer:** Proteins take an essential part in the formation of protoplasm which is the essence of all forms of life.

**Q2168.** The name 'protein' is derived from?
**Answer:** The name protein is derived from the Greek word proteios meaning of prime importance.

**Q2169.** Which type of simple protein is found in connective tissues throughout the body and is the most abundant protein in the animal kingdom?
**Answer:** Legumin and collagen proteins are present in the connective tissues throughout the body. They are the most abundant proteins in the animal kingdom.

**Q2170.** Phosphoproteins are examples of conjugated proteins where the protein is conjugated with:
**Answer:** Phospho-proteins are conjugated with phosphoric acid.

**Q2171.** What is the typical effect of heating on a thermosetting polymer?
**Answer:** A thermosetting polymer, on heating, decomposes instead of melting.

**Q2172.** The highly convoluted and compact molecules with precisely determined atomic positions describe the general structure of:
**Answer:** The majority of proteins are compact, highly convoluted molecules with the position of each atom relative to the others determined with great precision.

**Q2173.** When referring to the three-dimensional shape that a polypeptide chain assumes, what levels of structural organization are necessary to specify it?
**Answer:** Proteins assume at least three levels of structural organization: Primary, Secondary, and Tertiary. Quaternary structure is optional and only in proteins with multiple polypeptide chains.

**Q2174.** Denaturation of proteins refers to the disruption of their structure. This change is often irreversible in the case of:
**Answer:** The most familiar example of denaturation is the change that takes place in albumin, the principal component of egg white, when it is cooked. In this particular case the change is irreversible.

**Q2175.** The interaction that primarily stabilizes the secondary structure of a protein is:
**Answer:** The secondary structure of a protein is a regular coiling or zigzagging of polypeptide chains caused by hydrogen bonding between NH and C=O groups of amino acids near each other in the chains.

**Q2176.** Which of the following is a type of protein classification based on physico-chemical properties?
**Answer:** Based on the physico-chemical properties, proteins may be classified into three types: Simple proteins, Compound or Conjugated proteins, and Derived proteins.

**Q2177.** What is the approximate percentage of body protein that simple proteins like albumins and globulins form in animals?
**Answer:** Simple proteins like albumins and globulins are found in animals and form some 25 to 35% of body protein.

**Q2178.** The non-protein groups attached to conjugated proteins are called:
**Answer:** In these molecules the protein is attached or conjugated to some non-protein groups which are called prosthetic groups.

**Q2179.** Which of the following is a derived protein?
**Answer:** This class of protein includes substances which are derived from simple and conjugated proteins. For example, proteoses, enzymes, peptones, oligopeptides, polypeptides, etc.

**Q2180.** The highest level of structural organization achieved by some proteins, involving the association of multiple polypeptide chains, is known as the:
**Answer:** Proteins assume at least three levels of structural organization. Some proteins also possesses a fourth structure called the quaternary structure.

**Q2181.** Collagen, a simple protein, is prominently present in which part of the body?
**Answer:** Legumin and collagen proteins are present in the connective tissues throughout the body.

**Q2182.** What happens to proteins under strongly oxidizing or reducing conditions?
**Answer:** The structure of proteins can be disrupted easily by heat, change in pH and under strongly oxidizing or reducing conditions. Under such conditions the proteins undergo denaturation.

**Q2183.** The term that describes the exact arrangement of amino acids within a polypeptide chain is:
**Answer:** The sequence of the amino acids combined in a peptide chain is referred to as the primary structure.

**Q2184.** Proteins are essential for all forms of life because they take an essential part in the formation of what?
**Answer:** Proteins take an essential part in the formation of protoplasm which is the essence of all forms of life.

**Q2185.** Complexes of proteins with nucleic acids are known as nucleoproteins. What is their primary role?
**Answer:** Nucleoproteins which are complexes of proteins with nucleic acids serve as carriers of heredity from one generation to the other.

**Q2186.** Enzymes are biological catalysts. What is their chemical nature?
**Answer:** Enzymes which are biological catalysts are protein in nature.

**Q2187.** Which protein is specifically mentioned for its specialized function as a carrier of O₂?
**Answer:** Many proteins have specialized functions. Haemoglobin acts as a carrier of O₂.

**Q2188.** Insulin and thyroxine are examples of proteins that act as:
**Answer:** Some proteins act as hormones which have regulatory functions, for example; insulin, thyroxine etc.

**Q2189.** Beyond their edible qualities, fats and oils, which are lipids, also find use as raw materials for the manufacture of:
**Answer:** Not only the edible fats and oils occupy a place of pride in human diet but they also find use as raw materials for the manufacture of soaps and detergents, paints, varnishes, polishes, cosmetics, printing inks and pharmaceuticals.

**Q2190.** Gelatin is obtained by heating which of the following?
**Answer:** Gelatin is obtained by heating bones, skin and tendons in water.

**Q2191.** What is casein, another industrially important protein, used in the manufacture of?
**Answer:** Caesein is another protein used in the manufacture of buttons and buckles.

**Q2192.** The process of making leather from hides essentially involves the precipitation of proteins with:
**Answer:** We are familiar with the use of leather made by tanning of hides. This is essentially a precipitation of the proteins with tannic acid.

**Q2193.** Besides proteins and carbohydrates, what is the third major "food factor" needed for the human body, according to the text?
**Answer:** Fats and oils are the most important lipids found in nature. They are one of the three major "food factors" needed for human body, the other two being proteins and carbohydrates.

**Q2194.** Which of the following is a primary function of enzymes in the body?
**Answer:** Enzymes which are biological catalysts are protein in nature and play a crucial role in speeding up biochemical reactions in the body.

**Q2195.** What is the main characteristic that allows hemoglobin to perform its function as a carrier?
**Answer:** Haemoglobin acts as a carrier of O₂ (oxygen), implying its ability to bind and release oxygen.

**Q2196.** Proteins like insulin and thyroxine are important for:
**Answer:** Some proteins act as hormones which have regulatory functions, for example; insulin, thyroxine etc.

**Q2197.** The term 'protoplasm' is described in the text as the 'essence of all forms of life.' What is the essential role of proteins in relation to protoplasm?
**Answer:** Proteins take an essential part in the formation of protoplasm which is the essence of all forms of life.

**Q2198.** The process of tanning hides to make leather primarily involves the precipitation of proteins using:
**Answer:** We are familiar with the use of leather made by tanning of hides. This is essentially a precipitation of the proteins with tannic acid.

**Q2199.** Which category of macromolecules are 'one of the three major 'food factors' needed for the human body,' alongside proteins and carbohydrates?
**Answer:** Fats and oils are the most important lipids found in nature. They are one of the three major 'food factors' needed for human body, the other two being proteins and carbohydrates.

**Q2200.** The specialized function of certain proteins, such as hemoglobin acting as a carrier and insulin as a hormone, highlights their importance in:
**Answer:** The text mentions hemoglobin acting as a carrier of O₂ and insulin as a hormone with regulatory functions, both of which are critical for maintaining specific bodily processes and overall homeostasis.

**Q2201.** Which of the following is a key characteristic of lipids that distinguishes them from carbohydrates and proteins?
**Answer:** Lipids are naturally occurring organic compounds of animals and plants origin which are soluble in organic solvents like ether, chloroform and benzene, etc. and are insoluble in water.

**Q2202.** The term 'fatty acids' are mentioned as primary building blocks for which group of biomolecules?
**Answer:** The primary building blocks of lipids are fatty acids, glycerol and sterols.

**Q2203.** Which statement accurately describes the difference between fats and oils based on their composition?
**Answer:** The glycerides in which long-chain saturated acid components predominate tend to be solid or semi-solid and are termed as fats. On the other hand, oils are glycerol esters which contain higher proportion of unsaturated fatty acid components.

**Q2204.** Marine oils are obtained from which type of animals?
**Answer:** Marine oils are obtained form sea animals like salmons and whales etc.

**Q2205.** Proteins are described as fundamental to life due to their role in forming:
**Answer:** Proteins take an essential part in the formation of protoplasm which is the essence of all forms of life.

**Q2206.** Which of the following is a crucial function of nucleoproteins?
**Answer:** Nucleoproteins which are complexes of proteins with nucleic acids serve as carriers of heredity from one generation to the other.

**Q2207.** The protein insulin plays a vital role as a hormone, indicating its function in:
**Answer:** Some proteins act as hormones which have regulatory functions, for example; insulin, thyroxine etc.

**Q2208.** The industrial use of leather, made by tanning hides, involves the precipitation of proteins with:
**Answer:** This is essentially a precipitation of the proteins with tannic acid.

**Q2209.** The importance of proteins as 'food factors' implies their role in providing:
**Answer:** Proteins are mentioned as one of the three major 'food factors' needed for the human body, alongside carbohydrates and fats. This directly implies their nutritional importance for bodily functions, growth, and maintenance.

**Q2210.** What is the primary chemical nature of enzymes?
**Answer:** Enzymes which are biological catalysts are protein in nature.

**Q2211.** Enzymes are known for their function as:
**Answer:** Enzymes which are biological catalysts are protein in nature.

**Q2212.** The term 'biocatalyst' refers to substances that:
**Answer:** Enzymes are biological catalysts, meaning they speed up biochemical reactions.

**Q2213.** Which of the following is a characteristic of enzymes?
**Answer:** A fundamental characteristic of enzymes is their high specificity.

**Q2214.** What can be inferred about the structure of enzymes, given they are protein in nature?
**Answer:** Enzymes possess specific three-dimensional structures essential for their catalytic activity.

**Q2215.** Enzymes facilitate biochemical reactions by:
**Answer:** Catalysts lower the activation energy to speed up biochemical reactions.

**Q2216.** Which type of protein structure is most directly related to the active site and catalytic function of an enzyme?
**Answer:** Tertiary structure defines the active site where catalysis occurs.

**Q2217.** Denaturation of an enzyme, due to factors like extreme heat or pH, would most likely lead to:
**Answer:** Denaturation disrupts protein structure, leading to loss of enzyme function.

**Q2218.** Compared to inorganic catalysts, enzymes are generally known for their:
**Answer:** Enzymes function effectively within the mild conditions of living organisms.

**Q2219.** What is the fundamental role that enzymes play in metabolic pathways within living organisms?
**Answer:** Enzymes regulate the speed of biochemical reactions in metabolic pathways.

**Q2220.** Which of the following statements about enzyme activity is most accurate?
**Answer:** Enzymes are sensitive to pH changes and function within a narrow pH range.

**Q2221.** What specific term describes the location on an enzyme where the substrate binds and the reaction takes place?
**Answer:** The active site is the region on an enzyme where catalysis occurs.

**Q2222.** Enzymes are sometimes referred to as 'biological catalysts' because they:
**Answer:** Catalysts speed up reactions without being consumed; enzymes do this biologically.

**Q2223.** The efficiency and specificity of an enzyme are primarily determined by its:
**Answer:** An enzyme’s amino acid sequence and 3D structure create its active site and specificity.

**Q2224.** If a protein undergoes denaturation, how would this typically affect its enzymatic activity?
**Answer:** Denaturation disrupts the enzyme's structure, causing loss of catalytic activity.

 **A.** 2Fe + 3H₂O + O₂ → Fe₂O₃·3H₂O ✓
**Answer:** 2Fe + 3H₂O + O₂ → Fe₂O₃·3H₂O

### S And P Block Elements (72 Questions)

**Q2232.** Elements in which block are generally placed at the bottom of the periodic table as two separate rows?
**Answer:** The f-block elements, consisting of Lanthanides and Actinides, are conventionally placed in two separate rows at the bottom of the main periodic table.

**Q2233.** Which statement correctly describes the location of d-block elements in the periodic table?
**Answer:** The d-block elements are situated in the middle of the periodic table, between the s-block and p-block elements.

**Q2234.** The elements with the general outer electronic configuration (n-2)f¹⁻¹⁴(n-1)d⁰⁻¹ns² are found in which block?
**Answer:** This general electronic configuration corresponds to the f-block elements, where the f-orbitals are being filled.

**Q2235.** Which of the following elements is an s-block element?
**Answer:** Sodium (Na) is in Group 1, making it an s-block element with an outer electronic configuration of 3s¹.

**Q2236.** An element with the valence electronic configuration 3d⁵ 4s² would most likely belong to which block?
**Answer:** The presence of partially filled 'd' orbitals as the differentiating electron indicates that the element belongs to the d-block.

**Q2237.** Which of the following statements about p-block elements is true?
**Answer:** The p-block is unique because it is the only block containing elements of all three types: metals, non-metals, and metalloids, demonstrating a wide range of properties.

**Q2238.** Which block of elements typically forms colored ions and exhibits variable valency?
**Answer:** d-block elements, also known as transition metals, are known for forming colored ions and exhibiting variable oxidation states (valency) due to the involvement of d-electrons in bonding.

**Q2239.** Moving from left to right across a period in the periodic table, how does the atomic radius generally change?
**Answer:** Across a period, the effective nuclear charge increases due to the increasing number of protons, pulling the valence electrons closer to the nucleus, thus decreasing the atomic radius.

**Q2240.** As one descends a group in the periodic table, what is the usual trend for ionization energy?
**Answer:** Down a group, the atomic size increases due to the addition of new shells, and shielding effect increases. This weakens the attraction between the nucleus and the outermost electrons, making it easier to remove an electron, hence decreasing ionization energy.

**Q2241.** Which factor primarily contributes to the decrease in atomic size from left to right across a period?
**Answer:** Across a period, electrons are added to the same valence shell while the nuclear charge increases, leading to a stronger attraction and a smaller atomic size.

**Q2242.** For a given element, how does the ionic radius of its cation compare to its neutral atomic radius?
**Answer:** When an atom loses electrons to form a cation, the effective nuclear charge per electron increases, and often a whole shell is lost, leading to a reduction in size.

**Q2243.** Which of the following elements would generally have the highest electronegativity?
**Answer:** Electronegativity generally increases across a period from left to right and decreases down a group. Chlorine is a p-block element located in Group 17, making it highly electronegative.

**Q2244.** The ability of an atom to attract a shared pair of electrons towards itself in a covalent bond is called:
**Answer:** This describes the concept of electronegativity.

**Q2245.** How does the metallic character of elements typically change as you move from left to right across a period?
**Answer:** As you move across a period, elements become less metallic and more non-metallic, primarily due to increasing effective nuclear charge and decreasing atomic size, making it harder to lose electrons.

**Q2246.** Which property is inversely related to atomic size within a period?
**Answer:** As atomic size decreases across a period, the outermost electrons are held more tightly, requiring more energy to remove them, thus increasing ionization energy.

**Q2247.** Why do Group 1 elements have lower ionization energies compared to Group 17 elements in the same period?
**Answer:** Group 1 elements have a single valence electron relatively far from the nucleus and experience less effective nuclear charge compared to Group 17 elements in the same period, making their valence electron easier to remove.

**Q2248.** Consider elements X, Y, and Z in the same period. If X has the highest electrical conductivity and Z has the lowest, what is their likely order in the periodic table from left to right?
**Answer:** Electrical conductivity is highest for metals and decreases across a period as elements become less metallic (non-metals are poor conductors). Thus, X (metal), Y (metalloid/less metallic), Z (non-metal) would be the typical order.

**Q2249.** Which of the following elements would generally exhibit the highest melting point among these options?
**Answer:** Within a short period, melting and boiling points generally increase from Group 1 to Group 14 (Carbon), then decrease. Carbon, especially in its network solid forms like diamond or graphite, has very high melting points due to strong covalent bonding.

**Q2250.** If Element A is in Period 3, Group 1, and Element B is in Period 3, Group 17, which statement about their ionization energy is correct?
**Answer:** Ionization energy generally increases across a period due to increasing effective nuclear charge and decreasing atomic size. Therefore, Group 17 elements have higher ionization energies than Group 1 elements in the same period.

**Q2251.** Why does the electron affinity of elements generally become less negative (or more positive) as you move down a group?
**Answer:** As atomic size increases down a group, the incoming electron is further from the nucleus and experiences greater shielding, leading to a weaker attraction and thus a less favorable (less negative or more positive) electron affinity.

**Q2252.** Which type of elements typically shows a significant drop in melting and boiling points after Group 14 across a period?
**Answer:** After Group 14 (e.g., Carbon, Silicon), elements become non-metals (like nitrogen, oxygen, fluorine) that exist as discrete molecules or gases with weaker intermolecular forces, leading to much lower melting and boiling points.

**Q2253.** Which change in properties indicates a transition from metallic to non-metallic character across a period?
**Answer:** Non-metals are characterized by higher electronegativity (tendency to gain electrons) and lower electrical conductivity compared to metals.

**Q2254.** When an alkali metal reacts with water, what gas is typically produced along with a metal hydroxide?
**Answer:** Alkali metals react vigorously with water to produce hydrogen gas and a metal hydroxide. For example, 2Na(s) + 2H₂O(l) → 2NaOH(aq) + H₂(g).

**Q2255.** What is the general trend in reactivity of Group I elements with water as you move down the group?
**Answer:** As you move down Group I, the atomic size increases, and ionization energy decreases, making it easier for the outermost electron to be lost to water, thus increasing reactivity.

**Q2256.** When lithium reacts with oxygen, what is the primary product formed?
**Answer:** Lithium is unique among alkali metals in primarily forming a normal oxide (Li₂O) when reacting with oxygen, unlike larger alkali metals which form peroxides or superoxides.

**Q2257.** Which type of oxide is predominantly formed when potassium reacts with excess oxygen?
**Answer:** Larger alkali metals like potassium and rubidium react with excess oxygen to form superoxides (KO₂), containing the superoxide ion (O₂⁻).

**Q2258.** What is the common formula for the halides formed when Group I elements react with chlorine?
**Answer:** Alkali metals (M) are monovalent, and chlorine is monovalent, so they combine in a 1:1 ratio to form metal chlorides with the general formula MCl (e.g., NaCl, KCl).

**Q2259.** The reaction of an alkali metal with oxygen is often controlled due to the vigorous nature of the reaction. Which property of alkali metals contributes most to this high reactivity with oxygen?
**Answer:** Alkali metals have very low ionization energies, meaning they readily lose their single valence electron to form a positive ion, which facilitates their vigorous reactions with oxygen.

**Q2260.** When sodium is exposed to moist air, it readily forms a compound. This reaction is a result of sodium's high reactivity with:
**Answer:** Sodium reacts readily with moisture (water) and oxygen in the air. The highly reactive nature of sodium with these components leads to the formation of hydroxides and oxides, causing it to tarnish quickly.

**Q2261.** Which of the following is a characteristic product formed when sodium reacts with a limited supply of oxygen?
**Answer:** When sodium reacts with a limited supply of oxygen, it primarily forms the normal oxide, sodium oxide (Na₂O). With excess oxygen, it forms sodium peroxide (Na₂O₂).

**Q2262.** The solution formed after an alkali metal reacts with water will typically exhibit which property?
**Answer:** The reaction of alkali metals with water produces metal hydroxides (e.g., NaOH, KOH), which are strong bases, making the resulting solution basic.

**Q2263.** Consider the reaction of cesium with water. This reaction would be expected to be:
**Answer:** Reactivity with water increases down Group I, so cesium, being below sodium, reacts even more vigorously due to its larger atomic size and lower ionization energy.

**Q2264.** Which alkali metal would react least vigorously with chlorine gas?
**Answer:** Reactivity of alkali metals with halogens increases down the group. Therefore, lithium, being at the top, would react least vigorously among the given options.

**Q2265.** The reaction between sodium metal and chlorine gas is exothermic. What type of bond is formed in the product?
**Answer:** Sodium (an alkali metal) readily loses an electron, and chlorine (a halogen) readily gains an electron, leading to the formation of an ionic bond (e.g., NaCl).

**Q2266.** If an alkali metal (M) forms a compound with oxygen as MO₂, which type of oxide is it?
**Answer:** The formula MO₂ indicates the presence of the superoxide ion (O₂⁻), which is characteristic of superoxides formed by larger alkali metals.

**Q2267.** When lithium reacts with nitrogen at high temperatures, what is the product formed?
**Answer:** Lithium is the only alkali metal that directly reacts with nitrogen to form a nitride (Li₃N), due to the high charge density of the Li⁺ ion.

**Q2268.** Which of the following compounds would result from the reaction of sodium with excess oxygen?
**Answer:** Sodium reacts with excess oxygen to form sodium peroxide (Na₂O₂), which contains the peroxide ion (O₂²⁻).

**Q2269.** Which Group II element reacts most vigorously with cold water?
**Answer:** Reactivity of alkaline earth metals with water generally increases down the group as atomic size increases and ionization energy decreases, making it easier to lose electrons. Barium is at the bottom of the typical Group II elements.

**Q2270.** When a Group II metal reacts with water, what type of compound is formed along with hydrogen gas?
**Answer:** Group II metals react with water to form metal hydroxides, which are generally less soluble than alkali metal hydroxides, and hydrogen gas. For example, Ca(s) + 2H₂O(l) → Ca(OH)₂(aq) + H₂(g).

**Q2271.** Which Group II element requires hot water or steam to react significantly?
**Answer:** Magnesium reacts very slowly with cold water but reacts readily with hot water or steam to form magnesium hydroxide or magnesium oxide, respectively, and hydrogen gas.

**Q2272.** All alkaline earth metals react with oxygen to form what type of oxide?
**Answer:** All Group II elements typically form normal oxides with the general formula MO, where M is the metal and O is the oxide ion (O²⁻).

**Q2273.** What is the primary product formed when magnesium ribbon is burned in air?
**Answer:** When magnesium burns in air, it predominantly reacts with oxygen to form magnesium oxide (MgO), a white powder.

**Q2274.** The reaction of beryllium with oxygen at room temperature is generally described as:
**Answer:** Beryllium forms a coherent and stable oxide layer on its surface, which protects it from further reaction with oxygen, making its reaction at room temperature slow or negligible.

**Q2275.** When calcium is heated in a nitrogen atmosphere, what compound is formed?
**Answer:** Group II elements, when heated in nitrogen, can react to form nitrides with the general formula M3N2. For calcium, it's calcium nitride (Ca3N2).

**Q2276.** Which of the following Group II elements exhibits the least metallic character?
**Answer:** Metallic character increases down a group. Beryllium is at the top of Group II, thus having the least metallic character among the options.

**Q2277.** The solubility of Group II metal hydroxides in water generally:
**Answer:** While not a direct reaction, the solubility of Group II hydroxides (M(OH)2) generally increases as you go down the group (e.g., Mg(OH)2 is sparingly soluble, Ba(OH)2 is more soluble).

**Q2278.** Why is the reactivity of Group II metals generally lower than that of Group I metals in the same period?
**Answer:** Group II elements have two valence electrons to lose and generally have smaller atomic sizes and higher ionization energies than their Group I counterparts in the same period, making them less reactive.

**Q2279.** Which Group II metal is used in flashlight powders due to its vigorous reaction with oxygen, producing a bright white light?
**Answer:** Magnesium burns with a dazzling white light when it reacts with oxygen, which made it useful in old camera flashbulbs and fireworks.

**Q2280.** If a Group II element forms an oxide MO, what is the oxidation state of the metal (M) in this compound?
**Answer:** Oxygen typically has an oxidation state of -2 in oxides. Since the ratio is 1:1, the metal (M) must have an oxidation state of +2. Group II elements exclusively exhibit a +2 oxidation state in their compounds.

**Q2281.** Which Group II element's reaction with water produces very little hydrogen gas due to the formation of a protective hydroxide layer on its surface?
**Answer:** While magnesium reacts with hot water/steam, its reaction with cold water is very slow because a sparingly soluble layer of magnesium hydroxide forms on its surface, inhibiting further reaction.

**Q2282.** When barium reacts with nitrogen at elevated temperatures, the product formed is:
**Answer:** Barium, a highly electropositive Group II metal, will form an ionic nitride (Ba3N2) with nitrogen, where the nitrogen exists as the nitride ion (N3−).

**Q2283.** Consider the reaction of calcium with oxygen. The reaction is typically:
**Answer:** Calcium, like other alkaline earth metals, readily reacts with oxygen to form its oxide, a process that is quite rapid when heated.

**Q2284.** Which element in Group IV exhibits unique catenation ability, forming long chains and rings with itself?
**Answer:** Carbon's strong carbon-carbon bonds allow it to form extensive chains and rings, a property known as catenation, which is much more pronounced than in other Group IV elements.

**Q2285.** What is the most common oxidation state exhibited by Group IV elements in their stable compounds?
**Answer:** Group IV elements typically form four covalent bonds, exhibiting a +4 oxidation state by sharing their four valence electrons. Heavier elements can also show +2.

**Q2286.** Which of the following describes the nature of silicon dioxide (SiO₂)?
**Answer:** Silicon dioxide exists as a giant covalent network structure where silicon atoms are tetrahedrally bonded to oxygen atoms, resulting in a very stable, high-melting-point solid.

**Q2287.** Unlike carbon dioxide, which is a gas, why is silicon dioxide a solid at room temperature?
**Answer:** CO₂ is a discrete molecular compound with weak intermolecular forces, while SiO₂ forms a giant covalent network structure, requiring much more energy to break bonds and melt, thus being a solid.

**Q2288.** Which of the following forms of carbon is considered the hardest known natural substance?
**Answer:** Diamond is a crystalline allotrope of carbon where each carbon atom is tetrahedrally bonded to four other carbon atoms, resulting in an extremely strong and hard structure.

**Q2289.** The term 'silicones' refers to a class of polymers containing silicon and oxygen backbones with organic groups attached. What is a key characteristic of silicones regarding their reactivity?
**Answer:** Silicones are known for their high thermal stability, chemical inertness, water repellency, and good electrical insulating properties due to the strength of the Si-O bond.

**Q2290.** Which Group IV element is a metalloid and is extensively used in semiconductor technology?
**Answer:** Silicon is a metalloid that exhibits properties between metals and non-metals and is the foundation of the modern electronics industry due to its semiconductor properties.

**Q2291.** How does the metallic character generally change as you move down Group IV from carbon to lead?
**Answer:** As you move down Group IV, the elements become more metallic in nature. Carbon is a non-metal, Silicon and Germanium are metalloids, and Tin and Lead are metals.

**Q2292.** Among the oxides of carbon, which one is acidic in character and responsible for forming carbonic acid in water?
**Answer:** Carbon dioxide (CO₂) is an acidic oxide that reacts with water to form carbonic acid (H₂CO₃), which is a weak acid.

**Q2293.** Which property of lead oxides (like red lead, Pb₃O₄) makes them important components in paints and pigments?
**Answer:** Lead oxides are used as pigments in paints due to their distinct colors and provide protective properties, such as corrosion resistance for metal surfaces.

**Q2294.** Which of the following describes the bonding in diamond?
**Answer:** Diamond is an allotrope of carbon where each carbon atom is sp3 hybridized and covalently bonded to four other carbon atoms in a rigid three-dimensional network structure.

**Q2295.** What is the primary reason for the lubricating property of graphite?
**Answer:** Graphite has a layered structure where carbon atoms are covalently bonded within layers, but the layers are held by weak intermolecular forces, allowing them to slide easily, giving it lubricating properties.

**Q2296.** Which Group IV element readily forms stable +2 oxidation state compounds, especially among the heavier members?
**Answer:** Due to the inert pair effect, the stability of the +2 oxidation state increases down Group IV. Lead (Pb) commonly exhibits a stable +2 oxidation state in addition to +4.

**Q2297.** When silicon reacts with oxygen, what is the most common oxide formed?
**Answer:** Silicon readily reacts with oxygen to form silicon dioxide (SiO₂), also known as silica, which is the most common oxide of silicon.

**Q2298.** Which allotrope of carbon is a good conductor of electricity?
**Answer:** In graphite, each carbon atom is sp2 hybridized, leaving one unhybridized p-orbital per carbon atom. These p-orbitals overlap to form delocalized pi-electron clouds that can move freely, making graphite a good conductor of electricity.

**Q2299.** Why is carbon monoxide (CO) considered poisonous?
**Answer:** Carbon monoxide is highly toxic because it has a much stronger affinity for hemoglobin in the blood than oxygen, forming carboxyhemoglobin and preventing oxygen from being carried to the body's tissues.

**Q2300.** The reaction of tin (Sn) with dilute acids typically produces:
**Answer:** Tin is a metal and generally reacts with dilute non-oxidizing acids to form its salt and hydrogen gas (e.g., Sn+2HCl → SnCl₂+H₂).

**Q2301.** What is the general trend in the stability of the +4 oxidation state versus the +2 oxidation state as you move down Group IV?
**Answer:** This trend is due to the "inert pair effect," where the reluctance of the outermost s-electrons to participate in bonding increases down the group, making the +2 oxidation state more stable for heavier elements like Tin and Lead.

**Q2302.** Which of the following is an example of an organosilicon polymer?
**Answer:** Silicones are a class of synthetic polymers that include silicon, oxygen, carbon, and hydrogen, with a backbone of silicon-oxygen chains.

**Q2303.** What is a major environmental concern associated with lead compounds?
**Answer:** Lead and its compounds are known to be highly toxic to living organisms, causing various health problems, and they bioaccumulate in the environment and food chains.

**Answer:** In an exothermic reaction
