### Transition Elements (64 Questions)

**Q2315.** Which of the following defines transition elements?
**Answer:** Transition elements are defined as those elements which have partially filled d or f-subshells in their atomic state or in any of their commonly occurring oxidation states.

**Q2316.** According to the definition, which of the following elements is NOT considered a typical transition element?
**Answer:** Zinc (Zn) is not considered a typical transition element because its d-subshell is completely filled (3d¹⁰) in both its atomic state and its common +2 oxidation state (Zn²⁺: 3d¹⁰). Typical transition elements have partially filled d-subshells.

**Q2317.** What is the common characteristic of d-block elements that leads to their classification as transition elements?
**Answer:** The defining characteristic of transition elements (which are primarily d-block elements) is the presence of partially filled d-orbitals, either in their elemental state or in one of their stable oxidation states.

**Q2318.** The electronic configuration of Scandium (Sc) is [Ar] 3d¹ 4s². Based on this, it is classified as a:
**Answer:** Scandium is classified as a transition element because it has a partially filled 3d subshell (3d¹) in its atomic state.

**Q2319.** Which of the following blocks in the periodic table primarily contains transition elements?
**Answer:** Transition elements are primarily found in the d-block of the periodic table, where they are characterized by the filling of d-orbitals. The f-block elements are sometimes referred to as inner transition elements.

**Q2320.** The general electronic configuration for the valence shell of d-block transition elements is:
**Answer:** The general electronic configuration for the valence shell of d-block transition elements is (n-1)d¹⁻¹⁰ ns¹⁻², where (n-1) refers to the penultimate shell containing d-orbitals and n refers to the outermost shell containing s-orbitals.

**Q2321.** Which of the following electronic configurations represents a d-block element?
**Answer:** [Ar] 3d⁵ 4s¹ is the electronic configuration of Chromium (Cr), which is a d-block element characterized by the filling of d-orbitals. The other options represent p-block, s-block, and p-block elements respectively.

**Q2322.** Transition elements typically exhibit high melting and boiling points due to:
**Answer:** Transition elements have high melting and boiling points due to strong metallic bonding resulting from the delocalization of both s and d electrons, leading to strong cohesive forces.

**Q2323.** The ability of transition elements to form alloys is primarily due to:
**Answer:** Transition elements readily form alloys because they have similar atomic radii and exhibit metallic bonding, allowing atoms to substitute one another in the crystal lattice.

**Q2324.** Which property of transition elements is responsible for their ability to form colored compounds?
**Answer:** Many transition metal compounds are colored due to the presence of partially filled d-orbitals, allowing for d-d electron transitions when visible light is absorbed.

**Q2325.** What type of magnetic behavior is exhibited by transition elements with unpaired electrons?
**Answer:** Paramagnetism is the property of a substance that is weakly attracted by a magnetic field. It arises due to the presence of unpaired electrons in the d-orbitals of transition metal ions.

**Q2326.** The variable oxidation states of transition elements are mainly due to the involvement of electrons from:
**Answer:** Transition elements show variable oxidation states because the energies of the (n-1)d and ns orbitals are very close, allowing electrons from both subshells to participate in bonding.

**Q2327.** Which of the following is true regarding the atomic radii of transition elements across a period?
**Answer:** Across a transition series, the atomic radii generally decrease initially due to increasing nuclear charge, then stabilize, and then may slightly increase towards the end due to increased electron-electron repulsion.

**Q2328.** Why do transition elements exhibit high binding energies?
**Answer:** High binding energies in transition elements are attributed to the strong metallic bonds formed by the delocalization of electrons from both the outermost s-subshell and the inner (n-1)d subshell.

**Q2329.** Which of the following statements is incorrect about transition elements?
**Answer:** Transition elements generally have high densities due to their small atomic radii and strong metallic bonding. They are indeed all metals, often form interstitial compounds, and are known for their catalytic properties.

**Q2330.** The ability of transition metals to act as good catalysts is linked to their:
**Answer:** Transition metals and their compounds often act as catalysts due to their ability to exhibit variable oxidation states and their large surface area, which provides sites for adsorption of reactants.

**Q2331.** Which term describes the property of an atom or ion having unpaired electrons, causing it to be weakly attracted by a magnetic field?
**Answer:** Paramagnetism is the magnetic property of a substance containing one or more unpaired electrons, which are weakly attracted by an external magnetic field.

**Q2332.** Which of the following electronic configurations would result in a diamagnetic ion?
**Answer:** A diamagnetic ion has all its electrons paired. An electronic configuration of [Ar] 3d¹⁰ indicates a completely filled d-subshell, meaning all d-electrons are paired, leading to diamagnetism. The other options have unpaired d-electrons.

**Q2333.** The decrease in atomic size across a transition series is primarily due to:
**Answer:** Across a transition series, the effective nuclear charge increases as electrons are added to the inner (n-1)d subshell, pulling the valence electrons closer to the nucleus and causing a decrease in atomic size.

**Q2334.** Which of the following elements has the most common oxidation state of +1 and +2?
**Answer:** Copper (Cu) commonly exhibits +1 and +2 oxidation states. While other transition metals show variable oxidation states, Cu is notable for these specific common states.

**Q2335.** The relatively small change in atomic radii from left to right in a transition series is attributed to:
**Answer:** As electrons are added to the inner (n-1)d orbitals across a transition series, the increased nuclear charge is partially compensated by the shielding effect of these d-electrons, leading to only a small decrease in atomic radii.

**Q2336.** What causes the paramagnetism in transition metal ions?
**Answer:** Paramagnetism in transition metal ions is caused by the presence of unpaired electrons in their d-orbitals. Each unpaired electron acts as a tiny magnet.

**Q2337.** Transition elements generally exhibit high electrical and thermal conductivity because of:
**Answer:** Transition elements are good conductors of electricity and heat due to the presence of mobile, delocalized valence electrons (both s and d electrons) in their metallic lattice.

**Q2338.** Which of the following is the most stable common oxidation state for Manganese (Mn)?
**Answer:** Mn²⁺ ([Ar] 3d⁵) is particularly stable due to its half-filled d-subshell configuration, making +2 a very common and stable oxidation state for manganese.

**Q2339.** The formation of interstitial compounds by transition metals is due to:
**Answer:** Transition metals form interstitial compounds because their crystal lattices have small vacant spaces (interstitial sites) where small non-metal atoms (like H, B, C, N) can fit.

**Q2340.** Why do transition elements often form colored ions in solution?
**Answer:** The color of transition metal ions in solution is due to the absorption of specific wavelengths of visible light, causing electrons to jump from lower to higher energy d-orbitals (d-d transitions). The complementary color is then observed.

**Q2341.** Which of the following statements is true about the metallic character of transition elements?
**Answer:** Transition elements are true metals and exhibit characteristic metallic properties such as high tensile strength, ductility, malleability, and metallic luster.

**Q2342.** The magnetic moment of a transition metal ion is primarily calculated using the 'spin-only' formula, which depends on the number of:
**Answer:** The spin-only magnetic moment (μ) of a transition metal ion is calculated using the formula μ = √n(n+2) BM, where 'n' is the number of unpaired electrons.

**Q2343.** Which factor contributes to the high density of transition metals?
**Answer:** Transition metals generally have high densities due to their relatively small atomic volumes (compact crystal structures) and high atomic masses.

**Q2344.** What is the most common oxidation state exhibited by nearly all transition elements?
**Answer:** The +2 oxidation state is common for most transition elements, resulting from the loss of the two outermost s-electrons. This is often the first stable oxidation state observed.

**Q2345.** A complex compound consists of a central metal atom or ion surrounded by a number of ions or neutral molecules. These surrounding species are called:
**Answer:** Ligands are ions or neutral molecules that bind to the central metal atom or ion in a complex compound, forming a coordinate bond.

**Q2346.** The number of ligands directly attached to the central metal atom or ion in a complex is known as its:
**Answer:** The coordination number is defined as the number of ligand atoms or ions directly bonded to the central metal ion in a complex.

**Q2347.** Which of the following is an example of a bidentate ligand?
**Answer:** Ethylenediamine (en) is a bidentate ligand because it can form two coordinate bonds with the central metal atom through its two nitrogen atoms. Chloride, water, and ammonia are monodentate.

**Q2348.** What is the coordination number of the central metal ion in [Co(NH₃)₆]Cl₃?
**Answer:** In [Co(NH₃)₆]Cl₃, there are six ammonia (NH₃) ligands directly attached to the central cobalt (Co) ion. Thus, the coordination number is 6.

**Q2349.** According to IUPAC nomenclature, what is the name of the complex ion [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺?
**Answer:** The ligands are named first, followed by the metal. "di" indicates two ammonia ligands (ammine). The oxidation state of silver is +1, indicated by (I).

**Q2350.** What is the correct formula for Potassium hexacyanoferrate(II)?
**Answer:** In hexacyanoferrate(II), the iron is in the +2 oxidation state. With six cyanide ligands (each -1 charge), the complex ion [Fe(CN)₆] has a charge of -4. To balance this, four potassium ions (each +1) are needed, hence K₄[Fe(CN)₆].

**Q2351.** A complex with a coordination number of 4 can exhibit which of the following geometries?
**Answer:** Complexes with a coordination number of 4 commonly adopt either a tetrahedral geometry (e.g., [NiCl₄]²⁻) or a square planar geometry (e.g., [Pt(NH₃)₂Cl₂]).

**Q2352.** The term "coordination sphere" refers to:
**Answer:** The coordination sphere includes the central metal atom or ion and the ligands directly bonded to it, enclosed in a square bracket in the formula, indicating they are part of the complex ion.

**Q2353.** What type of bonding exists between the central metal ion and the ligands in a complex compound?
**Answer:** In complex compounds, ligands donate electron pairs to the central metal ion to form coordinate covalent or dative bonds.

**Q2354.** Which of the following ligands is a neutral molecule?
**Answer:** Water (H₂O) acts as a neutral ligand (aqua) in complex compounds, donating an electron pair from the oxygen atom. Cyanide, chloride, and sulfate are anionic ligands.

**Q2355.** What is the oxidation state of Nickel in the complex [Ni(CO)₄]?
**Answer:** Carbonyl (CO) is a neutral ligand. Since there are no other charged species, the oxidation state of Nickel must be 0 to maintain overall neutrality of the complex.

**Q2356.** The complex ion [PtCl₄]²⁻ has a square planar geometry. This is typical for complexes involving which type of central metal ions?
**Answer:** Square planar geometry is characteristic of many complexes involving d⁸ central metal ions, especially those of 2nd and 3rd row transition elements (like Pt²⁺, Pd²⁺, Au³⁺).

**Q2357.** What is the correct name for the complex [Cr(H₂O)₄Cl₂]Cl?
**Answer:** Ligands are named alphabetically: 'aqua' (water) comes before 'chloro'. The prefix 'tetra' indicates four waters, 'di' for two chlorides. The oxidation state of Cr is +3. The counter ion is chloride.

**Q2358.** Which coordination number typically results in a linear geometry?
**Answer:** A coordination number of 2 typically results in a linear geometry, as seen in complexes like [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺.

**Q2359.** What is the charge on the complex ion in K₃[Fe(C₂O₄)₃]?
**Answer:** Potassium (K) has a +1 charge. Since there are three K⁺ ions, the complex ion [Fe(C₂O₄)₃] must have a charge of -3 to balance the compound's overall neutrality.

**Q2360.** A ligand that binds to the central metal ion through a single donor atom is called a:
**Answer:** A monodentate ligand forms only one coordinate bond with the central metal atom, meaning it has only one donor atom.

**Q2361.** Which commercial form of iron contains the lowest percentage of carbon (0.12% to 0.25%)?
**Answer:** Wrought iron is the purest commercial form of iron and contains the lowest percentage of carbon (0.12% to 0.25%).

**Q2362.** Which of the following forms of iron is obtained directly from the blast furnace and contains about 2.5% to 4.5% carbon?
**Answer:** Pig iron is the crude product obtained from the blast furnace and contains a high percentage of carbon (2.5% to 4.5%) along with other impurities.

**Q2363.** What is the primary difference between steel and wrought iron regarding their carbon content?
**Answer:** Steel contains a higher percentage of carbon (0.25% to 2.5%) compared to wrought iron (0.12% to 0.25%).

**Q2364.** The Bessemer's process is used for the manufacture of:
**Answer:** The Bessemer's process is one of the earliest methods for the large-scale production of steel from pig iron by blowing air through molten pig iron to oxidize impurities.

**Q2365.** Which of the following is NOT a type of steel based on carbon content?
**Answer:** Steel is classified into mild, medium carbon, and high carbon steel based on its carbon percentage. "Pure steel" is not a standard classification; steel always contains some carbon.

**Q2366.** What is the main purpose of the puddling furnace in the manufacture of wrought iron?
**Answer:** The puddling furnace is used to convert pig iron into wrought iron by oxidizing and removing impurities like carbon, silicon, and phosphorus.

**Q2367.** High carbon steel is characterized by its:
**Answer:** High carbon steel (0.5% to 1.5% carbon) is very hard and strong, but also more brittle compared to lower carbon steels.

**Q2368.** The Open Hearth process for steel manufacture is known for its:
**Answer:** The Open Hearth process is a slower but more controllable process than the Bessemer's process, and it can effectively utilize a significant amount of scrap iron in the steel production.

**Q2369.** Which commercial form of iron is typically used for making malleable castings due to its relatively low melting point and good fluidity when molten?
**Answer:** Cast iron (which is essentially pig iron when solidified and shaped) has a lower melting point and good fluidity, making it suitable for casting into various shapes.

**Q2370.** The carbon content in mild steel usually ranges from:
**Answer:** Mild steel typically has a carbon content ranging from 0.05% to 0.25%, making it relatively soft and ductile.

**Q2371.** Corrosion is essentially a process of:
**Answer:** Corrosion is an electrochemical process that involves the oxidation of a metal due to its reaction with the environment, often forming oxides or other salts.

**Q2372.** Which of the following is a necessary condition for the rusting of iron?
**Answer:** Rusting of iron, a common form of corrosion, requires both oxygen and water to occur. It's an electrochemical process where iron acts as the anode.

**Q2373.** In the electrochemical theory of corrosion, the metallic iron acts as the:
**Answer:** In the rusting of iron, iron itself undergoes oxidation (loss of electrons) at the anode, where it forms Fe²⁺ ions.

**Q2374.** Galvanizing is a method of corrosion prevention that involves:
**Answer:** Galvanizing is the process of coating iron or steel with a thin layer of zinc. Zinc acts as a sacrificial anode, providing cathodic protection to iron.

**Q2375.** Why does damaged tin-plated iron rust more quickly than undamaged tin-plated iron?
**Answer:** When the tin coating is scratched or damaged, iron is exposed. Since tin is less reactive than iron, iron acts as the anode and corrodes rapidly in the presence of oxygen and water, sacrificing itself to protect the tin.

**Q2376.** Which of the following methods is used to prevent corrosion by making the metal part of an electrical circuit as a cathode?
**Answer:** Sacrificial protection (a type of cathodic protection) involves connecting a more reactive metal (which acts as a sacrificial anode) to the metal to be protected, making the protected metal the cathode and preventing its oxidation.

**Q2377.** Corrosion of iron is accelerated by the presence of:
**Answer:** The presence of electrolytes (like salts, acids, or bases) in water increases its electrical conductivity, which facilitates the flow of electrons in the electrochemical corrosion process, thus accelerating rusting.

**Q2378.** The conversion of the silvery surface of aluminum into a dull layer upon exposure to air is an example of:
**Answer:** Aluminum forms a thin, tough, non-porous, and adherent layer of aluminum oxide (Al₂O₃) on its surface when exposed to air. This oxide layer prevents further corrosion, a phenomenon known as passivation.

## Physics (1216 Questions)

### Atomic Spectra (59 Questions)

**Q2379.** The spectral series of the hydrogen atom that lies in the ultraviolet region of the electromagnetic spectrum is called the:
**Answer:** The Lyman series involves electron transitions down to the ground state (n=1). These are the largest energy drops, producing high-energy ultraviolet photons.

**Q2380.** According to Bohr's atomic model, the angular momentum of an electron in a stationary orbit is:
**Answer:** Bohr's second postulate states that the angular momentum (L) is quantized and given by the formula L = n(h/2π), showing a direct proportionality to n.

**Q2381.** The transition of an electron from n=4 to n=2 in a hydrogen atom results in the emission of a photon in which series?
**Answer:** The Balmer series is defined by all electron transitions that terminate at the n=2 energy level.

**Q2382.** The phenomenon where an excited atom emits a photon without any external trigger is known as:
**Answer:** Spontaneous emission occurs naturally when an atom in an excited state returns to a lower energy state on its own, releasing a photon.

**Q2383.** The production of characteristic X-rays is a result of:
**Answer:** When an inner-shell electron is ejected, an electron from a higher shell drops down to fill the vacancy, emitting a photon with energy characteristic of that specific element.

**Q2384.** For laser action to occur, a necessary condition is that there are more atoms in a higher energy state than in a lower one. This condition is called:
**Answer:** Population inversion precisely describes the 'inverted' population of energy levels required for light amplification in a laser, where a higher state is more populated than a lower one.

**Q2385.** The energy of an electron in the nth orbit of a hydrogen atom is proportional to:
**Answer:** The formula for the energy of an electron in the nth orbit is Eₙ = -E₀/n², showing it is proportional to 1/n².

**Q2386.** Which of the following properties is NOT characteristic of laser light?
**Answer:** Incoherence is a property of ordinary light (like from a bulb), where photons are out of phase. Laser light is highly coherent.

**Q2387.** The minimum energy required to remove an electron from an atom in its ground state is known as:
**Answer:** Ionization energy is the precise term for the energy needed to ionize an atom by completely removing an electron from its ground state.

**Q2388.** The Paschen series of the hydrogen spectrum is in which region?
**Answer:** Transitions ending at n=3 (Paschen series) have smaller energy changes than Balmer transitions, placing their emitted photons in the infrared region.

**Q2389.** For an electron in a stationary Bohr orbit, its total energy is -3.4 eV. What is its kinetic energy (KE)?
**Answer:** In the Bohr model, the total energy E\_T is the negative of the kinetic energy (E\_T = -KE). Therefore, the kinetic energy must be the positive value of the total energy, so KE = -(-3.4 eV) = +3.4 eV.

**Q2390.** The continuous spectrum of X-rays is produced due to:
**Answer:** This process, known as Bremsstrahlung or 'braking radiation', produces photons of all energies up to the electron's maximum kinetic energy, creating a continuous spectrum.

**Q2391.** A state in which an excited atom can stay for a relatively long time (e.g., 10⁻³ s) is called a:
**Answer:** A metastable state is an excited state with an unusually long lifetime, which is crucial for achieving population inversion in lasers.

**Q2392.** The radius of the first Bohr orbit for the hydrogen atom is r₁. The radius of the third Bohr orbit is:
**Answer:** According to the Bohr model, the radius of the nth orbit is given by rₙ = n²r₁. For n=3, the radius is 3²r₁ = 9r₁.

**Q2393.** An absorption spectrum is characterized by:
**Answer:** An absorption spectrum is created when atoms absorb light at specific wavelengths from a continuous source, leaving dark gaps or lines in the spectrum.

**Q2394.** In a He-Ne laser, the process of exciting Ne atoms through collisions with excited He atoms is called:
**Answer:** This is called resonant energy transfer because the metastable energy levels of Helium and Neon atoms are very closely matched, allowing for an efficient transfer of energy via collision.

**Q2395.** The series limit of a spectral series corresponds to an electron transition from:
**Answer:** The series limit represents the maximum energy photon (shortest wavelength) for that series, which occurs when a free electron (from n = ∞) is captured into the final state n.

**Q2396.** The energy of a photon emitted from a hydrogen atom is 10.2 eV. This corresponds to a transition from:
**Answer:** The energy levels are E₁ = -13.6 eV and E₂ = -3.4 eV. The difference is ΔE = E₂ - E₁ = (-3.4) - (-13.6) = 10.2 eV.

**Q2397.** Rydberg's constant (R) is a physical constant related to:
**Answer:** Rydberg's constant is the key component in the Rydberg formula, which accurately predicts the wavelengths of spectral lines in hydrogen and other atoms.

**Q2398.** The process of supplying energy to the atoms of a laser medium to achieve population inversion is called:
**Answer:** 'Pumping' is the general term for the process of supplying energy to the laser medium, whether through light (optical pumping) or electrical discharge.

**Q2399.** The wavelength of characteristic X-rays depends on:
**Answer:** The energy levels of atoms are unique to each element. Therefore, the energy (and wavelength) of photons emitted during inner-shell transitions is a 'characteristic' of the target material.

**Q2400.** The lines in the Balmer series of the hydrogen spectrum are formed by electron transitions from higher energy levels to:
**Answer:** The Balmer series is specifically defined as the set of transitions where the electron's final energy level is n=2.

**Q2401.** The lifetime of an atom in an ordinary excited state is typically about:
**Answer:** An ordinary excited state is very unstable, and the atom will spontaneously de-excite in a very short time, typically on the order of nanoseconds (10⁻⁸ s).

**Q2402.** An electron in a hydrogen atom jumps from n=3 to n=1. The number of possible spectral lines that can be emitted is:
**Answer:** The electron can take two paths: a direct jump (3→1) or a cascade (3→2, then 2→1). These three distinct transitions (3→1, 3→2, 2→1) produce three different spectral lines.

**Q2403.** Bohr's model of the atom successfully explained:
**Answer:** The model's greatest success was its accurate prediction of the specific wavelengths of the spectral lines of hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (those with only one electron).

**Q2404.** In the process of stimulated emission, the emitted photon is identical to the incident photon in all aspects EXCEPT:
**Answer:** The key principle of stimulated emission is that it produces a perfect 'clone' of the incident photon in terms of frequency, phase, and direction. This is what allows for light amplification.

**Q2405.** The Brackett series in the hydrogen spectrum is formed by electron transitions ending at which energy level?
**Answer:** The Brackett series consists of all transitions where the electron's final energy level is n=4.

**Q2406.** The ground state energy of a hydrogen atom is -13.6 eV. What is the energy of the first excited state?
**Answer:** The first excited state corresponds to n=2. Using the formula Eₙ = -13.6/n² eV, we get E₂ = -13.6/2² = -13.6/4 = -3.4 eV.

**Q2407.** The concept of discrete energy levels in an atom was first introduced by:
**Answer:** Niels Bohr's key postulate was that electrons could only exist in specific, quantized energy levels, which directly explained the existence of atomic line spectra.

**Q2408.** The velocity of an electron in the first Bohr orbit is v₁. The velocity in the second orbit would be:
**Answer:** The velocity of an electron in the nth orbit is inversely proportional to n (vₙ ∝ 1/n). Therefore, for n=2, the velocity is v₂ = v₁/2.

**Q2409.** The shortest wavelength in the Balmer series corresponds to a transition from:
**Answer:** Shortest wavelength corresponds to the largest energy difference. For the Balmer series (ending at n=2), the largest energy drop is from the ionization limit (n = ∞).

**Q2410.** The laser used in barcode scanners is typically a:
**Answer:** Helium-Neon (He-Ne) lasers produce a characteristic low-power red light, making them ideal and common for applications like barcode scanning.

**Q2411.** According to Bohr's third postulate, an electron emits a photon when it:
**Answer:** A transition from a higher to a lower energy state releases a specific amount of energy, which is carried away by the emitted photon.

**Q2412.** The Pfund series of the hydrogen spectrum lies in the:
**Answer:** Transitions ending at n=5 (Pfund series) involve very small energy changes, placing these lines in the far-infrared part of the spectrum.

**Q2413.** What is the energy required to ionize a singly ionized helium atom (He⁺)?
**Answer:** The energy of an electron in a hydrogen-like ion is given by E\_n = -13.6 \* (Z²/n²) eV. For helium, the atomic number Z=2. Ionization energy is the energy needed to remove the electron from the ground state (n=1). So, E\_ion = -E₁ = -(-13.6 \* (2²/1²)) = 13.6 × 4 = 54.4 eV.

**Q2414.** A collection of atoms is bombarded with electrons. The resulting spectrum will most likely be:
**Answer:** When atoms are excited by collisions and then de-excite, they emit photons at specific, discrete wavelengths, creating an emission line spectrum.

**Q2415.** The ratio of the longest wavelength of the Lyman series to the longest wavelength of the Balmer series is:
**Answer:** Using the Rydberg formula, the longest Lyman wavelength (2→1) and longest Balmer wavelength (3→2) can be calculated. Their ratio λ\_Lyman / λ\_Balmer simplifies to 5/27.

**Q2416.** One of the major failings of the Rutherford atomic model was its inability to explain:
**Answer:** According to classical physics, an orbiting electron should continuously radiate energy and spiral into the nucleus. The Rutherford model couldn't explain why atoms are stable.

**Q2417.** The total energy of an electron in a stationary orbit is negative. This indicates that:
**Answer:** A negative total energy signifies a 'bound state'. It means that energy must be supplied to the electron to remove it from the influence of the nucleus.

**Q2418.** The minimum accelerating potential required to produce X-rays of wavelength λ is given by:
**Answer:** This is derived from equating the kinetic energy of the electron (eV) to the maximum energy of the emitted photon (hf = hc/λ).

**Q2419.** In stimulated absorption, an atom in the ground state absorbs a photon and moves to an excited state. This is the reverse process of:
**Answer:** Stimulated emission is the exact reverse: an incident photon causes an atom in an excited state to drop to the ground state, releasing a second photon.

**Q2420.** Which transition in a hydrogen atom emits a photon with the highest frequency?
**Answer:** Highest frequency means highest energy (E=hf). The largest possible energy drop in a hydrogen atom is from the ionization limit (n=∞) to the ground state (n=1).

**Q2421.** X-rays are a form of:
**Answer:** X-rays are high-energy photons and are part of the electromagnetic spectrum, with wavelengths shorter than ultraviolet light but longer than gamma rays.

**Q2422.** The 'pumping' mechanism in a ruby laser is:
**Answer:** A ruby laser uses a powerful flash lamp (a light source) to excite the chromium ions in the ruby crystal. This method is known as optical pumping.

**Q2423.** As the principal quantum number 'n' increases, the difference in energy between consecutive orbits:
**Answer:** The energy levels are given by Eₙ = -E₀/n². As n gets larger, the energy levels get closer together, so the gap between them decreases.

**Q2424.** The fact that each element has a unique line spectrum can be used for:
**Answer:** Since each element has a unique spectral 'fingerprint', spectroscopy is a powerful tool for chemical analysis, like determining the composition of distant stars.

**Q2425.** According to de Broglie's hypothesis, the reason for an electron remaining in a stable orbit is that the circumference of the orbit must be:
**Answer:** For a stable orbit, the electron must form a standing wave, which means the circumference (2πr) must contain an integer number of wavelengths (nλ). This condition, 2πr = nλ, directly leads to Bohr's postulate for the quantization of angular momentum.

**Q2426.** An X-ray tube operates with an accelerating potential of 50 kV. What is the minimum wavelength (cutoff wavelength) of the continuous X-ray spectrum produced?
**Answer:** The kinetic energy of the electron (eV) is completely converted into the energy of the highest-energy photon (hf\_max = hc/λ\_min). So, eV = hc/λ\_min. A useful shortcut is λ\_min (in nm) ≈ 1240 / V (in volts). Thus, λ\_min ≈ 1240 / 50000 = 0.0248 nm, which is approximately 0.025 nm.

**Q2427.** If an electron in a hydrogen atom is in the n=4 state, what is the maximum number of unique spectral lines that can be emitted as it transitions to lower energy levels?
**Answer:** The electron can cascade down in multiple steps. The total number of possible unique lines is given by the formula N = (n(n-1))/2, where n is the initial state. For n=4, N = (4(4-1))/2 = (4 × 3)/2 = 6. The possible transitions are 4→3, 4→2, 4→1, 3→2, 3→1, and 2→1.

**Q2428.** According to Moseley's Law, if the atomic number (Z) of the target anode in an X-ray tube is increased, the frequency of the characteristic X-rays:
**Answer:** Moseley's Law states that the frequency of characteristic X-rays is proportional to the square of the atomic number (√f ∝ Z). A higher Z means the inner-shell electrons are more tightly bound, leading to larger energy differences during transitions and thus higher frequency photons.

**Q2429.** A four-level laser system is more efficient than a three-level system primarily because:
**Answer:** In a three-level laser, the lower lasing level is the ground state, requiring over half the atoms to be pumped up. In a four-level laser, the lower lasing level is an intermediate excited state that is initially empty, making it much easier and less energy-intensive to create a population inversion.

**Q2430.** What is the ratio of the radius of the second Bohr orbit (n=2) to the radius of the fourth Bohr orbit (n=4) in a hydrogen atom?
**Answer:** The radius of a Bohr orbit is directly proportional to the square of the principal quantum number (rₙ ∝ n²). Therefore, the ratio r₂ / r₄ = 2² / 4² = 4 / 16 = 1/4. The ratio is 1:4.

**Q2431.** Which of the following phenomena could NOT be explained by Bohr's atomic model?
**Answer:** While Bohr's model successfully explained the spectra of single-electron systems like H and He⁺, it failed to account for several phenomena. These include the spectra of multi-electron atoms, the relative intensities of spectral lines, and the splitting of spectral lines in magnetic (Zeeman effect) and electric (Stark effect) fields.

**Q2432.** What is the function of the resonant cavity (mirrors) in a laser?
**Answer:** The mirrors trap photons, forcing them to pass through the active medium multiple times. This causes more stimulated emission and amplifies the light into a powerful beam.

**Q2433.** The penetration power of X-rays is high because they have:
**Answer:** A short wavelength implies high frequency and high photon energy (E=hf). This high energy allows them to pass through materials that block lower-energy visible light.

**Q2434.** The first line of the Paschen series results from the transition:
**Answer:** The Paschen series ends at n=3. The 'first line' (longest wavelength) corresponds to the smallest energy jump, which is from the next level up, n=4.

**Q2435.** The concept of a central positive nucleus was a conclusion from:
**Answer:** Rutherford's experiment, where alpha particles were deflected by a thin gold foil, led to the conclusion of a small, dense, positively charged nucleus.

**Q2436.** In a three-level laser system, the lasing transition occurs between the metastable state and:
**Answer:** In a three-level system, atoms are pumped to a high energy state, quickly decay to the metastable state, and then lase by transitioning from the metastable state back down to the ground state.

**Q2437.** The color of light emitted in the Balmer series with the longest wavelength is:
**Answer:** The longest wavelength (lowest energy) transition in the visible Balmer series is from n=3 to n=2. This specific line is called the H-alpha line and has a prominent red color.

---

**Q2438.** The direction of conventional current is the same as the direction of flow of:

**Answer:** Positive charges

**Q2439.** Ohm's law states that the current through a conductor is directly proportional to the:

 **B.** Ampere (A)
 **C.** Siemens (S)
 **D.** Ohm (Ω) ✓
**Answer:** Ohm (Ω)

**Q2441.** A carbon resistor is colored with Brown, Black, Orange, and Gold bands. What is its resistance and tolerance?

 **D.** 10 kΩ ± 5% ✓
**Answer:** 10 kΩ ± 5%

**Q2442.** Resistivity is the resistance of a conductor of unit length and unit:
 **A.** Cross-sectional area ✓
**Answer:** Cross-sectional area

**Q2443.** The reciprocal of resistivity is called:
**Answer:** Resistance

**Q2444.** For metallic conductors, the resistance increases with an increase in:
 **A.** Cross-sectional area
 **B.** Length
 **C.** Temperature ✓
**Answer:** Temperature

**Q2445.** Electric power is given by the formula:
 **A.** P = V/I
 **B.** P = V/R
 **C.** P = IR
 **D.** P = I²R ✓
**Answer:** P = I²R

**Q2446.** An electric iron of resistance 50 Ω is connected to a 200 V source. What is the amount of heat energy produced in 30 seconds?
 **A.** 800 J
 **B.** 1200 J
 **C.** 60000 J
 **D.** 24000 J ✓
**Answer:** 24000 J

**Q2447.** The commercial unit of electrical energy is:
 **A.** Kilowatt-hour (kWh) ✓
**Answer:** Kilowatt-hour (kWh)

**Q2448.** One kilowatt-hour is equal to:
 **A.** 3.6 × 10³ J
 **B.** 36 × 10⁵ J
 **C.** 6.3 × 10⁵ J
 **D.** 3.6 × 10⁶ J ✓
**Answer:** 3.6 × 10⁶ J

**Q2449.** Electromotive force (EMF) is most accurately described as:
 **A.** A force that pushes electrons
 **B.** The potential difference across a resistor
 **C.** The power dissipated in a circuit
 **D.** Energy supplied per unit charge by the source ✓
**Answer:** Energy supplied per unit charge by the source

**Q2450.** The terminal potential difference of a battery is always less than its EMF when:
 **A.** The battery is being charged
 **B.** The circuit is open
 **C.** The internal resistance is zero
 **D.** The battery is discharging (supplying current) ✓
**Answer:** The battery is discharging (supplying current)

**Q2451.** Kirchhoff's first rule is a statement of the conservation of:
 **A.** Energy
 **B.** Momentum
 **C.** Mass
 **D.** Charge ✓
**Answer:** Charge

**Q2452.** Kirchhoff's second rule is a statement of the conservation of:
 **A.** Charge
 **B.** Voltage
 **C.** Current
 **D.** Energy ✓
**Answer:** Energy

**Q2453.** A Wheatstone bridge is said to be balanced when:
 **A.** A large current flows through the galvanometer
 **B.** All four resistances are equal
 **C.** The battery EMF is high
 **D.** No current flows through the galvanometer ✓
**Answer:** No current flows through the galvanometer

**Q2454.** The condition for a balanced Wheatstone bridge is:
 **A.** R₁/R₂ = R₄/R₃
 **B.** R₁ + R₂ = R₃ + R₄
 **C.** R₁R₂ = R₃R₄
 **D.** R₁/R₂ = R₃/R₄ ✓
**Answer:** R₁/R₂ = R₃/R₄

**Q2455.** A potentiometer is an ideal device for measuring potential difference because:
 **A.** It has a very high resistance
 **B.** It is highly sensitive
 **C.** It is easy to use
 **D.** It draws no current from the source being measured at the balance point ✓
**Answer:** It draws no current from the source being measured at the balance point

**Q2456.** In a potentiometer experiment, a cell of EMF 1.25 V gives a balance point at 35 cm length of the wire. If this cell is replaced by another cell and the balance point shifts to 63 cm, what is the EMF of the second cell?
 **A.** 0.69 V
 **B.** 1.80 V
 **C.** 1.25 V
 **D.** 2.25 V ✓
**Answer:** 2.25 V

**Q2457.** To increase the sensitivity of a potentiometer, one should:
 **A.** Increase the current through the potentiometer wire
 **B.** Decrease the length of the potentiometer wire
 **C.** Decrease the resistance of the potentiometer wire
 **D.** Increase the length of the potentiometer wire ✓
**Answer:** Increase the length of the potentiometer wire

**Q2458.** The drift velocity of free electrons in a conductor is of the order of:
 **A.** 3 × 10⁸ m/s
 **B.** 10³ m/s
 **C.** 0 m/s
 **D.** 10⁻³ m/s ✓
**Answer:** 10⁻³ m/s

**Q2459.** If three resistors of 2 Ω, 3 Ω, and 6 Ω are connected in parallel, their equivalent resistance is:
 **A.** 11 Ω
 **B.** 36 Ω
 **C.** 5.5 Ω

**Answer:** 1 Ω

**Q2460.** The colour code for a resistor of 270 Ω with 5% tolerance is:

**Answer:** Red, Violet, Brown, Gold

**Q2462.** Which of the following is a non-ohmic device?

**Q2463.** The temperature coefficient of resistance is negative for:

**Q2465.** For maximum power output from a source, the external resistance must be:
 **A.** Greater than the internal resistance

**Q2466.** If two bulbs rated 60W and 100W are connected in series to a mains supply, which one will glow brighter?
 **A.** The 100W bulb
 **B.** Both will glow with the same brightness
 **C.** Neither will glow
 **D.** The 60W bulb ✓
**Answer:** The 60W bulb

**Q2467.** What is the primary function of a rheostat in a circuit?

 **A.** Equal to the total EMF

**Q2470.** A meter bridge is an application of which principle?

**Q2472.** An ideal ammeter has a resistance that is:

**Q2473.** Two bulbs are rated 25 W - 220 V and 100 W - 220 V. They are connected in series to a 440 V supply. Which bulb will fuse?

**Q2509.** When a battery is being charged, its terminal potential difference (V) is related to its EMF (E) and internal resistance (r) by:

**Q2510.** An NTC thermistor is a device where the resistance:
 **A.** Increases with increasing temperature

 **D.** Decreases with increasing temperature ✓
**Answer:** Decreases with increasing temperature

**Q2511.** The energy dissipated by a resistor can be calculated using:
 **A.** E = V/I
 **B.** E = IR
 **C.** E = V/t
 **D.** E = VIt ✓
**Answer:** E = VIt

**Q2512.** If a voltmeter is connected in series in a circuit, the current will be:
 **A.** Very high
 **B.** Unchanged
 **C.** The same as the voltage reading
 **D.** Very low or nearly zero ✓
**Answer:** Very low or nearly zero

**Q2513.** A 2 kW electric heater is operated for 5 hours. If the cost per unit (kWh) is Rs. 9, what is the total cost of the energy consumed?
 **A.** Rs. 18
 **B.** Rs. 45
 **C.** Rs. 9000
 **D.** Rs. 90 ✓
**Answer:** Rs. 90

**Q2514.** In a household circuit, appliances are connected in parallel so that:
 **A.** The total current is minimized
 **B.** The total power consumed is low
 **C.** If one appliance fails, all others stop working
 **D.** Each appliance gets the same voltage ✓
**Answer:** Each appliance gets the same voltage

**Q2515.** The relationship between current density (J), conductivity (σ), and electric field (E) is:
 **A.** J = E / σ
 **B.** J = σ/E
 **C.** J = E + σ
 **D.** J = σE ✓
**Answer:** J = σE

**Q2516.** The SI unit of charge is the Coulomb. One Coulomb is equivalent to:
 **A.** Ampere per second
 **B.** Volt per meter
 **C.** Joule per Volt
 **D.** Ampere-second ✓
**Answer:** Ampere-second

**Q2517.** Which device is based on the heating effect of current?
 **A.** Electric Motor
 **B.** Galvanometer
 **C.** Transformer
 **D.** Electric Fuse ✓
**Answer:** Electric Fuse

**Q2518.** A material with a resistivity between that of conductors and insulators is called a:
 **A.** Superconductor
 **B.** Dielectric
 **C.** Electrolyte
 **D.** Semiconductor ✓
**Answer:** Semiconductor

**Q2519.** The slope of a current-voltage (I-V) graph represents:
 **A.** Resistance
 **B.** Resistivity
 **C.** Power
 **D.** Conductance ✓
**Answer:** Conductance

**Q2520.** Ohm's law is NOT obeyed by:
 **A.** Conductors at constant temperature
 **B.** Electrolytes
 **C.** Carbon resistors
 **D.** Vacuum tubes and transistors ✓
**Answer:** Vacuum tubes and transistors

**Q2521.** A battery of EMF 6 V and internal resistance 0.5 Ω is connected to a resistor. If the current in the circuit is 1.0 A, what is the resistance of the external resistor?
 **A.** 6.0 Ω
 **B.** 12.0 Ω
 **C.** 6.5 Ω
 **D.** 5.5 Ω ✓
**Answer:** 5.5 Ω

**Q2522.** For a potentiometer to function properly, the EMF of the driver cell in the primary circuit must be:
 **A.** Less than the EMF of the cell to be measured
 **B.** Exactly equal to the EMF of the cell to be measured
 **C.** Zero
 **D.** Greater than the EMF of the cell to be measured ✓
**Answer:** Greater than the EMF of the cell to be measured

**Q2523.** A shunt is a low-resistance wire connected in parallel with a galvanometer to convert it into:
 **A.** A voltmeter
 **B.** A potentiometer
 **C.** A rheostat
 **D.** An ammeter ✓
**Answer:** An ammeter

**Q2524.** What happens to the drift velocity of electrons if the cross-sectional area of a current-carrying wire is decreased, while the current remains constant?
 **A.** It decreases
 **B.** It remains the same
 **C.** It becomes zero
 **D.** It increases ✓
**Answer:** It increases

**Q2525.** The power dissipated in a circuit is 12W when the voltage is 6V. What is the resistance of the circuit?
 **A.** 2 Ω
 **B.** 72 Ω
 **C.** 0.5 Ω
 **D.** 3 Ω ✓
**Answer:** 3 Ω

**Q2526.** In a meter bridge experiment, the null point is found at 40 cm from one end. If a known resistance of 6 Ω is in the left gap, the unknown resistance is:
 **A.** 4 Ω
 **B.** 2.4 Ω
 **C.** 15 Ω
 **D.** 9 Ω ✓
**Answer:** 9 Ω

**Q2527.** If the temperature of a metallic conductor is decreased, the relaxation time of free electrons will:
**Answer:** Remain the same

**Q2528.** A battery with an EMF of 1.5 V is connected to a 0.5 Ω resistor, and a current of 2 A flows. The internal resistance of the battery is:
 **A.** 1.0 Ω
 **B.** 0.5 Ω
 **C.** 0.75 Ω
 **D.** 0.25 Ω ✓
**Answer:** 0.25 Ω

**Q2529.** Which of these is NOT a source of EMF?
 **A.** A solar cell
 **B.** A thermocouple
 **C.** An electric generator
 **D.** A resistor ✓
**Answer:** A resistor

**Q2530.** The Ampere-hour (Ah) is a unit of:
 **A.** Power
 **B.** Energy
 **C.** Current
 **D.** Electric Charge ✓
**Answer:** Electric Charge

---

### Dawn Of Modern Physics (107 Questions)

**Q2531.** An ideal black body is:
**Answer:** An ideal black body is defined as a body that absorbs all incident radiation and is also a perfect emitter of radiation, with the emission spectrum depending only on its temperature.

**Q2532.** According to Planck's quantum theory, energy is radiated or absorbed:
**Answer:** Planck's fundamental postulate is that energy is quantized, meaning it is emitted or absorbed in discrete packets (quanta), resolving the ultraviolet catastrophe.

**Q2533.** The energy of a single photon is directly proportional to its:
**Answer:** The relationship is given by the formula E = hf, where 'h' is Planck's constant. Energy is directly proportional to frequency.

**Q2534.** The photoelectric effect is the phenomenon where:
**Answer:** The photoelectric effect is correctly defined as the emission of electrons (photoelectrons) from a material upon exposure to electromagnetic radiation of a suitable frequency.

**Q2535.** In the photoelectric effect, the kinetic energy of the emitted photoelectrons depends on the:
**Answer:** According to Einstein's photoelectric equation (K.E. = hf - φ), the maximum kinetic energy of photoelectrons is determined by the frequency of the incident light, not its intensity.

**Q2536.** The minimum frequency of incident light required to cause photoemission is called the:
**Answer:** The threshold frequency is the correct term for the minimum frequency needed to overcome the work function of the metal and eject an electron.

**Q2537.** The Compton effect demonstrates that:
**Answer:** The Compton effect, where an X-ray photon scatters off an electron and loses energy, is explained by treating the photon as a particle with momentum (p = h/λ), conserving both energy and momentum in the collision.

**Q2538.** Pair production is a process where a photon creates:
**Answer:** Pair production is the conversion of energy (a high-energy photon) into matter, creating a particle-antiparticle pair (electron and positron) to conserve charge and other quantum numbers.

**Q2539.** For pair production to occur, the minimum energy of the photon must be:
**Answer:** The photon's energy must be at least equal to the sum of the rest mass energies of the electron (0.51 MeV) and the positron (0.51 MeV), totaling 1.02 MeV.

**Q2540.** The reverse process of pair production is known as:
**Answer:** Pair annihilation occurs when a particle and its antiparticle (e.g., electron and positron) collide, converting their mass back into energy, typically in the form of two photons.

**Q2541.** The de Broglie hypothesis states that:
**Answer:** De Broglie extended wave-particle duality to all matter, proposing that any moving particle with momentum 'p' has an associated wavelength given by λ = h/p.

**Q2542.** The de Broglie wavelength of a particle is given by the equation:
**Answer:** The de Broglie equation correctly relates a particle's wavelength (λ) to its momentum (p) via Planck's constant (h).

**Q2543.** The wave nature of electrons was experimentally confirmed by:
**Answer:** Davisson and Germer observed that a beam of electrons scattered by a nickel crystal produced a diffraction pattern, which is a definitive characteristic of waves.

**Q2544.** Heisenberg's Uncertainty Principle states it is impossible to simultaneously know a particle's:
**Answer:** The principle's most common formulation states a fundamental limit to the precision with which the position and momentum of a particle can be known simultaneously (ΔxΔp ≥ h/4π).

**Q2545.** If the uncertainty in the position of a particle is decreased, the uncertainty in its momentum:
**Answer:** The relationship is inverse; according to the uncertainty principle, if you know the position more precisely (smaller Δx), you must know the momentum less precisely (larger Δp).

**Q2546.** The work function (φ) of a metal is:
**Answer:** The work function is a property of a given metal that defines the minimum energy (binding energy) needed for an electron to escape from its surface.

**Q2547.** A photocell is a device that converts:
**Answer:** Photocells operate based on the photoelectric effect, generating a current when exposed to light, thus converting light energy into electrical energy.

**Q2548.** A radioactive nucleus has a half-life of 5000 years. What fraction of the original sample will be left after 20,000 years?
**Answer:** Number of half-lives = 20,000 years / 5000 years = 4. The fraction remaining is (1/2) to the power of 4, which is 1/16.

**Q2549.** The binding energy of a Uranium-235 nucleus is 1786 MeV. What is the binding energy per nucleon?
**Answer:** Binding energy per nucleon = Total binding energy / number of nucleons = 1786 MeV / 235 = 7.6 MeV.

**Q2550.** According to special relativity, the laws of physics are the same in all:
**Answer:** This is the first postulate of special relativity. It applies to inertial (non-accelerating) frames of reference, stating that there is no absolute or preferred state of rest.

**Q2551.** The second postulate of special relativity states that the speed of light in a vacuum:
**Answer:** This radical postulate states that the speed of light, 'c', is a universal constant, independent of the motion of the source or the observer.

**Q2552.** Which of the following is a consequence of special relativity?
**Answer:** Time dilation (moving clocks run slower), length contraction, and mass-energy equivalence are all key consequences derived from the two postulates of special relativity.

**Q2553.** The equation E=mc² expresses the:
**Answer:** This famous equation shows that mass is a form of energy and that a small amount of mass can be converted into a large amount of energy, and vice versa.

**Q2554.** An electron microscope has higher resolution than an optical one because:
**Answer:** The ability to resolve fine details is limited by the wavelength of the probe. High-energy electrons have a very short de Broglie wavelength, allowing for much higher resolution than is possible with longer-wavelength visible light.

**Q2555.** In a nuclear reaction, the mass defect is calculated to be 0.05 atomic mass units (amu). How much energy is released in Mega-electron Volts (MeV)?
**Answer:** The energy released is found by multiplying the mass defect by 931.5 MeV/amu. Energy = 0.05 amu \* 931.5 MeV/amu = 46.575 MeV.

**Q2556.** In blackbody radiation, the 'ultraviolet catastrophe' was the failure of:
**Answer:** The classical Rayleigh-Jeans law incorrectly predicted that the energy radiated by a blackbody would increase infinitely as the wavelength decreased into the UV region, a clear contradiction of experimental data.

**Q2557.** If light below the threshold frequency hits a metal, what happens if its intensity is increased?
**Answer:** Photoemission depends on the energy of individual photons (frequency), not the number of photons (intensity). If the photon energy is below the work function, no amount of intensity will cause emission.

**Q2558.** A radioactive isotope has a half-life of 15 hours. If a sample initially contains 800 grams of this isotope, how much will remain after 60 hours?
**Answer:** The number of half-lives is 60 hours / 15 hours = 4. After 1st half-life: 400g. After 2nd: 200g. After 3rd: 100g. After 4th: 50g.

**Q2559.** Which particle has the longest de Broglie wavelength when all are moving at the same speed?
**Answer:** Since de Broglie wavelength is inversely proportional to momentum (λ = h/mv), the particle with the smallest mass (the electron) will have the longest wavelength for a given speed.

**Q2560.** The rest mass of a photon is:
**Answer:** Photons are massless particles. They possess energy and momentum due to their motion at speed 'c', but they have no rest mass.

**Q2561.** The half-life of Iodine-131 is 8 days. If a hospital receives a shipment of 20 mg, how much Iodine-131 will be left after 24 days?
**Answer:** The number of half-lives is 24 days / 8 days = 3. After 1st half-life: 10 mg. After 2nd: 5 mg. After 3rd: 2.5 mg.

**Q2562.** In pair annihilation, an electron and a positron typically produce:
**Answer:** Producing two photons traveling in opposite directions allows for the conservation of both energy and momentum, as the initial momentum of the electron-positron system is often zero.

**Q2563.** The stopping potential in a photoelectric experiment is a measure of the:
**Answer:** The stopping potential is the reverse voltage required to stop the most energetic electrons, so their maximum kinetic energy is equal to the work done by this potential (K.E.max = eVs).

**Q2564.** The concept of wave-particle duality applies to:
**Answer:** Wave-particle duality is a fundamental principle of quantum mechanics, stating that everything exhibits both wave and particle properties depending on the experiment being performed.

**Q2565.** Wien's displacement law states the peak wavelength of blackbody radiation is:
**Answer:** The law (λ\_max \* T = constant) correctly states that as an object's temperature increases, the peak of its emission spectrum shifts to shorter wavelengths.

**Q2566.** The Compton shift (Δλ) is maximum when the scattering angle is:
**Answer:** The formula for the shift is Δλ = (h/mₑc)(1 - cosθ). This value is maximized when cosθ = -1, which occurs at a backscattering angle of θ = 180°.

**Q2567.** The existence of a threshold frequency in the photoelectric effect is best explained by:
**Answer:** The particle theory states that a single photon must have enough energy (hf) to overcome the work function (φ). If hf < φ, no emission occurs, regardless of intensity, thus explaining the threshold.

**Q2568.** The NAVSTAR Global Positioning System (GPS) must account for effects from:
**Answer:** GPS requires corrections from both: special relativity for the high speed of the satellites (time dilation) and general relativity for the weaker gravitational field they experience.

**Q2569.** Planck's constant (h) has units of:
**Answer:** From the equation E=hf, we can see that h=E/f. The units are Joules / (1/second), which is Joule-seconds (J·s), a unit of angular momentum or action.

**Q2570.** Which phenomenon provides evidence for the quantization of energy?
**Answer:** Max Planck's successful solution to the blackbody radiation problem required the revolutionary assumption that the energy of oscillators was quantized.

**Q2571.** Annihilation of matter results in the creation of:
**Answer:** Mass is converted into energy according to E=mc², typically producing high-energy photons (gamma rays).

**Q2572.** Relativistic mass increase is significant only at:
**Answer:** The relativistic factor gamma (γ) only becomes significantly greater than 1, causing a noticeable mass increase, as velocity approaches the speed of light, c.

**Q2573.** The uncertainty principle implies that:
**Answer:** The principle is a fundamental property of nature. The wavelike nature of a particle means its position and momentum are inherently 'fuzzy' and cannot be simultaneously defined with perfect precision.

**Q2574.** If a red light and a blue light have the same intensity, which one has more photons per second?
**Answer:** Intensity is total energy per second. Since blue light photons have more energy individually than red light photons, more of the lower-energy red photons are needed to deliver the same total energy per second.

**Q2575.** The Davisson-Germer experiment involved the scattering of electrons from a:
**Answer:** The regular atomic spacing in the nickel crystal acted as a natural diffraction grating for the electron waves, producing the observed interference pattern.

**Q2576.** A particle's de Broglie wavelength is 1 nm. If its momentum is doubled, its new wavelength will be:
**Answer:** Since wavelength is inversely proportional to momentum (λ = h/p), doubling the momentum will halve the wavelength.

**Q2577.** Classical physics is a good approximation of modern physics at:
**Answer:** In the realm of everyday experience (slow speeds, large objects), the effects of relativity and quantum mechanics are negligible, and classical physics provides extremely accurate predictions.

**Q2578.** The photoelectric current is directly proportional to the:
**Answer:** Higher intensity means more photons arriving per second. If the frequency is above the threshold, this results in more electrons being ejected per second, leading to a larger current.

**Q2579.** The 'h' in the equation E=hf stands for:
**Answer:** Max Planck introduced this fundamental constant of nature (h ≈ 6.626 x 10⁻³⁴ J·s) to explain the quantization of energy in blackbody radiation.

**Q2580.** The fact that the photoelectric effect is almost instantaneous supports:
**Answer:** The wave model predicted a time delay for energy to accumulate. The instantaneous emission supports the particle model, where a single photon's energy is transferred to an electron in a single, immediate event.

**Q2581.** An observer on a spaceship moving at 0.9c would observe time on Earth to be:
**Answer:** This is a consequence of time dilation and the principle of relativity. From the spaceship's frame of reference, Earth is the moving clock, and it is therefore observed to be running slower.

**Q2582.** The energy-time uncertainty principle is given by:
**Answer:** This relation (often written with ħ = h/2π) indicates that a state that exists for a short time (small Δt) has a large uncertainty in its energy (large ΔE).

**Q2583.** Modern physics began as an attempt to explain phenomena that:
**Answer:** Phenomena like blackbody radiation, the photoelectric effect, and atomic spectra directly contradicted the predictions of classical theories, necessitating the new approaches of relativity and quantum mechanics.

**Q2584.** An observer moving at a relativistic speed with a light source will measure the speed of the emitted light as:
**Answer:** This is the second postulate of special relativity: the speed of light in a vacuum, 'c', is constant for all inertial observers, regardless of the source's or observer's motion.

**Q2585.** The Stefan-Boltzmann law relates the total power radiated by a black body to its:
**Answer:** The law (P = σAT⁴) states that the total power radiated is directly proportional to the fourth power of the absolute temperature.

**Q2586.** In the photoelectric effect, if the frequency of the incident light is doubled, the max K.E. of photoelectrons will:
**Answer:** Let K₁ = hf - φ. The new energy is K₂ = h(2f) - φ = 2hf - φ. Substituting hf = K₁ + φ gives K₂ = 2(K₁ + φ) - φ = 2K₁ + φ. Since φ > 0, K₂ > 2K₁.

**Q2587.** A radioactive isotope has a half-life of 15 hours. If a sample initially contains 800 grams of this isotope, how much will remain after 60 hours?
**Answer:** The number of half-lives is 60 hours / 15 hours = 4. After 1st half-life: 400g. After 2nd: 200g. After 3rd: 100g. After 4th: 50g.

**Q2588.** Which statement best describes the 'Compton Wavelength'?
**Answer:** Setting the photon energy equal to the electron's rest mass energy (E = hc/λ = mₑc²) gives the Compton wavelength, λ\_c = h/(mₑc).

**Q2589.** Macroscopic objects in motion do not show observable wave properties because:
**Answer:** Due to their large mass, macroscopic objects have enormous momentum, resulting in a de Broglie wavelength (λ = h/p) so small that diffraction effects are impossible to detect.

**Q2590.** Pair production can only occur in the vicinity of a nucleus because it is needed to:
**Answer:** A photon cannot decay into a pair in empty space as momentum cannot be conserved. The nearby nucleus absorbs some recoil momentum, satisfying the conservation law.

**Q2591.** According to the energy-time uncertainty principle, a virtual particle that exists for a very short time can have a:
**Answer:** The relation ΔE Δt ≥ ħ/2 implies that if the lifetime Δt is small, the uncertainty in energy ΔE must be large, allowing for temporary 'borrowing' of energy.

**Q2592.** A sample has an initial activity of 1200 decays per minute. If its half-life is 4 minutes, what will its activity be after 12 minutes?
**Answer:** The number of half-lives is 12 minutes / 4 minutes = 3. After 1st half-life: 600. After 2nd: 300. After 3rd: 150 decays per minute.

**Q2593.** An astronaut ages slower traveling at near-light speed. From the astronaut's perspective:
**Answer:** According to the principle of relativity, all motion is relative. From the astronaut's frame, Earth is the moving object, so its clock appears to run slower.

**Q2594.** What happens to the total energy radiated by a black body as its temperature is increased?
**Answer:** According to the Stefan-Boltzmann law, the total radiated power (energy per time) increases dramatically with the fourth power of the temperature.

**Q2595.** A metal has a work function of 2.0 eV. What is the maximum wavelength of light that can cause photoemission?
**Answer:** The threshold wavelength is λ₀ = hc/φ. First convert φ to Joules: 2.0 eV \* 1.6e-19 J/eV = 3.2e-19 J. Then λ₀ = (6.63e-34 \* 3e8) / 3.2e-19 ≈ 6.2e-7 m = 620 nm.

**Q2596.** The momentum of a photon is given by:
**Answer:** This relationship is derived from the de Broglie relation (p = h/λ) and applies to photons as well as massive particles.

**Q2597.** An electron and a proton are accelerated through the same potential difference. Which has the shorter de Broglie wavelength?
**Answer:** Both gain the same kinetic energy (K.E.). Since p = √(2m\*K.E.), the more massive proton has greater momentum and therefore a shorter wavelength (λ = h/p).

**Q2598.** The 'frame dragging' effect is a prediction of:
**Answer:** Frame dragging is a prediction of General Relativity where a massive rotating object 'drags' the fabric of spacetime around with it.

**Q2599.** The intensity of a beam of light is a measure of:
**Answer:** Intensity is power per unit area. Since power is energy per time, intensity is proportional to the number of photons arriving per unit area per unit time.

**Q2600.** A 1-meter stick on a spaceship moves at 0.8c. What length would an Earth observer measure?
**Answer:** The contracted length is L = L₀√(1 - v²/c²). So, L = 1 \* √(1 - 0.8²) = √(1 - 0.64) = √0.36 = 0.6 m.

**Q2601.** Which of these is NOT a postulate of the special theory of relativity?
**Answer:** Mass-energy equivalence (E=mc²) is a profound consequence derived from the two postulates, but it is not a starting assumption (postulate) itself.

**Q2602.** In an electron microscope, the 'lenses' used to focus the electron beam are:
**Answer:** Since electrons are charged particles, their path can be bent and focused by carefully shaped magnetic fields, which act as magnetic lenses.

**Q2603.** A photon scatters off a stationary electron. Which of the following is impossible?
**Answer:** A decrease in wavelength would mean the photon gained energy. This is impossible as it would violate the conservation of energy, since the electron was initially at rest.

**Q2604.** The resolution of a measurement is fundamentally limited by:
**Answer:** Due to the wave nature of matter and radiation, it is impossible to resolve details smaller than the wavelength of the probe being used to observe the system. This is a fundamental limit.

**Q2605.** If you determine an electron's position with great accuracy, what is the consequence?
**Answer:** The uncertainty principle dictates an inverse relationship. A small uncertainty in position (Δx) necessarily leads to a large uncertainty in momentum (Δp).

**Q2606.** The total relativistic energy of a particle (E = γm₀c²) consists of:
**Answer:** The total energy is the sum of the energy a particle has due to its mass (m₀c²) and the additional energy it has due to its motion (K.E.).

**Q2607.** The idea that light is quantized (photons) was first proposed by Einstein to explain:
**Answer:** Einstein's 1905 paper explained the features of the photoelectric effect by postulating that light itself is made of discrete energy quanta, which he called photons.

**Q2608.** A star appears yellowish-white. If its temperature were to double, it would likely appear:
**Answer:** According to Wien's displacement law, doubling the temperature shifts the peak emission to a much shorter wavelength, into the blue or ultraviolet part of the spectrum.

**Q2609.** Which of the following is conserved during pair annihilation?
**Answer:** Momentum, total energy (mass-energy), and charge are all fundamental quantities that are conserved in this process.

**Q2610.** The 'relativity of simultaneity' means that:
**Answer:** This is a key consequence of special relativity. Whether two spatially separated events occur at the same time depends on the observer's state of motion.

**Q2611.** The energy of a photon in a beam of light depends only on its:
**Answer:** The relationship is given by Planck's fundamental equation, E = hf.

**Q2612.** In the Davisson-Germer experiment, the diffraction pattern was produced because:
**Answer:** Diffraction effects are prominent only when the wavelength of a wave is similar in size to the aperture or spacing of a grating, which in this case was the crystal lattice.

**Q2613.** The Michelson-Morley experiment was designed to detect:
**Answer:** The experiment's 'null result' famously failed to detect the hypothetical ether wind, which was a crucial step towards the development of special relativity.

**Q2614.** A particle with zero rest mass must travel:
**Answer:** From the energy-momentum relation E² = (pc)² + (m₀c²)², if rest mass m₀=0, then E=pc. This relationship only holds for particles, like photons, that travel at speed c.

**Q2615.** What is the relativistic momentum of a proton moving at 0.99c?
**Answer:** Relativistic momentum is p = γm₀v. At 0.99c, the Lorentz factor γ is approximately 7.1, so the momentum is over 7 times its classical value.

**Q2616.** If a particle's energy uncertainty is zero, what does this imply about the measurement time?
**Answer:** From ΔE Δt ≥ ħ/2, if ΔE = 0, then Δt must be infinitely large. This means a state with a perfectly defined energy must be stable and exist forever.

**Q2617.** The 'quantization' of a physical quantity means that it:
**Answer:** This is the definition of quantization. The quantity is restricted to specific, discrete levels rather than being able to take on any continuous value.

**Q2618.** Why can pair production not be caused by a visible light photon?
**Answer:** Visible light photons have energies of a few electron-volts, which is far below the minimum energy threshold of 1.02 MeV required to create an electron-positron pair.

**Q2619.** The phenomenon of wave-particle duality was first introduced for:
**Answer:** Einstein's explanation of the photoelectric effect solidified the idea that light, long considered a wave, also has particle-like properties (photons).

**Q2620.** According to special relativity, which of the following is an invariant quantity?
**Answer:** The spacetime interval, defined as (Δs)² = (cΔt)² - (Δx)², is an invariant quantity in special relativity, meaning all inertial observers will calculate the same value for it.

**Q2621.** The failure of classical physics to explain blackbody radiation is known as:
**Answer:** The classical Rayleigh-Jeans law predicted infinite energy emission at short wavelengths, a failure famously dubbed the 'Ultraviolet Catastrophe'.

**Q2622.** If the stopping potential for a certain metal is 1.5 V, what is the max K.E. of the photoelectrons?
**Answer:** The maximum kinetic energy is directly related to the stopping potential by K.E.max = e\*Vs. A stopping potential of 1.5 Volts corresponds to a kinetic energy of 1.5 electron-Volts.

**Q2623.** In the Compton effect, the greatest change in wavelength occurs when the photon is scattered by:
**Answer:** This 'backscattering' angle of 180° corresponds to the maximum energy transfer to the electron and thus the maximum increase in the photon's wavelength.

**Q2624.** A particle is confined to a box. If the size of the box is decreased, the uncertainty in its momentum will:
**Answer:** Confining the particle to a smaller space decreases the uncertainty in its position (Δx). By the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle, this must cause an increase in the uncertainty of its momentum (Δp).

**Q2625.** The 'twin paradox' is resolved by considering that:
**Answer:** The situation is not symmetrical. The traveling twin must accelerate to leave, turn around, and return. This acceleration places them in non-inertial frames, breaking the symmetry and causing a real age difference.

**Q2626.** The spectrum of radiation from a black body is:
**Answer:** A black body emits radiation at all wavelengths, forming a continuous spectrum whose shape and peak intensity depend only on the body's temperature.

**Q2627.** If an incident photon in a Compton scattering event has energy E, the scattered photon will have energy E' such that:
**Answer:** The photon transfers some of its energy to the electron, so its final energy (E') must be less than its initial energy (E), unless the scattering angle is zero.

**Q2628.** A photocell works on the principle of:
**Answer:** A photocell is a direct application where incident light ejects electrons from a surface, creating a measurable electric current, which is the definition of the photoelectric effect.

**Q2629.** According to de Broglie, the wave associated with a particle is a:
**Answer:** In the modern quantum interpretation, the square of the amplitude of the matter wave at a point is proportional to the probability of finding the particle at that point.

**Q2630.** The equation m = m₀ / √(1 - v²/c²) describes:
**Answer:** This formula shows that the measured (relativistic) mass 'm' of an object increases with its velocity relative to the observer, from its rest mass 'm₀'.

**Q2631.** Which experiment provided the most direct evidence for the wave nature of electrons?
**Answer:** The Davisson-Germer experiment showed that a beam of electrons could be diffracted by a crystal lattice, a behavior that is exclusively characteristic of waves.

**Q2632.** The work function of a material depends on:
**Answer:** The work function is an intrinsic, characteristic property of a specific material, representing how tightly its electrons are bound to the surface.

**Q2633.** A sample initially contains 6.4 x 10²⁰ radioactive atoms. If its half-life is 100 years, how many atoms will have decayed after 500 years?
**Answer:** Number of half-lives = 500 / 100 = 5. Atoms remaining = (6.4 x 10²⁰) \* (1/32) = 0.2 x 10²⁰. Atoms decayed = Initial - Remaining = (6.4 x 10²⁰) - (0.2 x 10²⁰) = 6.2 x 10²⁰ atoms.

**Q2634.** The activity of a sample is 8000 Bq. After 6 hours, its activity is 1000 Bq. What is its half-life?
**Answer:** The activity reduced from 8000 -> 4000 -> 2000 -> 1000. This is a reduction by a factor of 8 (2\*2\*2), which means 3 half-lives have passed in 6 hours. So, the half-life is 6 hours / 3 = 2 hours.

**Q2635.** An electron and a positron, each with a rest mass energy of 0.511 MeV, annihilate each other. What is the total energy of the photons produced?
**Answer:** The total energy produced is the sum of the rest mass energies of both particles. Total Energy = 0.511 MeV + 0.511 MeV = 1.022 MeV.

**Q2636.** In order to observe an object, the wavelength of the probing radiation must be:
**Answer:** The resolution of any imaging system is fundamentally limited by diffraction. To 'see' an object's features, the wavelength used must be smaller than those features.

**Q2637.** The 'Dawn of Modern Physics' refers to the period when:
**Answer:** The late 19th and early 20th centuries saw the emergence of problems (like blackbody radiation) that classical physics could not solve, heralding the new era of modern physics.
